@@ i | About Bhakti – Introduction |
About Bhakti – INTRODUCTION
In this world full of desire, restlessness and duties, all of us are on a search.
What is that search? — for peace, for bliss, for a love that never diminishes.
But there is one truth that the great souls have given us.
What we seek is not somewhere outside; it is within us.
What awakens it? — Bhakti, devotion.

In the Bhagavad Gita, Lord Sri Krishna speaks of four kinds of devotees:
The four kinds of devotees
Ārta Bhakta – the devotee who takes refuge in the Lord in times of suffering
Arthārthī Bhakta – one who approaches the Lord seeking worldly good
Jijñāsu Bhakta – the seeker who longs to know God
Jñānī Bhakta – the devotee who asks for nothing and desires only the Lord
The Lord accepts all of them with compassion.
But the devotee who loves the Lord alone, expecting nothing, is the most dear to Him.
That is Prema Bhakti, devotion of pure love.
It is not an emotion;
it is a state;
it is not something we do; it is something we become.

The teaching of Prahlada
The boy Prahlada lived and showed the supreme truth of devotion.
He said:
śravaṇaṁ kīrtanaṁ viṣṇōḥ smaraṇaṁ pādasēvanam ।
arcanaṁ vandanaṁ dāsyaṁ sakhyamātmanivēdanam ॥
Meaning:
Devotion is —
listening (śravaṇam) to the Lord's divine play
singing (kīrtanam) His name
always remembering (smaraṇam) Him
serving His feet (pādasēvanam)
worshipping (arcanam) Him
bowing (vandanam) to Him
living as His servant (dāsyam)
being with Him as a friend (sakhyam)
offering oneself completely to Him (ātmanivēdanam)

The path of the great souls
Who showed us this path of devotion?
Bhadrachala Ramadasa – who saw Rama even in a prison cell
Purandaradasa – who spread devotion into every home
Sadashiva Brahmendra – the yogi absorbed in silence
Tyagaraja Swamigal – who melted in the name of Rama
Annamacharya – who sang of the Lord of Tirupati
Narayana Tirtha – who turned Krishna's divine play into music
Vijaya Gopala – who spread the glory of the divine name
Meera Bai – who melted completely in devotion to Krishna
Tulasidasa – for whom the name of Rama was life itself
The Alvars – who poured out devotion in Tamil
The Marathi saints – who lifted villages into devotion with their abhangs
All of them are united in one thing —
Nama Sankirtanam and Bhakti.

The glory of Nama Sankirtanam
Nama Sankirtanam is not a music performance.
It is not an achievement.
It is a path that dissolves the ego.
A way that opens the heart.
The Lord once said:
“There are only two simple ways to liberation —
Nama Sankirtanam and Prapatti (surrender).”
No great sacrifice, no difficult practice — one name is enough.
Even if that name begins on the tongue,
it will reach the heart.

What is bliss (ānandam)?
Bliss is not ordinary pleasure.
Pleasure comes and goes.
But bliss is steady.
It does not change even when the world changes.
That is ānandam.
The feeling “I am not alone; I belong to the Lord”
is bliss.

The role of kirtans
The kirtans we sing are not mere songs.
They are lamps on the path of devotion.
Devotion is not something done one day a week.
It is the flow of our life.
When we sing together, we grow together.
When we remember the name, the ego disappears.

About Harinamadwar
A sacred platform created to bring this journey of devotion together —
“Harinamadwar”
It is a confluence of:
satsang
nama kirtanam
sharing of devotion
spiritual growth
It is not a group.
It is a movement.
The name should live not only in books or on the stage,
but in our homes, in our breath, in our lives.

Conclusion
Let us walk in the path of the great souls.
Let us chant the name.
Let us live in love.
Let us abide in bliss.

Hari Nāma Saṅkīrtanam Jayati!

@@ 1.3 | Dhyana Slokam |
Overall Summary
These thirteen opening verses (avatārikā ślōkams) prepare the singers before the bhajan begins. In order, they:
- pray to Ganesha to remove obstacles;
- bow to Bodhendra Swamigal, the master of the Name;
- remember the great devotees, from Prahlada to Vibhishana;
- proclaim the glory of singing the Name in this age (Kali yuga);
- conclude that the essence of all scriptures is to remember Narayana;
- meditate on Rama with Sita, teaching the sages;
- pray to Hanuman for strength, courage and clear speech.

Key Takeaway
Begin humbly: remove obstacles, remember the Guru and the devotees, and know why we sing.
For each of us: life is short and the Name is the simplest, surest path in this age. Sing it without shyness, every day.

Deeper Meaning
1. Clearing the way (sarva vighna upaśāntayē)
"śuklāmbaradharam" and "śrīkāntō mātulō" pray to Ganesha. The second verse calls Vishnu his uncle and Shiva his father, binding the Shaiva and Vaishnava traditions into one family.

2. The Guru of the Name (bhagavan nāma sāmrājya)
Bodhendra Swamigal is called the treasure of "the kingdom of the Lord's Name". We bow to the one who showed us this path.

3. The great devotees (parama bhāgavatān smarāmi)
Prahlada, Narada, Vyasa, Shuka, Bhishma, Arjuna, Vibhishana and others are remembered, because the Lord's grace flows through His devotees.

4. Sing without shyness (visṛjya lajjām)
Whoever sets aside embarrassment and chants the Lord's names constantly reaches Him, together with countless generations of his family.

5. The heart of these verses (harēr nāmaiva … kalau nāstyēva gatiranyathā)
"Hari's name alone is my life; in Kali yuga there is no other way." Repeated three times for certainty, this is the reason we sing. Time is running out every moment (kṣīṇam āyuḥ kṣaṇē kṣaṇē), so we should not delay.

6. Where the Lord lives (madbhaktā yatra gāyanti)
"I do not live in Vaikuntha, nor in the hearts of yogis; I stay where My devotees sing." The bhajan hall becomes His home.

7. The essence of all scriptures (dhyēyō nārāyaṇaḥ sadā)
After churning all the scriptures again and again, one conclusion stands: always remember Narayana.

8. Rama and Hanuman
We meditate on Rama with Sita in the golden hall, with Hanuman reading out the highest truth. Then we pray to Hanuman for intelligence, strength, courage, health and eloquence (buddhir balaṁ yaśō dhairyam).

@@ 1.4 | Tōḍaya Maṅgaḷam |
Overall Summary
The Tōḍaya Maṅgaḷam is the auspicious opening set of the bhajan. It is a garland of short songs and verses that blesses the Lord in His many forms:
- Rama, the beloved of Janaki and refuge of Vibhishana;
- Krishna, playing the flute among the cows and gopis of Gokula;
- Lord Venkatesha of Tirumala, to whom we cry "śaraṇu" (I surrender);
- Narayana, praised by Narada;
- Narasimha, Rama and Krishna again, ending with "Govindam bhajarē mānasa".

Key Takeaway
Before the bhajan we wish the Lord well (maṅgaḷam) and surrender to Him in every form He has taken.
For each of us: the Lord who saved Vibhishana, Prahlada and Gajendra is the same Lord we sing to. Whoever comes to Him for refuge is accepted.

Deeper Meaning
1. The refuge of the one who surrendered (vibhīṣaṇa śaraṇa)
The very first line praises Rama as the protector of Vibhishana, who left everything and came to Him. Surrender (śaraṇāgati) is set as the theme of the whole bhajan.

2. Kind to the helpless (dīna karuṇa)
His compassion is for those who have nothing to offer. That is why anyone may begin.

3. Krishna, the full moon of Gokula (gōkula pūrṇa candram)
Standing under the heavenly tree, flute to His lips, surrounded by cows, cowherds and gopis, Krishna brings the sweetness of loving devotion into the opening.

4. "I surrender, I surrender" (śaraṇu śaraṇu)
The Venkatesha verses repeat "śaraṇu" again and again. Gods, sages and heavenly beings all stand before Him; we too ask Him to hear our plea (vinnapamu vinavayya), as He heard Prahlada.

5. The names of all His forms (murahara nagadhara …)
Slayer of Mura, lifter of the mountain, rider of Garuda, the man-lion (naramṛga śarīra): each name recalls a time He came to protect.

6. Wishing the Lord well (maṅgaḷam)
"mādhava bhavatu tē maṅgaḷam": may all be auspicious for You. Out of love, the devotee blesses even the Lord.

7. Turning to the mind (gōvindaṁ bhajarē mānasa)
The set ends by telling our own mind: sing of Govinda, sing of Gopala. The bhajan has begun.

@@ 2.1 | Śrī Bōdhēndra Svāmigaḷ |
Overall Summary
This section honours Śrī Bōdhēndra Svāmigaḷ, the first of the three Gurus of the bhajan tradition. A sannyasi of the Kanchi Kamakoti Peetham, he spread the Name of God through his life and writings, and his samadhi is at Govindapuram on the Kaveri.
The opening verse says that merely remembering him gives birth to love of the Name. The song "Bhajarē mānasa" (Rāgam Behāg) then asks the mind to worship him:
- the moon who delights those devoted to bhajan;
- the master who conquered the ego and is full of bliss;
- beautiful, holding his japa beads, beyond the reach of argumentative scholars;
- deep as an ocean in the bliss of Rama's name;
- the destroyer of the six inner enemies and an ocean of compassion;
- the one who taught the doctrine of Rama's name, living in devotees' hearts like a parrot in a cage.
It ends with the prayer: "Give me a taste for Govinda's name."

Key Takeaway
Bodhendra Swamigal showed that the Name alone is enough, and his life proved it.
For each of us: remembering such a Guru makes the Name sweet to us. Ask, as the song does, for a real taste for the Lord's name (gōvinda nāma rucim).

Deeper Meaning
1. The key verse: love of the Name is born by remembering him (yasya smaraṇa mātrēṇa nāma bhaktiḥ prajāyatē)
This is the highest praise a Guru can receive: not that he knew much, but that thinking of him makes us want to sing.

2. The moon of bhajan (bhajana parāśaya āhlādana candram)
Like the cool moon, his presence gave joy to those absorbed in bhajan. The Name was not a duty for him; it was delight.

3. The ego conquered (jita vāraṇēndram)
The proud elephant is the ego. He tamed it, and so his bliss (ānanda pīyūṣa) was steady.

4. Beyond arguments (unmada vādi vacōti durāpam)
Those who only debate cannot reach him. Truth is reached by love of the Name, not by winning arguments.

5. The mission of his life (rāma nāma siddhānta vicāram)
He taught, wrote about and lived the doctrine that Rama's name is the path in this age.

6. A parrot in devotees' hearts (rāmadāsa hṛt pañjara kīram)
A parrot repeats the name it is taught. Bodhendra lives in the heart of Rama's devotees, repeating Rama's name there.

7. The closing prayer (gōvinda nāma rucim dēhi mē)
"Give me a taste for Govinda's name." Not wealth, not knowledge: just the taste, because once we love the Name, everything follows.

@@ 2.2 | Śrīdhara Veṅkaṭēśa Ayyāvāḷ |
Overall Summary
This section honours Śrīdhara Veṅkaṭēśa Ayyāvāḷ of Tiruvisanallur, the second of the three Gurus, a contemporary of Bodhendra Swamigal. He lived wholly in the name of Shiva (śiva nāma parāyaṇa).
The song "Śrī guru śrīdhara" (Rāgam Śivarañjani) says:
- he gave the Supreme Shiva's name to devotees and showed them the highest goal;
- with Bodhendra Swamigal he spread the Name everywhere;
- he called the Ganga into the well of his own house, and the world saw his greatness;
- at Tiruvidaimarudur (Madhyārjuna) he merged into the Lord as light;
- he is a part of Shiva himself, light of wisdom to countless disciples.
It ends with the chant "Gaṅgādhara".

Key Takeaway
Ayyāvāḷ showed that the Name and compassion go together, and that God cares more for love than for rules.
For each of us: feed the hungry, chant the Name, and do not let pride of birth stand between us and other people.

Deeper Meaning
1. Immersed in Shiva's name (śiva nāma parāyaṇa)
This one phrase sums up his life. Like Bodhendra with Rama's name, Ayyāvāḷ lived in Shiva's name.

2. Giving the Name away (bhaktulaku osagi)
He did not keep the Name for himself. Sharing it, he showed people the highest goal (paragati).

3. Partners in the Name (yati bōdhēndrula … kalasi)
Ayyāvāḷ and Bodhendra worked together. A householder and a sannyasi, devoted to Shiva and to Rama, joined in one mission.

4. The key verse: the Ganga in his well (nadi gaṅgana iṇṭi bāvilōna pilici)
On a shrāddha day he fed a hungry man before the ritual meal. The orthodox told him he must bathe in the Ganga to be purified. He sang to the Ganga, and she rose in the well of his house. This is still celebrated at Tiruvisanallur every year on the new moon of Kārtikai. The Lord answered pride of birth with compassion.

5. Merging as light (jyōti rūpamaitivi)
At the Mahalingeswara temple of Tiruvidaimarudur he merged into the Lord as light. His life ended in the Lord he had sung to.

6. A part of Shiva (śiva aṁśamaina)
For his disciples he was not only a teacher but a ray of Shiva himself, a light of pure awareness (cinmaya).

@@ 2.3 | Śrī Sadguru Svāmigaḷ |
Overall Summary
This section honours Śrī Sadguru Svāmigaḷ of Marudanallur (Veṅkaṭarāma), the third of the three Gurus. He gathered the songs and order of the South Indian bhajan into the form we sing today.
The opening verse describes him: shining like gold, sacred ash on his forehead, speech soaked in the nectar of Rama's name, and Krishna playing with Radha in his heart.
The song "Bhajē sadgurum" (Rāgam Sahānā) then pictures him:
- radiant and full of divine qualities, come to the earth to uplift everyone;
- with a tulasi garland, an alms bowl on his shoulder and a vina in his hand;
- with a cloth of the Lord's names wrapped on his head, lotus eyes and a gentle smile;
- surrounded by umbrella, drums, whisks and golden staffs, the honours of a bhajan procession;
- one with Vishnu and Shiva, his feet a refuge that gives wisdom, his mind a bee at Krishna's lotus feet.
It ends with prayers to his feet, which carry us across the ocean of worldly life.

Key Takeaway
The Sadguru gave the Name a home: an ordered bhajan that anyone can join.
For each of us: the order we follow in bhajan is his gift. Holding on to the Guru's feet (guru caraṇam) carries us across what we could never cross alone.

Deeper Meaning
1. The key verse: Rama on his tongue, Krishna in his heart (raghunātha nāma sudhayā … rādhayā khēlati)
His speech was wet with Rama's name, while Krishna played with Radha in his heart. Both streams of devotion flowed in one person.

2. Come for everyone (akhilōddharaṇa nimittam)
He was not a hermit seeking only his own liberation. He came "to uplift all".

3. The bhajan singer's form (tuḷasī mālam … karavīṇam)
Tulasi garland, alms bowl, vina in hand, a cloth printed with the Lord's names: his very appearance was a picture of the bhajan way of life.

4. A royal procession for the Name (succhatram, maddaḷa gānam, cāmara)
Umbrella, drum, whisks and golden staffs are royal honours. In his bhajan the Name itself was treated as the king.

5. Hari and Hara as one (viṣṇu vidhīśa svarūpiṇam)
Like the other two Gurus, he saw no division between Vishnu and Shiva.

6. The feet that take us across (dustara bhava sāgara taraṇam)
The closing lines turn to his feet: they remove the cycle of birth and carry us across an ocean too hard to cross alone.

@@ 2.4 | Guru Trayam |
Overall Summary
"Guru mauḷi maṇē" (Rāgam Behāg) is a single song to all three Gurus of the bhajan tradition together (guru trayam). It calls them the crest-jewel of Gurus and the wish-fulfilling gem for those who come for refuge.
- Bodhendra, the ascetic equal to the great sages, is asked for auspiciousness.
- Sridhara Venkatesa Ayyaval is praised as the family Guru, honoured by holy people.
- Sadguru Swamigal (Venkatarama) is praised for his sweet speech stamped with Hari's name.
Their Lord, Kodanda Rama who destroys the enemies of the mind, and Narasimha who purifies even the simplest people, are also praised. It ends with "Guru maharaj, guru jay jay" for each Guru.

Key Takeaway
Three Gurus, one light: Bodhendra gave the teaching of the Name, Ayyāvāḷ gave it compassion, and Sadguru Swamigal gave it the bhajan we sing.
For each of us: whoever takes refuge in the Guru finds that what they truly need is given (śaraṇāgata cintāmaṇi).

Deeper Meaning
1. The crest-jewel and the wish-gem (guru mauḷi maṇē … śaraṇāgata cintāmaṇē)
The chintamani was said to grant every wish. The Guru grants the one wish that matters, to reach the Lord, to whoever comes for refuge.

2. Bodhendra, equal to the sages (munīndra tulya vibhava)
His greatness was like that of the ancient rishis. We ask him for auspiciousness (kalyāṇam).

3. Ayyāvāḷ, the family Guru (kula guru)
Through him the Name entered ordinary households. He is the Guru of every family that sings.

4. Sadguru Swamigal, stamped with Hari's name (aṅkita harināma)
His every word carried Hari's name, sweet as the moonlike face that spoke it.

5. The enemies inside (mānasa śatru stōma)
Desire, anger, greed, delusion, pride and envy are the real enemies. Rama with his bow destroys them for those who sing.

6. The Name for everyone (pāmarajana pāvana)
The Name as Brahman (nāma brahma) purifies even the simplest person. This was the shared mission of the three Gurus.

@@ 2.5 | Guru Abhang |
Six Marathi abhangs by the saints of Maharashtra on the glory of the Guru and the saints. Each abhang is explained on its own under each heading.

Overall Summary
2.5.1 Guru dhyāyī (Śāntīśvara)
The singer says that meditating on the Guru, again and again, is his only spiritual practice. Worshipping the Sadguru is his daily observance, and the resolve to serve the Guru is his complete penance. Śāntīśvara ends by revealing the secret: the Guru himself is the Supreme.

2.5.2 Hē guru pāya (Samartha Rāmdās)
A garland of praise for the Guru's feet; every line ends "hē guru pāya" (these are the Guru's feet). They remove the three kinds of suffering and carry us across the ocean of birth. They are the seed of the Self's joy and the secret of knowledge. They set us on the path of devotion and make Raghuvira come running before our eyes. They are the home of natural peace and an ocean of grace, the very life of Ramdas, purifying every living being.

2.5.3 Sadguru vāñcōnī (Tukārām)
Without the Sadguru the way cannot be found, so first of all hold on to his feet. He makes you like himself at once, without any delay. Even the philosopher's stone is no comparison; the Sadguru's greatness is beyond measure. Tukaram grieves that people are so blind they have forgotten the true God.

2.5.4 Hari bhakta santa (Jñānēśvar)
There have been countless devotees of Hari, and Jnaneshwar names some of the steadfast ones: Shuka, Vamadeva, Shaunaka, Narada, Ajamila, Prahlada and Vibhishana; Dhruva, Parashara, Vyasa, Pundalika, Arjuna, Valmiki and Ambarisha; Rukmangada, Bhishma, Dalbhya, Suta, Shibi, Hanuman and Parikshit; the sages Kashyapa, Atri, Gautama, Vasishtha, Bharadvaja and Markandeya; and Bali, Kripacharya, Indra, Surya, Chandra, Brahma and Rudra. He ends: "I am the servant of every one of them."

2.5.5 Santa padācī jōḍa (Amṛtarāya)
The poet asks Hari for one thing: to be joined to the feet of the saints. In their company a beautiful shoot of the Self's joy sprouts. There every wish of the heart bears fruit and the longing of life is fulfilled. "Amrut says: O Hari, You make the end of Your devotees' lives sweet."

2.5.6 Guru mātā guru pitā (Ēknāth)
The Guru is my mother, my father and our family deity. When hard times come, the Guru protects me from behind and in front. Body, speech and mind are offered at the Guru's feet. "Eka of Janardana takes refuge: the Guru and Janardana are one."

Key Takeaway
2.5.1 Guru dhyāyī (Śāntīśvara)
All spiritual practices meet in the Guru.
For each of us: sincere love and service for the one who guides us is itself the highest practice. We do not need a long list of rituals.

2.5.2 Hē guru pāya (Samartha Rāmdās)
Everything a seeker longs for (peace, knowledge, devotion, a vision of Rama) is found at the Guru's feet.
For each of us: humility before a true guide opens every door.

2.5.3 Sadguru vāñcōnī (Tukārām)
The Guru does not merely improve us; he makes us like himself.
For each of us: seek a true teacher first, and do not miss the God who stands before us in him.

2.5.4 Hari bhakta santa (Jñānēśvar)
Devotion knows no barrier of birth, status or species, and humility is the mark of a true devotee.
For each of us: whoever we are, the path is open to us. Be a servant of the Lord's devotees.

2.5.5 Santa padācī jōḍa (Amṛtarāya)
The company of saints (satsang) is the soil in which devotion grows.
For each of us: choose our company with care. Time spent with good people changes who we become.

2.5.6 Guru mātā guru pitā (Ēknāth)
The Guru is family and protector, so surrender to him completely.
For each of us: trust the guide who stands both behind us and before us in difficult times.

Deeper Meaning
2.5.1 Guru dhyāyī (Śāntīśvara)
1. Single-minded (guru dhyāyī guru dhyāyī)
The repeated words show a mind that holds on to one thing. Beyond the Guru there is no other practice (guru paratē sādhana nāhī).

2. Worship as daily duty (sadguru pūjana … anuṣṭhāna)
An anuṣṭhāna is a daily religious discipline. For him, that discipline is worship of the Guru.

3. Service as the whole penance (guru sēvēcā saṅkalpa … pūrṇa tapa)
Penance (tapa) usually means hardship in a forest. Here even the firm resolve to serve the Guru counts as complete penance.

4. The secret (śāntīśvara sāṅgē varma: guru tōci parabrahma)
"varma" is the hidden key. The Guru is not a step on the way to the Supreme; he is the Supreme. It echoes the verse "gurur sākṣāt parabrahma".

2.5.2 Hē guru pāya (Samartha Rāmdās)
1. The three sufferings removed (trividha tāpāsi hāraka)
The three are suffering from our own body and mind, from other beings, and from nature and fate. The Guru's feet relieve all three.

2. The boat across (bhavasindhu tāraka)
The cycle of birth and death is an ocean (bhavasindhu). The Guru's feet are the boat that takes us across.

3. Seed and secret (svātma sukhācē bīja, jñānācē nija guja)
Inner joy grows from them like a plant from a seed, and they hold the real secret of knowledge.

4. The key line: Rama comes running (nayanī raghuvīra dhāvitī)
For Ramdas, devoted to Rama, the greatest gift is that the Guru's feet make Raghuvira run to appear before his eyes.

5. The life of Ramdas (rāmadāsācē jīvana)
He signs the song by calling the Guru's feet his very life, and the purifier of every being (sakala jīvāṁsi pāvana).

2.5.3 Sadguru vāñcōnī (Tukārām)
1. First things first (dharāvē tē pāya ādhī ādhī)
"ādhī ādhī" means "first, first". Before any other practice, hold the Guru's feet.

2. Like himself, at once (āpaṇā sārikhē karitī tātkāḷa)
He does not keep the disciple below him. He lifts him to his own state, without waiting for the right time (nāhī kāḷa vēḷa).

3. The key verse: better than the philosopher's stone (lōha parisācī na sāhē upamā)
The philosopher's stone (parīs) turns iron into gold, but the gold cannot turn other iron into gold. The Sadguru makes the disciple a Guru who can transform others. His greatness is unfathomable (agādha).

4. Blind to the true God (āndhaḷē hē jana … kharyā dēvā)
Tukaram's sorrow: people chase lesser things and forget the true God, who is present in the Guru.

2.5.4 Hari bhakta santa (Jñānēśvar)
1. Steadfast devotees (niṣṭaṅka)
He does not list famous people; he lists those whose devotion never wavered.

2. Every kind of person
A sinner saved by the Name (Ajamila), children (Prahlada, Dhruva), a demon's brother (Vibhishana), kings (Ambarisha, Shibi), a poet (Valmiki), a vānara (Hanuman) and the gods themselves: devotion belongs to everyone.

3. A Pandharpur touch (puṇḍalīka)
Pundalika, whose devotion to his parents brought Vitthala to Pandharpur, stands beside Vyasa and Arjuna.

4. The key line: servant of all (sarvāñcā hā ēka āhē mī kiṅkara)
Jnaneshwar, author of the Jnaneshwari and himself one of the greatest saints, calls himself the servant (kiṅkara) of all these devotees.

2.5.5 Santa padācī jōḍa (Amṛtarāya)
1. What he asks for (santa padācī jōḍa dē rē harī)
Not wealth, not even liberation: only a place at the feet of the saints.

2. A shoot sprouts (sundara ugavē mōḍa)
Satsang is like rain on a seed. The joy of the Self (ātma sukha) begins to sprout on its own.

3. Every wish fulfilled (suphaḷita karunī pūrṇa manōratha)
In that company the heart's real wishes come to fruit.

4. A sweet ending (sēvaṭā karisī gōḍa)
The Lord makes the last chapter of a devotee's life sweet. Devotion ends in peace, not regret.

2.5.6 Guru mātā guru pitā (Ēknāth)
1. Mother, father, family deity (guru mātā guru pitā … kula dēvatā)
Everyone we naturally trust and turn to is found in the Guru.

2. Protection in front and behind (rakṣī māgē puḍhē)
Like parents walking with a small child, the Guru guards the way ahead and the danger behind.

3. Offering all three (kāyā vācā āṇi mana)
Body, speech and mind are the three ways we act. All three are placed at the Guru's feet.

4. The key line: the Guru and the Lord are one (ēkā janārdanī)
Eknath signs his songs as "Eka of Janardana": Janardana Swami was his Guru, and Janardana is also a name of the Lord. In one word he shows that the two are one.

@@ 2.6.1 | En Manattudittavanē |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song to the Guru, Śrī Jñānānanda of Tapovanam, which ends with the chant "jñānānandā sadguru dēvā". The singer calls him the one who has risen in his own heart, who lives in the forest of penance (Tapovanam), and who grants everything if he is remembered for even a moment.

The Guru is praised as one who has conquered anger and stands firm in dharma, the Lord of this world who gives happiness here and hereafter. The singer then pleads: "Will your heart not melt? Will my confusion not clear?"

He wonders whether the Guru is Murugan, the child of wisdom who taught his own father, or Kaṇṇan (Krishna), who eagerly ate the poor friend's handfuls of flattened rice. He sees in him Shiva too, with spread matted hair, who burnt the three cities and has now stolen the singer's heart. The song ends: "You were born under the Kṛttikā star, appeared in Maṅgaḷapuri, and came to set me right; bless this Haridāsa who bows to you."

Key Takeaway
The Guru is not a distant figure. He rises within the heart, and a single moment of remembering him is enough.
For each of us: we do not need long hours to begin. One honest moment of turning towards the Guru opens the door to his grace.

Deeper Meaning
1. The Guru rises within (en manattu udittavanē)
"Udittal" is the word for the sun rising. The Guru is not found far away; he dawns inside us. Like the sunrise, it is not something we make happen: we only have to be awake to it.

2. One moment is enough (oru kaṇattil ninaittāl anaittum tarubavanē)
The promise of the pallavi: think of him for a moment and he gives everything. Remembrance, not effort, is the key.

3. Conquering anger, standing in dharma (sinattai venṟavanē, aṟamenum tanattil ninṟavanē)
The Guru's greatness is first shown in his character: anger mastered, dharma treated as his only wealth.

4. All forms in one (ñāna subbanum nī tānō … kaṇṇappanum nī tānō)
Murugan the wise child, Krishna who loved a poor friend's simple gift, Shiva who destroys evil: the singer sees all of them in his Guru.

5. He came to correct me (ennai tirutti amaikka vandavā)
The Guru's coming has a purpose: to reshape us patiently, the way a craftsman corrects his work. The singer, Haridāsa, asks for that grace for himself, bowing as a humble servant.

6. Can your heart not melt? (cittam iraṅgādā)
The singer is not polite and distant; he pleads openly, as a child pleads with a parent. That honest asking is itself a form of devotion.

@@ 2.6.2 | Tuṅgātīra Nivāsī |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Señjuruṭṭi to Śrī Vidyātīrtha, the renunciate Ācārya of Sringeri, who lives on the bank of the river Tuṅgā.

The opening verse surrenders to him as one who is the Supreme Brahman and master of māyā, an ocean of compassion, who took a body by his own will yet is never bound, and who reached perfection through spiritual practice with ease.

The song then praises him line by line. He lives by the Tuṅgā and moves in Narasimha's forest; he gives happiness to all who come to him; he roams the forest of the Vedas; he makes good hearts blossom and carries the power of the Divine Mother. He is full of compassion and the other noble qualities, gives the bliss of Brahman, and dwells within us as the inner Guru. He destroys heaps of sin, wears sacred ash, teaches the highest goal, knows the Truth and is established in it, and his compassion needs no reason. Every line ends with his name, "vidyātīrtha virāgī".

Key Takeaway
The true Guru is both outside us, on the bank of a sacred river, and inside us, as the inner witness.
For each of us: his kindness asks for no reason. We do not have to earn it before we come to him.

Deeper Meaning
1. The bank of the Tuṅgā (tuṅgātīra nivāsī)
The place where a great soul lives becomes holy. Devotees feel Sringeri's peace because of who lives there. The song names him again and again with the same refrain, "vidyātīrtha virāgī": the renunciate full of knowledge.

2. Making good hearts blossom (sajjana hṛdaya vikāsī)
"Vikāsa" is the opening of a flower. In his presence, a good person's goodness opens fully.

3. The Guru within (śrī gurur antaryāmī)
He is called "antaryāmī", the one who dwells inside. The Guru we see outside points us to the Guru in our own heart.

4. Teaching the highest goal (parapada bōdhanakārī)
He removes sin, and then he points beyond: to the supreme state, not merely a better life.

5. Compassion without a cause (ahētu karuṇāśālī)
Most kindness has a reason. His needs none: it flows because that is his nature.

6. Beyond māyā, yet in a body (svamāyā dhṛtāṅgam … bandha śūnyam)
The opening verse says he took a body by his own will, yet is never bound by it. He lives among us, but the world does not hold him.

@@ 2.6.3 | Bhaja Rē Lōkagurum |
Overall Summary
A short song in Rāgam Ābhēri asking every human being to worship Ādi Śaṅkara, the Guru of the world.

He is the sun who made his mother Āryāmbā's lotus face bloom, and a bee at the lotus feet of his own Guru. He is expert in telling the eternal from the passing, and ever absorbed in the one, non-dual Truth. Staff and water pot in hand, he is worshipped by scholars and simple people alike. He is Śaṅkara, the wish-fulfilling tree for all who take refuge, served with love by his servant Bhāratī Tīrtha. The last line carries the name of the composer, the present Ācārya of Sringeri, Śrī Bhāratī Tīrtha Mahāsvāmigaḷ.

Key Takeaway
Śaṅkara joined the highest knowledge with the simplest love: for his mother, for his Guru, for everyone.
For each of us: wisdom is not cold. The wisest Guru was also the most loving son and disciple.

Deeper Meaning
1. "O human being, worship" (bhaja rē manuja)
The call is to every human being, not only to scholars or monks. A human birth is the chance to turn to the Guru. The song gives no long reasons; it simply says "bhaja", worship, and trusts us to begin.

2. A son and a disciple (āryāmbā mukha paṅkaja bhānum, āryājāni padāmbuja bhṛṅgam)
The sun makes the lotus open; Śaṅkara brought joy to his mother. A bee never leaves the flower; he never left his Guru's feet.

3. Knowing what lasts (nityānitya vivēcana)
His teaching begins with one skill: telling the eternal from the passing, so the heart holds on to what lasts.

4. Honoured by all (paṇḍita pāmara vandita pādam)
The learned and the unlettered bowed alike. True wisdom reaches everyone.

5. The composer's humility (kiṅkara bhāratī tīrtha)
The composer, himself a Jagadguru, signs as Śaṅkara's "kiṅkara", his servant. The greater the Guru, the humbler he is before his own Guru.

6. The wish-fulfilling tree (āśrita jana mandāram)
The mandāra is the heavenly tree that grants every wish. Śaṅkara is that tree for all who take refuge in him.

@@ 2.6.4 | Śāradā Pīṭha Nilayam |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Sindhu Bhairavī to the Śaṅkarācārya of the Sringeri Śāradā Pīṭha, Śrī Abhinava Vidyātīrtha Mahāsvāmigaḷ.

He lives at Sringeri, worships Śrī Candramaulīśvara, knows the sacred place and its meaning, and is a master of the Advaita scriptures. He is the very form of bliss, wisdom and joy without a break. The last line says he shines, all-knowing, in the heart of Haridāsa, the singer.

The song closes with calls for protection to the Guru of Sringeri and to the line that follows him, Śrī Bhāratī Tīrtha and Śrī Vidhuśēkhara Bhāratī.

Key Takeaway
The Sringeri Pīṭha is a living line: the same light passes from Guru to Guru.
For each of us: we may never meet a great Ācārya, but we can keep him in our heart, and he shines there.

Deeper Meaning
1. The seat of Śāradā (śāradā pīṭha nilayam)
Sringeri is the seat of Śāradā, the Mother of learning, established by Ādi Śaṅkara. The Ācārya who sits there carries her light. The opening line calls him "śaṅkara varyam", the best among the Śaṅkarācāryas.

2. Worship and wisdom together (śrita candramaulīśam, advaita śāstrajñam)
Daily worship of Candramaulīśvara and mastery of Advaita go hand in hand. Knowledge does not replace devotion.

3. The Guru as bliss (ānanda guru mūrtim)
He is called the form of bliss itself. Peace is felt simply by being near him.

4. In the devotee's heart (haridāsa hṛdayē kalpita)
The Guru the singer carries within is just as real as the one on the throne.

5. The line continues (bhāratī tīrtha pāhimām, vidhuśēkhara tīrtha pāhimām)
The closing prayer goes to the Gurus who follow him. Devotion to one Ācārya flows on to the whole line.

6. Unbroken wisdom and bliss (akhaṇḍa maṇḍita jñānānanda rūpam)
"Akhaṇḍa" means without a break. His wisdom and joy are not moods that come and go; they are steady, like the river that flows past Sringeri.

@@ 2.6.5 | Guru Kṛpai |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song in Rāgam Dēś that sets out the whole spiritual path as one chain.

Without the Guru's grace there is no grace of Hari; without Hari's grace there is no devotion; without devotion there is no liberation; and without liberation sorrow never truly ends. The Guru's form and Hari's form are one. If you hold your mind on that one form, the meaning of Ōm itself will shine as the Sadguru Jñānānanda, as Brahman that is wisdom and bliss.

Key Takeaway
The path begins with the Guru's grace and ends in freedom from sorrow.
For each of us: we often begin by asking God directly. This song says: start with the Guru, and the rest follows.

Deeper Meaning
1. The first link (guru kṛpai illāmal hari kṛpai illai)
The Guru's grace comes first. It is the doorway to the Lord's grace. The song begins with the Guru because that is where every seeker in this tradition begins.

2. The chain (bhakti, mukti, duḥkha nivṛtti)
Grace leads to devotion, devotion to liberation, and liberation to the real end of sorrow. Each step rests on the one before.

3. One form, not two (guru uruvum hari uruvum ōr uruvāgumē)
The Guru and the Lord are not two. Seeing God in the Guru is the heart of this tradition.

4. Holding the mind on one form (ōr uruvil un manadai niṟuttināl)
The practice is simple: keep the mind on that one form. A scattered mind finds nothing; a steady mind finds everything.

5. Ōm shining as the Guru (ōṅkārap poruḷ … sadguru jñānānanda)
The meaning of Ōm, the Supreme itself, shines in the Sadguru. The Guru is not separate from the goal; he is its living face.

6. Real freedom from sorrow (duḥkha nivṛtti)
The song ends the chain with the end of sorrow. Not a short break from trouble, but the end of its root.

@@ 2.6.6 | Bhaja Bhaja Mānasa |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Tilaṅg asking the mind to worship Śrī Rāghavēndra Svāmi, the Guru and ascetic of Mantralayam.

He lives on the bank of the river Tuṅgā, at the holy place of Mantralayam, in his Bṛndāvana (the shrine of his samādhi). He loves the humble, and his music is full of feeling. He is honoured as Prahlāda come again, of gentle nature. The earth of Mantralayam (mṛttikā) is called the supreme medicine for this age of Kali.

Key Takeaway
Rāghavēndra Svāmi is a living refuge: devotees feel his care even today at Mantralayam.
For each of us: the Guru's grace is medicine for the troubles of our time, available to everyone who turns to him.

Deeper Meaning
1. Mind, worship him (bhaja bhaja mānasa)
The song speaks to our own mind, telling it again and again what to hold on to. The mind wanders easily; the song keeps bringing it back to the Guru.

2. The holy place (mantrālaya kṣētram, bṛndāvana vāsam)
At Mantralayam he entered his Bṛndāvana while still alive, and devotees believe he still blesses from there.

3. Prahlāda once more (prahlāda avatāram)
He is honoured as Prahlāda reborn: the boy whose unshakeable faith brought Narasimha out of a pillar.

4. Dear to the humble (dīna jana priya)
He is especially close to people in distress. No one is too small for his attention.

5. Medicine for this age (mṛttika kaliyuga parama auṣadham)
The sacred earth of Mantralayam, given to devotees, is called the best medicine for Kali yuga: a sign that his grace touches even ordinary things.

6. Full of the joy of music (gāna rasa pūrṇam)
He is described as full of the sweetness of music. Singing to him is a way of meeting him.

@@ 2.6.7 | Jagadgurō Śrī Śaṅkara |
Overall Summary
A nāmāvaḷi (chain of names) in Rāgam Ābhēri, praising Ādi Śaṅkara in ten short titles.

He is the Guru of the world who grants liberation, worshipped by renunciates and adorned with sacred ash. He wrote the great commentaries and gives what is good. He is the Sadguru who removes trouble, Shiva come in human form, and the one who works for the good of his disciples.

Key Takeaway
Each name is a door to one quality of Śaṅkara, and singing them together fills the mind with him.
For each of us: when we repeat a Guru's names, we slowly take in the qualities they describe.

Deeper Meaning
1. The Guru of the world (jagadgurō)
Śaṅkara is not the Guru of one group. His teaching belongs to everyone. In a few short years he walked across India and set up seats of learning in its four corners.

2. The giver of freedom (mukti pradāyaka)
His teaching does not stop at good conduct. It leads all the way to liberation.

3. The commentator (bhāṣyakāra)
He wrote commentaries on the Upaniṣads, the Bhagavad Gītā and the Brahma Sūtras, giving clear sense to the oldest scriptures.

4. Shiva in human form (śivāvatāra)
Tradition sees him as Shiva himself, come as a young sannyāsī to revive dharma.

5. Caring for disciples (śiṣya hitaṅkara, saṅkaṭa vāraka)
He works for his disciples' good and removes their troubles. The greatest teacher is also a caring one.

6. Adorned with sacred ash (vibhūti bhūṣita)
The ash is a reminder that everything passing turns to dust. He wore it as his only ornament.

7. Worshipped by renunciates (virāgi pūjita)
Even those who have given up everything still bow to him.

@@ 2.6.8 | Akhilānanda Sandāyī |
Overall Summary
A hymn in Rāgam Yaman Kalyāṇī to Śrī Vidyātīrtha, the Jagadguru of Sringeri, ending in a prayer to Śāradā and Śaṅkara.

Each verse ends "I take refuge in Vidyātīrtha". His lotus face gives joy to all; he is worshipped by the world. When the Supreme is seen, says the Vēda, the knot of the heart is cut, and he is that peaceful one who knows the Supreme. Sarasvatī herself dances on the stage of his face. He has left the banyan tree's root (where Dakṣiṇāmūrti teaches in silence) to live on the Sringeri hill and teach the Truth to his devotees. Through his grace sages give up desire and take up renunciation. On the bank of the Tuṅgā he teaches his disciples the truth and keeps them on the path of dharma.

Key Takeaway
The Sringeri Guru is Dakṣiṇāmūrti himself, the silent teacher, now teaching in words on the bank of the Tuṅgā.
For each of us: when we meet real wisdom, the knots in our heart begin to loosen.

Deeper Meaning
1. Joy to all (akhilānanda sandāyī)
The first quality named is not learning but joy: everyone who sees him is made happy. Joy is the first sign that we are near a real Guru.

2. The knot of the heart is cut (bhidyatē hṛdaya granthiḥ)
The hymn quotes the Upaniṣad: when the Supreme is seen, the heart's knot is cut and doubts fall away. The Guru is the one who has seen it.

3. Sarasvatī dances in his speech (naṭatyānana raṅgē … sarasvatī)
His words are so full of wisdom that the goddess of learning seems to dance on his face.

4. Dakṣiṇāmūrti come to Sringeri (vaṭamūlam parityajya)
Dakṣiṇāmūrti teaches in silence under a banyan tree. Here he has come down from it to teach openly on the Sringeri hill.

5. Giving up desire (viṣayāśām parityajya)
His grace turns sages away from desire towards true renunciation.

6. Teaching on the bank of the Tuṅgā (tīrē tuṅgā taṭinyāḥ)
He sits by the river, explaining the truth to his disciples and keeping them on the path of dharma.

7. Ending with the Mother (śāradē pāhimām)
The hymn closes by asking Śāradā and Śaṅkara to protect us. The Guru always leads us on to the Divine.

@@ 2.6.9 | Śaṅkara Dēśika |
Overall Summary
A nāmāvaḷi in Rāgam Nāṭa (Nādanāmakriyā) in which every line ends "mām pāhi", "protect me".

Ādi Śaṅkara is called: the Guru, son of Śivaguru, worshipped by good people and shining with good qualities; born at Kālaḍi, free of desire; the son of Āryāmbā, the remover of distress; the all-knowing Guru, who does good to all.

Key Takeaway
A single plea, "protect me", repeated with each name, becomes complete surrender.
For each of us: we do not need many words to pray. One sincere request, said again and again, is enough.

Deeper Meaning
1. "Protect me" (mām pāhi)
The same two words close every line. The repetition is the prayer: we keep asking until the heart rests. A nāmāvaḷi is meant to be sung together, so each voice strengthens the others.

2. A real family (śivaguru tanaya, āryāmbā suta)
He is named as the son of Śivaguru and Āryāmbā. The Jagadguru was also a child of a simple home.

3. Born at Kālaḍi (kālaṭyudbhava)
Kālaḍi, on the river Pūrṇā in Kerala, is remembered because he was born there.

4. Free of desire (kāma vivarjita)
He wanted nothing for himself, and so he could give everything to others.

5. All-knowing, all-good (sarvajña gurō, sarva śivaṅkara)
His knowledge is complete, and it is used only for the good of all. "Śiva" means auspicious: whatever he touches becomes auspicious.

6. Worshipped by good people (sajjana pūjita)
Good people recognise goodness. Those who honour him are the best judges of his worth.

@@ 2.6.10 | Śaṅkara Pūjitē |
Overall Summary
A nāmāvaḷi in Rāgam Sāramati to Śāradā, the Mother of learning at Sringeri, whom Śaṅkara himself worshipped. Every line ends with her name, "śāradē".

She is worshipped by Śaṅkara, sister to all, the giver of what we wish for, wondrous in her ways. She lives on the Sringeri hill, and the Vēdas speak of her. She gives auspiciousness, loves music, grants every wish, and has gentle feet.

Key Takeaway
Śāradā is the Mother of all knowledge, and even the greatest Guru bowed to her.
For each of us: learning and music are her gifts. We can offer them back to her with love.

Deeper Meaning
1. Worshipped by Śaṅkara (śaṅkara pūjitē)
Ādi Śaṅkara, himself the Guru of the world, installed and worshipped her at Sringeri. The wisest bow to the Mother. His own hymns to her show that knowledge and devotion are never apart.

2. Sister to all (sarva sahōdari)
She is not distant. She is like a sister to everyone who comes to her.

3. She lives on Sringeri hill (śṛṅga giristhē)
The Sringeri temple is her home, and the Ācāryas there serve her daily.

4. Revealed by the Vēdas (śruti pratipādyē)
The Vēdas themselves point to her, because she is the source of all knowledge.

5. She loves music (saṅgīta priyē)
She holds the vīṇā. Singing her names is itself an offering she enjoys.

6. Gentle feet (kōmala caraṇē)
Her feet are called soft and gentle. The Mother of all knowledge is also tender, and we can approach her without fear.

7. Giver of what we wish (abhīṣṭa varadē, kāmita varadē)
Twice the song calls her the giver of wishes. She cares about our needs, not only our learning.

@@ 2.6.11 | Śrī Śaṅkarācārya Varyam |
Overall Summary
A hymn in praise of Ādi Śaṅkara, composed by Śrī Bhāratī Tīrtha Mahāsvāmigaḷ of Sringeri, who names himself in the last verse.

"I worship the great Śaṅkarācārya, honoured by every world." The hymn describes him: expert in spreading dharma, given sannyāsa by Gōvinda Bhagavatpāda, the one who humbled proud debaters, served by Padmapāda and his other disciples. He is a thunderbolt that breaks mountains of doubt and the protector of all his disciples. Dressed in ochre like the rising sun, he explains the hidden meaning of the Vēdānta, wears rudrākṣa beads and worships Candramaulīśvara. He removes faults, gives what is good, burns the forest of sin like fire, and with the lamp of knowledge drives away the darkness in the heart. He is dear to Vyāsa, honoured by kings, lives at Sringeri, and worships Gaṇapati with love.

The last verse promises: whoever reads this hymn with devotion receives all they wish, quickly, by Śaṅkara's grace.

Key Takeaway
Śaṅkara is the teacher who breaks doubt and lights the lamp of knowledge in our hearts.
For each of us: when doubts pile up like mountains, the clear teaching of a true Guru breaks them.

Deeper Meaning
1. Taking sannyāsa from his Guru (gōvinda pādāpta sannyāsa dīkṣam)
Even the Jagadguru first became a disciple, receiving sannyāsa from Gōvinda Bhagavatpāda. Humility comes before greatness.

2. Breaking doubt (śaṅkādri dambhōli)
"Dambhōli" is Indra's thunderbolt. His teaching shatters mountains of doubt the way lightning splits a rock.

3. The rising sun (bālārka nīkāśa cēlam)
His ochre robe is like the morning sun: a sign of renunciation, and of a new day for dharma.

4. Fire and lamp (pāpāṭavī citrabhānum, jñāna dīpēna)
To sin he is fire; to our inner darkness he is a lamp. He destroys what harms us and lights what helps.

5. The composer's promise (śrī bhāratī tīrtha gītam)
The closing verse, signed by its composer, promises that devotion to this hymn brings every good wish through Śaṅkara's grace.

6. Served by his disciples (padmapādādi śiṣyāli)
Padmapāda and the other disciples served him with love. A Guru's greatness is also seen in the devotion of his students.

@@ 4.1 | Śrī Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha Dhyāna Ślōkas |
Overall Summary
Two verses of meditation (dhyāna ślōkas) on Śrī Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha, sung before his songs from the Kṛṣṇa Līlā Taraṅgiṇī.

The first verse says: by drinking the water that has touched the dust of his lotus feet, my mind became pure, and I came to know the truth, my own Self. I bow to that noble Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha.

The second verse says: reciting his name even a little is worth more to a renunciate than a hundred thousand repetitions of "Om". Worshipping his form is counted as worship of Hari, Hara, Brahma and all the gods. His work settles the meaning of the scriptures for the whole world in three ways. I bow to Śrī Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha, the best of Sadgurus, for the wealth of liberation.

Key Takeaway
Before singing the Guru's songs, we bow to the Guru. His grace purifies the mind, and a pure mind sees the Truth.
For each of us: the songs we are about to sing came to us through a person. Remembering him first makes the singing an act of gratitude.

Deeper Meaning
1. The dust of his feet (yadaṅghri paṅkēruha rēṇu)
The tradition treasures the water that has touched a great soul's feet. It is a picture of humility: we take what is lowest about the Guru and find it purifies us.

2. Purity before knowledge (vimala antaraṅga)
The verse puts the steps in order: first the mind becomes clear (vimala), then the Self is known. Knowledge of God does not need more cleverness; it needs a clean heart.

3. Knowing my own Self (jānāmi tattvaṁ nijam ātmarūpam)
What is known is not something far away. It is "my own form", the Self within. The Guru does not give us something new; he shows us what was always ours.

4. His name is greater than Om (ōṁkāra lakṣa adhikam)
To a monk, Om is the highest chant. The verse says the Guru's name, said even a little, is worth more. This is the reverence the Name tradition has for its Gurus.

5. All gods in one form (hari hara brahmādi pūjā)
Worshipping the Guru is worshipping Hari, Hara and Brahma together. The Guru is not one more deity; he is the way all of them reach us.

6. For the wealth of liberation (mukti śriyai)
The verse ends with what we bow for: not wealth, but freedom.

@@ 4.2 | Ēhi Mudam Dēhi |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Ānanda Bhairavi calling the child Krishna with a mother's tenderness: "Come, Krishna, give me joy; protect me, O little cowherd boy."

He is called the son of Nanda the cowherd, the joy of the Yadu clan, cooling sandal-paste to His devotees. The singer asks Him to show His sweet playful walk, to wiggle His little ears, to come running, to grab the fresh butter, and to dance His lovely dance with His brother Balabhadra. The song ends by naming Him as the one sung by Nārada and the sages, who blesses Śrī Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha.

Key Takeaway
God can be loved as a little child. In this song there is no fear and no formality, only the joy of calling Krishna to come and play.
For each of us: we may speak to Krishna as simply as we would speak to a child we love. That simple affection is enough to bring Him close.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come and give joy (ēhi mudaṁ dēhi)
The first two words carry the whole song: "come" and "give joy". The devotee asks for nothing else. Krishna's presence is itself the joy.

2. Love like a mother's (vātsalya)
The requests are what a mother says to a toddler: walk for me, wiggle your ears, come running. This is vātsalya bhakti, the love of a parent for a child, the love of Yaśōdā.

3. Cool sandal-paste to devotees (bhakta candana)
Sandal-paste soothes heat. Krishna soothes the burning of worldly worry in those who love Him.

4. Fresh butter (navya navanīta)
The singer invites Him to take the butter. Tradition reads the butter as a soft, pure heart: Krishna steals what is offered with love.

5. Dance for us (bhavya naṭanaṁ kuru)
The song asks Krishna to dance with Balarāma beside Him. In this tradition the bhajan itself becomes the place where the Lord dances.

6. The one who blessed Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha (nārāyaṇa tīrtha varada)
The last line names the composer, not as a signature of pride, but as a witness: the same Krishna who blessed him will bless us.

@@ 4.3 | Kṛṣṇam Kalaya Sakhi |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Muhāri in which one friend tells another: "Look at Krishna, my friend, the beautiful child Krishna; keep Him always in your mind."

Each verse gives a reason to hold Him in mind. He is free of all craving for worldly things, the all-pervading Viṣṇu who conquers the enemies of the gods. He is steady and calm, the one who carries us across the sea of worldly life, the essence of all the Vedas, the saviour of all yogis. He plays in the Ganga-like waves of music and poetry full of love. He is the delight of the world together with Balarāma, and through Him every wish is fulfilled. The last verse says He loosens all that is harmful in us, and He is the highest goal of life for Śrī Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha.

Key Takeaway
The best thing one friend can tell another is: "Think of Krishna." Holding His form in the mind is itself the practice.
For each of us: we can be that friend. Sharing the Lord's name with someone we love is a gift that lasts.

Deeper Meaning
1. Look, and keep looking (kalaya)
"Kalaya" means to see, to understand and to keep in mind. It is not one glance; it is a steady holding of Krishna in the heart.

2. Friend to friend (sakhi)
The song is spoken between companions, as the gōpīs spoke to each other. Devotion grows in good company, where each reminds the other.

3. Free of craving (gata viṣaya tṛṣṇam)
Krishna Himself wants nothing from the world. That is why He can free us from wanting. We become like what we keep in mind.

4. The boat across (bhava jaladhi pāram)
Worldly life is pictured as an ocean. Krishna is the far shore and the one who takes us there.

5. The essence of the Vedas (sakala vēda sāram)
All the scriptures point to Him. Holding Him in mind is the short road to what the Vedas teach.

6. The highest goal (paramapuruṣārtham)
Life has four goals: dharma, wealth, desire and liberation. The song calls Krishna Himself the highest goal, beyond all four.

@@ 4.4 | Pāhi Pāhi Jaganmōhana |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Nādanāmakriyā praying "Protect me, protect me, O Krishna, enchanter of the world, supreme bliss."

The verses praise Him through His forms and His play. He is the son of Dēvakī and Vasudēva and of Nanda and Yaśōdā, divinely beautiful with a moon-like face. His little waist-bells jingle, He loves to play, and His body is smeared with saffron paste, yet His greatness is hidden. His earrings sway as He dances. He is praised as the good dancer and as one whose word is always true. The song ends: Krishna, who protected Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha, the purest of the pure.

Key Takeaway
The Krishna who enchants the whole world is the same one who protects each devotee. We ask Him, again and again, to keep us safe.
For each of us: "pāhi" (protect me) is the simplest prayer there is. We can say it at any moment, in any trouble.

Deeper Meaning
1. Enchanter of the world (jaganmōhana)
Everything in the world attracts us. Krishna is the one who attracts the attractive. When the heart is drawn to Him, other pulls lose their force.

2. Two mothers, two fathers (dēvakī vasudēva … nanda yaśōdā)
He was born to Dēvakī and Vasudēva and raised by Nanda and Yaśōdā. The Lord belongs to everyone who loves Him, by birth or by love.

3. Jingling bells and play (kiṅkiṇi … krīḍālōla)
The song lingers on small childhood details. To this tradition, every detail of His play is worth remembering.

4. Hidden greatness (gūḍha mahima)
Under the playful child is the Lord of all. His greatness is hidden so that we can come close without fear.

5. True to His word (satya sandha)
He keeps His promises. That is why the prayer "protect me" can be made with confidence.

6. Protector of Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha (pālita nārāyaṇa tīrtha)
The composer signs the song as one who was protected. His life is offered to us as proof that Krishna answers.

@@ 4.5 | Nanda Nandana Gōpāla Jaya |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Bilahari hailing Gōpāla: "Victory to Gōpāla, son of Nanda, the butter-thief."

He is more beautiful than a hundred million Manmathas, with a gentle smile that gives happiness. He is the moon-faced joy of Vṛndāvana, of its cows and of the Yamunā. He has taken the form of a human child in play, the younger brother of Balarāma. He holds the flute, sages meditate on Him, and He is the refuge of the liberated. Nanda and Sunanda bow at His lotus feet.

He enchants the gōpīs, protects the cowherds and lifted the Gōvardhana hill. He dances in the circle of the rāsa, protects His servants, and is praised by Indra and the gods. He is the root cause of all, removes delusion, is Lakṣmī's beloved and the supreme Lord. The song ends: Gōpāla, who carries us across the terrible ocean of worldly life and who protected Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha.

Key Takeaway
The butter-thief of Gōkula is the cause of the whole universe. The tradition loves to hold both truths together.
For each of us: God is near enough to be a child in the house and great enough to carry us across every difficulty.

Deeper Meaning
1. The butter-thief (navanīta cōra)
The very first name is a playful one. The Lord who owns everything "steals" butter, and through it steals hearts.

2. More beautiful than countless Manmathas (kandarpa śatakōṭi sundara)
Manmatha is the god of beauty and desire. Krishna's beauty draws the heart away from lesser desire towards Himself.

3. A human child in play (līlā mānuṣa bālaka vēṣa)
He took a human form as a game (līlā), not because He had to. His coming is pure grace.

4. Lifter of the hill (gōvardhana uddhara)
When Indra sent storms, Krishna lifted the Gōvardhana hill like an umbrella to shelter everyone. He protects those who depend on Him.

5. Remover of delusion (mōha vāraka)
Beauty, play and protection all lead here: Krishna removes the confusion that keeps us from seeing what is real.

6. Across the terrible sea (ghōra bhavārṇava tāraka)
The song ends with the deepest need. Worldly life is a frightening ocean, and Gōpāla is the one who takes us across.

@@ 4.6 | Nanda Nandanam Nijabhakta |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Tilang in which every line ends with "ākalaya": see Him, hold Him in your mind.

See the son of Nanda, cooling sandal-paste to His own devotees, the joy that Nanda and the great sages meditate on. See His beautiful moon-like face, the home of Lakṣmī, the treasure that overflows with the flavour of love. See His graceful walk, like a young elephant's, and His readiness to protect His own people. See the famous Vijaya Gōpāla, the arm of Balabhadra that gives blessings. See the one who captivates the proud women of Mathurā, and His divine feet, served by Śiva Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha.

Key Takeaway
Devotion begins with seeing. When we hold Krishna's form steadily in the mind, the heart follows.
For each of us: a few quiet moments picturing the Lord's face can change the colour of a whole day.

Deeper Meaning
1. See Him, hold Him (ākalaya)
The same word ends every line. "Ākalaya" means to look closely and hold in mind. The song is a guided meditation: each line adds one more detail to the picture.

2. His own devotees (nija bhakta candanam)
"Nija" means His very own. Krishna is cool sandal-paste especially to those who have made Him their own.

3. A face like the moon (sundarānana candra)
The moon cools and calms. Krishna's face gives the same calm to the mind that rests on it.

4. Ready to protect (parijana rakṣaṇa baddha parikaram)
"Baddha parikaram" means one who has tied up his garment for work. Krishna is pictured as always ready, sleeves rolled up, to protect His people.

5. Vijaya Gōpāla (vijaya gōpāla)
The song names Krishna as Vijaya Gōpāla, the victorious cowherd, the name dear to this tradition and to Śrī Vijaya Gōpāla Svāmi (Chapter 5).

6. Feet served by the Guru (nārāyaṇa tīrtha sēvya divya padam)
The last picture is the Lord's feet, served by the composer himself. Meditation ends where service begins.

@@ 4.7 | Ālōkayē Rukmiṇī |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Kāmbhōji: "I behold Gōpāla, the bridegroom of Rukmiṇī." (In the bhajan it is also sung as Rādhā kalyāṇa Mādhava and Sītā kalyāṇa Rāghava.)

The singer pictures Krishna on His wedding day in Dvārakā. He is dark like a rain cloud, dressed in cloth bright as the rising sun, the younger brother of Balarāma, with dark curls. In the wedding hall of Dvārakā, bright as twelve suns, He sits at ease on a jewelled throne among the learned, giving blessings to the whole world, served by yogis, the son of Dēvakī and Vasudēva.

He is with His eight queens, served by heavenly women, surrounded by happy people, sung by Tumburu, praised by Indra and the gods. Conch shells and drums sound, gandharvas sing, scholars chant the Vedas one after another, dancers like Rambhā perform, and saffron is showered. He wears a crown, shining earrings, yellow silk, pearl garlands and the Kaustubha gem, and lives in His devotees' hearts. A golden umbrella, whisks and fans surround Him. The last verse names His queens, Rukmiṇī, Satyabhāmā, Jāmbavatī, Mitravindā, Bhadrā, Lakṣmaṇā and Kāḷindī, and calls Him the root of joy who delights Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha.

Key Takeaway
The Lord's wedding is celebrated as the union of the soul with God. Seeing it with the heart's eye is itself a blessing.
For each of us: festivals of the Lord are not only stories. When we sing them, we become guests at the celebration.

Deeper Meaning
1. I behold (ālōkayē)
The singer does not tell a story; he says "I see". The whole song is a vision of the wedding hall, offered so that we can see it too.

2. Three weddings in one (rukmiṇī … rādhā … sītā kalyāṇa)
The bhajan sings the same song for Rukmiṇī and Krishna, Rādhā and Mādhava, Sītā and Rāma. The divine wedding is one celebration in many forms.

3. Rukmiṇī's choice
Rukmiṇī chose Krishna in her heart and wrote to Him to come for her. Tradition sees in her the soul that chooses God above everything else.

4. Giver of blessings to the world (viśva maṅgaḷa dāyinam)
Even at His own wedding, the Lord is the one who gives. His joy becomes the world's good fortune.

5. Living in devotees' hearts (bhakta mānasa sañcāram)
Among all the jewels and splendour, one phrase stands out: He moves about in His devotees' minds. The real wedding hall is the heart.

6. Uplifter of those who surrender (śaraṇāgata uddhāra)
All this glory belongs to the one who lifts up anyone who comes to Him for refuge.

@@ 4.8 | Kalyāṇa Kṛṣṇa |
Overall Summary
A short song in Rāgam Khamās, a garland of names for Krishna: auspicious Krishna, lovely Krishna, who danced on the serpent Kāḷiya.

He lifted the Gōvardhana hill, He is Murāri (slayer of Mura), and He moves in the hearts of the gōpīs. He wears the tulasi garland of Vṛndāvana and yellow silk. He is the dark and beautiful one who lifts up the world, the kind protector of good people. He is Nanda's darling, the butter-thief, who loves the sound of His flute, the merciful one.

Key Takeaway
Each name of Krishna recalls one act of love. Singing the names one after another, we walk through His whole life.
For each of us: we do not need long prayers. A few names, sung with feeling, carry everything.

Deeper Meaning
1. Auspicious and lovely (kalyāṇa … kamanīya)
The first two names set the tone. Krishna is "kalyāṇa", the one who brings all that is good, and "kamanīya", the one the heart longs for.

2. Dancing on Kāḷiya (kāḷīya nartana)
The serpent Kāḷiya poisoned the Yamunā. Krishna danced on his hoods until the poison of his pride was gone, and then let him go. He destroys the evil, not the one who repents.

3. In the gōpīs' hearts (gōpiyar mana sañcāri)
The gōpīs are the great example of love for God. Krishna lives and moves in such hearts.

4. The tulasi garland (śrī tuḷasi māla)
Tulasi is dearest to Krishna. Offering even one leaf with love pleases Him more than rich gifts.

5. Lifter of the world (jagad uddhāra)
The same Krishna who lifted a hill lifts the whole world out of sorrow.

6. Lover of the flute (nāda vēṇu lōla)
The song ends with sound. His flute calls every soul home, and the bhajan answers that call with His names.

@@ 4.9 | Paramapuruṣam |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Behāg in which a gōpī says to her friend: "Let us follow the Supreme Person, my friend."

She describes Him: a charming smile, a fine nose, lips glowing like the rays of the young sun. He is Nanda's son, firm as a great mountain, playing in many ways in Vṛndāvana. The gods bow at His feet, and bees gather round His jewelled ornaments.

He is the refuge of the devoted and carries us across worldly life. He gives good fortune to His faithful servants, and His body shines with pure, unending splendour and jewelled ornaments. He is most generous, removes sin, wears a garland of flowers, and is very clever. The nectar of His flute-song fills the clouds, and He wears the Kaustubha gem. The last verse says this song, sung by young women, dear to yogis and shining in the poets' lotus hearts, was composed by Śiva Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha and is full of Gōpāla's mercy.

Key Takeaway
Following Krishna is not a duty but a joy. The gōpī wants to go where He goes, because He is the Supreme Person.
For each of us: the question is not "Must I follow?" but "How can I keep away?" The more we know Him, the more we want to follow.

Deeper Meaning
1. Let us follow (anuyāma vayam)
"Anuyāma" means "let us go after". The gōpī does not go alone; she invites her friend. Devotion is shared.

2. The Supreme Person (parama puruṣa)
The cowherd boy of Vṛndāvana is called by the highest name of the Upaniṣads. The tradition never separates the playful Krishna from the Supreme.

3. Firm as a mountain (nagavara dhīram)
Playful though He is, He is steady and unshakable. One who holds on to Him becomes steady too.

4. The refuge of the devoted (bhāvuka śaraṇam)
"Bhāvuka" are those with deep feeling for Him. He shelters them and carries them across the ocean of birth and death.

5. Remover of sin (pāpa vidūram)
Following Him is enough to leave our faults behind. We do not have to be perfect first.

6. Full of His mercy (dayārasa miḷitam)
The composer says the song itself is soaked in Gōpāla's mercy. Singing it, we taste that mercy.

@@ 4.10 | Jaya Jaya Gōkulabāla |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Kuriñji: "Victory, victory to the child of Gōkula, the root of all scriptures; out of mercy, protect me, the helpless one, O Gōpāla."

The last verse explains whose prayer this is: the hymn was sung by Vidhi (Brahmā) in the words of the Vedas, the story of Hari as told by the monk Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha, and it pleased the jewel of the Yadu clan. In it, Brahmā confesses: "O Lord, this offence was done by me with a poor understanding; O husband of Lakṣmī and Earth, wash it away by Your mercy alone."

He sees whole worlds in Krishna's body, the Lord who wants nothing. His glance teaches even delusion a lesson; He is the matchless home of Vēdānta. Countless universes are His body; the whole world lives within Him; He cares for countless cows. Brahmā prays: shower Your mercy on me, break the veil of delusion, be my refuge always, and carry me across worldly life.

Key Takeaway
Even Brahmā, the creator, has to ask for forgiveness. Before the Lord, the right attitude is humility, and His answer is mercy.
For each of us: when we have gone wrong, we can say so honestly to God. "Wash it away by Your mercy alone" is a prayer that is always heard.

Deeper Meaning
1. Brahmā's mistake
Tradition tells how Brahmā hid Krishna's calves and cowherd friends to test Him, and Krishna Himself became all of them. Brahmā saw his error and sang this praise. The song is a confession set to music.

2. Mercy alone (kṛpayā kēvalam ēva)
Brahmā does not say he deserves forgiveness. He asks for it "by Your mercy alone". Grace, not merit, is the ground of hope.

3. Worlds within the child (īkṣē tāvaka dēhē … jaganti)
In the little cowherd boy Brahmā sees whole universes. The child of Gōkula is the Lord of all creation.

4. The root of the Vedas (sakalāgama mūla)
All scriptures grow from Him as branches grow from a root. That is why the hymn is sung "in the words of the Vedas".

5. Break the veil (vighaṭaya mōhāvaraṇam)
Brahmā's real request is to be freed from delusion. The worst trouble is not outer danger but not seeing clearly.

6. Be my refuge always (satataṁ mē bhava śaraṇam)
The prayer ends in surrender: "always", not just in this one crisis.

@@ 4.11 | Vīkṣēham Kadā |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Āhiri full of longing: "When will I see the form of Gōpāla, the treasure-chest of beauty greater than countless Manmathas?"

The singer pictures what he longs to see: a broad forehead shining with a musk tilaka, the one full of every good quality, whose play has no end. He wanders in Vṛndāvana with a peacock feather tied in His hair, Nanda's son, whose form gives joy to all. His body is dark like a rain cloud, His garment bright as lightning, and His lips curl a little in a playful smile. He dances to "taddhimi dhimi dhimi", and His glance of mercy can end the three kinds of suffering. He is the swan who lives in the pure mind of Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha, who is always immersed in the nectar-waves of the flute's song.

Key Takeaway
Longing to see God is itself devotion. The question "When will I see Him?" keeps the heart turned towards Him.
For each of us: the ache to know God is not a lack. It is the first sign that He is already calling us.

Deeper Meaning
1. When will I see? (vīkṣēhaṁ kadā)
The song is built on a question. The singer does not claim to have seen Krishna; he longs to. That longing is what draws the Lord.

2. A treasure-chest of beauty (saundarya rasa pēṭim)
"Pēṭi" is a box or chest. Krishna's form holds all beauty, as a treasure-chest holds jewels.

3. Dark cloud, bright lightning (kālamēgha śarīram, vidyud vasanam)
A dark rain-cloud brings relief to a dry land. Krishna's dark form, with His bright yellow garment like lightning, brings relief to the dry heart.

4. The dancing Lord (tāṇḍava lōlam)
The drum syllables "taddhimi dhimi" bring His dance into the song. The bhajan is meant to feel that rhythm.

5. A glance that ends suffering (tāpatraya … karuṇā kaṭākṣam)
Tradition speaks of three kinds of suffering: from our own body and mind, from other beings, and from nature. One merciful glance from Him can calm all three.

6. The swan in a pure mind (nirmala mānasa haṁsam)
A swan lives on clear lakes. Krishna lives in a pure mind, like the composer's. Purifying the mind makes a lake for Him to rest in.

@@ 4.12 | Gōvardhana Giridhara |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Darbāri Kānaḍā: "O Gōvinda, lifter of the Gōvardhana hill, protector of Gōkula, supreme bliss; You who bear the Śrīvatsa mark and the Kaustubha gem, who remove the fear of the devoted, protect me, O Mukunda."

He has countless good qualities and endless play; He is a mass of limitless bliss, Nanda's child. He crushed the pride of the demons, is the root of peace and every virtue, and loves to live in pure minds. He plays in the ocean of the nectar of joy, has great strength and matchless play. He is Nanda's son, protects those who take refuge, and enjoys being cuddled by Lakṣmī's tender hands. He uprooted the "trees" of the gods' enemies; His story is holy, and He is the source of supreme nectar. He delights in dance, wears many ornaments, and His feet are worshipped by Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha.

Key Takeaway
Krishna protects from every fear, as He sheltered all of Gōkula under the hill. We can bring our fears to Him.
For each of us: when life feels like a storm, we can remember the boy who held up a mountain for His people.

Deeper Meaning
1. Lifter of the hill (gōvardhana giridhara)
When Indra sent a terrible storm on Gōkula, little Krishna lifted the Gōvardhana hill on one finger and kept it up for seven days. Everyone, people and cows, was sheltered underneath.

2. Remover of fear (bhāvaka bhayahara)
He takes away the fear of those who love Him. The hill is a picture of His care: wide enough for everyone.

3. Countless qualities (amita kalyāṇa guṇa)
His good qualities cannot be counted. Every name in the song points to one of them.

4. Living in pure minds (vimala mānasa vṛtti nivasana)
Again the tradition says: He loves to live in a clean mind. Purity is an invitation to Him.

5. Protector of those who take refuge (śritajana pāla)
He is not a distant ruler. He takes personal care of those who come to Him.

6. Feet worshipped by the Guru (nārāyaṇa tīrtha arcita caraṇa)
The song ends at His feet, worshipped by Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha. The Guru shows us where to bow.

@@ 4.13 | Mādhava Māmava Dēva |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Nīlāmbari: "Mādhava, protect me, O Lord; Krishna of the Yādavas, support of good people, ruler of all."

He has lotus eyes, a beautiful neck like a conch, red lips and a moon-like face, with a fine pearl at the tip of His nose. He carries the conch, discus, lotus, bow and sword, and wears the Vaijayantī garland and the Kaustubha gem, yet He has taken the lovely form of a cowherd boy. In a seemingly human body He bewildered even Brahmā, the husband of Bhāratī, by showing millions of universes; out of compassion for His own people He took a body with every auspicious mark.

His swaying earrings shine like the sun, opening the lotus hearts of His devotees; His smile is a nectar-ocean of mercy that cools the heat of worldly life. The nectar of His flute fills the ocean of the gōpīs' hearts. He is an ocean of good qualities, has crossed the ocean of worldly life, and His fame is always sung. He is the sun of the bliss of the one Truth, rising on the peak of the Vedas; He lies on the serpent and shines with Lakṣmī. With Indirā (Lakṣmī) beside Him, Indra bows at His feet. He is Nanda's son, the leader of yogis, who plays in the mind of Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha.

Key Takeaway
The Lord who carries all the divine weapons chose to come as a cowherd boy. His greatness and His gentleness are one.
For each of us: "protect me" (māmava) can be said by anyone. The Lord who rules all is also the one who listens to each.

Deeper Meaning
1. Protect me (māmava)
"Mām ava" means "protect me". The whole song returns to this simple request, after every verse of praise.

2. Weapons and a cowherd's dress (śaṅkha cakra … gōpa bāla vēṣa)
The song lists the divine weapons of Viṣṇu and then says He wears the dress of a cowherd boy. The Supreme hides His might so that love can come close.

3. Even Brahmā was bewildered (mōhita bhāratī ramaṇa)
Brahmā, the creator, could not understand Krishna's play. If the creator is amazed, how much more should we be humble.

4. A body taken for His people (karuṇā dhṛta dēha)
He took a body "out of compassion". His coming is for our sake, not His.

5. A smile that cools (karuṇā hāsa sudhā)
His smile is like moonlight that cools the heat of worldly worries.

6. The sun of the one Truth (satya cidadvaya … bhānō)
Rising on the peak of the Vedas, He is the sun of Truth, Consciousness and the one without a second. The playful child and the Truth of Vēdānta are the same.

@@ 4.14 | Pāhi Pāhimām |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Jōnpuri: "Protect me, protect me, O most merciful one; give me true devotion to You, O Lord."

Every line then names Him as Śrī Vijaya Gōpāla. He destroys demons and protects the gods; He is Vasudēva's son, praised by Indra and the others. He loves music, is of good character, nourishes the sweetness of love, and protects those who take refuge in Him. He is noble and steady, the slayer of Dhēnuka and other demons, sung by the sage Nārada, and He is the Vijaya Gōpāla of Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha.

Key Takeaway
The best thing to ask of God is devotion to God. Every other gift comes and goes; devotion stays.
For each of us: when we pray, we can ask first for a heart that loves Him. The rest can wait.

Deeper Meaning
1. Protect me (pāhi pāhi mām)
The cry is repeated: "protect me, protect me". It is the voice of a child running to a parent.

2. Most merciful (parama kṛpāḷō)
He is not just merciful but "supremely" merciful. That is why we can ask without fear.

3. Give me devotion (dēhi mē tvayi … bhaktim)
The real request is devotion "to You". The singer does not ask for wealth, health or even liberation, only for love of the Lord.

4. The victorious cowherd (śrī vijaya gōpāla)
Every line ends with this name. "Vijaya" means victory: He wins over every demon, outer and inner.

5. Lover of music (saṅgīta lōla)
He delights in music. That is why the Name tradition sings, and why the bhajan pleases Him.

6. Protector of those who come to Him (śritajana pāla)
Taking refuge is our part; protecting is His. The song ends by trusting Him to keep His side.

@@ 4.15 | Jaya Jaya Bālagōpāla |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Varamu: "Victory, victory to the child Gōpāla, the enchanting Gōpāla, the victorious Gōpāla, the auspicious Gōpāla."

He is an ocean of matchless jewel-like qualities, and His pure fame fills every direction. He lives in the middle of the ocean, the lotus-eyed Lord of Lakṣmī. Brahmā and the gods worship His feet; He breaks the net of sins; He is the Lord of the Brahmins who upheld the Vedas, Balarāma's younger brother, who blesses His devotees. His power shines in the hearts of His people; His flute's call is well known; to serve His story is the one holy act; He is the Lord of Śiva Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha. The song closes in Hindi: Yaśōdā's darling who steals hearts, the butter-thief Gōpāl, Gōpāl who held up Gōvardhana.

Key Takeaway
Gōpāla is a child, a lover, a victor and the source of all good, all at once. Each "jaya" (victory) celebrates one side of Him.
For each of us: we can begin and end any day with "jaya jaya Gōpāla". Celebrating Him lifts the mind.

Deeper Meaning
1. Four Gōpālas in one (bāla, madana, vijaya, kalyāṇa)
The opening calls Him the child, the enchanter, the victor and the auspicious one. The bhajan holds all His moods in one song.

2. A jewel-ocean of qualities (nirupama guṇa ratnākara)
The ocean holds countless jewels. Krishna holds countless good qualities, each beyond compare.

3. Breaker of the net of sins (pātaka jāla vibhēda)
Our faults catch us like a net. He cuts through it, so that we can move freely towards Him.

4. Giver of blessings to devotees (bhakta varada)
He is the one who gives. Devotees ask; He answers, often with more than they asked.

5. Serving His story (carita puṇyaika sēva)
Listening to and singing His story is called the one holy service. The bhajan itself is that service.

6. The heart-thief (citta cōra)
The song ends in Hindi with the dearest name: Yaśōdā's little one who steals hearts. The butter was only an excuse; the heart is what He wanted.

@@ 5.1 | Gōvinda Kṛṣṇa Pāhi |
Overall Summary
A song by Śrī Vijaya Gōpāla Svāmi in Rāgam Yadukula Kāmbhōji: "Protect me, Gōvinda, Krishna, hero of the Yadus; royal Gōpālakṛṣṇa, protect me."

He is the full moon that makes the lily of Rādhā's heart bloom, whose holy story gladdens the gathering of sages. He is Lakṣmī's beloved, with divine lotus eyes, the dark-bodied cowherd who broke the twin Arjuna trees. His jewelled earrings sway, His smile is lovely, and He laughed as He overcame the fierce wrestlers Cāṇūra and Muṣṭika. He loved to dance on the hoods of Kāḷiya, was skilled in childhood games, and His play delights the mind. He is free from all suffering, dark like the tamāla tree, the Vijaya Gōpāla who plays with the gōpīs.

Key Takeaway
Krishna is both a hero and a child at play. The same hands that freed the twin trees and overcame the wrestlers play games in Gōkula.
For each of us: the Lord we sing to is strong enough to protect us and gentle enough to delight us.

Deeper Meaning
1. Hero of the Yadus (yaduvīra)
The refrain calls Krishna a hero. He fights for what is right and protects His people.

2. The full moon for Rādhā's heart (rādhā mana kumuda pūrṇacandra)
The night-lily opens only in moonlight. Rādhā's heart opens fully only to Krishna. The tradition sees Rādhā as the highest love for God.

3. Freeing the twin trees (yamaḷārjuna bhañjana)
Tied to a mortar by Yaśōdā, baby Krishna crawled between two trees and pulled them down. Two cursed souls trapped inside were set free. Even His mischief brings liberation.

4. Laughing at the wrestlers (cāṇūra muṣṭikāsura damana hāsa)
In Kaṁsa's arena, Krishna overcame the giant wrestlers with a smile. Before Him, the strongest danger is small.

5. Dancing on Kāḷiya (kāḷīya phaṇa raṅga nāṭya lōla)
He turned the poisonous serpent's hoods into a dance stage. He can turn our worst troubles into a place for His play.

6. Free from suffering (tāpādi rahita)
He Himself is untouched by sorrow, and that is why He can lift us out of ours.

@@ 5.2 | Kṛpayā Māmuddhara |
Overall Summary
A song by Śrī Vijaya Gōpāla Svāmi in Rāgam Madhyamāvati: "By Your mercy, lift me up, Śrī Krishna, Hari, Mukunda."

His feet removed the sorrow of Draupadī, the daughter of King Drupada; He destroyed deceitful enemies and wears the Kaustubha gem. He is deep as the ocean and steady, the support of all the worlds, the thief of fresh butter, the lover of the cowherd girls, more charming than Manmatha, beyond the reach of the mind. He shines with a garland of stars and is the hero who broke the pride of demons.

He is terrible to the enemies of the gods, Mādhava, who enchants Lakṣmī's heart; He ended the curse of the twin Arjuna trees and plays by the Yamunā; His wonderful gentle words draw the hearts of women. Pārvatī praises His fame; He has no beginning or end; the Vedas describe His play; He shows the path of happiness to His dearest devotees. He danced on Kāḷiya's head to the beat "takiṭa dhimi dhimiti".
