He wears yellow silk and is dark like a cloud; He was the messenger of the Pāṇḍavas and death to Duryōdhana; He is beyond all worlds, with a musk tilaka and a forest garland; He removes heaps of sin, has holy qualities, and is the Vijaya Gōpāla praised by the Vedas.

Key Takeaway
We cannot lift ourselves out of the world's troubles by our own strength. We ask Krishna to lift us up "by Your mercy".
For each of us: Draupadī's story shows that when we stop relying on ourselves and call on Him fully, His help comes.

Deeper Meaning
1. Lift me up by mercy (kṛpayā mām uddhara)
"Uddhara" means to lift out, as one lifts someone out of deep water. The singer asks for this not because he deserves it, but "by Your mercy".

2. Draupadī's protector (drupada nṛpa tanayā durita hara)
When Draupadī was being shamed in the Kaurava court, she held up both hands and called Krishna, and her sari became endless. This is the tradition's great example of total surrender.

3. Deep and steady (dhīra vāridhi gambhīra)
Like the ocean, He is deep and unmoved by storms. One who rests in Him shares that depth.

4. Beyond the mind (manō vidūra)
He cannot be grasped by thinking alone. He is known by love and by His own grace.

5. The messenger of the Pāṇḍavas (bhakta pāṇḍava dūta)
The Lord of all walked as a messenger for His devotees. No service is too humble for Him if it is for those who love Him.

6. The happy path (sukha mārga pradāna)
He shows His devotees the way to real happiness. That way is the path of devotion this song is sung on.

@@ 5.3 | Pāhi Madanagōpāla |
Overall Summary
A song by Śrī Vijaya Gōpāla Svāmi in Rāgam Tōḍi: "Protect me, Madana Gōpāla, Mukunda, who protected Mucukunda." Each of its ten lines ends "harē kṛṣṇa" (one ends "harē rāma").

He is Nanda's son, whose smiling face delights the sages. He punishes enemies, rides the Garuḍa bird and has lotus eyes. He purifies the fallen, protects those who bow to Him, and is always full of bliss. He gave Arjuna his garment of honour, protects good people and burnt the hosts of enemies. He is Indirā's beloved, who bore the Mandara mountain, and the gods bow to Him. He saved the elephant, speaks sweetly, and wears anklets on His feet. He grants what is wished for blessed the king of elephants, and plays the lovely flute. He is the father of Manmatha, protects those who come to Him, the jewel of the Yadu clan. As Rāma He killed Vāli, cared for the monkeys and bridged the ocean. And He is Vijaya Gōpāla, praised by Brahmā, protector of the Vedas.

Key Takeaway
Krishna and Rāma are one Lord. Every rescue in the old stories, from the elephant to Mucukunda, shows the same mercy.
For each of us: the Lord who saved them is our Lord too. Their stories are promises to us.

Deeper Meaning
1. Protector of Mucukunda (pālita mucukunda)
King Mucukunda slept for ages in a cave, and Krishna appeared to him and gave him freedom. Krishna finds those who have waited long for Him.

2. Purifier of the fallen (patita pāvana)
No one is too low for His grace. The name is a door left open for everyone.

3. Rescuer of the elephant (sāmaja varada)
The king of elephants, caught by a crocodile, called on the Lord with a lotus held up in his trunk, and the Lord came at once. This is the tradition's picture of a true cry for help.

4. Bearer of the mountain (mandara bharaṇa)
As the tortoise, He held up the Mandara mountain when the ocean was churned. In every form, He carries the load for the world.

5. Krishna and Rāma together (harē kṛṣṇa … harē rāma)
One line praises Rāma's deeds, killing Vāli and building the bridge to Laṅkā. The song makes no difference between Krishna and Rāma.

6. Always full of bliss (satata ānanda ghana)
He is "a solid mass of bliss", always. Remembering Him lets a little of that bliss into our own lives.

@@ 5.4 | Dēva Dēva Dēvakītanaya |
Overall Summary
A song by Śrī Vijaya Gōpāla Svāmi in Rāgam Kēdāram: "God of gods, son of Dēvakī, protect me." Its thirty-two lines tell Krishna's whole life in order, one deed per line.

He came to do the gods' work and relieve the earth's burden; He was born to Vasudēva and Dēvakī and raised by Nanda as the butter-thief. As a baby He destroyed Pūtanā, the cart-demon Śakaṭa and the whirlwind demon Tṛṇāvarta, and showed His universal form to His mother. He broke the twin Arjuna trees, played with Balarāma, tended the calves and destroyed the calf-demon. He enchanted the gōpīs of Vṛndāvana with His flute and was sung by Nārada.

Akrūra bowed at His feet; He overcame the wrestler Cāṇūra and the elephant Kuvalayāpīḍa, destroyed Kaṁsa, ended Vasudēva's sorrow, gave the kingdom back to Ugrasēna and studied the Vedas with Sāndīpani. He became king of Dvārakā, freed His devotees, married Rukmiṇī, Satyabhāmā, Jāmbavatī and Kāḷindī and sixteen thousand others. He protected Kuntī's sons, ended the Kuru clan's pride, gave liberation to Bhīṣma, taught Uddhava the Truth, and removed the burden of the earth for dharma. The last line names Him the Vijaya Gōpāla, the home of every pure quality.

Key Takeaway
Krishna's whole life, from the prison in Mathurā to Uddhava's teaching, was lived for His devotees and for dharma. Singing it line by line is remembering all of it.
For each of us: every part of His story has something for us: His childhood brings joy, His battles bring courage, and His teaching brings wisdom.

Deeper Meaning
1. A life told in names (dēva dēva dēvakī tanaya)
The refrain calls Him "God of gods" and "Dēvakī's son" in one breath. The song then tells His life in order, so that singing it is like reading the Bhāgavatam.

2. For the gods' work (dēva kārya dhurandhara)
He came for a purpose: to protect the good, remove evil and restore dharma. Every deed in the song serves that purpose.

3. Childhood wonders (pūtanā … śakaṭa … tṛṇāvarta)
Even as a baby He overcame demons. Some teachers read these as the faults that attack a young devotee: Pūtanā's false love, Śakaṭa's heavy load, Tṛṇāvarta's whirlwind of confusion.

4. Showing His mother the universe (mātṛ darśita svarūpa)
When Yaśōdā looked into His mouth, she saw the whole universe. The child she fed was the Lord of all.

5. Freeing those who were bound (bhakta bandha mōcana)
As king of Dvārakā He set free kings held captive. He breaks every kind of bondage for those who love Him.

6. Liberation for Bhīṣma (gāṅgēya mōkṣa pradāna)
Bhīṣma, lying on a bed of arrows, saw Krishna before him at the end and was freed. Remembering Him at the last moment is the greatest blessing.

7. Teaching Uddhava (uddhava tattva bōdhana)
At the end of His life, Krishna gave His dear friend Uddhava the highest teaching. The story ends not in battle but in wisdom.

@@ 6.1 | Rāmā Daya Jūḍavē |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Bhadrācala Rāmadās in Rāgam Kīravāṇi: "Rāma, look on me with kindness; You who live in Bhadrācala, protect me." He calls Rāma by His four great forms: Jānaki's Rāma, the bridegroom Rāma, Rāma with the Kōdaṇḍa bow, and the crowned Rāma.

He asks Rāma to look on him, protect him and rule over him, the hero of the battlefield who delights the world. Rāma has lotus-petal eyes, puts Himself in His devotees' hands, and frees them from worldly bondage; He is worshipped by kings and is the jewel of kings. He killed Tāṭakā and broke the pride of countless demons. Rāmadās says: "I wrote Śrī Rāma a crore of times with devotion, and begged You again and again, knowing there is none equal to You. I trusted You as my only refuge and bowed at Your feet with love. Do not trouble Rāmadās; protect him well, O lovely one of Jānaki."

Key Takeaway
Rāmadās asks for only one thing, Rāma's kind look. With it, every trouble is already solved.
For each of us: we can say, as he did, "You are my only refuge." Trusting the Lord fully is the heart of this song.

Deeper Meaning
1. Look on me with kindness (daya jūḍavē)
"Daya jūḍu" means "look with compassion". One glance of grace is all the devotee asks for. He knows the Lord's look is enough.

2. Four Rāmas in one (jānaki rāma, kalyāṇa rāma, kōdaṇḍa rāma, paṭṭābhi rāma)
Rāma with Sītā, Rāma at His wedding, Rāma with His bow, Rāma on the throne: the song holds His whole life in four names.

3. In His devotees' hands (bhakta parādhīna)
The Lord of all lets Himself be "controlled" by those who love Him. Love has that power over Him.

4. A crore of names (śrī rāma kōṭi vrāsiti)
Rāmadās wrote Rāma's name ten million times. Writing the Name is one of the ways this tradition keeps the mind on Him.

5. You are my refuge (dikku nīvani nammiti)
"Dikku" means a direction, a place to turn to. When we have nowhere else to go, the Lord is that direction.

@@ 6.2 | Rāmajōgi Mandu Gonarē |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Vasantā: "Take the medicine of the yogi Rāma, you foolish people!"

When you take the Rāmajōgi medicine with love, it drives lust, anger and greed far away. It removes karma piled up like mountains of soot. Nothing in all the worlds equals it. It makes the tricks of those who wear false costumes fall away, and turns heretics and wrongdoers into pure souls. In Bhadrādri it happily gives liberation. It is the medicine that kind Rāmadās worships with true devotion.

Key Takeaway
Rāma's name is medicine for the soul. It cures the inner diseases of lust, anger and greed that no doctor can treat.
For each of us: the medicine works only if we take it. Saying the Name every day is taking the dose.

Deeper Meaning
1. The doctor and the cure (rāmajōgi mandu)
Rāmadās pictures Rāma as a wandering yogi-healer selling medicine in the street. The medicine is His own Name.

2. The inner diseases (kāma krōdha lōbha)
Lust, anger and greed are the real illnesses. They cause most of our suffering, and the Name drives them out.

3. Mountains of karma (kāṭuka koṇḍala vaṇṭi karmamulu)
Our past actions can feel like mountains of soot. The Name clears them away, however large they seem.

4. Turning wrongdoers pure (pāṣaṇḍa durmārgulanu pāvanula)
No one is too far gone. The medicine changes even the worst into the good.

5. Take it with love (prēmatō bhujincina)
The dose has one condition: "with love". The Name said with feeling works best, and liberation is its final cure.

6. Better than any other cure (sāṭilēdu jagamulandu)
Rāmadās says there is nothing like it in all the worlds. Other remedies treat one illness at a time; the Name heals the root of all of them, the forgetting of God.

@@ 6.3 | Hari Hari Rāma |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Kānaḍā: "Hari, Hari, Rāma, do not look at me as a stranger; I will never forget to remember Your name."

Rāma is Daśaratha's son who crushed the ten-headed Rāvaṇa, the delight of Paśupati (Śiva), and the remover of sin. He wears jewelled ornaments and speaks sweetly; He is fearsome and victorious in battle, and He nourishes the Raghu clan. His name purifies the fallen; He lives in Bhadrācala. The song ends: "Always rule over me, Śrī Rāmadās."

Key Takeaway
The devotee promises never to forget the Name, and asks Rāma never to treat him as a stranger.
For each of us: remembering the Name is our side of the relationship. His care is His side, and He never fails.

Deeper Meaning
1. Don't treat me as a stranger (nannaramara jūḍaku)
Rāmadās asks Rāma not to look at him with distance or suspicion. He wants to be counted as one of Rāma's own.

2. Never forgetting the Name (niratamu nī nāma smaraṇayē maranu)
His promise is constant remembrance. The Name becomes the bond between them.

3. The delight of Śiva (paśupati rañjana)
Śiva Himself loves Rāma's name. In this tradition Rāma and Śiva are close, each delighting in the other.

4. Remover of sin (pāpa vimōcana)
Rāma frees us from what we have done wrong. That is why the fallen can still come to Him.

5. The Name that purifies (patita pāvana nāma)
The song ends with the Name's great promise: it makes the fallen pure. We need no other qualification.

6. Victorious in battle (raṇa jaya bhīṣaṇa)
Rāma is gentle with devotees but fearsome to evil. The same Lord who speaks sweetly to us is a terror to what harms us, and that is why we feel safe with Him.

@@ 6.4 | Śrī Rāmula Divyanāma |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Sāvēri, speaking to his own mind: "Remembering Śrī Rāma's divine name is enough; why long for hard penances, O mind? Meditating on the saving name of Rāma is enough; why go looking for other gods?"

It is enough to sprinkle on yourself the water from the feet of devotees; why imagine you must go to the Ganga? It is enough to drink the nectar of devotees' words; why want the gods' nectar? Not harming others is the highest dharma; why boast about protecting others? Not stealing others' wealth is enough; why build towers as monuments? Feeding a hungry guest who comes to the door is enough; why long to perform great sacrifices? When the Lord of Bhadragiri is always there for Rāmadās, why wander after other paths, O mind?

Key Takeaway
Simple things done sincerely are enough: the Name, the company of devotees, not harming others, feeding the hungry. Grand show is not needed.
For each of us: we do not have to do something huge to please God. The small, honest acts in front of us are the real worship.

Deeper Meaning
1. "It is enough" (cālu)
Every line ends with "cālu", enough. Rāmadās keeps cutting away what is not needed, until only the essential is left.

2. The Name above penance (divyanāma smaraṇa … ghōramaina tapamulu)
Severe austerities are hard and can feed pride. Remembering the Name is simple, and it is enough.

3. Devotees are the holy river (bhāgavatula pāda jalamu)
The company of devotees purifies as much as the Ganga. Their words are sweeter than heavenly nectar.

4. Real dharma is simple (parula himsa sēyakunna)
Not harming anyone, not taking what is not ours, feeding a hungry guest: these are the true sacrifices, better than grand rituals or monuments.

5. Speaking to the mind (manasā)
The whole song is said to "O mind". Our own mind is the one we have to convince first.

6. No other path needed (itara matamulani … vetala nēṭikē)
Rāmadās ends by asking why he should go searching among other paths when the Lord of Bhadragiri is always with him. Having found the Lord, he no longer needs to keep looking elsewhere.

@@ 6.5 | Palukē Baṅgāra |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Ānanda Bhairavi, written while he suffered in prison: "Has Your speech become gold, O Rāma with the bow, that You will not answer when I call?" (Is a word from You so precious that You will not spend it?)

"Even in my dreams I never forgot to remember Your name, dear Father. I firmly believe You were kind to the squirrel that rolled in the sand to help You, and protected it. I lovingly believe You turned a stone into a woman (Ahalyā) and became famous on earth. However much I beg, You show me not even a little mercy. How could I be stubborn with You? Are You not known by the title of protector of those who surrender? Be kind, O Lord of Bhadrācala who cares for Rāmadās."

Key Takeaway
Even in deep pain, Rāmadās speaks to Rāma lovingly, like a child to a father who seems silent. He never stops trusting.
For each of us: when God seems silent, we can still speak to Him honestly. Faith keeps calling even when no answer seems to come.

Deeper Meaning
1. Have Your words turned to gold? (palukē baṅgāramāyenā)
A loving complaint. Rāmadās teases Rāma as if every word had become too precious to give. The complaint itself is a sign of closeness.

2. Even in dreams (kalalō nī nāmasmaraṇa)
His devotion has gone so deep that even his dreams are full of the Name.

3. The squirrel and the stone (isukalōna poralina uḍuta … rāti nātiga)
Rāma blessed a tiny squirrel that helped build the bridge, and freed Ahalyā from being a stone. Rāmadās remembers these kindnesses and asks for the same.

4. "Dear Father" (tanḍri)
He keeps calling Rāma "father". The relationship is not of a servant afraid of a master, but of a child who can complain and plead.

5. The protector of those who surrender (śaraṇāgata trāṇa biruda)
Rāmadās reminds Rāma of His own famous title. His promise to protect those who surrender is what the devotee holds on to.

@@ 6.6 | Ētīruga Nanu |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Nādanāmakriyā: "In what way will You look on me with mercy, O Rāma, best of the Sun dynasty? Is it in my power to cross the ocean of worldly life, O lotus-eyed Rāma?"

He is the joy of the Raghus, Sītā's beloved, who supports those who take refuge, the home of compassion, the giver of blessings to devotees. "I first told You happily that You are my Lord; now, without forgetting, show me Your affection; I have come to Your shelter. I did cruel deeds without knowing better; do not count my faults. Remove my poverty, O crown-jewel of gods. Salutations to Ayōdhyā's Rāma, praised by Indra, who cares for Rāmadās; O son of Daśaratha, worshipped by His servants, give us fearlessness."

Key Takeaway
We cannot cross the ocean of worldly life by our own strength. Rāmadās honestly admits his faults and asks Rāma not to count them.
For each of us: we can admit our mistakes to God openly. He is the one who forgives what we cannot fix ourselves.

Deeper Meaning
1. How will You save me? (ētīruga)
Rāmadās cannot see a way out himself. He leaves the "how" to Rāma.

2. Not in my power (nā taramā)
This honest admission is the start of surrender. When we stop relying only on ourselves, grace can work.

3. I came to Your shelter (nī marugu joccitini)
"Maruguju" is a sheltering place. He has come to stand under Rāma's protection.

4. Don't count my faults (nēramulencaku)
He confesses that he did wrong out of ignorance. He does not defend himself; he asks for mercy.

5. Give us fearlessness (mākabhaya mosaṅgavē)
The song ends by asking for "abhaya", freedom from fear. Rāma's raised hand of protection is the traditional sign of this gift.

6. Remove my poverty (dāridryamu parihāramu)
Rāmadās also asks plainly for help with poverty. Devotion does not forbid us from bringing our everyday needs to the Lord; He is our Father, and a child may ask for anything.

7. Best of the Sun dynasty (inavaṁśōttama)
He calls Rāma the finest of the Sun dynasty. Like the sun, Rāma gives light and warmth to everyone without choosing, and so the devotee can hope even with his faults.

@@ 6.7 | Rāmakṛṣṇa Gōvinda |
Overall Summary
A Telugu nāmāvaḷi-style song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Sindhu Bhairavi, built on the chant "Rāma Kṛṣṇa Gōvinda Nārāyaṇa".

"When I call You lovingly 'Rāma, Kṛṣṇa', why don't You show Your face, Nārāyaṇa? O lotus-eyed one who rides Garuḍa, I have come close to You." He calls Him Mādhava, Viṣṇu, Madhusūdana, Śrīdhara, lying on Ādiśēṣa; Vāsudēva, Mukunda, who wears the forest garland and holds the discus, Narasimha, Acyuta. The song ends: Nārāyaṇa, You who became the Lord of Bhadrācala out of love, to protect Rāmadās.

Key Takeaway
Rāma, Kṛṣṇa and Nārāyaṇa are one. Calling any name with love reaches the same Lord.
For each of us: we do not need to choose the "right" name. Any name of God, said with love, is heard.

Deeper Meaning
1. One Lord, many names (rāma kṛṣṇa gōvinda nārāyaṇa)
The chant mixes Rāma, Kṛṣṇa and Nārāyaṇa freely. The tradition sees no division among them.

2. Show me Your face (mōmaina jūpavēmi)
Rāmadās longs to see the Lord. Seeing His face is the deepest wish of a devotee.

3. I have come close (nī daṇḍa jērinānayya)
"Daṇḍa" means near, at the side. He has come right up to the Lord and waits there.

4. A string of names (mādhava viṣṇu madhusūdana …)
Each name recalls a quality or a deed: Madhusūdana who killed Madhu, Śēṣaśayana who rests on the serpent, Narasimha who saved Prahlāda.

5. Lord of Bhadrācala out of love (prēmatō bhadrācala dhāmuḍu)
The Lord settled in Bhadrācala "with love", to protect Rāmadās. He comes to where His devotee is.

6. Seen by love (prēmatō pilicitē)
The key word is "prēma", love. Names alone are not the secret; it is the love with which they are called that makes the Lord turn towards us.

@@ 6.8 | Gōpikā Ramaṇa |
Overall Summary
A song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Śaṅkarābharaṇam, each line ending "jaya pāhi mām" (victory to You, protect me).

He is the beloved of the gōpīs, the festival of Gōkula, Gōvinda; Nanda's son with a moon-like face. He is adorned with earrings, rides Garuḍa and has lotus eyes. He ends the demons and is the wish-fulfilling tree for the helpless; He is of the Sun dynasty. His lotus feet are worshipped by Nārada and praised by the Vedas. He is of the Moon dynasty, with teeth like jasmine buds, anointed with sandal-paste. He is of auspicious qualities, Subhadrā's elder brother, the Lord of Bhadrādri.

Key Takeaway
The song moves freely between Krishna (Moon dynasty) and Rāma (Sun dynasty). For Rāmadās, the Lord of Bhadrācala is both.
For each of us: "jaya pāhi mām" is a whole prayer in three words: praise and a request for protection.

Deeper Meaning
1. Victory to You, protect me (jaya pāhi mām)
Every line joins praise with a plea. We celebrate the Lord and lean on Him at the same time.

2. The festival of Gōkula (gōkulōtsava)
Wherever Krishna was, Gōkula was celebrating. His presence turns ordinary life into a festival.

3. Sun and Moon dynasties (bhānu vaṁśaja … indu vaṁśaja)
Rāma was born in the Sun dynasty, Krishna in the Moon dynasty. Naming both in one song shows they are one Lord.

4. The wish-fulfilling tree for the helpless (dīna mandāra)
The mandāra is a heavenly tree that grants wishes. He is especially that tree for those who have nothing.

5. Lord of Bhadrādri (bhadrādrīśvara)
All the names lead to Bhadrācala, the hill where Rāmadās built Rāma's temple.

6. Subhadrā's elder brother (subhadrā pūrvaja)
Krishna is called the elder brother of Subhadrā. Even the Lord of all loves His family and is loved as a brother, close and human.

@@ 6.9 | Murahara Nagadhara |
Overall Summary
A long nāmāvaḷi in Rāgam Señjuruṭṭi, each line pairing a name of Krishna ending "gōvinda" with a name of Rāma ending "rāma".

It names Him slayer of Mura, lifter of the mountain, Mukunda, Mādhava, Vāsudēva, wearer of the forest garland, Lord of Vaikuṇṭha. He is Lakṣmī's beloved, with a lovely face and conch-like neck, wearing bracelets and holding the bow. He loves the king of elephants, has lotus eyes, wears golden cloth and the Kaustubha gem, and gives liberation. He is the lotus-eyed ancient Person, whose fame is holy, who freed Pūtanā. He is undivided, of endless valour, beyond limits, the inner ruler, dear to the gods. He supports good people, is easy for the good to reach, is Truth-Consciousness-Bliss, and loves the Sāma chant. He is Nanda's son who loves butter, rests on the serpent, held up the Mandara mountain, is beyond māyā, father of Manmatha, protector of the elephant, Lord of Mathurā, child of Manu's line. He lived in the forest served by His brother, loves Āñjanēya and His devotees, humbled Paraśurāma, is Bharata's brother, and lives in Bhadrādri.

Key Takeaway
Gōvinda and Rāma are one Lord. Each pair of names joins His two great lives into one song.
For each of us: a nāmāvaḷi is easy to sing together. Simply joining in the names, we are carried along by the group's devotion.

Deeper Meaning
1. Two lives, one Lord (gōvinda … rāma)
Every line ends first with Gōvinda and then with Rāma. The song makes no difference between Krishna and Rāma.

2. Easy for the good to reach (sajjana sulabha)
He is "sulabha", easily reached. One does not need to be great; one needs to be sincere.

3. The inner ruler (antaryāmin)
He is not only in Bhadrācala or Vaikuṇṭha. He lives within each of us, guiding from inside.

4. Lover of His devotees (bhakta vatsala, bhāgavata priya)
He is fond of His devotees like a mother cow of her calf. Those who love Him are dear to Him.

5. Undivided and limitless (akhaṇḍa rūpa … aparicchinna)
He cannot be divided or measured. All the names together still do not exhaust Him, which is why the list goes on and on.

6. Freer of Pūtanā (pūtanā muktita)
Pūtanā came to kill baby Krishna with poison, yet He gave her liberation, because she had held Him like a mother. Even one who comes with ill intent is touched by His mercy.

@@ 6.10 | Pāvana Rāma Nāma |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Kāpi, full of longing: "When will I drink the nectar of the holy name of Rāma? When will I serve Śrī Hari's lotus feet and keep my mind there?"

"When will I see the Lord's servants with joy and say 'I am your servant'? When will I see the best of persons, dearer than life to the daughter of the Earth (Sītā)? When will I give up my restless nature and keep a steady mind always? When will I recite the saving name, the essence of the five elements? When will I describe the Lord of Bhadrādri without any falseness? When will I rejoice, calling myself truly Rāmadās?"

Key Takeaway
Rāmadās longs to live fully in the Name. Each "when?" is a step he wants to take on the path.
For each of us: the questions in this song can be ours too. They show where we want to grow.

Deeper Meaning
1. When? (ennaṭikō)
Every line ends with "when will it be?". The longing itself keeps the devotee moving forward.

2. Drinking the Name (nāma sudhārasa pānamu)
The Name is nectar to be drunk, not just words to be said. It is meant to be tasted.

3. Servant of servants (tava dāsōham)
He wants to bow even to the Lord's devotees. Humility before them is a sign of love for Him.

4. A steady mind (niścalamati)
The restless mind is the main obstacle. He longs for a mind that stays still on Rāma.

5. Truly Rāmadās (rāmadāsuḍa nanukoni)
His name means "servant of Rāma". He wants to live up to it fully, without pretence.

6. The best of persons (puruṣōttamu)
He longs to see Rāma, the best of all persons, dearer than life to Sītā. Rāma is the model of what a person can be.

7. Without falseness (vañcana lēkanu)
He wants to praise the Lord of Bhadrādri "without deceit". Real devotion is honest; it does not perform for others.

@@ 6.11 | Gōvinda Rāma Harē |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Kānaḍā that alternates between Rāma and Krishna: "Gōvinda, Rāma, Hari, victory! Gōpāla Krishna, Hari, victory!"

Rāma is the Lord of Sākēta (Ayōdhyā), praised by the king of serpents, with a face like the full moon. Krishna is Dēvakī's son, protector of the sages, more beautiful than countless Manmathas. Rāma is praised by Gautama, steals Sītā's heart, and obeyed His father's command. Krishna killed Pūtanā and ate fresh curds and butter. Rāma lived on Citrakūṭa hill, and His virtues were praised even by His foes. Krishna's beautiful form shone in Vṛndāvana, praised by Sanandana and the sages. The song ends: Krishna, younger brother of Balabhadra; Rāma, best of the Raghus, Lord of Bhadrādri.

Key Takeaway
Each line of Rāma is answered by a line of Krishna. The two lives mirror each other, because the Lord is one.
For each of us: we can love both Rāma and Krishna without choosing. The same grace comes through both.

Deeper Meaning
1. Rāma and Krishna in turn (harē rāma … harē kṛṣṇa)
The song swings between them line by line, like the two halves of one chant.

2. Obeying His father (tāta nidēśakara)
Rāma gave up His kingdom to keep His father's word. He shows us dharma even when it is hard.

3. The butter-loving child (nūtana dadhi navanīta)
Krishna's playful love of curds and butter is set beside Rāma's sacrifice. Devotion can feel both reverence and delight.

4. Life on Citrakūṭa (citrakūṭācala)
Rāma lived simply in the forest on Citrakūṭa. The Lord of the world chose a hut.

5. Lord of Bhadrādri (bhadrādri raghu puṅgava)
The song ends at Bhadrācala, where Rāmadās saw both Rāma and Krishna in one deity.

6. Praised by sages (gautama sannuta … sanandana nanduta)
Both Rāma and Krishna are praised by great sages: Gautama for Rāma, Sanandana for Krishna. The wisest of beings find their joy in Him.

7. Beautiful beyond measure (bhāvaja kōṭi sama)
Krishna is as beautiful as countless Manmathas. His beauty draws the mind away from lesser attractions.

@@ 6.12 | Kṛṣṇa Kṛṣṇa |
Overall Summary
A song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Kuntaḷavarāḷi, each line ending "gōpālakṛṣṇa".

He is Krishna, Gōvinda, royal Gōpāla with a charming form, Rādhā's beloved. He is Lakṣmī's beloved with long lotus eyes, who crushed Kaṁsa. He is honoured with offerings by Śiva, who wears the crescent moon, and He destroyed the demons. He protected the king of elephants, has a deep heart, and walks more gracefully than an elephant. His beautiful body is smeared with sandal and His nose is like a campaka bud. He is the beloved of the women in the rāsa dance, father of Manmatha, the home of noble qualities, who removes the sorrow of worldly life, and lives in Bhadrādri.

Key Takeaway
Krishna is the same Lord who lives in Bhadrācala. Rāmadās sees Gōpāla in Rāma's temple.
For each of us: repeating "Gōpālakṛṣṇa" again and again quietens the mind and fills it with sweetness.

Deeper Meaning
1. Royal Gōpāla (rājagōpāla)
Krishna is a cowherd and a king at once. "Rājagōpāla" holds both.

2. Honoured by Śiva (khaṇḍēndu kōṭīra kalitōpacāra)
Śiva, with the crescent moon in His hair, offers Krishna worship. The gods honour one another.

3. Protector of the elephant (gajarāja rakṣaka)
When the king of elephants cried out, the Lord came running. He answers a heartfelt call.

4. A deep heart (gambhīra hṛdaya)
His heart is deep like the ocean, full of compassion that cannot be measured.

5. Remover of worldly sorrow (bhava duḥkha hara)
The song's last request is the oldest one: take away the sorrow of birth and death.

6. Lord of the rāsa dance (rāsakrīḍā ramaṇī vallabha)
In the rāsa dance Krishna danced with each gōpī as if she alone were with Him. Tradition sees in this how the Lord gives Himself fully to each soul.

7. Graceful as an elephant (gati jita gajarāja)
His walk is more graceful than an elephant's. Even the way He moves is a delight to those who love Him.

8. Living in Bhadrādri (bhadrādri vāsa)
The song ends at Bhadrācala, Rāma's temple. For Rāmadās, Gōpālakṛṣṇa and Rāma live there together.

@@ 6.13 | Nārāyaṇa Harē Gōvinda |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Ānanda Bhairavi, each line ending "harē mām pāhi" (O Hari, protect me).

He is Nārāyaṇa, Hari, Gōvinda, lotus-eyed Kēśava, Mukunda. He wears a swaying tulasi garland and a forehead full of ornaments. He protected the boy Prahlāda and wears golden cloth on His dark body. He is steady as a golden mountain, of fierce strength with the Kōdaṇḍa bow. He gave the kingdom to Rāvaṇa's brother (Vibhīṣaṇa), took a human form for a reason, and gives liberation. He enjoys Nārada's music and wears a garland of many flowers. He is an ocean of mercy, lovely, lotus-eyed, sung by the best of devotees, living in their hearts, Bharata's elder brother who lives in Bhadrācala.

Key Takeaway
The Lord protects those who come to Him, as He protected Prahlāda and Vibhīṣaṇa. We ask Him, line after line, "protect me".
For each of us: "Harē mām pāhi" is a prayer for any moment, in joy or in trouble.

Deeper Meaning
1. Protect me (mām pāhi)
Every line ends with this request. The song is one long, trusting plea.

2. Protector of the child Prahlāda (bālaka prahlāda paripāla)
Prahlāda was a little boy whose father tried to kill him for his devotion. The Lord protected him at every step.

3. Giver of a kingdom to Vibhīṣaṇa (rāvaṇa sōdara rājya prada)
Vibhīṣaṇa left his brother Rāvaṇa to take refuge in Rāma, and Rāma crowned him king of Laṅkā. Whoever surrenders to Him loses nothing.

4. A human form for a reason (kāraṇa mānuṣa)
He took a human body for a purpose: to show the way and to free us.

5. Living in devotees' hearts (bhaktajana hṛdaya nivāsa)
He lives in Bhadrācala, and He lives in the hearts of those who love Him.

6. Steady as a golden mountain (kanaka parvata sama dhairya)
His courage is firm like Mount Mēru. In Him, the devotee finds steadiness when life shakes.

@@ 6.14 | Rāma Rārā |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Khamās: "Rāma, come; Sītā's Rāma, come; Rāma, come to protect me."

"Rāma, all the kings came to call on You. You shine as the Lord of Bhadraśaila (Bhadrācala), dear Father, to give us welfare." He recalls Rāma's story: guarding the sage's sacrifice, turning the stone into a woman (Ahalyā), breaking Śiva's bow in Janaka's house and winning Sītā. Dressed as an ascetic, He went to the forest for His father's sake, protected the sun's son (Sugrīva), crossed the ocean and destroyed Rāvaṇa. He gave Laṅkā to Vibhīṣaṇa, returned to Ayōdhyā with Sītā and ruled it without fear, and now lives in Bhadrācala in His kindness.

Key Takeaway
"Rāma, come" is the simplest call of a devotee. Rāmadās recalls Rāma's whole story as reasons to trust that He will come.
For each of us: when we call the Lord, we can remember how He has helped before. His past kindness is our hope.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come (rārā)
"Rārā" is an affectionate "come". It is how one calls a dear friend or child, not a distant ruler.

2. The story as a reason (muni yāgamu gāci …)
Rāmadās tells Rāma's deeds in order: protecting sages, freeing Ahalyā, winning Sītā, keeping His father's word. Each one shows why Rāma can be trusted.

3. For His father's sake (taṇḍri koraku vanamunakēgi)
Rāma went to the forest to keep His father's promise. His love of dharma was greater than His love of comfort.

4. Protecting Sugrīva (tapana sutuni gāci)
Rāma befriended and protected Sugrīva, who was in trouble. He stands by those who seek His help.

5. Living among us (bhadrācala nivāsa)
The Rāma of the old story now lives at Bhadrācala, near His devotees.

6. Breaking Śiva's bow (śivuni villu viraci)
At Janaka's court, Rāma broke the mighty bow of Śiva and won Sītā. What others could not lift, He broke with ease.

@@ 6.15 | Ālōla Tulasi |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Bhairavi, each line ending "śrī rāma rāma harē", with the refrain "Śrīman Nārāyaṇa, Krishna, Gōvinda, Lord of the world, best of persons, protect us."

He wears a swaying tulasi garland. He is Nanda's son with a moon-like face; He lies on the milk ocean and bridged the salt ocean. He is Daśaratha's son and death to the ten-headed Rāvaṇa. His life is blessed, He wears a forest garland, He killed Tāṭakā and tore the demons apart. He protected the lotus-like Sugrīva and destroyed Vāli. He protects devotees and gives liberation; He wears bracelets and has lotus eyes. He is Bharata's joy and lives in Bhadrādri.

Key Takeaway
Rāma, Krishna and Nārāyaṇa are named together in each line. All of them are the one Lord who protects and frees.
For each of us: singing "śrī rāma rāma harē" again and again lets the mind settle on Him.

Deeper Meaning
1. The swaying tulasi (ālōla tulasi)
The tulasi garland moves as He moves. Tulasi is dearest to Him; even one leaf offered with love pleases Him.

2. Two oceans (kṣīrābdhi śayana, kṣārābdhi bandhana)
He sleeps on the ocean of milk as Viṣṇu and built a bridge over the salt ocean as Rāma. The resting Lord rises to act for His devotees.

3. Protector of Sugrīva (pālita … vāli nāśaka)
He took Sugrīva's side and removed his oppressor. He protects the weak who come to Him.

4. Giver of liberation (mukti dāyaka)
He protects in this life and frees beyond it.

5. Bharata's joy (bharatānanda)
Bharata loved Rāma so much that he ruled only as His servant. Rāma is the joy of such devotees.

6. Killer of Tāṭakā (tāṭakāntaka)
As a boy, Rāma protected sage Viśvāmitra's sacrifice by ending the demon Tāṭakā. From the start, His strength served the good.

7. Lord of the world (jagannātha)
The refrain calls Him Jagannātha and Puruṣōttama, Lord of the world and best of persons. The same Lord is Krishna, Rāma and Nārāyaṇa.

@@ 6.16 | Kamalā Vallabha |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Śaṅkarābharaṇam, each line ending "mām pāhi kalyāṇa kṛṣṇa gōvinda" (protect me, auspicious Krishna, Gōvinda).

He is Gōvinda, Lakṣmī's beloved, with a lovely face and lotus eyes, who crushed Kāḷiya. He is Yaśōdā's child, the jewel of the Yadu clan, whose life is an endless festival. He is eternal bliss, who loves the flute, the Vijaya Gōpāla. He loves His devotees and their company, is Bharata's joy, and lives in Bhadrādri.

Key Takeaway
Krishna's life is called "an eternal festival". Singing His names brings that festival into our own lives.
For each of us: repeating a name three times, as the song does, gives the mind time to rest in it.

Deeper Meaning
1. Auspicious Krishna (kalyāṇa kṛṣṇa)
Krishna is "kalyāṇa": He brings all that is good and beautiful.

2. Crusher of Kāḷiya (kāḷiya mardana)
He danced the poison out of the serpent. He crushes the poison of pride and ill will in us.

3. An eternal festival (nitya mahōtsava)
Where He is, there is celebration. Devotees live in that festival every day.

4. Eternal bliss (nityānanda)
His bliss never fades. Our joys come and go; His is constant.

5. Bharata's joy, Lord of Bhadrādri (bharatānanda … bhadrādrivāsa)
The song ends with Rāma's names. Krishna and Rāma are again the one Lord of Bhadrācala.

6. Yaśōdā's child (yaśōda bāla)
He is called Yaśōdā's little one. Her love for Him as a child is one of the sweetest forms of devotion.

7. Lover of the flute (vēṇu vilōla)
Krishna delights in His flute. Its music draws every soul home, and the bhajan answers it.

8. Vijaya Gōpāla (vijayagōpāla)
The song uses the name Vijaya Gōpāla, the victorious cowherd, also dear to Śrī Vijaya Gōpāla Svāmi (Chapter 5).

@@ 6.17 | Ghōra Bhavārṇava |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Nādanāmakriyā: "Your name carries us across the terrible ocean of worldly life, O lotus-eyed Śrī Nārāyaṇa, Hari." The refrain repeats "Gōvinda, Gōpāla, Nārāyaṇa, Hari."

He removes suffering, gives pure fame, and took a body for our sake. His feet, under a jewelled crown, are worshipped by Brahmā; a peacock feather is tied in His hair. He holds the Kōdaṇḍa bow, is a vessel of the nectar of mercy, and rushed to save the king of elephants. He is the friend of the friendless, Lakṣmī's Lord, Lord of the world, whose flag carries Garuḍa. He is Mādhava, a form of bliss, with Rādhā beside Him. Victory to the glory of the Name that destroys masses of terrible sin; He is Rāma, Lord of Bhadrādri.

Key Takeaway
The Lord's name is the boat that carries us across the frightening sea of worldly life.
For each of us: when life feels overwhelming, the Name is something we can hold on to.

Deeper Meaning
1. The terrible ocean (ghōra bhavārṇava)
Birth, death, desire and fear make up a vast, rough sea. Alone we cannot cross it.

2. The Name is the boat (tāraka nāmadēya)
"Tāraka" means one who carries across. His name does what we cannot.

3. Rushing to the elephant (karivarāvana śauṇḍa)
He was quick to save the king of elephants. He does not delay when someone truly calls.

4. Friend of the friendless (anātha bandhō)
Those who have no one else have Him. No one is truly alone.

5. The glory of the Name (nāma vaibhava jaya)
The song ends in praise of the Name itself, which destroys even great sins.

6. Rādhā beside Him (rādhā samēta)
He is Mādhava with Rādhā at His side. Devotion and the Lord are always together.

7. Garuḍa on His flag (vinātha kētana)
Garuḍa, His devoted carrier, is on His flag. The Lord honours His servants by keeping them close.

@@ 6.18 | Nārāyaṇam Bhajē |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Māyāmāḷavagauḷa: "I worship Nārāyaṇa; I worship Lakṣmī Nārāyaṇa."

He is Nārāyaṇa who lives in Vṛndāvana, praised by the gods. His cloth shines like the sun's rays, and He wears divine golden bracelets. He has lotus eyes and frees His devotees from trouble. He is an ocean of mercy, the treasure of those who surrender. He protects the three worlds and teaches the demons a lesson with His discus. He destroys ignorance and lights up wisdom in His devotees. He wears the Śrīvatsa mark and cares for Nanda's calves. He is the hero of love, and the Ganga flows from His feet. He is the companion of Śiva, who moves in divine Vaikuṇṭha. He gave liberation to the elephant and is the jewel-Lord of Bhadragiri.

Key Takeaway
Worship of Nārāyaṇa is simple: say His name, again and again, with love.
For each of us: one name, repeated with attention, can become a whole practice.

Deeper Meaning
1. I worship (bhajē)
"Bhajē" is the root of "bhajan": to worship with love and singing. Every line repeats the act.

2. With Lakṣmī (lakṣmī nārāyaṇam)
He is worshipped together with Lakṣmī. Grace and the giver of grace are inseparable.

3. Freer from trouble (bhakta saṅkaṭa vimōcanam)
He removes the difficulties of those who love Him.

4. Remover of ignorance (ajñāna nāśakam)
His greatest gift is light within: He removes ignorance and makes wisdom shine.

5. Source of the Ganga (pada gaṅgā vidhāyakam)
The holy Ganga flows from His feet. Everything that purifies the world begins at His feet.

6. Protector of the three worlds (rakṣita jagatrayam)
He guards all three worlds. Nothing is outside His care.

7. Giver of liberation to the elephant (kari mukti dāyakam)
When the king of elephants called, the Lord came and freed him. The song ends with this story of rescue, closing with the Lord of Bhadragiri.

8. Treasure of those who surrender (śaraṇāgata nidhim)
For those who take refuge in Him, He is a treasure. Nothing given up for Him is ever lost.

@@ 6.19 | Narahari Dēva |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Yaman Kalyāṇi: "Narahari, Lord, Janārdana, Kēśava, Nārāyaṇa, who wears golden cloth." Each verse returns to the chant "rāma rāma rāma śrī raghu rāma rāma rām".

He is the jewel of the Sun dynasty, friend of the sun's son (Sugrīva), destroyer of demons, served by kings. He rests on the serpent and purifies the fallen; He is our own dear Father, an ocean of mercy, our kinsman and father, whose arrow was used against the three cities, Sītā's Lord, Lord of the Raghus. He is beautiful, Lakṣmī's Lord, who lifted the Mandara mountain; He wears a crown and a soft feather; Nanda's son, the joy-giver Mukunda; Gōvinda who plays in Vṛndāvana.

Key Takeaway
Narasimha, Rāma and Krishna are all named here. The repeated "Rāma" chant ties them together.
For each of us: when we do not know what to say, "Rāma Rāma" is enough.

Deeper Meaning
1. Narahari (narahari dēva)
Narahari is the man-lion form, Narasimha, who protected Prahlāda. The song opens with the Lord who appears suddenly to save.

2. Our own dear Father (kanna taṇḍri)
"Kanna taṇḍri" means one's own father. The Lord of all is close and personal.

3. Ocean of mercy (karuṇā sāgara)
His mercy has no shore. However much we need, there is more.

4. Many forms, one chant (rāma rāma rāma)
Narasimha, Rāma and Krishna are all praised, but the chorus is always "Rāma". One Name holds all forms.

5. Purifier of the fallen (patita pāvana)
He lifts up those who have fallen. That is why the song can be sung by anyone.

6. Friend of Sugrīva (ravi suta sakhya)
Rāma befriended Sugrīva, the son of the sun, when he was in trouble. The Lord chooses to be a friend.

7. Who plays in Vṛndāvana (bṛndāvana vihārī)
The last names are Krishna's: Nanda's son, Mukunda, Gōvinda of Vṛndāvana. Narasimha, Rāma and Krishna are all here.

@@ 6.20 | Caraṇamulē Nammiti |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Kāpi: "I trusted Your feet alone, O Rāma, I trusted Your feet."

"O giver of boons of Bhadrācala, who built the bridge across the sea, I trusted Your divine feet. Your feet turned a stone in the forest into a woman (Ahalyā). I held on to Your lotus feet as my only support. Rule over me well, O Rāma of Bhadrācala; I am Your servant, Your servant, Your servant."

Key Takeaway
The Lord's feet are the place of refuge. Rāmadās holds on to them alone.
For each of us: in any trouble, we can picture the Lord's feet and hold on. That is the whole of surrender.

Deeper Meaning
1. Only Your feet (caraṇamulē)
The "ē" means "only". He has stopped trusting anything else and holds the Lord's feet alone.

2. The feet that freed Ahalyā (rātini vanitaga jēsina caraṇamu)
The touch of Rāma's feet turned the stone back into Ahalyā. The same feet can free us from what has made us hard.

3. I held on (paṭṭiti paṭṭiti paṭṭiti)
Repeated three times: "I held, I held, I held". The grip of surrender is firm.

4. Builder of the bridge (vāridhi gaṭṭina)
He built a bridge across the ocean for His devotees. He makes a way where there is none.

5. Your servant (dāsuḍa dāsuḍa dāsuḍa)
The song ends as it began, with total belonging: "Your servant, Your servant, Your servant."

6. Giver of boons (varadā varadā varadā)
He is called "giver of boons" three times. Those who hold His feet receive more than they ask. That is why holding them is never a loss.

@@ 6.21 | Narasimha Dēvara |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Behāg: "Lord Narasimha, protect me, O Śrīdhara."

He destroys demons and is the sun that makes His servants' lotus hearts bloom. "Please be kind to me in the cage of my mind." For the devotion of the child Prahlāda, He destroyed the night-walker Hiraṇyakaśipu. The song ends: "In the form of Narahari, O Śrī Guru Pūraṇānanda, come in the form of sound and take care of me."

Key Takeaway
Narasimha appears suddenly to protect a devotee in danger. His fierce form is all love for Prahlāda.
For each of us: the Lord's fierceness is aimed at what harms us, never at us. We can come to Him without fear.

Deeper Meaning
1. The man-lion (narasimha)
Half man, half lion, He came out of a pillar to save Prahlāda. Nothing can stop Him from reaching His devotee.

2. The sun for lotus hearts (dāsābja bhāskara)
Lotuses open in sunlight. Devotees' hearts open in His presence.

3. The cage of the mind (mānasa pañjaramandu)
The mind is pictured as a cage. The singer asks the Lord to be kind and live inside it, so that it becomes a home.

4. For a child's devotion (paśibāluḍaina prahlāduni bhaktiki)
Prahlāda was only a child, but his faith was complete. The Lord answers devotion, not age or status.

5. In the form of sound (nāda rūpuḍai vacci)
The singer asks the Lord to come as sound. In the bhajan, the Name and the music are His presence.

6. The Guru's name (śrī guru pūraṇānanda)
The last verse names Śrī Guru Pūraṇānanda, asking Narasimha to come. In this tradition the Guru and the Lord are approached together.

@@ 6.22 | Adigō Bhadrādri |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Varāḷi: "There is Bhadrādri, here is the Gautamī (Gōdāvarī) river; look!"

There, with joy, Rāma holds court with a happy Sītā and Lakṣmaṇa. The temple is beautiful with its fine golden walls, towers and gates. Unequalled and very lovely, the discus on top shines and glitters. It is the Vaikuṇṭha of the Kali age, blossoming here; bow to it properly. It is the home of the Lord who openly protects Rāmadās and brings good fortune.

Key Takeaway
Rāmadās points to Bhadrācala like a pilgrim showing friends the temple. To him it is heaven on earth.
For each of us: a holy place is where the Lord is close. Visiting or picturing it lifts the heart.

Deeper Meaning
1. There it is! (adigō)
The song begins with a joyful "there!". It is the excitement of a pilgrim who sees the temple at last.

2. Rāma holding court (koluvugā raghupati)
Rāma sits with Sītā and Lakṣmaṇa as on a throne. The deity is not a statue to Rāmadās; it is Rāma Himself, present.

3. The temple Rāmadās built
Rāmadās spent the revenue he collected on building this temple, and was imprisoned for it. This song shows the joy behind that sacrifice.

4. Vaikuṇṭha of the Kali age (kaliyuga manduna gala vaikuṇṭhamu)
In this difficult age, the Lord's own heaven has come down to Bhadrācala.

5. Bow to it (nayamuga mrokkuḍi)
The song ends with an invitation: bow. Humility is the right response to the Lord's presence.

6. The Gōdāvarī (gautami)
Bhadrācala stands on the bank of the Gōdāvarī, here called Gautamī. The river and the hill together make the holy place.

@@ 6.23 | Śrī Rāma Nāmamē |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Aṭhāṇā: "Śrī Rāma's name has settled firmly on my tongue; Śrī Rāma's grace has brought me good fortune."

The Name has struck down terrible sins and kept dangers from coming near us. It has told the clinging, evil desires to let go. In my mind, singing to Hari has become my wealth. It is the root of the path to liberation, says the detached Rāmadās of Bhadrācala.

Key Takeaway
When the Name settles on the tongue, it starts to change everything: it removes sins, keeps away danger and loosens desire.
For each of us: the more we say the Name, the more natural it becomes, until it stays with us on its own.

Deeper Meaning
1. Settled on the tongue (jihvaku sthiramai)
The Name has become steady on his tongue. Repeated practice makes it a habit that stays.

2. Grace brings good fortune (karuṇayē lakṣmīkaramai)
Rāma's grace is the real wealth. It brings every good thing with it.

3. The Name speaks (annadi)
Each line ends "it said": the Name itself tells sins and dangers to go. The Name is alive and active.

4. Bhajan as wealth (hari bhajana sampatkaramai)
Singing to Hari has become his treasure. This wealth grows the more it is spent.

5. The root of liberation (mukti mārgamunakidi mūlamu)
The Name is the root from which the whole path to freedom grows.

6. Letting go of desires (vadalani durviṣaya vāñca)
The Name tells the stubborn desires to leave. What we could not drop by effort, the Name loosens gently.

7. The detached Rāmadās (viraktuḍu)
He calls himself "virakta", detached. Detachment here is not coldness; it is freedom that comes from being full of Rāma.

@@ 6.24 | Tāraka Mantramu |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Dhanyāsi: "I wished for the saving mantra (Rāma's name) and I got it; I am blessed, brother! I trust that it is death to the messengers of Yama."

"Do not fall into the illusions of other paths out of attachment. There is no need to wander through the hundred and eight Tirupatis. What need is there to dip in holy rivers? Do not keep wasting the days of your youth. Whichever births you have looked at, Nārāyaṇa is the One; I have heard the story of that great Supreme who is in every form. The sin of many births will leave with this birth; this is the last birth for all of it, believe me, this is true. Holding firmly to dharma and trusting the Lord of Bhadrādri in his heart, Rāmadās knows the secret; the Lord is in his heart-temple."

Key Takeaway
Rāma's name is the saving mantra. With it, the devotee needs no endless pilgrimages or rituals; the Lord lives in the heart.
For each of us: we have already been given the mantra. What remains is to use it while there is time.

Deeper Meaning
1. The saving mantra (tāraka mantramu)
"Tāraka" means that which carries across. Rāma's name is the mantra that takes us across the ocean of birth and death.

2. I am blessed (dhanyuḍanaitini)
Receiving the Name is the greatest good fortune. Rāmadās feels truly blessed.

3. No need to wander (tirupatulelami tiruga pani lēdu)
Pilgrimages and holy baths are good, but they are not the essence. The Name brings the Lord to us wherever we are.

4. Don't waste your youth (paruvapu dinamulalō)
The song urges us to begin now, not later. Time is precious.

5. The heart-temple (hṛn-mandiramunanē)
The last line places the Lord in the heart's temple. The temple Rāmadās built in Bhadrācala is also built inside him.

6. Death to Yama's messengers (kāluni dūtala pāliṭi mṛtyuvu)
The Name is "death" to the messengers of death. Those who hold it need not fear the end.

7. The last birth (kaḍasāri janmamidi)
Rāmadās is sure: with the Name, this birth can be the last. All past sins can be finished here.

@@ 6.25 | Takkuvēmi |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Saurāṣṭra: "What do we lack, as long as we have Rāma? The Lord is right beside us, holding His discus."

The song walks through His avatāras. The Fish who once killed the thief Sōmaka is on our side. We have the mercy of the Tortoise who carried the Mandara mountain for the gods. Narahari, who tore Hiraṇyakaśipu in two, is right beside us. Vāmana, who measured earth and heaven, is our own. We have the kindness of Daśaratha's Rāma, who punished the ten-headed Rāvaṇa. Krishna, who destroyed the wicked Kaṁsa, is kind to us. And the Lord who lovingly said "I will protect Rāmadās" is with us.

Key Takeaway
When the Lord is with us, nothing is lacking. Every avatāra is a reminder that He has always protected His devotees.
For each of us: in any worry we can ask, as Rāmadās did, "What do we lack, with Rāma here?"

Deeper Meaning
1. What do we lack? (takkuvēmi manaku)
A joyful, confident question. With Rāma, nothing is missing.

2. Right beside us (prakka tōḍugā)
The Lord is "at our side", like a companion. He is not far away.

3. All the avatāras (matsya … kūrma … narahari … vāmana)
The song names His forms one by one: Fish, Tortoise, Man-Lion, Dwarf, Rāma and Krishna. Each came to protect.

4. He is ours (manavāḍai)
Vāmana, who measured the universe, is called "our own". The infinite Lord belongs to His devotees.

5. His promise (rakṣincedanani prēmatō palkina)
The song ends with His own word: "I will protect." That promise is the reason nothing is lacking.

6. Krishna's kindness (kṛṣṇuḍu … kṛpatō)
Krishna, who ended the cruel Kaṁsa, is kind to us. The same power that removes evil is gentle with those who love Him.

7. Joyful confidence
This is a happy song. Rāmadās does not just hope for protection; he is sure of it, and that confidence is itself a gift of faith.

@@ 6.26 | Kaṇṭi Nēḍu |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Rāmadās in Rāgam Nādanāmakriyā: "Today I saw our Rāma; I found our Rāma!"

"Today I saw the family deity who protects the crowds of devotees, the one who lives on Bhadragiri. I saw our Kōdaṇḍa Rāma, holding court with the lovely Sītā beside Him. He is the jewel of the Sun dynasty, with a body dark like a rain cloud and two eyes that shower the nectar of mercy. Short strings of pearls shine on Him, and His face has a charming smile. His golden garment's glow covers the worlds, and He holds the bow and arrows in His hands. He is the one who rules Rāmadās on earth, the Supreme Person, the Lord of Bhadragiri."

Key Takeaway
This is the joy of a devotee who has finally seen his Lord. Every detail of Rāma's form is remembered with delight.
For each of us: the Lord we long for can be "seen" in the heart. Picturing His kind eyes and smile is a way of meeting Him.

Deeper Meaning
1. Today I saw (kaṇṭi nēḍu)
The song bursts out with joy: "I have seen Him today!". After long waiting, the vision has come.

2. Our family deity (iṇṭi vēlupu)
"Iṇṭi vēlupu" is the god of one's own home. Rāma is not a distant god; He belongs to the family.

3. Eyes full of mercy (karuṇā rasamu guriyu kanudōyi)
His two eyes "rain" the nectar of compassion. To be seen by Him is to be showered with kindness.

4. A charming smile (muripempu cirunavvu)
Rāmadās remembers Rāma's gentle smile. The Lord's beauty draws the heart and calms it.

5. The one who rules Rāmadās (rāmadāsunēlēḍu vāni)
The song ends with belonging: this Supreme Person is the one who rules over Rāmadās. To be ruled by such a Lord is freedom.

6. The bow and arrows (śara cāpamulu)
Rāma holds His bow and arrows. He is ready to protect His devotees at any time.

@@ 7.1 | Narajanma Bandāga |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Yadukula Kāmbhōji: "Now that you have got a human birth and you have a tongue, can't you say 'Krishna'? If you say 'Krishna', every trouble is solved; can't you say 'Krishna'?" The refrain sings "Krishna, ye Krishna, Gōpāla Krishna, Rādhā Krishna."

He lists ordinary moments when the Name can be said. Lying down, stretching your body or getting up, can't you say "Krishna" once? Even while wandering about the house, once? While bathing, drinking, doing japa and penance, or after eating a full meal of fine rice and six flavours, can't you say "Krishna"? Even while hugging and kissing your child, or joking with your wife, can't you say "Krishna" once? Krishna, who cuts away and throws off heaps of sin, the rider of Garuḍa, our Purandara Viṭhala: can't you say His name, now, here, while you have this human birth?

Key Takeaway
A human birth and a tongue are precious gifts. The Name of Krishna can be said at every moment of ordinary life, and it removes every trouble.
For each of us: we do not need a special time or place. Waking up, eating, playing with our children: any moment can hold the Name.

Deeper Meaning
1. A rare human birth (nara janma)
The tradition teaches that a human birth is rare and precious, because only a human can consciously turn to God. Purandaradāsa reminds us not to waste it.

2. While you have a tongue (nālige iruvāga)
The tongue is the tool of the Name. While it still works, he asks, why not use it for the best thing?

3. In every ordinary moment (malagiddu … snāna pāna …)
He does not ask us to leave our daily life. He names lying down, bathing, eating and playing, and asks us to add the Name to each.

4. Even in family love (kandana … muddāḍuta)
Even when cuddling a child or laughing with one's wife, Krishna can be remembered. Family love and love of God are not enemies.

5. Every trouble removed (sakala kaṣṭavu parihāra)
Saying "Krishna" solves all difficulties. The Name is not one remedy among many; it is the cure for all.

6. Now (illi … īga … ivāga)
The song ends with three words: here, now, at this very moment. Don't wait for later.

@@ 7.2 | Viṭhala Salahō |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Cakravākam: "Viṭhala, protect me, Lord, who are within everything in the world."

Then he sees Viṭhala everywhere: behind and in front, left and right, above and below, inside and outside. Viṭhala is his father and his mother, his relative and his friends, Purandara Viṭhala.

Key Takeaway
Viṭhala is everywhere and is everything to the devotee. There is no direction where He is not.
For each of us: wherever we turn, the Lord is already there. We are never outside His care.

Deeper Meaning
1. Protect me (salahō)
The song begins with a simple request for protection. Everything after it is the reason the devotee feels safe.

2. Within the world (jagad antaryāmi)
Viṭhala is the inner ruler of the whole world. He is not only in Paṇḍharpur; He is within all things.

3. In every direction (hindē mundē … eḍagē balagē …)
Behind, in front, left, right, above, below. Purandaradāsa walks through all directions and finds Viṭhala in each.

4. Inside and outside (oḷagē horagē)
He is inside the heart and outside in the world. The devotee is surrounded and filled.

5. Father and mother (tandē tāyi)
Viṭhala is both father and mother. He gives protection and tender care at once.

6. All our relations (bandhu baḷaga)
Relatives and friends are also Viṭhala. Every human bond becomes a way of meeting Him. The song is easy to sing, so that everyone can join in.

@@ 7.3 | Dēvaki Nandana |
Overall Summary
A Kannada-Sanskrit song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Janasammōdini, a garland of Krishna's names.

He is Dēvakī's son, Nanda's Mukunda, the eternal bliss that delights the sages. He rescued the Vedas, steals butter, rides Garuḍa and lifts up the world. He wears fish-shaped earrings and has a charming form; He is Rukmiṇī's beloved and the Pāṇḍavas' protector. He holds the conch and discus, is Śrī Gōvinda, lotus-eyed, supreme bliss. He crushed Kaṁsa, wears the Kaustubha gem, and His feet are worshipped by Brahmā, who rides the swan. He is the giver of boons, the handsome Cenna Kēśava of Bēlūr, ever gracious, Purandara Viṭhala, full of every good quality.

Key Takeaway
Each name of Krishna is a reason to love Him. Singing them one by one is a simple, complete prayer.
For each of us: a list of names may look plain, but each one opens a story of His kindness.

Deeper Meaning
1. Son of two families (dēvaki nandana nanda mukunda)
The first line names both Dēvakī and Nanda. Krishna belongs to the mother who bore Him and the father who raised Him.

2. Rescuer of the Vedas (nigama uddhāra)
As the Fish, He saved the Vedas from the flood. The playful butter-thief is the same Lord.

3. Protector of the Pāṇḍavas (pāṇḍava pōṣa)
He stood by the Pāṇḍavas through all their troubles. He is the friend of the good in hard times.

4. Eternal bliss (nityānanda)
The sages are delighted by Him because He is bliss itself.

5. Cenna Kēśava of Bēlūr (bēlāpura cenna)
Purandaradāsa names the beautiful Kēśava of the Bēlūr temple. The Lord of Paṇḍharpur and the Lord of Bēlūr are one.

6. Full of every quality (guṇa paripūrṇa)
He lacks nothing. That is why all the names together still cannot describe Him fully.

7. Worshipped by Brahmā (haṁsa vāhana pūjita caraṇa)
Brahmā, who rides the swan, worships His feet. The creator bows to the cowherd.

@@ 7.4 | Rāgi Tandīrā |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Āhir Bhairavi, opening with "Viṭhala, Viṭhala, Viṭhala, Pāṇḍuraṅga." It plays on the word "rāgi", which is a grain (finger millet) given as alms and also means "having become".

A wandering devotee asks at the door: "Have you brought rāgi (alms) for the beggar?" But he means: have you become worthy, become fit to enjoy, become fortunate? Have you become one who gives food and builds rest-houses for the hungry? Have you given up idle talk and do you sing bhajans every day? Do you serve your mother and father, have you given up sinful deeds, do you live by right conduct and avoid craving for fame? Do you always remember Lakṣmī's Lord, do you bow to your Guru, have you crossed the worldly life without melting, and do you serve Purandara Viṭhala?

Key Takeaway
The real alms God asks of us is a good life. Each "rāgi" is a quality to become, not grain to give.
For each of us: the song is a checklist for a devotee's life: feed others, serve parents, live honestly, sing His name, honour the Guru.

Deeper Meaning
1. A clever play on words (rāgi)
"Rāgi" is millet, the simple grain given to a beggar. It also sounds like "-āgi", "having become". Purandaradāsa turns a request for alms into a question about who we have become.

2. Becoming worthy (yōgyarāgi)
The first thing asked is whether we have become worthy. Devotion changes us, step by step.

3. Feeding others (annadānava māḍuvarāgi)
Giving food is praised again and again in this tradition. Feeding the hungry is feeding God.

4. Daily bhajan (anudina bhajanaya)
Leaving idle talk and singing His name every day is one of the marks of a devotee.

5. Parents and Guru (mātā pitaranu … guruvigē bāgōvanta)
Serving parents and bowing to the Guru are part of the path. Respect for those who raised and taught us is respect for God.

6. Serving Viṭhala (purandara viṭhalana sēvipa)
Every quality in the list leads to this: becoming one who serves the Lord. That is the true alms.

@@ 7.5 | Kaliyugadalli Harināmava |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Śivarañjani: "In the Kali age, if you remember Hari's name, crores of generations of your family will be lifted up."

O mind, think of the lotus-naveled Lord, who considers Himself easy to reach for devotees who are easy to please. Do not wander about saying "I don't know how to bathe properly, I don't know silence, I don't know meditation"; think of Jānakī's beloved, Daśaratha's son, who loves music. Do not wander about saying "I don't know mantras, I don't know yantras, I don't know tantras"; think of the name of the ever-blissful one without worry. Do not wander about saying "I know no japa, no penance, no fasting"; think of the infinitely great Śrī Purandara Viṭhala as the way.

Key Takeaway
In this age, the Name is enough. We do not need to master rituals, mantras or austerities; remembering Hari's name lifts up generations.
For each of us: if we feel we "don't know enough" to be devoted, this song is for us. The Name asks for no qualifications.

Deeper Meaning
1. The Kali age (kaliyuga)
The tradition teaches that in this age, when life is short and the mind restless, the simplest practice, the Name, is the most powerful.

2. Crores of generations (kula kōṭigaḷ uddharisuvavu)
Remembering the Name lifts up not only us, but countless generations of our family. Its blessing spreads.

3. Easy for the simple (sulabhada bhaktige sulabha)
The Lord is easy to reach for those who are simple-hearted. He does not ask for complicated methods.

4. Don't say "I don't know" (ariyenu … alaiya bēḍā)
Three times Purandaradāsa says: don't worry that you don't know rituals, mantras or penances. The Name makes up for all of them.

5. Without worry (cintagaḷillade)
Remember Him without anxiety. Devotion is not a test we can fail.

6. The Name as the means (upāyadindali)
The last line calls the Name the "upāya", the means. It is both the path and the strength to walk it.

@@ 7.6 | Dāsana Māḍikō |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Nādanāmakriyā: "Make me Your servant, Lord, Vēṅkaṭaramaṇa of a thousand names."

"Free me from all my bad thoughts; put on me the armour of Your mercy for my protection. Grant me service at Your feet, and place on my head the flower of Your fearlessness-giving hand. I ask for firm devotion to You, Lord; I will bow at Your feet and sing every day. Look at me from the corner of Your eye; do not let me go; give me meditation on You and make my mind pure. You have the title of protecting those who come to You; do not forget me, protect and look after me. Cut away all my sins, Śrī Purandara Viṭhala, and take care of me."

Key Takeaway
The highest thing to ask is to become the Lord's servant. Purandaradāsa asks for a pure mind, firm devotion and service at His feet.
For each of us: we can ask God for a better heart, not only for an easier life. That prayer is always answered.

Deeper Meaning
1. Make me Your servant (dāsana māḍikō)
Purandaradāsa's whole tradition is called "dāsa", servant. To be the Lord's servant is the highest honour.

2. The armour of mercy (karuṇa kavaca)
He asks the Lord to put His mercy on him like armour. Grace protects us from inner and outer dangers.

3. The flower of His hand (abhaya kara puṣpa)
He asks the Lord to place His blessing hand, raised in "fear not", on his head like a flower.

4. Firm devotion (dṛḍha bhakti)
He asks for devotion that does not waver. Feelings come and go; firm devotion stays.

5. A glance from the corner of the eye (kaḍegaṇṇinde)
Even a sideways glance from the Lord is enough to change a life.

6. A pure mind (mana śuci)
He asks for meditation on the Lord and a clean mind. The two go together: thinking of Him purifies.

7. His famous promise (mora hokkavara kāyva birudu)
He is known for protecting all who come to Him. Purandaradāsa holds Him to that promise.

@@ 7.7 | Rāma Mantrava Japisō |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Jōnpuri: "Chant the Rāma mantra, O man! Don't ruin yourself by liking this mantra and that mantra."

It is the mantra that Śiva, who wears the moon, told His wife. It is the mantra that even one of low birth can chant aloud, that can be spoken in the street, that destroys many sins, and that easily wins heaven. It is the mantra that supports bathing and silence, that the wise meditate on in their minds, that drives away bad qualities, and that crowned Vibhīṣaṇa king. It is the essence of all the Vedas, the root mantra for the path of liberation, the mantra that shows at once the way to the sweetness of devotion, the great mantra of Purandara Viṭhala, the treasure of happiness.

Key Takeaway
Rāma's name is the one mantra for everyone. It needs no special rules, no special birth and no secret teaching.
For each of us: we already have the greatest mantra. Chanting "Rāma" is enough.

Deeper Meaning
1. Don't chase many mantras (ā mantra ī mantra)
People run from one mantra to another. Purandaradāsa says: hold on to one, the Rāma mantra.

2. Śiva's own mantra (sōmaśēkhara … hēḷida)
Śiva taught Pārvatī that Rāma's name is equal to all the names of Viṣṇu. The mantra comes with the highest authority.

3. Open to all (kula hīnanādaru)
Some mantras are kept secret or limited. Rāma's name can be chanted aloud by anyone, anywhere, even in the street.

4. Vibhīṣaṇa's crown (vibhīṣaṇage paṭṭakaṭṭida)
When Vibhīṣaṇa took refuge in Rāma, he was crowned king of Laṅkā. The mantra rewards those who surrender.

5. The essence of the Vedas (sakala vēdaṅgaḷige sāra)
All the Vedas are summed up in this Name. One who chants it holds their essence.

6. The way to devotion's sweetness (bhakuti rasake … baṭṭe)
The mantra shows the path to the sweetness of devotion, and liberation grows from it as from a root.

@@ 7.8 | Vēṅkaṭācala Nilayam |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Sindhu Bhairavi to Lord Vēṅkaṭēśvara.

He lives on the Vēṅkaṭa hill and in Vaikuṇṭha. He has lotus eyes, is supremely holy, holds the conch and discus, and His form is pure consciousness. He is praised by Brahmā, born of the lotus, and His qualities and names are countless. He loves the songs of Tumburu and Nārada. He wears fish-shaped earrings, is the charming Madana Gōpāla, nourishes His devotees, and is Śrī Purandara Viṭhala.

Key Takeaway
The Lord of Tirumala is the Lord of Vaikuṇṭha, come close to His devotees. Purandaradāsa sees in Him his own Viṭhala.
For each of us: wherever we worship, the same Lord receives it. Tirumala, Paṇḍharpur and our own home shrine meet in Him.

Deeper Meaning
1. On the Vēṅkaṭa hill (vēṅkaṭācala nilayam)
Lord Vēṅkaṭēśvara lives on the seven hills of Tirumala. Devotees climb the hills to see Him, and He comes down to meet them in grace.

2. Also in Vaikuṇṭha (vaikuṇṭha puravāsam)
He lives both on earth and in His own heaven. The temple is a doorway to Vaikuṇṭha.

3. Pure consciousness (cinmaya rūpam)
His form is not made of ordinary matter but of consciousness. The image we see is a window to the Divine.

4. Countless names and qualities (agaṇita guṇa nāmam)
His qualities and names cannot be counted. That is why devotees never tire of singing them.

5. Lover of music (tumburu nārada gānavilōlam)
He delights in the singing of Tumburu and Nārada. Music offered with love pleases Him.

6. Nourisher of devotees (bhakta pōṣaka)
He feeds and cares for those who love Him, as a parent feeds a child.

7. The charming cowherd (madana gōpālam)
The Lord of the hill is also the charming cowherd boy. Grandeur and sweetness meet in Him.

@@ 7.9 | Nīnē Anātha Bandhu |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Kāpi: "You alone are the friend of the friendless, an ocean of mercy."

"What use was it that all the mighty elephants were together? None came to help at the time of need. When the elephant called 'Madhusūdana, father of Manmatha!', You came happily and helped him, Śrī Krishna. What use was it that the woman had five husbands? None helped her when she needed it. When she said 'You are my only refuge, Śrī Krishna!', You came very quickly and helped her. You protected the stone (Ahalyā) and became a father to her line; You were pleased with Bali and gave him a good place. You are the God who protects the devotees who trust You, handsome Purandara Viṭhala, Śrī Krishna."

Key Takeaway
When every human help fails, the Lord comes. The elephant and Draupadī show that a true call to Him is never left unanswered.
For each of us: friends and family are blessings, but in the end only God is always there. We can call Him in any trouble.

Deeper Meaning
1. Friend of the friendless (anātha bandhu)
"Anātha" means one with no protector. The Lord becomes the friend of exactly such people.

2. The elephant's call (madagaja … madhusūdanā endarē)
The king of elephants was caught by a crocodile, and his herd could not help. When he called the Lord, the Lord came.

3. Draupadī's call (patigaḷu aivaru … gati nīnē)
Draupadī had five mighty husbands, yet none could save her in the court. When she called Krishna as her only refuge, He came "very quickly".

4. Ahalyā and Bali (śilaya rakṣisi … baliya golidu)
He freed Ahalyā from the stone and gave Bali a high place. Different stories, the same mercy.

5. Only when we stop relying on others
The help came when the devotee stopped relying on others and turned fully to Him.

6. For those who trust (nambida bhakutara)
He protects "those who trust". Faith is the hand that receives His help.

@@ 7.10 | Murahara Nagadhara |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Ārabhi, each line ending "nīnē gati" (You are my only refuge).

Slayer of Mura, lifter of the mountain, husband of the Earth and Lakṣmī: You are my refuge. Crusher of the cart-demon, loving to those who surrender, wearer of fish-shaped earrings, of spotless life, the original Nārāyaṇa, Rukmiṇī's husband Krishna: You are my refuge. Hari, who entered house after house and drank the milk and ate the butter: You are my refuge. Krishna of great glory, who protects devotees every day without ever leaving them: You are my refuge. Hari who rests on the serpent and rides Garuḍa, whose story is complete: You are my refuge. Handsome Lord of Lakṣmī, Purandara Viṭhala of Honnahoḷe: You are my refuge.

Key Takeaway
"You alone are my refuge" is the whole of surrender. Each name of Krishna is one more reason to say it.
For each of us: whatever we lean on in life, we can remember that the Lord is the one support that never gives way.

Deeper Meaning
1. You alone are my refuge (nīnē gati)
"Gati" means the way, the goal and the shelter. The "ē" means "only". Every line repeats this complete trust.

2. Loving to those who surrender (śaraṇāgata vatsala)
He loves those who take refuge in Him as a cow loves her calf.

3. The butter-thief in every house (mana manegaḷa pokku)
Krishna went from house to house in Gōkula, drinking milk and eating butter. Tradition sees in this how He enters every heart that keeps love for Him.

4. Never leaving His devotees (biḍada kāyva)
He protects devotees every day "without leaving them". His care is constant.

5. Spotless life (akaḷaṅka carita)
His story has no stain. Everything He did was for the good of others.

6. The Lord of Honnahoḷe (honna hoḷeyali)
Purandaradāsa names a local shrine. The Lord of the universe is also the Lord of a particular village and its people.

@@ 7.11 | Mōsa Hōdēnallō |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song of regret by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Śubhapantuvarāḷi: "I have been cheated, oh, I have been cheated!"

"I did not become a servant to the Lord who rests on Śēṣa; I did not become a dāsa of Vāsudēva. I did not bring jasmine, mullai, marugu and davana and worship the Lord with blooming lotus eyes. In my youth I did not trust Śrī Hari's feet; I fell into the ocean of delusion for sixty years. I did not praise Satyabhāmā's beloved, and became a target for the swords in the hands of death's soldiers. I did not throw away shame, tie on ankle-bells and dance always with good people. I did not hold the feet of Purandara Viṭhala, the wish-fulfilling tree of the gods, who is steady."

Key Takeaway
The real loss in life is not serving God while we could. Purandaradāsa speaks this regret so that we can avoid it.
For each of us: it is never too late to begin. The song's sorrow is a call to start now.

Deeper Meaning
1. I was cheated (mōsa hōdenallō)
He was cheated not by others but by his own delay and attachment. The song is honest self-examination.

2. Not becoming His servant (dāsanāgadē)
The first regret is not serving the Lord. Everything else follows from that.

3. Flowers never offered (mallige mulle … pūjaya māḍadē)
Simple worship with flowers was always possible. Not doing it is a lost chance of joy.

4. Sixty years in delusion (movattu varuṣa mōhābdhi)
Many spend their whole lives caught in delusion. Tradition says this song comes from Purandaradāsa's own change of heart after a life focused on wealth.

5. Dancing without shame (lajje biṭṭu … gajje kaṭṭi)
He regrets not having danced in bhajan with ankle-bells, without caring what people think. Devotion should be free of embarrassment.

6. The steady wish-tree (surava taru … susthira)
The Lord is like the heavenly tree that grants every wish, and He is steady. Holding His feet is the one thing that never fails.

@@ 7.12 | Narasimha Pāda |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Behāg: "Worship the feet of Narasimha."

Worshipping Narasimha's feet is like the thunderbolt that shatters mountains of sin. When His devotee (Prahlāda) cried out, He came in great haste and tore out the wicked one's entrails, wearing them at His throat. While the gods trembled and Śrī Dēvi (Lakṣmī) pleaded, our Lord of Vaikuṇṭha came shaking with fury. When Hari (here, the gods led by Brahmā) raised their hands and bowed, He became supremely calm: Purandara Viṭhala.

Key Takeaway
Narasimha's anger is only against evil. To His devotee and to those who bow, He is supremely calm and loving.
For each of us: worshipping His feet breaks down even mountains of wrongdoing. His fierceness protects us.

Deeper Meaning
1. The thunderbolt for sin (durita parvatava … kuliśa)
Indra's thunderbolt can break mountains. Worship of Narasimha breaks the mountains of our sins.

2. Coming in haste (tavakatidali bandu)
When Prahlāda called, the Lord came at once, out of a pillar. He does not delay for His devotee.

3. Ending the wicked (duruḷana karuḷa)
He destroyed Hiraṇyakaśipu, who tormented his own child for his devotion. His fierce form was needed to end such cruelty.

4. Even the gods trembled (suraraḷḷa naḍugalu)
His fury was so great that even the gods and Lakṣmī were afraid. Only Prahlāda's love could approach Him.

5. Supremely calm (parama śāntanāda)
When the gods bowed, He became calm. His true nature is peace; the fury was only for protection.

6. Worship His feet (pāda bhajanaya māḍō)
The song tells us to sing to His feet. Humility before Him is the right response to His power and love.

@@ 7.13 | Bārō Brahmādi Vandyā |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Madamatsarang: "Come, You who are bowed to by Brahmā and the gods; come, son of Vasudēva; come, Krishna, come!"

"Come dancing to the beat 'dhigi dhigi', protector of the humble; come dancing, Lord of the world, handsome Kēśava. Don't go to the cowherds' houses; Gōvinda, listen: I'll give You milk, butter and curds; come and eat. I'll put on You garlands of big, big pearls and look at You; You live in the great city of Dvārakā, Purandara Viṭhala. Come, Krishna, come!"

Key Takeaway
The devotee invites Krishna home like a beloved child: "Come, I will feed You and dress You." That loving invitation is the heart of devotion.
For each of us: we can invite the Lord into our home and our day with the same simple love.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come (bārō)
The whole song is one long, joyful "come!". It is how a mother calls her child home.

2. Bowed to by Brahmā (brahmādi vandyā)
The child being called home is the Lord whom Brahmā and all the gods worship.

3. Come dancing (kuṇidāḍuta bārō)
The devotee asks Krishna to come dancing to the drum's "dhigi dhigi". Bhajan invites the Lord to dance among us.

4. Don't go to other houses (gollara manege pōgalu bēḍa)
"Don't go to the cowherds' houses; I'll give You milk and butter here." The devotee wants Krishna's love for herself, with a playful jealousy.

5. Feeding and dressing Him (hālu beṇṇe … muttina hāra)
Offering food and ornaments to the Lord is a way of loving Him personally, as one would care for a child.

6. Protector of the humble (dīna rakṣaka)
The playful song also calls Him the protector of the humble. The child we invite is our protector.

@@ 7.14 | Gōvinda Ninna Nāmave |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Janasammōdini: "Gōvinda, Your name is beautiful."

Gōvinda fills every atom, every speck of dust, every blade of grass and every stick. Being pure in heart is joy. Gōvinda is the cause of creation, preservation and dissolution; knowing such greatness is joy. Protecting His servants in this way is the joy of the Supreme Person, Śrī Purandara Viṭhala.

Key Takeaway
Gōvinda's name is beautiful because He is everywhere and in everything. Knowing this fills life with joy.
