For each of us: even the smallest thing around us, a grain of dust or a blade of grass, holds Him.

Deeper Meaning
1. Your name is beautiful (ninna nāmave candā)
"Candā" means beautiful, lovely. Purandaradāsa does not argue for the Name; he simply delights in it.

2. In every atom (aṇu rēṇu tṛṇa kāṣṭha)
Gōvinda is in the tiniest particle, the dust, the grass and the wood. Nothing is too small to hold Him.

3. Purity is joy (nirmalātmakanāgi iruvude ānanda)
Keeping a pure heart is itself joy. Purity is not a burden; it is happiness.

4. Cause of all (sṛṣṭi sthiti laya kāraṇa)
He creates, sustains and dissolves the universe. The whole cosmos is His play.

5. Knowing His greatness (mahimeya tiḷiyuvude)
Understanding His glory brings joy. The more we know Him, the more we are delighted.

6. Protecting His servants (dāsara salahuvude)
The song ends with His own joy: protecting His devotees. He delights in caring for us as we delight in Him.

7. Easy to sing
The song is short and simple, so that anyone can join. Its few lines carry a whole teaching about seeing God everywhere.

@@ 8.1 | Smara Vāram Vāram |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Sadāśiva Brahmēndra in Rāgam Jōg: "Remember, O mind, again and again; remember Nanda's son, again and again."

Remember the thief of milk and ghee from the cowherds' huts, who wanders in Gōkula and Vṛndāvana. Remember the one who is fierce in drinking the nectar of His flute's sound, and whose play is the cause of the world's sustaining and dissolving. Remember the parrot in the cage of the paramahaṁsas' hearts, who destroyed the mighty demons Dhēnuka and Baka.

Key Takeaway
Remembering Krishna again and again is the practice. Each memory of His play draws the mind back to Him.
For each of us: the mind wanders, so we keep bringing it back. "Again and again" is simply how devotion works.

Deeper Meaning
1. Again and again (vāraṁ vāram)
The mind forgets quickly. Sadāśiva Brahmēndra, a great sage, tells his own mind to remember Krishna over and over.

2. Speaking to the mind (cētaḥ)
The song is addressed to the mind, as one would gently correct a child. We have to keep reminding ourselves.

3. The butter-thief (payō ghṛta cōram)
Remembering His childhood mischief is joyful. Joy makes remembering easy.

4. The cosmic play (viśva sthiti laya hētu vihāram)
The playful child's game is the cause of the universe. Behind the simple stories is the Supreme.

5. A parrot in the sage's heart (paramahaṁsa hṛt pañjara kīram)
The greatest sages keep Krishna in their hearts like a pet parrot in a cage, always near. Sadāśiva Brahmēndra himself was such a paramahaṁsa.

6. Destroyer of demons (dhēnuka baka saṁhāram)
He ended Dhēnuka and Baka. Remembering Him ends the demons of our own mind.

7. Wandering in Vṛndāvana (gōkula bṛndāvana sañcāram)
Remembering where He walked brings Him near.

@@ 8.2 | Khēlati Mama Hṛdayē |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Sadāśiva Brahmēndra in Rāgam Aṭhāṇā: "Śrī Rāma plays in my heart."

He carries us across the great ocean of delusion and kills the demons led by attachment and hatred. He walks with Peace, the daughter of Vidēha (Sītā). He wanders in the city of Ayōdhyā that is the inner space of the heart (dahara). He lifts up the empire of the paramahaṁsas; His body is truth, knowledge and bliss.

Key Takeaway
Rāma's story is also happening inside us. He crosses our delusion, defeats our inner demons, and lives in our heart as in Ayōdhyā.
For each of us: when we are peaceful inside, Rāma and Sītā are walking together in our heart.

Deeper Meaning
1. Playing in my heart (khēlati mama hṛdayē)
The sage does not look for Rāma outside. Rāma is playing, happily, in his own heart.

2. Across the ocean of delusion (mōha mahārṇava tāraṇa)
Rāma crossed the ocean to Laṅkā. Inside us, He takes us across the ocean of delusion.

3. Inner demons (rāga dvēṣa mukhāsura)
Attachment and hatred are the real demons. Rāma, in the heart, destroys them.

4. Sītā as Peace (śānti vidēhasutā)
Sītā is pictured as Peace itself, walking with Rāma. Where Rāma is, inner peace follows.

5. The heart is Ayōdhyā (dahara ayōdhyā)
The Upaniṣads speak of a small space in the heart (dahara) where the Lord lives. Here that space becomes Ayōdhyā, Rāma's city.

6. Truth, knowledge and bliss (satya jñāna ānanda śarīrī)
His very body is made of truth, knowledge and bliss. Having Him in the heart means having these.

7. Lifting up the sages (paramahaṁsa sāmrājya uddhārī)
He upholds the kingdom of the sages, the inner freedom of those who know Him.

@@ 8.3 | Jaya Tuṅga Taraṅgē |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Sadāśiva Brahmēndra in Rāgam Kuntaḷavarāḷi: "Victory to you, Gaṅgā with lofty waves!"

She is pure enough to purify the whole universe, the box of creation born from the lotus; she is a sickle that cuts many kinds of bondage. She drives away the heaps of people's sins and is full of tortoises, crowds of fish and crocodiles. Her story is told by the paramahaṁsa Guru, and she is worthy of praise by Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Śiva.

Key Takeaway
The holy river is a living form of grace. Her waters wash away sin and cut the bonds that tie us.
For each of us: water, so ordinary, can remind us daily of purity. Bathing can become a small prayer.

Deeper Meaning
1. Lofty waves (tuṅga taraṅgē)
"Tuṅga" means high; it may also recall the river Tuṅgā. The song celebrates the river's powerful flow.

2. Purifying the universe (kamalabhava aṇḍa karaṇḍa pavitrē)
The universe is pictured as a box made by Brahmā. The river's holiness purifies all of it.

3. Cutting bonds (bandha cchēda lavitrē)
She is like a sickle that cuts through the many bonds of worldly life.

4. Removing sins (jana pāpa samūhē)
People come to bathe in holy rivers to be cleansed. Faith in her purifying power is old and deep.

5. Full of life (kacchapa guccha grāhē)
Tortoises, fish and crocodiles live in her. The holy river is also a living home to many creatures.

6. Praised by the Trinity (brahma viṣṇu śaṅkara nuti)
Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Śiva all honour her. The Gaṅgā flows from Viṣṇu's feet and through Śiva's hair.

7. A song of praise for a river
The bhajan sings to the river as to a mother. Reverence for nature is part of devotion.

@@ 8.4 | Bhajarē Yadunātham |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Sadāśiva Brahmēndra in Rāgam Pīlu: "Worship the Lord of the Yadus, O mind."

He loves to be embraced by the cowherd women; He is the young cowherd of wonderful play. He has taken a human form as if in disguise, and in that pretend play He even wore odd costumes. He is the very truth in the hearts of the paramahaṁsas; He is the form of Om, like a rain cloud of Om.

Key Takeaway
The playful cowherd is the truth of Om itself. Worshipping Him joins love and wisdom.
For each of us: we can love Krishna as a child and know Him as the Supreme, both at once.

Deeper Meaning
1. Worship, O mind (bhajarē mānasa)
Again the sage speaks to his own mind. Turning the mind to bhajan is the first practice.

2. The wonderful cowherd boy (gōpa kiśōrakam adbhuta līlam)
His play as a young cowherd is wonderful. It draws the heart effortlessly.

3. A human body as a disguise (kapaṭa aṅgīkṛta mānuṣa vēṣam)
He wears a human form like a disguise. Under it is the Lord of all.

4. Loved by the gōpīs (gōpa vadhū parirambhaṇa lōlam)
The gōpīs' love for Him is the model of pure devotion. He delights in their love.

5. The truth in sages' hearts (paramahaṁsa hṛt tattva)
The greatest sages find Him as the truth in their hearts. The cowherd and the Truth are one.

6. The form of Om (praṇava svarūpam)
Om is the sound of the Supreme. Krishna is that Om, like a rain cloud full of it.

7. Wonderful play (adbhuta līlam)
His play is full of wonder. The sage never tires of it.

@@ 8.5 | Brūhi Mukundēti |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Sadāśiva Brahmēndra in Rāgam Kuriñji: "Say 'Mukunda', O tongue!"

Say "Kēśava, Mādhava, Gōvinda", say "Krishna, Bliss, Eternal Bliss". Say "Rādhā's beloved, Hari, Rāma", say "lotus-eyed, dark as a cloud". Say "rider of Garuḍa, holder of the Nandaka sword", say "the one who cut off the ten-headed one's heads". Say "dear to Akrūra, holder of the discus", say "the pure swan, slayer of Kaṁsa".

Key Takeaway
The tongue is given to us to say the Lord's names. This song simply teaches the tongue what to say.
For each of us: whenever the tongue is idle, it can say one of these names.

Deeper Meaning
1. O tongue (rasanē)
The sage speaks to his own tongue. The tongue that tastes food can also taste the Name.

2. Mukunda, the giver of freedom (mukunda)
"Mukunda" is the one who gives liberation. The first name asked for is the one that frees.

3. Krishna and Rāma together (rādhā ramaṇa harē rāma)
The list mixes Krishna and Rāma freely. Any name of the Lord is welcome on the tongue.

4. Bliss (kṛṣṇānanda sadānanda)
He is bliss and eternal bliss. Saying His names brings a taste of that bliss.

5. Dear to Akrūra (akrūra priya)
Akrūra was sent to bring Krishna to Mathurā and became His beloved devotee. Even a messenger on a hard errand became dear to Him.

6. The pure swan (haṁsa nirañjana)
He is the pure swan, untouched by any stain. Saying His names cleans the tongue and the heart.

7. The cutter of Rāvaṇa's heads (khaṇḍita daśakandhara)
Rāma ended the ten-headed Rāvaṇa. The tongue that names Him remembers His victory over evil.

@@ 8.6 | Pibarē Rāmarasam |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Sadāśiva Brahmēndra in Rāgam Āhir Bhairavi: "Drink the nectar of Rāma, O tongue!"

It keeps the company of sin far away and is full of many kinds of good results. It removes the fear and sorrow of birth and death, and it is the essence of all scriptures, Vedas and Āgamas. It protects the universe born from the lotus and makes even the heretic supremely pure. It is sung in the pure hermitage of the paramahaṁsas and was drunk by sages like Śuka, Śaunaka and Kauśika.

Key Takeaway
Rāma's name is a nectar to be drunk, not just words. It removes fear and sorrow and purifies anyone.
For each of us: we can "drink" the Name slowly, letting its sweetness fill us.

Deeper Meaning
1. Drink it (pibarē)
The Name is not just to be spoken but drunk, tasted and enjoyed.

2. The flavour of Rāma (rāma rasam)
"Rasa" means juice, flavour and the deep taste of feeling. Rāma's name has a sweetness that no worldly taste equals.

3. Far from sin (dūrīkṛta pātaka saṁsargam)
The Name keeps us away from the company of sin.

4. Free from fear of death (janana maraṇa bhaya)
It removes the fear and sorrow of birth and death. The Name is the medicine for our deepest fear.

5. The essence of scriptures (sakala śāstra nigamāgama sāram)
All the Vedas and scriptures are summed up in it.

6. Drunk by the great sages (śuka śaunaka kauśika)
Śuka, Śaunaka and Viśvāmitra (Kauśika) drank this nectar. We drink from the same cup as the greatest sages.

7. Full of good results (pūrita nānāvidha phalavargam)
The Name brings every kind of good fruit, in this life and beyond.

8. Purifying the heretic (pavitrīkṛta pāṣaṇḍam)
Even one who has rejected the scriptures is made pure by it. No one is shut out.

@@ 8.7 | Mānasa Sañcara Rē |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Sadāśiva Brahmēndra in Rāgam Sāma: "O mind, roam in Brahman, roam."

Roam in the one whose locks are decorated with the feathers of the joyful peacock, whose glorious cheeks outshine a mirror. Roam in the one who plays in the fortress of Lakṣmī's heart, the wish-fulfilling tree in His servants' homes. Roam in the moon to the cakōra birds that are the paramahaṁsas, who holds and pours out the full sound of the flute.

Key Takeaway
Brahman, the Supreme, is not a cold idea here: it is Krishna with a peacock feather and a flute. The mind is invited to wander happily in Him.
For each of us: instead of letting the mind wander anywhere, we can let it wander in the Lord's beauty.

Deeper Meaning
1. Roam, O mind (mānasa sañcara rē)
The mind loves to wander. The sage does not fight that; he gives it a place to wander: the Lord.

2. In Brahman (brahmaṇi)
Brahman is the Supreme Reality of the Upaniṣads. The song describes that Reality as Krishna.

3. The peacock feather (śikhi piñcha alaṅkṛta cikurē)
His hair is decorated with a peacock feather. The Supreme is given a beautiful, playful form so that the mind can rest on it.

4. The wish-tree in servants' homes (sēvaka jana mandira mandārē)
He grants wishes in the homes of His servants. He is near those who serve Him.

5. The moon for the cakōra (paramahaṁsa mukha candra cakōrē)
The cakōra bird is said to live on moonlight. The sages live on the sight of the Lord as the cakōra lives on the moon.

6. The flute's full sound (paripūrita muraḷī rava)
The song ends with His flute. Its sound fills everything, and the mind, roaming, finds Him in it.

@@ 8.8 | Sarvam Brahmamayam |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Sadāśiva Brahmēndra in Rāgam Madhuvanti: "Everything is full of Brahman, oh, everything is Brahman."

What is to be said and what is not to be said? What is to be made and what is not? What is to be studied and what is not? What is to be worshipped and what is not? What is to be known and what is not? What is to be enjoyed and what is not? Everywhere and always, meditation on the Self (haṁsa) is what must be done: it is the source of liberation.

Key Takeaway
When everything is seen as God, the difference between "this" and "that" falls away. What remains is steady remembrance of the Self.
For each of us: we can practise seeing the Divine in everyone and everything, even a little, every day.

Deeper Meaning
1. All is Brahman (sarvaṁ brahmamayam)
The Upaniṣads teach that everything is the one Reality. Sadāśiva Brahmēndra lived in this vision.

2. Questions that dissolve (kiṁ vacanīyam kim avacanīyam)
If all is Brahman, what is there to say or not say, to do or not do? The usual divisions lose their hold.

3. Beyond likes and dislikes
To one who sees God everywhere, nothing is "mine" or "not mine", "good to have" or "bad to have" in the old selfish sense.

4. Not careless, but free
This is not permission to be careless. It describes the freedom of a sage who sees God in all.

5. Meditation on the Self (haṁsa dhyānam)
"Haṁsa" here means the Self, the inner witness, like the breath's "so'ham". Constant awareness of it is the one duty.

6. The source of liberation (mukti nidānam)
This awareness is the root of freedom. The bhajan tradition sings even this highest teaching.

@@ 9.1 | Āḍum Cidambaramō |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song by Gōpālakṛṣṇa Bhārati in Rāgam Behāg: "Is this Cidambaram, where my Lord dances? Is this Cidambaram where He performs His dance?"

It is the dancing Cidambaram, where devotees rejoice; the Cidambaram they seek, chanting "namaśśivāya". Unknown to anyone, the Lord of the hall (ambalavāṇar) stands with one holy foot raised, for His devotees to see. The Lord with the cool moon in His matted hair, whom Bālakṛṣṇa (the composer) praises, dances as the cymbals and drums beat "tāttattai".

Key Takeaway
Śiva as Naṭarāja dances in Cidambaram for His devotees. His raised foot is a promise of grace.
For each of us: the cosmic dance goes on everywhere. When we feel joy in devotion, we are near His dance.

Deeper Meaning
1. The dancing Lord (āḍum)
Śiva at Cidambaram is Naṭarāja, the Lord of dance. His dance is creation, protection and release.

2. Devotees' joy (anbar kaḷikka)
The place exists for devotees to rejoice. His dance is for our delight.

3. Namaśśivāya
Devotees seek Cidambaram chanting the five-syllable mantra. Śiva's name leads to His dance.

4. The raised foot (sēvaḍi tūkki)
Naṭarāja stands with one foot lifted. Tradition says the raised foot offers refuge and liberation to those who come.

5. The moon in His hair (panimadi saḍai)
The cool crescent moon rests in His hair. His fierce dance is full of calm and coolness for devotees.

6. The composer's signature (bālakṛṣṇan pōṭrum)
Gōpālakṛṣṇa Bhārati signs as Bālakṛṣṇa, a name of Krishna, praising Śiva. In this tradition the names of Śiva and Viṣṇu meet in one song.

7. The beat of His dance (tāttattai)
The song ends with the sound of cymbals and drums. Bhajan joins the rhythm of His dance.

8. Unknown to anyone (yārum ariyāmal)
He dances quietly, without show. Only devotees see Him.

@@ 9.2 | Eppō Varuvārō |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song by Gōpālakṛṣṇa Bhārati in Rāgam Señjuruṭṭi: "When will He come, to end my sorrow? The Lord of Cidambaram has not told me."

"My good time has come; I seek the Lord. Will He show Himself, so that all my imaginings come true, and take me as His own? I have enjoyed bliss in this small, passing life, but I have not seen the golden feet of the Lord of the golden hall. I have fallen in love with the Lord to whom Bālakṛṣṇa bows in praise, but I do not see Him openly."

Key Takeaway
The longing to see God is itself a form of love. The singer waits, sure that the Lord will come, though he does not know when.
For each of us: the waiting and longing are part of devotion. They keep the heart open for Him.

Deeper Meaning
1. When will He come? (eppō varuvārō)
The song is one long question. Not knowing "when" makes the longing deeper.

2. To end my sorrow (endan kali tīra)
The singer's sorrow will end only when the Lord comes. Nothing else can fill that place.

3. He hasn't told me (ceppiyadillai)
The Lord keeps His timing secret. The devotee trusts without knowing.

4. My time has come (narparuvam vandu)
He feels that the right time for seeking God has arrived. When longing awakens, the search begins.

5. The golden feet (porpadam)
Naṭarāja's feet are golden, in the golden hall of Cidambaram. Seeing them is the goal.

6. In love with the Lord (mēlē kādal koṇḍēn)
The singer says plainly: "I have fallen in love with Him." This love, not duty, keeps the longing alive.

7. A little, passing joy (arpasuga vāzhvil)
The joys of this short life are real but small. The singer wants the lasting joy of seeing the Lord.

8. Bālakṛṣṇa's Lord (bālakṛṣṇan pōṭrip paṇindiḍum īsan)
The composer signs as Bālakṛṣṇa, bowing to Śiva. Love for one form of God does not shut out another.

@@ 10.1 | Tava Dāsōham |
Overall Summary
A song by Tyāgarāja in Rāgam Punnāgavarāḷi: "I am Your servant, I am Your servant, O son of Daśaratha."

He is sweet and gentle in speech, without any fault, the best among men. He is lotus-eyed, supremely holy, the friend of Indra. "I have longed for You, O hero beyond compare; rule over me. Listen to my plea; is this the time to forget me, O treasure of the Sun dynasty? You are dark as a rain cloud, protector of sages, wearing golden silk. I have decided there is no god like You on earth, and I have taken refuge in You. You are praised by the scriptures, free from attachment, and praised by Tyāgarāja."

Key Takeaway
"I am Your servant" is the whole of Tyāgarāja's devotion. He has looked everywhere and found no god like Rāma.
For each of us: saying "I am Yours" to God, honestly, is a complete prayer.

Deeper Meaning
1. I am Your servant (tava dāsōham)
Repeated three times at the start, this is the theme of the song. Being Rāma's servant is Tyāgarāja's identity.

2. Gentle speech (vara mṛdu bhāṣa)
Rāma is known for kind, gentle words. The Lord speaks to us with love.

3. Faultless (virahita dōṣa)
Rāma is without fault. That is why His servant can trust Him completely.

4. Rule over me (nannēlukōrā)
"Ēlukō" means take charge, rule. Tyāgarāja asks Rāma to take full charge of his life.

5. No god like You (nīvaṇṭi daivamu lēdu)
He has decided there is no god equal to Rāma on earth, and has taken refuge in Him.

6. Free from attachment (rāga virahita)
Rāma is untouched by attachment. Tyāgarāja's own name ("rāga virahita tyāgarāja") suggests a play: free of rāga (attachment), but full of rāga (music).

7. Protector of sages (muni janapāla)
Rāma protected the sages in the forest. He protects all who seek the truth.

8. Is this the time to forget? (marava samayamā)
Tyāgarāja gently asks whether this is the moment to forget him. It is a child's loving reminder.

@@ 10.2 | Śrī Rāma Śrī Rāma |
Overall Summary
A song by Tyāgarāja in Rāgam Sahānā, a loving complaint: "Śrī Rāma, Śrī Rāma, charming one, why won't Your mercy come even a little?"

"Is this not enough, O kind Lord? Can You not bear it? They say You won't protect me now, but later; alas, You may be kind someday. Is there still some secret? Is this fair of You? O lotus-faced one, look at me properly. Is it a sin from some birth, or someone's curse? Is it anger from some old day, or my own fault? How long this misery? Is this right for You? They say You speak and are filled with joy, that You are supremely calm, that You are the beloved of devotees, lotus-eyed, that You are everything, the form of truth. O Rāma, free from attachment, praised by Tyāgarāja."

Key Takeaway
Tyāgarāja argues lovingly with Rāma, using Rāma's own good name: "They say You are kind; then why not to me?" Such honesty is a sign of closeness.
For each of us: we can be honest with God about our pain. Questioning out of love is still prayer.

Deeper Meaning
1. Why not even a little mercy? (intaina rādayā)
Tyāgarāja is not asking for a lot, only "a little". The complaint is gentle.

2. Is it fair? (idi nīku dharmamā)
He asks Rāma whether this is just. Only someone very close dares to ask God this.

3. What is the cause? (ē janma pāpamō)
He wonders whether a past sin, a curse or an old anger is the reason. He is willing to accept his own fault.

4. "They say You are…" (aṭa)
Many lines end "aṭa", "so they say". He reminds Rāma of His reputation: kind, calm, the beloved of devotees.

5. Look at me properly (bāguga jūḍumā)
He asks Rāma just to look at him fully. One real glance would be enough.

6. Love behind the complaint
The whole song is still addressed to "Śrī Rāma, charming one". The complaint is wrapped in love.

7. Supremely calm (parama śāntuḍu)
Rāma is known to be calm and gentle. Tyāgarāja appeals to that calm.

@@ 10.3 | Pāhi Kalyāṇa Rām |
Overall Summary
A song by Tyāgarāja in Rāgam Kharaharapriyā: "Protect me, auspicious Rāma, Rāma of pure qualities."

Then he tells Rāma what Rāma is to him: "My life's support, my auspicious form, the fruit of my vows, the strength of my body, the wealth of my family, my very own; the joy of my mind, the root of my happiness, my contentment, my darling form, my charm, my beauty, my share of good fortune, my detachment, my life, my youth." Then he tells what Rāma is to the world: the essence of the scriptures, far from the demons, the support of the three worlds, a pearl necklace, God of gods, the absence of the wicked, the Supreme Brahman, a lion to the elephant of sin, beyond all ideas, God's own birth, higher than all, beyond māyā, hidden in the ocean (as Viṣṇu), and Tyāgarāja's dearest friend.

Key Takeaway
For Tyāgarāja, Rāma is everything: his life, his wealth, his joy, his beauty. There is nothing in his life that is not Rāma.
For each of us: we can try to see God in each part of our own life: our work, our family, our joys.

Deeper Meaning
1. Protect me (pāhi)
The song begins with a simple plea, then explains why Rāma is the only one to ask.

2. "My" in every line (nā … nādu)
Almost every line starts with "my". Tyāgarāja makes Rāma his own in every way.

3. My detachment (nādu vairāgyamu)
Even his detachment is Rāma. Letting go of the world is not loss when it means holding Rāma.

4. The Supreme Brahman (paramaina brahmamu)
The same Rāma who is "my darling" is the Supreme Reality.

5. A lion to sin (pāpēbha siṁhamu)
Sin is like an elephant, huge and powerful. Rāma is the lion that defeats it.

6. Tyāgarāja's dear friend (tyāgarājāptamu)
The last word makes Rāma Tyāgarāja's close friend. The Lord of all is a friend to His devotee.

7. The fruit of my vows (nā nōmu phalamu)
All his vows and practices have borne one fruit: Rāma.

8. Beyond māyā (māyātītamu)
Rāma is beyond illusion. Holding Him, we are held beyond it too.

9. Hidden in the ocean (sāgara guptamu)
As Viṣṇu, He rests hidden in the milk-ocean. The treasure is deep, but it is found.

@@ 10.4 | Marugēlarā |
Overall Summary
A song by Tyāgarāja in Rāgam Jayantaśrī: "Why this veil, O Rāghava?"

"Why hide from me, You who have the form of all moving and unmoving things, beyond the highest, whose eyes are the sun and the moon? I searched deep inside my heart and came to know clearly that You are everything. I cannot think of anyone but You in my mind; please protect me, O one praised by Tyāgarāja."

Key Takeaway
Tyāgarāja has realised that Rāma is everything, yet Rāma still seems hidden. He asks Him to drop the veil.
For each of us: knowing God is everywhere is the start. Feeling His closeness is the longing that keeps us going.

Deeper Meaning
1. Why the veil? (marugēlarā)
"Marugu" is a screen or veil. Tyāgarāja asks why Rāma stays hidden from His own devotee.

2. All that moves and does not move (carācara rūpa)
Rāma is the form of everything in the universe. He hides by being everywhere.

3. Sun and moon as His eyes (sūrya sudhākara lōcana)
The sun and moon are His eyes. He sees all, even when we do not see Him.

4. Searching inside (antaraṅgamuna dinnagā vedaki)
Tyāgarāja looked deeply within. The search for God is an inner search.

5. You are everything (anni nīvanucu)
His search found one answer: Rāma is all. This is the vision of the Upaniṣads, felt with love.

6. No one else in my mind (ninnēgāni madinenna jāla)
He cannot think of anyone else. That single-minded love is why he dares to ask Rāma to remove the veil.

7. Beyond the highest (parātpara)
Rāma is beyond even the highest. Yet He can be asked, like a friend, to come out from behind the screen.

@@ 10.5 | Kamalāpta Kula |
Overall Summary
A song by Tyāgarāja in Rāgam Bṛndāvana Sāraṅgā: "O moon risen from the milk-ocean of the Sun dynasty, please protect me, ocean of mercy."

He is Lakṣmī's husband, Kausalyā's good son, of lovely body, the friend of Śiva (enemy of Kāma). "I have heard of all the ways You protected Your servants before; hearing it, I have taken refuge at Your feet. Seeing me, give me fearlessness with Your hand, O lotus-eyed one, praised by Śrī Tyāgarāja."

Key Takeaway
Tyāgarāja takes refuge in Rāma because he has heard how Rāma protected His devotees before. Rāma's past kindness gives him hope.
For each of us: the stories of how God helped others are promises to us. We can lean on them.

Deeper Meaning
1. A moon from the Sun dynasty (kamalāpta kula kalaśābdhi candra)
"Kamalāpta" is the sun, friend of the lotus. Rāma rises in the Sun dynasty like the moon from the ocean: cooling and calming.

2. Ocean of mercy (karuṇā samudra)
His mercy is as vast as the ocean. There is room for every devotee.

3. Friend of Śiva (kāmāri mitra)
Śiva, who burnt Kāma, is Rāma's friend. Rāma and Śiva are close in the tradition.

4. Hearing of His kindness (munu dāsula brōcinadella)
Tyāgarāja has heard all the stories of how Rāma saved His servants. Listening to these stories builds faith.

5. Refuge at His feet (caraṇāśrituḍai)
He has come to Rāma's feet for shelter. Hearing turns into surrender.

6. Give me fearlessness (abhayam iyyavayyā)
He asks Rāma to raise His hand in the "fear not" sign. That gesture is the promise of protection.

7. Kausalyā's good son (kausalyā suputra)
Rāma is remembered as a loving son. The Lord of all honours His mother.

@@ 10.6 | Jō Jō Rāma Ānandaghana |
Overall Summary
A lullaby by Tyāgarāja in Rāgam Rītigauḷa: "Sleep, sleep, Rāma, mass of bliss."

Sleep, son of Daśaratha; sleep, Rāma, who loves worship. Sleep, jewel of the Raghu clan; sleep, Rāma with curly locks. Sleep, Rāma, beyond all qualities; sleep, Rāma, a wish-tree of good qualities. Sleep, Rāma, whose eyes are the sun and moon; sleep, Rāma who rests on the great serpent. Sleep, Rāma of soft speech; sleep, Rāma of lovely form. Sleep, Rāma worshipped by Tyāgarāja; sleep, Rāma surrounded by devotees.

Key Takeaway
Singing a lullaby to Rāma is loving Him like a parent. The Lord accepts this tender, everyday love.
For each of us: we can end our day by "putting the Lord to sleep" in our heart, with a quiet song or a name.

Deeper Meaning
1. A lullaby for God (jō jō)
"Jō jō" is what mothers sing to put a baby to sleep. Tyāgarāja sings it to Rāma.

2. A mass of bliss (ānandaghana)
The baby being rocked is "solid bliss". Caring for Him fills the singer with joy.

3. Beyond qualities, full of qualities (nirguṇa rūpa … suguṇa kalpa)
Rāma is beyond all qualities, and also a wish-tree of good qualities. The lullaby holds both truths.

4. Resting on the serpent (phaṇivara śayana)
As Viṣṇu, He rests on Ādiśēṣa. The baby in the cradle is the Lord who rests on the cosmic serpent.

5. Surrounded by devotees (bhakta samāja)
He sleeps among His devotees. The bhajan itself is a cradle for Him.

6. Parental love (vātsalya)
This is the mood of Yaśōdā and Kausalyā: loving God as one's own child. It is one of the sweetest forms of devotion.

7. Lover of worship (pūjā lōla)
He loves to be worshipped. Every small offering pleases Him.

@@ 11.18 | Rāghavā Rāma |
Overall Summary
A nāmāvaḷi chanting the names of the Lord, the Guru and Viṭhala, each repeated many times: Rāghavā Rāma; Gōvindā Harē; Gōpālā Harē; Śaṅkarā Śiva; Ṣaṇmukhā Śiva, child Ṣaṇmukha; Sadgurō jaya; and Viṭhala Hari.

Key Takeaway
All forms of God are chanted in one flowing nāmāvaḷi: Rāma, Krishna, Śiva, Murugan, the Guru and Viṭhala.
For each of us: in a group nāmāvaḷi, we only have to answer the leader. The group's devotion carries us along.

Deeper Meaning
1. Rāghava and Gōvinda
The chant starts with Rāma and Krishna. In the bhajan, the names of Viṣṇu's avatāras flow together.

2. Śiva and Ṣaṇmukha (śaṅkarā śiva … ṣaṇmukhā)
Then come Śiva and His son Murugan (Ṣaṇmukha, the six-faced one). The tradition welcomes all forms.

3. The child Ṣaṇmukha (bāla ṣaṇmukhā)
Murugan is called as a child, with the same tenderness given to the child Krishna.

4. The Sadguru (sadgurō jaya)
The Guru is chanted among the gods. In this tradition the Guru is the way to all of them.

5. Viṭhala Hari
The chant ends with Viṭhala, the Lord of Paṇḍharpur, dear to the Name tradition.

6. Repetition (rāghavā rāma rāghavā …)
Each name is repeated again and again. Repetition quietens the mind and lets the Name sink in.

7. Many names, one goal
Rāma, Krishna, Śiva, Murugan, the Guru and Viṭhala are chanted one after another. In this tradition every form leads to the same Lord, and no devotee is left out.

@@ 11.19 | Śrī Rāmā Śrī Rāmā |
Overall Summary
A Marathi-style call in Rāgam Kēdāram, each line ending "yērē" (come!).

Śrī Rāma, moon of the ocean of the Sun dynasty, home of auspiciousness, come! Home of happiness, who fulfils every wish, dark as a cloud, come! Lotus-eyed, peaceful one, resting on the king of serpents, come! Dattātrēya, who removes the three kinds of suffering, of wonderful form, come! Gōvinda, the wish of good people, whose face is joy itself, come! Come, Rāma, dark as a cloud, home of auspiciousness; come, Krishna, Pāṇḍuraṅga, Lord of Paṇḍharpur; Hari Viṭhala, come; Guru Viṭhala, come; Prabhu Viṭhala, come!

Key Takeaway
The song calls Rāma, Dattātrēya, Gōvinda and Viṭhala to come. The devotee wants the Lord present, here and now.
For each of us: "come" is the simplest prayer. We can say it whenever we want to feel His nearness.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come (yērē)
"Yērē" is Marathi for "come". Each line ends in this call.

2. Moon of the Sun dynasty (sūrya kula ambudhi sōma)
Rāma rises in the Sun dynasty like the cool moon from the ocean. He brings calm.

3. Dattātrēya (dattātrēya)
The Guru-god Dattātrēya is called too. He removes the three kinds of suffering.

4. Gōvinda the joy (svānanda śrīmukha)
Gōvinda's face is joy itself. Good people long for Him.

5. Viṭhala in three forms (hari viṭhala, guru viṭhala, prabhu viṭhala)
Viṭhala is called as Hari, as Guru and as Lord. He is all three to His devotee.

6. Fulfiller of wishes (pūrṇa manōratha)
He fulfils every wish of the heart. The deepest wish is simply for Him to come.

7. Lotus-eyed and peaceful (śāntayana … kamala daḷāyata nayana)
He is calm and lotus-eyed, resting on the serpent. Peace flows from His form.

8. Lord of Paṇḍharpur (paṇḍharinātha)
Krishna is called as Pāṇḍuraṅga, the Lord of Paṇḍharpur, dear to the Marathi saints.

@@ 11.20 | Rāma Bhadra |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Bhadrācala Rāmadās in Rāgam Ānanda Bhairavi: "Rāmabhadra, come; Śrī Rāmacandra, come; lotus-eyed one, come with Sītā."

"Come sweetly, darling, come, O charming one; come, lotus-eyed one with cheeks like mirrors. Come, so that the love of Your chief queen holds my hand; come quickly, kissed firmly by Kausalyā. Come, original Brahman, root of the three worlds; come with Your anklet-bells jingling 'ghallu ghallu'. Come, lover of Sāma music, my handsome one; come, Lord of Bhadrācala, who rules Rāmadās."

Key Takeaway
Rāmadās calls Rāma like a parent calling a toddler, with His little bells jingling. The Supreme Brahman comes as a child for love.
For each of us: we can call the Lord with affection, not only with awe. He comes to love.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come (rārā)
"Rārā" is a tender "come". Every line invites Rāma closer.

2. Darling (muddu muddugā)
Rāma is called "muddu", darling. The relationship is full of affection.

3. Kissed by Kausalyā (kausalya muddu baṭṭi)
His mother Kausalyā kisses Him. The devotee shares in her love.

4. The root of the three worlds (mujjagamulagu ādi mūla brahma)
The toddler with jingling bells is the original Brahman. Love and wisdom see the same Lord.

5. Anklet bells (gajjala cappuḷḷu)
Rāmadās wants to hear Rāma's anklets jingle as He comes. The Lord's coming is joyful and near.

6. Lord of Bhadrācala (bhadrācalēśa)
The song ends with the Lord of Bhadrācala, who rules over Rāmadās.

7. Cheeks like mirrors (addampu cekkiḷḷavāḍa)
His cheeks are smooth and bright like mirrors. Rāmadās lingers on the details of the beloved child's face.

8. Lover of the Sāma chant (sāmagāna lōla)
Rāma loves the music of the Sāma Veda. The bhajan's music, too, delights Him.

9. My handsome one (nā cakkanayya)
Rāmadās calls Rāma "my handsome one". Love makes the Supreme personal and close.

@@ 11.21 | Bēga Bārō |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song in Rāgam Māṇḍ: "Come quickly, come quickly, You with the colour of a dark rain cloud; come quickly, handsome Lord of Bēlūr."

Come quickly, Gōvinda, beloved of Indirā; come quickly, Mukunda, Nanda's child. Come quickly, the steady, generous, deep one; come quickly, Raghuvīra, adorned with garlands. Come quickly, the lofty Raṅga, Narasimha; come quickly, Pāṇḍuraṅga of the Gaṅgā's bank. Come quickly, the one who helps us to victory; come quickly, Hayavadana, who lives on Uragādri.

Key Takeaway
"Come quickly!" is a devotee's urgent call. Every form of the Lord is named, and each is asked to hurry.
For each of us: when we need the Lord, we can call Him with urgency. He does not mind our impatience.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come quickly (bēga bārō)
The devotee is impatient for the Lord. This eagerness is a sign of love.

2. Dark as a rain cloud (nīla mēgha varṇā)
A dark cloud brings rain to dry land. His coming refreshes the heart.

3. Lord of Bēlūr (vēlāpurada cenna)
Cenna Kēśava of Bēlūr is named first. The Lord of a particular temple is called with love.

4. Many forms (gōvinda … raghuvīra … narasiṅgha … pāṇḍuraṅga)
Krishna, Rāma, Narasimha, Raṅganātha and Viṭhala are all called. The one Lord is welcome in any form.

5. The helper to victory (vijaya sahāya)
He helps us win the inner battle. With Him, victory is sure.

6. Hayavadana (hayavadana)
The song ends with Hayavadana, the horse-faced form dear to Vādirāja.

7. Steady and deep (dhīra udāra gambhīra)
He is steady, generous and deep. His coming brings calm, not excitement only.

8. Many shrines in one song
The song names Bēlūr, Śrīraṅgam, Paṇḍharpur and the hill of Hayavadana. The Lord of every temple is the same One.

@@ 11.22 | Dēva Bandā Namma |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa: "God has come, our Raṅga has come; the crown-jewel of gods has come!"

He who rests on the serpent has come; He who rides Garuḍa has come; Śrī Nārāyaṇa in the form of Narahari has come. Daśaratha's child has come; death to the ten-headed one has come; He of ten forms has come, with Sītā. He who is known through the Vedas has come; the ever-content one has come; the smiling Purandara Viṭhala has come!

Key Takeaway
This is the joyful cry of devotees when the Lord arrives. Every form of Him is welcomed at once.
For each of us: when we gather for bhajan, the Lord comes among us. We can feel that joy: "He has come!"

Deeper Meaning
1. He has come (bandā)
Every line announces His arrival. It is the excitement of a festival when the Lord's procession arrives.

2. Our Raṅga (namma raṅga)
He is "our" Raṅga, belonging to the devotees. The Lord comes as one of the family.

3. The ten forms (daśavidha rūpa)
He comes in all ten avatāras. Whatever form we love, He comes in it.

4. With Sītā (sītā samēta)
Rāma comes with Sītā. The Lord and His Śakti come together.

5. Known through the Vedas (nigama gōcara)
The one the Vedas describe is now here, seen with our eyes.

6. The smiling one (nagumukha)
Purandara Viṭhala comes smiling. His coming is full of joy.

7. The rider of Garuḍa (garuḍa gamana)
He comes on Garuḍa, His swift carrier. He hurries to His devotees.

8. Ever content (nitya tṛpta)
He needs nothing, yet He comes to us. His coming is pure grace.

9. Narahari (narahari rūpa)
He comes as the man-lion who saved Prahlāda. The Lord arrives ready to protect.

@@ 11.23 | Yēhi Gōpāla |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song inviting Krishna: "Come, Gōpāla, wearing the forest garland, Rādhā's darling, come; O son of Nanda, come; Krishna, butter-thief, come."

Come, dark as a cloud, of charming form, holder of the flute. Come, destroyer of Kaṁsa, crusher of Kāḷiya, wearer of golden cloth. Come, lotus-footed, lotus-naveled, lotus-eyed one. Come, whose feet are worshipped by loving devotees, honoured by Brahmā and all.

Key Takeaway
The devotee invites Krishna to come with every one of His lovely qualities. Each verse adds a new reason to call Him.
For each of us: we can begin our prayer or pūjā with a simple "come, Gōpāla".

Deeper Meaning
1. Come (yēhi, āyāhi)
The song repeats "come" in every line. It is the invitation at the start of worship.

2. Rādhā's darling (rādhā lōla)
Krishna is the one Rādhā loves, and who loves her. Calling Him by her name draws Him quickly.

3. The butter-thief (navanīta cōra)
His childhood mischief is lovingly remembered. The thief of butter is the thief of hearts.

4. Destroyer of Kaṁsa and Kāḷiya (kaṁsa dhvaṁsana kāḷiya mardana)
He removed the cruel king and the poisonous serpent. He removes the cruelty and poison in our lives.

5. All lotus (paṅkaja caraṇa, nābha, nayana)
His feet, navel and eyes are all compared to lotuses. Everything about Him is pure and beautiful.

6. Worshipped by loving devotees (prēmikā bhāvuka pūjita)
Those who love Him deeply worship His feet. Love is the right way to call Him.

7. Dark as a cloud (mēgha śyāmaḷa)
His dark form is like a rain cloud: it refreshes and gives life.

@@ 11.24 | Ambē Gaurī Maiyā |
Overall Summary
A Hindi ārati to the Divine Mother in Rāgam Māṇḍ: "Ambē Gaurī Mother, victory, auspicious form, O Mother."

Hari, Brahmā and Śiva sing to You day and night. The red sindūr shines in Your parting, with a soft musk tilaka. Earrings shine in Your ears, a pearl at the tip of Your nose; Your light is like millions of moons and suns. Your matted hair is golden; You are the wife of Hara (Śiva); with bright, lifted eyes, Your face is like the moon.

Key Takeaway
The Divine Mother is praised by Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Śiva. Her light is greater than countless suns and moons.
For each of us: we can come to God as Mother, with the trust of a child.

Deeper Meaning
1. Ambē, Mother (ambē gaurī maiyā)
"Ambē" and "maiyā" both mean Mother. The song speaks to the Goddess with the love of a child.

2. Auspicious form (maṅgaḷa mūrti)
She is the form of all that is auspicious. Where She is, good fortune comes.

3. Sung by the Trinity (hari brahma śiva)
Even Viṣṇu, Brahmā and Śiva sing to Her day and night. She is the power behind them all.

4. Simple marks of beauty (sindūra … ṭīkā … mōtī)
Sindūr, a tilaka, earrings and a nose-pearl. The ārati pictures the Mother as a devotee sees Her.

5. Light of countless suns (kōṭika candra divākara)
Her light outshines millions of suns and moons. Yet it is gentle to Her children.

6. Śiva's beloved (hara hārī)
She is Gaurī, Śiva's wife. In this bhajan the Mother is honoured within the Name tradition.

7. Bright, lifted eyes (ujjala tējō unnayanā)
Her eyes are bright and alert. The Mother watches over her children.

@@ 11.25 | Nāma Mahimai Ślōkams |
Overall Summary
Seven verses on the glory of the Divine Name (nāma mahimā), from scripture.

Verse 1: One who, leaving shyness aside, constantly sings My names, together with crores of generations of his family, reaches My abode.
Verse 2: Singing, dancing and acting out Viṣṇu's glories should be done by the twice-born as a daily duty.
Verse 3: Do not waste time; life is shrinking moment by moment; Yama (death) has no mercy; sing Hari's praise.
Verse 4: "I do not live in Vaikuṇṭha, nor in yogis' hearts, nor in the sun; wherever My devotees sing, there I stay, Nārada."
Verse 5: Hari's name alone, His name alone, His name alone is my life; in the Kali age there is no other way, no other way, no other way.
Verse 6: Devotees gather together, place a lamp in the middle, meditate on Me as light and sing My qualities, and walk around: they get the merit of walking around the whole earth, no doubt.
Verse 7: I bow to Hari, the Supreme, whose nāma saṅkīrtana destroys all sins, and bowing to whom ends sorrow.

Key Takeaway
The scriptures say plainly: in this age, the Name is the way. Wherever devotees sing, the Lord is present.
For each of us: when we sing His names with others, the Lord is there with us, as He promised Nārada.

Deeper Meaning
1. Without shyness (visṛjya lajjām)
The Name should be sung openly, without embarrassment. Shyness is a small form of pride.

2. Singing and dancing as duty (gānaṁ ca nṛtyaṁ ca)
Singing and dancing for the Lord are called a daily duty, not just a hobby.

3. Life is short (kṣīṇam āyuḥ)
Each moment, life grows shorter. Don't postpone the Name.

4. Where devotees sing (madbhaktā yatra gāyanti)
This verse is the heart of the Name tradition: the Lord lives where His devotees sing. Bhajan is His home.

5. Three times "no other way" (nāstyēva nāstyēva nāstyēva)
The verse repeats three times that in Kali there is no other way. It is meant to remove all doubt.

6. Around the lamp (dīpaṁ madhyē)
Devotees singing and walking around a lamp gain the merit of walking around the whole earth. This is the root of the dīpa pradakṣiṇam in the bhajan.

7. Bowing to Hari (namāmi harim param)
The verses end in a bow: to the Lord whose names destroy sin and whose worship ends sorrow.

@@ 11.26 | Dhanya Dhanya |
Overall Summary
A Marathi song for walking around the Sadguru (pradakṣiṇā): "Blessed, blessed is the circling of the Sadguru, of my Śrī Guru, the king!"

The best of gods hurried to bring down their heavenly chariots to see it. At every step there were endless heaps of merit; all holy places, starting with Kāśī, came to us. Devotees sing with mṛdaṅga, cymbals and drums, full of feeling; in nāma saṅkīrtana they dance in the bliss of Brahman. Prostrating destroys even crores of great sins; rolling on the ground, liberation falls at one's feet. The Vedas and Āgamas cannot grasp the glory of the Guru's bhajan; only those who live at the Guru's feet know it by experience. Walking around, the body-feeling was carried away; Śrī Raṅgātmaja Viṭhala stood before us.

Key Takeaway
Walking around the Guru with devotion is a pilgrimage in itself. All holy places come to those who honour the Guru.
For each of us: the dīpa pradakṣiṇa in bhajan is not a mere walk. Each step taken with devotion is blessed.

Deeper Meaning
1. Blessed (dhanya)
The word is repeated: blessed, blessed. The circling is a joyful act.

2. The gods come to watch (suravarā vimāna)
Even the gods come down in their chariots to see the devotees' circling. Bhajan is a sight for heaven.

3. All holy places (sarvahī tīrthē)
Every holy place, even Kāśī, comes to the devotees. The Guru's feet are the greatest pilgrimage.

4. Dancing in bliss (āndē nācatī)
Devotees sing and dance with drums and cymbals. The bliss of the Name moves the body.

5. Known by experience (anubhava tē jāṇatī)
Even the Vedas cannot explain the Guru's bhajan. Only those who live at the Guru's feet know it.

6. Forgetting the body (dēha bhāvē vāhilā)
As they circle, the feeling "I am this body" is carried away. Then Viṭhala stands before them.

7. The Guru as the king (sadgurū rāyācī)
The Guru is called "rāya", king. In this tradition the Guru is honoured above all, because he brings us to God.

@@ 11.27 | Nāmāvaḷi |
Overall Summary
A Hindi nāmāvaḷi: "Nārada, Prahlāda and the gathering of devotees sang: victory to Gōvinda, victory to Gōpāla, Mādhava, king of the Yadus." Mādhava, Yadu king, whose name is sweet; the devotees sang and found supreme bliss. Victory to the gathering of devotees, victory to Krishna's name; Gōvinda Gōpāla, victory, victory.

Key Takeaway
The great devotees Nārada and Prahlāda sang the same names we sing. Singing them, we join their company.
For each of us: we are part of a long line of singers. Our voices join theirs.

Deeper Meaning
1. Nārada and Prahlāda (nārada prahlāda)
Nārada, the singing sage, and Prahlāda, the child devotee, are the great examples of the Name tradition.

2. The gathering of devotees (bhakta bṛnda)
The Name is sung together. The group of devotees is honoured as much as the Lord.

3. A sweet name (madhura nāma dhēya)
Mādhava's name is "sweet". Singing it brings sweetness to the heart.

4. Supreme bliss (paramānanda pāyā)
The devotees who sang found supreme bliss. The promise is the same for us.

5. Victory to the Name (kṛṣṇa nāma kī jay)
The Name itself is hailed. In this tradition, the Name and the Lord are one.

6. A simple ending
The nāmāvaḷi ends with "Gōvinda Gōpāla, jay". Easy words, so that everyone can join in.

7. Victory to the devotees (bhakta bṛnda kī jay)
The nāmāvaḷi hails the gathering of devotees before the Name. Good company is itself a blessing.

8. King of the Yadus (yadurāyā)
Krishna is hailed as the king of His clan. The Lord we sing to is royal and close at once.

@@ 11.28 | Pāhi Pāhi Gajānana |
Overall Summary
A nāmāvaḷi to Gaṇēśa: "Protect us, protect us, Gajānana (elephant-faced one), son of Pārvatī." He rides the mouse, holds the mōdaka sweet, has ears like whisks and a hanging sacred thread, takes a short form, is the son of Mahēśvara, the remover of obstacles; we bow at His feet. It ends with a verse: "I bow to the lotus feet of Vighnēśvara, elephant-faced, served by the bhūta-gaṇas, who eats the essence of wood-apple and rose-apple fruits, son of Umā, the cause of the end of sorrow."

Key Takeaway
Gaṇēśa, the remover of obstacles, is greeted with love. His blessing clears the way.
For each of us: before any task, a moment with Gaṇēśa reminds us to begin with humility and trust.

Deeper Meaning
1. Protect us (pāhi pāhi)
The nāmāvaḷi asks Gaṇēśa's protection, again and again.

2. Son of Pārvatī (pārvati nandana)
He is the beloved child of the Divine Mother. Mothers' love flows through Him to us.

3. Rider of the mouse (mūṣika vāhana)
The great elephant-faced god rides a tiny mouse. Tradition sees in it the mastery of desire, which, like a mouse, gets into everything.

4. The mōdaka (mōdaka hasta)
He holds a sweet. His grace is sweet, and He gives it freely.

5. Remover of obstacles (vighna vināyaka)
He removes what stands in the way of good work and of devotion.

6. Cause of the end of sorrow (śōka vināśa kāraṇam)
The verse calls Him the one who ends sorrow. Bowing at His feet brings relief.

7. Short in form (vāmana rūpa)
He is called short and stout. Greatness does not depend on size.

8. Son of Mahēśvara (mahēśvara putra)
He is Śiva's son as well as Pārvatī's. The Divine family is honoured together.

@@ 11.29 | Kṛṣṇa Rāma Gōvinda |
Overall Summary
A nāmāvaḷi in Rāgam Punnāgavarāḷi: "Krishna, Rāma, Gōvinda, Harē Rāma Krishna Gōvinda; Krishna, Kēśava, Gōvinda."

It strings together Krishna's and Rāma's names: Krishna-bliss, Mukunda, Murāri, Vāmana, Mādhava, Gōvinda; Śrīdhara, Kēśava, Rāghava, Viṣṇu, Lord of Lakṣmī, Narasimha. Nanda's son, butter-thief, lover of devotees, dear to those devoted to Him, who loves the flute, Vijaya Gōpāla. It ends: Krishna Rāma Gōvinda Nārāyaṇa; Rāma Krishna Gōvinda Nārāyaṇa.

Key Takeaway
Krishna, Rāma and Nārāyaṇa are sung as one. The nāmāvaḷi lets the mind rest in a flow of His names.
For each of us: simple repetition, without thinking too much, can quieten the mind like nothing else.

Deeper Meaning
1. Krishna and Rāma together
The names swap places, Krishna Rāma and Rāma Krishna. There is no difference between them.

2. Bliss itself (kṛṣṇānanda)
Krishna is bliss. Saying His name brings a taste of it.

3. Lover of devotees (bhakta vatsala)
He loves those who love Him as a mother loves her child.

4. Dear to the devoted (bhāgavata priya)
His devotees are dear to Him. Being among them brings us close to Him.

5. Vijaya Gōpāla (vijaya gōpāla)
The name dear to this tradition appears again: the victorious cowherd.

6. Ending in Nārāyaṇa
The chant ends with Nārāyaṇa, the name of the Lord of all. Every name leads there.

7. Murāri and Vāmana (murārē vāmana)
He is Murāri who slew Mura, and Vāmana who took three steps. Each name recalls a deed.

8. Śrīdhara and Narasimha (śrīdhara … narasimha)
The list ends with Lakṣmī's Lord and Narasimha. Gentle and fierce forms are named together.

9. Joining in
A nāmāvaḷi needs no learning. Anyone can answer the leader, and the group's devotion lifts each voice.

@@ 11.30 | Gōpikā Gītam |
Overall Summary
The Song of the Gōpīs from the Śrīmad Bhāgavatam (Book 10, chapter 31), sung after a meditation verse. When Krishna disappeared from the rāsa dance, the gōpīs, their minds absorbed in Him, came back to the Yamunā bank and sang His praise, longing for Him to return.

In nineteen verses they say: Vraja is glorious because You were born here, and Lakṣmī lives here; Your people search for You, holding on to life for Your sake. You protected us again and again from poisoned water, the serpent, the demons, storms, fire and every fear. You are not just Yaśōdā's son; You are the inner witness of all beings, who came at Brahmā's request to protect the world. Place Your lotus hand, which gives fearlessness, on our heads. Show us Your lovely face, You who remove the sorrow of Vraja and whose smile breaks Your people's pride. Your feet remove the sins of those who bow; place them on our hearts. Your story is nectar for those who burn, praised by poets, removing sin, auspicious to hear; those who spread it are the most generous of people. Your smile, Your loving glance, Your play are beautiful to meditate on; when You go to graze the cows, we worry that stones and grass hurt Your soft feet. A moment without seeing You feels like an age. We left husbands, children and family to come to You; who would leave women like us at night? Your appearance in Vraja removes sorrow and is good for the world; give us a little of the medicine that cures the pain in Your devotees' hearts. Between the verses, the bhajan sings "Rāma Rāghava, Sītā Rāma Rāghava; Krishna Kēśava, Rādhā Gōpī Mādhava".

Key Takeaway
The Gōpikā Gītam is the cry of hearts that cannot live without Krishna. It is the highest example of longing in love for God.
For each of us: we may not feel the gōpīs' intensity, but singing their words lets a little of that longing grow in us.

Deeper Meaning
1. Absorbed in Him (tanmanaskāḥ tad ātmikāḥ)
The meditation verse says the gōpīs' minds, actions and very selves were filled with Krishna. They forgot their own homes.

2. Protected again and again (rakṣitā muhuḥ)
They remember how He saved them from many dangers. Love remembers every kindness.

3. Not just Yaśōdā's son (na khalu gōpikā nandanō bhavān)
They know He is the inner witness of all beings. Their love is not blind; it sees who He really is.

4. The hand of fearlessness (viracita abhayam … kara sarōruham)
They ask Him to place His lotus hand on their heads. His touch removes fear.

5. The nectar of His story (tava kathāmṛtam)
His story revives those who are burning in sorrow. This verse is often quoted as the glory of hearing about the Lord.

6. A moment like an age (truṭi yugāyatē)
When they cannot see Him, a moment feels like an age. That is the measure of their love.

7. Love beyond worldly ties
The gōpīs left everything for Him. The tradition reads their love not as worldly romance but as the soul's total turning towards God.

@@ 11.31 | Tvamēva Mama Śaraṇam |
Overall Summary
A song of surrender (śaraṇāgati kīrtan) in Rāgam Simhēndramadhyamam: "You alone are my refuge; I know no one but You."

"I remember no one else, take shelter in no one else, think of no one else, worship no one else. I have no merit, no skill, no compassion, no courtesy. I have no truthfulness, no learning, nothing I have done well, and I have not even served as Your messenger. I have no knowledge, no meditation on You, no good rites, no silence. O Mādhava, Madhusūdana, holder of the earth, refuge of Tāṭaṅki Vēṅkaṭa Dāsa." It ends with verses from the Bhāgavatam (Book 10, chapter 32): as the gōpīs sang and wept, longing to see Krishna, He appeared among them, smiling, in yellow silk, wearing a garland, the one who enchants even Manmatha. Seeing their beloved, they all rose at once, like a body when the life-breath returns. They drank in the sweetness of His face with their eyes and were never satisfied, as saints are never satisfied with His feet.

Key Takeaway
True surrender says: "I have nothing of my own to offer; You alone are my refuge." And the Lord answers such surrender by appearing.
For each of us: we do not need to be good enough before we come to God. Coming as we are is surrender.

Deeper Meaning
1. You alone (tvamēva)
"Ēva" means only. The devotee's refuge is the Lord and nothing else.

2. I have nothing (na hi mē puṇyam …)
Every line lists something the devotee lacks: merit, skill, learning, knowledge, meditation. He comes with empty hands.

3. Empty hands are enough
The tradition teaches that surrender does not need qualifications. The Lord accepts the one who admits he has nothing.

4. Tāṭaṅki Vēṅkaṭa Dāsa
The song names its composer, who calls the Lord his refuge.

5. The gōpīs' cry answered (tāsām āvirabhūt)
After the Gōpikā Gītam, Krishna appeared. The verses show that a true cry is always answered.

6. Like life returning (prāṇam ivāgatam)
When they saw Him, the gōpīs rose as a body rises when life returns. He is their very life.

7. Never satisfied (na atṛpyan)
They drank His beauty with their eyes and never had enough. Love for God only grows.

@@ 11.32 | Tava Caraṇāmbujamēva |
Overall Summary
A song of surrender (śaraṇāgati kīrtan): "Your lotus feet alone are my refuge, flute-holding Gōpāla."

Dark and beautiful, wearing yellow silk, with soft red lips, Gōpāla. Ocean of mercy, cast Your glance on me, You who love those who surrender. I have no spiritual practice; I have come for refuge; protect me, the helpless one, Gōpāla. Mukunda who wanders in Vṛndāvana, protector of those who love You, Gōpāla.

Key Takeaway
The devotee holds on to the Lord's feet alone. Even without any practice, surrender is enough.
For each of us: "I have nothing, but I have come to You" is a prayer anyone can make.

Deeper Meaning
1. Your lotus feet alone (tava caraṇāmbujamēva)
The feet are the place of refuge. "Ēva", only: the devotee holds nothing else.

2. The flute-holder (muraḷīdhara)
The Gōpāla with the flute calls souls home. The devotee answers by taking refuge.

3. A glance of mercy (kalaya kaṭākṣam)
He asks the ocean of mercy for just a glance. One look from Him is enough.

4. Without practice (sādhana rahitam)
He admits he has no spiritual practice. He comes only with surrender.

5. Protect the helpless (pālaya mām dīnam)
He calls himself helpless. The Lord is especially the protector of such people.

6. Protector of those who love Him (prēmika pālaka)
The song ends with the Lord who protects those who love Him. Love is the one thing the devotee brings.

7. Wanderer in Vṛndāvana (bṛndāvana sañcāra)
He walks through Vṛndāvana, near His devotees. The Lord we take refuge in is close at hand.

@@ 11.33 | Aṅganām Aṅganāmantarē |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song of the rāsa dance in Rāgam Śaṅkarābharaṇam, each verse ending "sañjagau vēṇunā dēvakī nandanaḥ" (Dēvakī's son sang on His flute).

Between each gōpī and the next is Mādhava, and between each Mādhava and the next is a gōpī: in the circle made this way, in the middle, Dēvakī's son played His flute. He is like a forest fire that burns the forest of Kaṁsa's line, lovely as swans resting on lotuses among peacocks. Sometimes He is shaken by the sound of the vīṇā, sometimes made to dance by the tinkling of bells, sometimes made to sing in between. He is kissed by the eyes of the lovely Candrāvaḷī, beloved of the cowherds and cowherd girls. Bees drunk on His crown's garland cling to Him; He embraces His frightened beloved. He shines like gold, the master of the assembly, His chest fragrant with the Vaijayantī garland, in the middle of Nanda's Vṛndāvana. He shows His playful eyebrows in the clapping rhythm of the girls, and listens to the gōpīs' songs. He brought the pārijāta tree and planted it in Satyabhāmā's courtyard. On the cool, cool Yamunā bank under the banyan tree, Dēvakī's son sang on His flute.

Key Takeaway
In the rāsa dance, Krishna multiplied Himself so that each gōpī had Him beside her. The Lord gives Himself fully to each soul.
For each of us: in the bhajan circle, each of us can feel that the Lord is beside us, as He was beside each gōpī.

Deeper Meaning
1. A Krishna between every gōpī (aṅganām aṅganām antarē mādhavaḥ)
The first verse paints the rāsa circle: gōpī, Krishna, gōpī, Krishna. He became as many as there were gōpīs.

2. Each soul gets all of Him
Tradition reads this as a teaching: the Lord gives Himself fully to every devotee, not a share.

3. Playing the flute in the middle (madhyagaḥ sañjagau)
He is also in the centre, playing the flute. He is both beside each one and at the heart of all.

4. Fire to Kaṁsa's forest (kaṁsa vaṁśa aṭavī dāvānala)
The playful dancer is also the one who ends evil. Sweetness and strength are one in Him.

5. Music and dance (vīṇā … kiṅkiṇī … gāpitaḥ)
The vīṇā, the bells and the songs make Him dance and sing. The bhajan, too, brings out His joy.

6. By the Yamunā (yāmunīyē taṭē)
The scene ends on the cool bank of the Yamunā under the banyan tree. The flute's sound still echoes there for devotees.

@@ 11.34 | Gōpī Gōpāla Lāla |
Overall Summary
A Hindi song of the rāsa by Sūrdās in Rāgam Pūrvikalyāṇi: "Gōpīs and dear Gōpāla dance in the rāsa circle to 'tā ttā thei tā', in perfect rhythm."

The mṛdaṅga sounds "druma druma", the bells "chana nana", full of colour and beauty; the rhythm goes "dhṛga tā dhṛga". In the middle is the dear one, and the girls are in the middle too, each one shining brightly. Seeing His noble, unseen ways, the eyes praise Him. Śrī Rādhā's face, like the autumn moon, wipes away drops of sweat in joy; the moon of Vraja sways, His crown shining. With "tattata" and sweet notes "sa ri ga ma pa dha ni", the dance goes on. Full of joy, the music rises; Hari is delighted with Rādhā. The gods' chariots fill the sky; Indra and the Sun watch; heavenly women give their hearts. The Yamunā's water is amazed and still; birds and animals shine. Sūrdās says: I offer myself again and again to the joy of Your rāsa.

Key Takeaway
The rāsa is the dance of the soul with God, so beautiful that even nature stops to watch. Sūrdās offers himself to that joy.
For each of us: when we sing with joy in bhajan, we share in the rāsa's delight.

Deeper Meaning
1. The dance circle (rāsa maṇḍala)
The rāsa is the great dance of Krishna with the gōpīs. It is seen as the meeting of the soul with God.

2. Rhythm and music (tāttā thēi … druma druma)
Sūrdās fills the song with drum and dance syllables. We are meant to feel the rhythm.

3. Rādhā at the centre (śrī rādhā mukha śarada canda)
Rādhā's moon-like face shines with joy. She is the heart of the dance.

4. Hari delighted (rījhata hari rādhahī)
Krishna is delighted with Rādhā. God rejoices in the love of His devotee.

5. All nature watches (cakita thakita yamunā nīra)
The Yamunā stops flowing, birds and animals shine. The whole creation joins the joy.

6. Offering myself (bali bali jāya sūradāsa)
Sūrdās offers himself again and again. This is the right response to such beauty.

7. Rādhā and Krishna as one couple (dampati)
Sūrdās calls them the divine couple. Their love is the model of the soul's love for God.

@@ 11.35 | Jhūlata Rādhā |
Overall Summary
A Hindi song by Mīrābāi in Rāgam Darbāri Kānaḍā: "Rādhā swings together with Giridhara (Krishna)."

They have raised a cloud of coloured powder (abīr and gulāl) and filled the syringes with colour. Vṛndāvana and the Yamunā have turned red with the colour, and saffron drips. The best of gods dance to the rhythm; the mṛdaṅga sounds "dhimi dhimi". Mīrā says: my Lord is Giridhara Nāgara, Mōhana with the flute, Giridhara.

Key Takeaway
Rādhā and Krishna swing together in the joy of Hōli colours. Mīrā sees her own Lord in Giridhara.
For each of us: the festival of colours can remind us that God's love colours everything.

Deeper Meaning
1. Swinging together (jhūlata … saṅga)
Rādhā and Krishna swing side by side. The swing shows the gentle rhythm of their love.

2. Colours of Hōli (abīra gulāla … picakārī)
The coloured powders and squirts of Hōli fill the air. Devotion is full of colour and fun.

3. Vṛndāvana turned red (lāla bhayī bṛndāvana)
The whole land and river turn red with colour. Love for God spreads everywhere.

4. The gods dance (nācata … suravara)
Even the gods dance to the drums. Heaven joins Vṛndāvana's joy.

5. Giridhara Nāgara
Mīrā's Lord is always "Giridhara Nāgara", the clever one who held up the hill. Her signature line names Him.

6. Mōhana with the flute (mōhana muraḷī)
He enchants with His flute. Mīrā gave her whole life to that enchanting Lord.

7. Colour on the Yamunā (lāla bhayī … jamunā)
Even the river turns red. When God is present, the whole world is coloured by love.

@@ 11.36 | Kōlāṭṭam |
Overall Summary
A Tamil kōlāṭṭam (stick-dance song) in Rāgam Sindhu Bhairavi: "O heart, seek Nanda's son; trusting, search for His golden feet every day. Sing His praise morning and evening, the one who danced in Vṛndāvana."

He makes even the grass rejoice, the creepers bloom and stones melt in the forest, filling the whole world with coolness through the sweet sound of His little flute. Bow every day to the one with a body the colour of a rain cloud, Mādhava with a peacock feather in His hair, the child of Nanda's Gōkula, known through the Āgamas and Vedas, Acyuta. He is lotus-eyed, pure, the cause of the world, complete, the very form of pure compassion, the Guru, the Lord of the temple of Guruvāyūr (Māruta mandira).

Key Takeaway
Kōlāṭṭam is devotion in dance. As the sticks keep rhythm, the heart is told again and again to seek Krishna.
For each of us: devotion can be joyful and physical: singing, clapping, dancing together.

Deeper Meaning
1. Seek Him, O heart (nāḍiḍuvāy neñjē)
The song speaks to the heart, telling it to seek Krishna every day.

2. The golden feet (ponnaḍi)
His feet are golden and precious. Searching for them is the devotee's daily work.

3. Grass rejoices, stones melt (pullum magizhndiḍum … kallum karaindiḍum)
His flute is so sweet that grass rejoices and stones melt. Even the hardest heart can soften.

4. Cooling the world (jagamellām kuḷirndiḍa)
The flute's sound brings coolness to the whole world, relief from the heat of worry.

5. Pure compassion (sudda kāruṇyamē uruvānavan)
He is compassion in bodily form. To see Him is to see compassion.

6. The Lord of Guruvāyūr (māruta mandira nāyagan)
The song ends with Guruvāyūrappan, the Krishna of Guruvāyūr, also called the Guru.

7. Pure and complete (nirmala … paripūraṇa)
He is spotless and whole, lacking nothing. Our search ends in Him, and the dance ends at His feet.

@@ 11.37 | Kummi |
Overall Summary
A Tamil kummi (clapping-dance song), each line ending "ēlēlō": "He is the child praised by the God of gods; He is Gōpāla who protects the gods."

The gōpīs tell each other: He comes alone and calls us; He draws us into His little games. He winks and calls us near; He waves His hand and makes us restless. His moon-face and gentle smile bewitch; we are afraid to get close to this māyāvi (magician). He braids our hair and ties it up, then looks for something else as if He hadn't noticed. Look at His sly glance; tell everyone it is not easy to resist. If mother-in-law sees this, we'll be in trouble; but my mind only enjoys being with Him. He protects those who love Him, holding their hands; He accepts the service of the world with joy.

Key Takeaway
The kummi plays out the gōpīs' fond, teasing love for Krishna. Underneath the fun is the deepest truth: He protects those who love Him.
For each of us: devotion does not have to be solemn. Delight, laughter and play can also bring us close to God.

Deeper Meaning
1. A clapping dance (kummi)
Kummi is a folk dance where women clap in a circle. Here it becomes a song of love for Krishna.

2. He calls us (azhaikkirāṇḍi)
Krishna comes alone and calls each gōpī. The Lord seeks us before we seek Him.

3. His little games (sinnañjiru līlai)
His small games draw everyone in. His playfulness is part of His grace.

4. The magician (māyan)
They call Him māyāvi, the magician. He enchants, yet He is beyond all enchantment.

5. My mind enjoys only Him (manam … ivaniḍamē rusikkudu)
Whatever the risks, the gōpī's mind tastes joy only with Him. This is single-minded love.

6. He protects those who love Him (anbargaḷaik kai koḍuttuk kāppavan)
The song ends seriously: He holds the hands of His devotees and protects them.

7. Accepting the world's service (visvan paṇi ērpavan)
He gladly accepts the service of the whole world. Every act done for Him becomes worship.

@@ 11.39 | Śiva Śiva |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Śrī Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha in Rāgam Saurāṣṭram: "Śiva, Śiva, Bhava, Bhava, be my refuge; may I always remember You."

He holds the Gaṅgā, wears the moon on His head, brings auspiciousness to the world, and is the home of all worlds. He lives on Kailāsa, brings good, destroyed the three cities and smiles gently. His body is smeared with ash; He is Śambhu, the Supreme Person who rides the bull. He has five faces, wears serpents as ornaments, the Supreme Person in a sage's dress. He delights in the blissful dance, is truth-consciousness-bliss, and removes sorrow. He is the Lord of the nine grammars, Śiva, the God of Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha.

Key Takeaway
Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha, the great singer of Krishna, also sings to Śiva. In this tradition, Hari and Hara are one.
For each of us: "may I always remember You" is a prayer that fits every form of God.

Deeper Meaning
1. Be my refuge (śaraṇaṁ mama bhavatu)
The song asks Śiva to be the devotee's shelter. The same surrender is offered to Him as to Krishna.

2. Always remembering (sadā tava smaraṇam)
The prayer is for constant remembrance. That is the heart of every practice.

3. Gaṅgā and the moon (gaṅgādhara candracūḍa)
He holds the river Gaṅgā in His hair and the crescent moon on His head. Both cool and purify.

4. Ash and serpents (bhasma … phaṇi bhūṣa)
He wears ash and snakes. He is beyond worldly beauty, the ascetic Lord.

5. The blissful dance (ānanda naṭana)
He dances in bliss as Naṭarāja. His dance removes sorrow.

6. Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha's God (nārāyaṇa tīrtha dēva)
The composer calls Śiva his own God. The singer of the Kṛṣṇa Līlā Taraṅgiṇī sees no division between Hari and Hara.

7. Remover of sorrow (vidaḷita khēda)
He breaks sorrow apart. Remembering Śiva brings relief.

@@ 11.40 | Dhārō Jagannātha |
Overall Summary
A song naming the Lord's ten avatāras, each line ending "dhārō harē" (You who took the form, Hari): "Lord of the world, You who took form; Supreme Brahman who took form."

It names the Fish, the Tortoise, the Boar, Narasimha, Vāmana, Paraśurāma, Rāma, Balarāma, the Buddha and Kalki: You of ten forms, so very kind. Then a chant: Rāmabhadra Gōvinda, Rāmacandra Gōvinda, Hari Gōvinda, Hari Gōpāla, Hari Nārāyaṇa who lifts up the world, supreme bliss, slayer of mighty enemies, ocean of mercy, Lord of Lakṣmī; Guru, give me devotion to Your lotus feet; protector of cows, Lord of the gōpīs. A list of names follows, ending in many "Gōvinda"s. Then verses to Hanumān: "I meditate on Āñjanēya, son of the Wind, whose face is red like the pāṭala flower, whose body is lovely like the golden mountain, who lives at the foot of the pārijāta tree. Bow to Māruti, destroyer of demons, who is present wherever Rāma's glory is sung, his hands folded above his head and his eyes full of tears. I bow my head to Rāma's messenger, swift as the mind, fast as the wind, master of the senses, wisest of the wise, son of the Wind, leader of the monkey army." Then the offering verse: "Whatever I do with body, speech, mind, senses, intellect or by nature, I offer it all to Nārāyaṇa." Then the prayer for the world: "May the people be well; may rulers protect the earth justly; may cows and the learned be well always; may all the worlds be happy." It ends: "Remember Jānakī's beloved: victory, victory, Rāma Rāma! Āñjanēya Svāmi kī jay!"

Key Takeaway
The bhajan closes by remembering all of the Lord's forms, bowing to Hanumān, offering every action to Nārāyaṇa and praying for the whole world.
For each of us: we can end every day as the bhajan ends: offering all we did to God and wishing well to all.

Deeper Meaning
1. The ten avatāras (daśavidha rūpa)
The Lord took ten forms to protect dharma. The song names them one by one, as a summary of His care for the world.

2. So very kind (bahu dayāḷu)
All the avatāras show one thing: His great kindness. He comes again and again for us.

3. Guru, give me devotion (guru mē bhaktiṁ tava pādābjē)
The request in the middle of the chant is for devotion to His feet. That is the gift the bhajan asks for.

4. Hanumān where Rāma is sung (yatra yatra raghunātha kīrtanam)
Wherever Rāma's name is sung, Hanumān is there, hands folded and eyes full of tears. The bhajan ends by bowing to him.

5. Offering everything (kāyēna vācā … samarpayāmi)
Every action, word and thought is offered to Nārāyaṇa. Then even daily life becomes worship.

6. Prayer for the world (lōkāḥ samastāḥ sukhinō bhavantu)
The bhajan ends by wishing happiness to all beings. Devotion widens into love for everyone.

@@ 11.41 | Dīpa Samārōpaṇam |
Overall Summary
The song for placing and honouring the lamp (dīpa samārōpaṇam). It begins with verses: "Krishna, Rāma, Mukunda, Mādhava, Vāsudēva, teacher of the world; Fish, Tortoise, Narasimha, Boar, Rāghava, protect me. Treasure of mercy, bowed to by gods, demons, Nārada and great sages; son of Dēvakī, give me devotion to Your feet as an offering. Rāma, Rāma, salutations; victory, Rāmacandra, salutations; Rāmabhadra, Rāghava, God of gods, king of gods, Vāsudēva, Varadarāja, salutations."

Then the third Aṣṭapadi of Śrī Jayadēva's Gīta Gōvindam is sung: in spring, Rādhā, delicate as spring flowers, wanders through the forest searching for Krishna, tormented by longing, and her friend tells her: Hari is playing here in the lovely spring, dancing with the young women, a time hard for those who are separated. The spring breeze from the Malaya hills is soft with clove creepers; bees and cuckoos fill the bowers; the bakula, tamāla, kiṁśuka, kēsara, pāṭala, kētakī, mādhavī and mallikā flowers bloom; the mango tree thrills in the embrace of the creeper; Vṛndāvana is purified by the Yamunā all around. Jayadēva says: this description of the spring forest, full of love, is the essence of remembering Hari's feet.

Key Takeaway
The lamp is honoured with the Lord's names and with Jayadēva's song of spring. Rādhā's longing for Krishna becomes the devotee's own longing.
For each of us: a lamp lit with love is a simple way to bring the Lord's light into our home.

Deeper Meaning
1. Give me devotion (dēhi mē tava pāda bhaktim)
The opening verses ask for one thing: devotion to His feet. That is the prayer behind the lamp.

2. Salutations again and again (namōstutē)
"Salutations to You" is repeated with each name. Bowing is the right mood before the lamp.

3. Rādhā searching (kṛṣṇa anusaraṇām)
In the Aṣṭapadi, Rādhā wanders, searching for Krishna. She stands for every soul that longs for God.

4. The spring forest (sarasa vasantē)
Jayadēva paints spring in full bloom. The beauty of nature is a picture of the joy of the Lord's presence.

5. Longing as devotion (virahi janasya duranta)
Spring is hard for those who are separated from their beloved. Longing for God is itself a form of love.

6. The essence of remembering Him (hari caraṇa smṛti sāram)
Jayadēva says this song is the essence of remembering Hari's feet. Even a love-song leads to Him.

@@ 11.42 | Maṅgaḷam |
Overall Summary
The closing maṅgaḷam: "Grant auspiciousness, O Śrī Raghurāma, grant auspiciousness."

It calls on Sītā's beloved, Rādhā's beloved, Girijā's beloved (Śiva), Vaḷḷī's and Sēnā's beloved (Murugan), Vallabhā's beloved (Gaṇēśa), the one who blessed the king of elephants, Akrūra, Prahlāda and Āñjanēya. Grant auspiciousness, child of the Sun dynasty, kind to the humble, of matchless good character; protector of Gōkula, good-natured in the cowherd's dress, who plays with the gōpīs; Lord who lives on the Vēṅkaṭa hill, lotus-eyed husband of Lakṣmī, who destroys danger and worldly life. Grant auspiciousness; auspiciousness to Vaidēhī (Sītā).

Key Takeaway
The maṅgaḷam asks every form of God, with His consort, to bless us. It ends the bhajan with a shower of auspiciousness.
For each of us: ending with "dēhi maṅgaḷam" sends us home blessed and carrying that blessing to others.

Deeper Meaning
1. Grant auspiciousness (dēhi maṅgaḷam)
The refrain is a simple request: give us all that is good.

2. Every Lord with His beloved (sītā manōhara … girijā manōhara …)
Rāma with Sītā, Krishna with Rādhā, Śiva with Pārvatī, Murugan with Vaḷḷī and Dēvasēnā, Gaṇēśa with Vallabhā. The Divine is honoured as couples, with the Mother.

3. Givers of boons to devotees (karirāja … akrūra … prahlāda … āñjanēya varada)
The Lord blessed the elephant, Akrūra, Prahlāda and Hanumān. Their stories show that He blesses.

4. Kind to the humble (dīna jana anukūla)
He favours those who have nothing. That is why anyone may ask.

5. Remover of danger (saṅkaṭa bhava nāśa)
He ends troubles and the cycle of worldly life. The blessing asked is both for now and for freedom.

6. Ending with Sītā (vaidēhi maṅgaḷam)
The last words bless Sītā. The bhajan closes with the Mother.

7. Lord of Vēṅkaṭa (vēṅkaṭagirivāsa)
The last verse names the Lord of Tirumala. All forms gather to bless at the close.

@@ 11.43 | Ratna Jaḍita |
Overall Summary
A Marathi prayer abhaṅg in Rāgam Kāpi picturing Rāma's court: "On the jewel-studded throne shines the joy of the Raghus."

On His left sits Sītā, the mother of the world, my own mother. Behind is Lakṣmaṇa; in front is Añjanā's son (Hanumān). The two brothers Bharata and Śatrughna wave whisks on both sides. Naḷa, Nīla, Jāmbavān, Sugrīva, Aṅgada and the devotee Vibhīṣaṇa are there. "I have no body-consciousness at all; I am a servant marked at Rāma's feet."

Key Takeaway
The devotee pictures Rāma crowned, with Sītā and all His devotees around Him. Seeing this, he forgets himself and becomes Rāma's servant.
For each of us: picturing the Lord's court, with His family and devotees, reminds us that we belong there too.

Deeper Meaning
1. The jewelled throne (ratna jaḍita siṁhāsana)
Rāma is crowned and shining. This is the Paṭṭābhiṣēkam scene, Rāma's coronation.

2. Sītā, my mother (jagajjananī mājhī āyī)
Sītā is mother of the world and "my own mother". The devotee feels like her child.

3. Hanumān in front (puḍhē añjanī nandana)
Hanumān sits in front, always facing Rāma. He is the devotee closest to the Lord.

4. The brothers serving (bharata śatrughna … cāmara)
Bharata and Śatrughna wave whisks. Even princes serve Rāma humbly.

5. All the devotees (naḷa nīla jāmbavanta …)
All who helped Rāma are there. The Lord's court is full of devotees.

6. A servant marked at His feet (dāsa aṅkita rāmā pāyī)
The devotee forgets his body and becomes Rāma's servant, marked as His own.

7. Forgetting the body (dēha buddhi nēṇō)
Seeing Rāma's court, the devotee forgets himself. That forgetting is joy, because what remains is only Rāma.

@@ 11.44 | Dēvā Hōyīna |
Overall Summary
A Marathi prayer abhaṅg by Sant Tukārām: "O God, I will become a beggar, a vārkarī (pilgrim) of Paṇḍharpur."

"This is my vow and my dharma: Viṭhala's name on my lips. This is my worship: to touch the saints' feet. Tukā says, O God, this is my simple, innocent service."

Key Takeaway
Tukārām's whole practice is simple: walk to Paṇḍharpur, say Viṭhala's name, bow to the saints. That is his worship.
For each of us: our devotion can be just as simple. The Name and respect for good people are enough.

Deeper Meaning
1. A beggar of God (hōyīna bhikārī)
Tukārām wants to be a beggar, with nothing but God. Owning nothing, he is free.

2. A vārkarī (paṇḍharīcā vārakarī)
Vārkarīs walk every year to Paṇḍharpur, singing. Tukārām wants to be one of them.

3. The Name on the lips (mukhī viṭhalācē nāma)
His one rule is to keep Viṭhala's name on his lips. That is his whole religion.

4. The saints' feet (santāñcyā caraṇa)
He worships by touching the feet of saints. Honouring devotees is honouring God.

5. Innocent service (bhōḷī sēvā)
"Bhōḷī" means simple, innocent. He offers no clever methods, only a simple heart.

6. Tukā says (tukā mhaṇē)
Tukārām signs every abhaṅg this way. His honest, plain voice has guided devotees for centuries.

7. Vow and dharma (nēma dharma)
The Name is his vow and his dharma. Tukārām needs no other rule, no other ritual, no other method to reach his Lord.

@@ 11.45 | Hē Dayāḷuvā |
Overall Summary
A Marathi prayer abhaṅg in Rāgam Māṇḍ: "O kind Rāma, O merciful one; O kind Lord Rāghava, the able one."

"First, Rāma, You set me to all kinds of work; then You neglected me, Rāma; is that worthy of You? You know everything inside me; then what can I plead before You, Rāma? Your servant waits for You, Rāma; I cannot speak, Rāma; my throat is choked." It ends: "Viṭhala Viṭhala Rāmā Viṭhala Viṭhala."

Key Takeaway
The devotee is so moved that he cannot speak. Since Rāma already knows everything inside him, words are not needed.
For each of us: when we cannot find words in prayer, it is all right. God knows our heart.

Deeper Meaning
1. Kind and merciful (dayāḷuvā … kṛpāḷuvā)
The song opens by naming Rāma's kindness. The devotee leans on it.

2. A loving complaint (maga upēkṣitā)
He says Rāma first gave him work, then seemed to neglect him. Only a close devotee speaks so frankly.

3. You know my heart (sakala jāṇasī maja antara sthiti)
Rāma knows everything inside him. So there is nothing to explain.

4. Waiting (vāṭa pāhatō)
The servant waits for Rāma, looking down the road. Waiting with love is devotion.

5. My throat is choked (kaṇṭha dāṭatō)
He is too moved to speak. Tears and silence can be the deepest prayer.

6. Rāma and Viṭhala (viṭhala viṭhala rāmā)
The song ends by calling Rāma and Viṭhala together. They are one Lord to the devotee.

7. Able Lord (sakyā svāmi)
