Rāma is "able": He can do everything. The devotee only has to wait for Him, with patience and trust.

@@ 12.1 | Gaurī Kalyāṇam – Rādhā Kalyāṇam |
Overall Summary
The Telugu song that opens the Rādhā Kalyāṇam, in Rāgam Kuriñji: "The splendour of Gaurī's wedding! The splendour of Rādhā's wedding!"

It describes the preparations. In a painted mansion they gathered for the assembly and tied festoons of pearls. They set up countless rows of jewels and built a gateway-tower of five gems. Among shining sapphire pillars they raised lamps of rubies. With great effort they filled the hall completely. Today there is auspiciousness for Śrī Rādhā and Krishna; with love they send for all the relatives.

Key Takeaway
The divine wedding is celebrated like a family wedding. Every detail of the hall is prepared with love.
For each of us: in the Rādhā Kalyāṇam, we are not watching from outside. We are the relatives invited to the wedding.

Deeper Meaning
1. Gaurī and Rādhā (gaurī kalyāṇa … rādhā kalyāṇa)
The song honours both weddings: Śiva and Gaurī, Krishna and Rādhā. The tradition sees one divine love in both.

2. The splendour (vaibhavamē)
"Vaibhavam" is festive glory. The wedding is the greatest celebration of the bhajan.

3. Pearls and jewels (mutyāla tōraṇālu … ratnāla kōṭalu)
The hall is decorated with pearls, rubies and sapphires. The devotees give their best to the Lord's wedding.

4. Lamps of rubies (māṇikya dīpālu)
Lamps are raised among the pillars. Light is the first sign of an auspicious event.

5. Great effort (yatnambu jēsi)
The preparations take great effort. Serving the Lord's celebration is a joy worth working for.

6. Calling the relatives (bandhuvulan piluvanambuduru)
Relatives are invited with love. Every devotee at the bhajan is family at this wedding.

7. Today is auspicious (śōbhanamu nēḍu)
The song ends with "today". The divine wedding happens now, each time it is sung.

@@ 12.2 | Śrī Kṛṣṇa Jaya Jaya |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song for the wedding in Rāgam Kuriñji: "Victory, victory, Śrī Krishna, who steals Rādhā's heart, ocean of compassion, treasure of mercy!"

For Śrī Krishna's wedding, all the married women have brought turmeric on golden plates. O Gōpāla, golden one, father of Kāma, You know all the magic; we bow at Mādhava's feet. These are the feet of Madhusūdana, who came back with the herd of cows as Indra and the gods came to meet Him.

Key Takeaway
The women bring turmeric for the Lord's wedding, as at any wedding. Devotion and family custom meet in the Rādhā Kalyāṇam.
For each of us: ordinary customs, done with love for God, become worship.

Deeper Meaning
1. Rādhā's heart-stealer (rādhā manōhara)
Krishna is the one who has stolen Rādhā's heart. Their love is the centre of the celebration.

2. Ocean of compassion (kāruṇya sāgara)
The bridegroom is also the ocean of mercy. His wedding is a festival of grace.

3. Turmeric on golden plates (baṅgāru taṭṭalō pasupulan)
Turmeric is part of every Telugu and Tamil wedding. The women bring it for Krishna as for their own family.

4. Father of Kāma (kāyajuni kannaṭṭi)
Krishna is called the father of Kāma, the god of love. All beauty and love come from Him.

5. Knower of all magic (māyalella telisērā)
He knows every trick of māyā. Yet He comes to be married simply, with love.

6. Bowing at His feet (mādhava pādamulīrā)
The song keeps returning to His feet. At the wedding too, devotees bow at His feet.

7. Coming home with the cows (gōvula mandala)
He comes home with the cows while the gods come to meet Him. The cowherd is honoured by heaven.

@@ 12.3 | Allō Nērē Ḍillō |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song for the wedding (muthu kuthal, the first pounding of pearls): "Allō nērē ḍillō! Rādhā's wedding is so lovely, so wonderful!"

Come, let us see the best Lord of the gods; come, let us see the one of good qualities. Come, let us see the one with a lovely body; come, let us see the auspicious Narasimha. Come, let us see the one who lives on Garuḍa hill, the one with a majestic form. Come, let us see the one who blessed the elephant, the one who grants every wish. Come, let us see the merciful one with a golden body. He is the one fit for the festival; oh, how enchanting He is! Serve the God of gods, served by Brahmā and the first gods. He shone on the Vēṅkaṭa hill; He came for the praise of Prahlāda. He went to the land of Vidarbha and quickly protected His devotee Rukmiṇī.

Key Takeaway
"Come, let us see Him!" The devotees invite each other to see the bridegroom in all His forms.
For each of us: inviting others to see the Lord, to come to a bhajan or a temple, is itself a service.

Deeper Meaning
1. A folk refrain (allō nērē ḍillō)
The refrain is a joyful folk call, sung while pounding pearls for the wedding rituals.

2. Come, let us see (jūtāmu rārē)
Every line invites: "come, let us see". Devotion is shared and spread.

3. Many forms of the bridegroom (narasimha … garuḍādri vāsa …)
Narasimha, the Lord of Garuḍa hill, the Lord of Vēṅkaṭa: the bridegroom is the same Lord in every temple.

4. Granter of wishes (kāmita phaluḍu)
He gives what is wished for. At His wedding, devotees' wishes are fulfilled.

5. How enchanting (mōhakuḍaina vāḍē)
"Oh, how enchanting He is!" The devotees cannot hide their delight.

6. For Prahlāda's praise (prahlāduni vinutikai)
He came for Prahlāda. The bridegroom is the one who always answers His devotees.

7. Rukmiṇī rescued (vidarbha … rukmiṇini pālincināvu)
He went to Vidarbha to bring Rukmiṇī, who had chosen Him. The wedding songs remember how He comes for those who love Him.

@@ 12.4 | Suvvi Bāla Kṛṣṇa |
Overall Summary
A Telugu "suvvi" song for the wedding (muthu kuthal, the second pounding), opening with the chant "Krishna Krishna … Gōpāla Krishna".

Suvvi, child Krishna; suvvi, Rādhā Krishna; suvvi, Gōpāla. He is Raṅga resting on the pure serpent, the inner meaning of the Vedas and Vēdānta, infinitely holy, Raṅga of all. Our Kastūri Raṅga lives steady in a mansion of rubies and cat's-eye. It is enough to sprinkle on oneself the water from the feet of devotees; why travel to the Gaṅgā? It is enough to recite the Bhāgavatam with one's tongue; why want the gods' nectar? He is Vasudēva's son, who breaks the pride of demon-kings, of wonderful form and fame, worshipped by the world. His feet are served by the bee that drinks from Rukmiṇī's lotus face, and bowed to by gods, kings and sages. When the elephant, caught by the crocodile, cried "save me", our great Raṅga saved it at once. The darling Raṅga who took the milk-pot from the hanging net is Nanda's son, Gōvinda Raṅga. It is enough to feed a hungry guest who comes to the door; why long to perform many sacrifices?

Key Takeaway
In the middle of the wedding fun, the song repeats a deep teaching: simple devotion is enough, more than pilgrimages and great rituals.
For each of us: honouring devotees, reading His story and feeding the hungry are true worship.

Deeper Meaning
1. Suvvi
"Suvvi" songs are sung by women while pounding grain or pearls for a wedding. The rhythm of work becomes the rhythm of praise.

2. The meaning of the Vedas (vēda vēdāntaraṅga)
The child Krishna is the inner meaning of all the scriptures. The wedding songs never forget who He is.

3. Kastūri Raṅga (kastūri raṅga)
Raṅganātha, fragrant with musk, lives steady in His jewelled home. He is the bridegroom too.

4. Devotees' feet are the Gaṅgā (bhāgavatula pāda jalamu)
These lines echo Rāmadās (6.4): the water from devotees' feet is as holy as the Gaṅgā. Honouring devotees is enough.

5. The Bhāgavatam on the tongue (bhāgavatamu jihvanunci)
Reciting His story is sweeter than nectar. Reading the Bhāgavatam is a simple, powerful practice.

6. The elephant saved (kariyu cikki)
When the elephant called, the Lord saved it at once. He hears every true cry.

7. Feeding a guest (atithi vacci)
Feeding a hungry guest is better than many sacrifices. Kindness is worship.

@@ 12.5 | Pāhimām Śrī Kṛṣṇa |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song for the wedding (muthu kuthal, the third pounding) in Rāgam Saurāṣṭram, each line ending "pāhi" (protect).

Protect me, Śrī Krishna, who loves to play with Rādhā. Support of the whole world, steady and gracious. Dear friend of Rādhikā; victory, ocean of mercy. Breaker of the firm bow (as Rāma), delight of the sages' minds. Home of compassion, auspicious dark one. Full of every good quality, coloured like a rain-cloud. Of enchanting form, whose story is auspicious. Gentle one surrounded by forest-dwellers, best of all rulers. Living in devotees' hearts, Lord of Vṛndāvana.

Key Takeaway
Even in the joyful wedding, the devotee asks for protection. The bridegroom is the protector of all.
For each of us: "pāhi mām" can be said in joy as well as in fear. We always need Him.

Deeper Meaning
1. Protect me (pāhi mām)
Every line repeats this request. The wedding song is also a prayer.

2. Rādhā's playmate (rādhā vilōla)
Krishna loves to be with Rādhā. Love between them is the heart of the celebration.

3. Support of the world (sakala jagad ādhāra)
The bridegroom holds up the whole world. His wedding is the world's good fortune.

4. Rāma too (dhanur bhaṅga)
One line praises Rāma, who broke Śiva's bow to win Sītā. The wedding of Rādhā recalls the wedding of Sītā.

5. Auspicious dark one (kalyāṇa śyāma)
His dark colour is auspicious, like a rain cloud that brings life.

6. Living in devotees' hearts (bhakta hṛdaya nivāsa)
He lives in Vṛndāvana and in the hearts of His devotees.

7. A pounding song
Each "pāhi" falls with the rhythm of the pounding. Work and prayer move together.

8. Full of every good quality (sakala sadguṇa pūrṇa)
The bridegroom lacks nothing. Every virtue is complete in Him, which is why the devotees can only ask for His protection.

@@ 12.6 | Suvvi Candraśēkharulaku |
Overall Summary
A long Telugu "suvvi" song for offering the pearls (muthu samarpaṇam) in Rāgam Madhyamāvati. It blesses the gods and describes the bride's preparation.

Suvvi to Candraśēkhara (Śiva), who rides Nandi, holds the Gaṅgā, is with Gaurī, with a dark throat, who lives on Kailāsa. Suvvi to Lakṣmī Narasimha, who rides Garuḍa, supports all, brings good fortune, gives boons and lives in Vaikuṇṭha. With a contented mind, after bowing to Gaṇapati, this great suvvi is composed and sung with love. The young women gather, joking and singing songs, competing in wit. Saying "auspicious, auspicious", the jewel-like women, overflowing with joy, with fine music, come together in their bright saris. For Rādhā, whose voice is sweet as a cuckoo's, they bring the finest gold; they rub campaka-scented oil on her hair, bathe her in warm water, and four women put fragrant paste on her. They put kohl in her eyes, dress her in silk, put flowers in her hair and ward off the evil eye. They adorn her with eight-part diamond tālis, lovely garlands and heavy pearl ornaments, and smile at the charming one. Pearl necklaces fit for Rādhā's body, diamonds, fine cloth and whisks: suvvi. You are the forms of Paṭṭābhirāma, the Supreme Souls, the bright lamps, the first forms who rule us. Whoever sings this suvvi composed with akṣatas (rice of blessing) by Gōpāla will be seen and protected at once, and will be lotus-eyed under Lakṣmī's glance.

Key Takeaway
The women prepare Rādhā for her wedding with love and care. The song turns that preparation into worship and promises blessings to all who sing it.
For each of us: caring for others with love, as the women care for Rādhā, is also service to God.

Deeper Meaning
1. Blessing the gods first (candraśēkharulaku … lakṣmī narasimhuniki)
The song first blesses Śiva with Gaurī and Lakṣmī Narasimha. A wedding begins by honouring the gods.

2. Bowing to Gaṇapati (gaṇapatini … praṇavilli)
Before singing, the composer bows to Gaṇapati. Every good work begins with Him.

3. The joy of the women (anganāmaṇulu uppoṅgucu)
The women sing, joke and laugh. The wedding is full of human warmth.

4. Preparing the bride (rādhā … snānam … kāṭuka …)
Oil, bath, kohl, silk, flowers and ornaments: Rādhā is prepared with love. The soul is made ready to meet the Lord.

5. Warding off the evil eye (dṛṣṭi)
They protect her from the evil eye. Love wants to keep the beloved safe.

6. First forms who rule us (ādi mūrtulu)
Rādhā and Krishna are called the first forms, Paṭṭābhirāma's own forms, who rule over us. The bride and groom are the Divine.

7. A blessing for singers (evaraina pāḍina)
Whoever sings this suvvi is protected at once. The song promises grace to all who join.

@@ 12.8 | Maṅgaḷa Snānam |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song for the bridal bath (maṅgaḷa snānam), with the refrain "śōbhānē śōbhanamē" (may it be auspicious).

Krishna, son of Vasudēva, with His tender body and sweet smile, who brought the ten directions under His control, the one with the moon on His crown, dressed in fine clothes, with a lovely face: Krishna, son of Vasudēva. Listen, He destroyed Pūtanā; O lady, see Śrī Krishna who ended the trouble. Rādhā, daughter of Vṛṣabhānu, longing for Him, lotus-eyed, hearing of Him every day, holding Him in her mind, thinking of nothing but Him: Rādhā, sunk in thought of Him, came to the end through experience, full of bliss. He is the one with great generosity, of matchless mind, whose acts are mercy, the Lord of love, the king of the Sun line, always the refuge of those who come to Him; He who crushed the demons, the purifier of the fallen, Balarāma's brother.

Key Takeaway
Rādhā's whole mind is filled with Krishna. Her longing ends in bliss when she reaches Him.
For each of us: Rādhā shows what it means to think of God all the time: not as a task, but as love.

Deeper Meaning
1. The bridal bath (maṅgaḷa snānam)
Before the wedding, the bride and groom are bathed with auspicious songs. This song accompanies that ritual.

2. Krishna's sweet smile (musi musi naguvula)
He is tender and smiling. The bridegroom's gentleness is praised.

3. Remover of trouble (kali dīrcenu)
He destroyed Pūtanā and ended the trouble. He protects His people from danger.

4. Rādhā's longing (vṛṣabhānu tanayu … kōri)
Rādhā, daughter of Vṛṣabhānu, longs for Krishna. She hears of Him every day and thinks only of Him.

5. Sunk in His thought (rādhā cintanu jēri)
Her mind is completely absorbed. This is the highest kind of meditation.

6. Bliss through experience (anubhavamuna … ānanda)
Her longing ends in experience and bliss. Love for God is fulfilled.

7. Purifier of the fallen (patita kula pāvanuḍē)
The song ends by praising Him as the one who purifies the fallen. The bridegroom is everyone's saviour.

8. A festive refrain (śōbhānē śōbhanamē)
The refrain wishes auspiciousness again and again, filling the ritual with joy.

@@ 12.9 | Māṅgalya Dhāraṇam |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song for tying the sacred thread (māṅgalya dhāraṇam): "Joy, oh joy! Our Krishna has become the bridegroom; our Rādhikā has become the bride; all the fruit of my vows has come true today."

The groom's family has come and brought their gifts; the bride's side went out to meet them, riding on elephants. The married women have all come and done the auspicious bath, and tied the thread with jaggery and cheer. People worshipped Krishna and seated Him with Rādhikā; Krishna tied the jewelled tāli at the beloved's neck.

Key Takeaway
The divine wedding is complete: Krishna ties the tāli on Rādhā. The devotee feels that all her vows have been fulfilled.
For each of us: when the Lord's wedding is celebrated, every devotee shares in the joy, as if it were her own family's.

Deeper Meaning
1. Joy (ānandam ānandamāyanē)
The song opens with pure joy. The long-awaited moment has come.

2. Our Krishna, our Rādhikā (mana kṛṣṇuḍu … mana rādhikā)
They are "our" Krishna and "our" Rādhā. The devotees are family to both.

3. My vows fulfilled (nā nōmu phalamella)
The devotee feels all her vows have borne fruit today. Seeing the divine wedding is the reward of devotion.

4. The families meet (viyyāla vārella vacciri)
The two families come together with gifts. The wedding is pictured with every Telugu custom.

5. The auspicious bath and thread (maṅgaḷa snānambu)
The married women perform the rituals. Ordinary customs become sacred.

6. The jewelled tāli (ratnāla boṭṭu)
Krishna ties the tāli at Rādhā's neck. The soul and God are joined forever.

7. Seated together (rādhikatō kūrcuṇḍa beṭṭiri)
Krishna is worshipped and seated with Rādhā. From now on, the Lord and His beloved are worshipped together.

8. Riding elephants (yēnuga lekki)
The bride's side rides out on elephants to meet the groom. The celebration is royal and grand.

@@ 13.1 | Haṁsayukta |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit Navarātri song in Rāgam Yaman Kalyāṇi to the Divine Mother: "Mother, Mother of the world, who travels in a chariot drawn by swans."

She gives every kind of auspiciousness and brings every aim to success. She loves music, protects the humble, is honoured and worshipped by the gods. She is Śaṅkarī, who tears apart terrible sins, Mother of heroic deeds. She lives on the bright slopes of the seven lovely blue snow mountains, loved by yakṣas, kinnaras, siddhas and cāraṇas, and protects them with her blessings.

Key Takeaway
The Divine Mother rides a swan-chariot and gives every good thing. She loves music and protects the humble.
For each of us: in Navarātri, we turn to God as Mother, with the trust of a child.

Deeper Meaning
1. The swan chariot (haṁsa yukta vimāna)
The swan stands for purity and wisdom. The Mother travels on it, especially as Sarasvatī.

2. Mother of the world (jagadambikē)
She is mother not just of one family but of the whole world.

3. Giver of all auspiciousness (sarva maṅgala dāyikē)
Everything good comes from Her. Her blessing makes life auspicious.

4. Lover of music (gāna lōlē)
She delights in music. The Navarātri bhajan pleases Her.

5. Protector of the humble (dīna pālē)
She cares especially for those who have little. No one is too small for Her.

6. Tearing apart sin (ghōra kaluṣa vidārthi)
She destroys even terrible sins. A mother forgives her child.

7. On the snow mountains (nīla himagiri)
She lives on the bright Himalayan slopes, as Pārvatī, daughter of the mountain.

8. Success in every aim (sarvārtha siddhi)
She brings every good purpose to fulfilment. Work begun with her blessing reaches its goal.

9. Of heroic deeds (vīra caritē)
Her story is full of courage. She fights evil for her children.

@@ 13.2 | Vānōr Vaṇaṅgum |
Overall Summary
A Tamil Navarātri song to Sarasvatī in Rāgam Ānanda Bhairavi: "O Mother, whom the gods bow to, grace me to worship You; out of kindness You gave me speech."

"Grace me, so that my singing is rewarded; come and grant the wishes of this humble one." She is Vāṇī, with fragrant flowers in her braid, whose voice puts the cuckoo to shame, sweet-voiced, holding a parrot in her hand, the queen of the four-faced Brahmā, whose eyes are like the deer's, Kalyāṇī, holding the Vedas as a book, worshipped by Indra and the others.

Key Takeaway
Our power of speech and music is Sarasvatī's gift. We ask her to bless our singing.
For each of us: before speaking, studying or singing, we can thank the Mother of learning for the gift of words.

Deeper Meaning
1. Bowed to by the gods (vānōr vaṇaṅgum)
Even the gods bow to Sarasvatī. Learning and wisdom are honoured in heaven.

2. She gave me speech (vākku tandāḷ)
The singer's ability to speak and sing is her gift, given out of kindness.

3. Rewarding the song (gānam seydu … sammānam)
He asks that his singing be rewarded with her grace. Music offered to her is worship.

4. A voice sweeter than the cuckoo (kuyil pazhikkum)
Her voice puts the cuckoo to shame. She is the source of all sweetness in sound.

5. The parrot in her hand (suka pāṇi)
She holds a parrot, a symbol of speech and learning.

6. The book of the Vedas (vēda pustaka pāṇi)
She holds the Vedas. All true knowledge is in her hands.

7. A humble wish (dīnan enadabhīṣṭa)
The singer calls himself humble and asks her to grant his wish. The Mother answers a child's simple request.

@@ 13.3 | Śāradē Karuṇānidhē |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit Navarātri song to Śāradā (Sarasvatī) in Rāgam Hamīr Kalyāṇi: "O Śāradā, treasure of compassion, protect all people always, Mother."

Your glory, sung by the cāraṇas and others, fills every direction. You are adorned with golden ornaments, Your crown shines with fine jewels; You give happiness and free us from karma; make my mind pure. In Your hands You hold a book, a rosary, a pot of nectar and the sign of teaching. Who can describe You, whether clever man or heavenly being?

Key Takeaway
Śāradā is the Mother of wisdom who frees us from karma and purifies the mind. Her greatness is beyond all words.
For each of us: we can ask the Mother for a pure mind, the best gift for learning and for life.

Deeper Meaning
1. Treasure of compassion (karuṇānidhē)
The Mother of learning is first of all compassionate. Wisdom and kindness go together.

2. Protect all people (sakalān ava)
She protects everyone, not only scholars. Her care is for all.

3. Freeing from karma (karma mōcini)
She releases us from the bonds of our actions. True knowledge sets us free.

4. Make my mind pure (nirmalaṁ kuru mānasam)
This is the heart of the prayer. A pure mind is where wisdom can grow.

5. Book, rosary, nectar, teaching (pustaka akṣa sudhā ghaṭa mudrikē)
The four things in her hands: learning, prayer, immortality and teaching. She gives all four.

6. Beyond description (kastavāsti hi varṇanē)
No one, human or divine, can fully describe her. We bow in wonder.

7. Śāradā of Sringeri
Śāradā is especially worshipped at Sringeri, the seat of the Śaṅkarācāryas. The Navarātri songs honour her there.

8. Glory in every direction (pūrita akhila dik)
Her fame fills all the directions. Wherever there is learning, there is her glory.

@@ 13.4 | Vīṇā Gāna Priyē |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit Navarātri song to Sarasvatī in Rāgam Darbāri Kānaḍā: "O Śāradā, who loves the music of the vīṇā; Sarasvatī, home of the Vedas and the arts."

You are with learning, spotless and pure; You delight in music full of feeling. You give liberation, O Mother of the world; You live in Sringeri, O Sarasvatī.

Key Takeaway
Sarasvatī loves music and gives both learning and liberation. She lives in Sringeri.
For each of us: music and learning, offered to the Mother, can become paths to freedom.

Deeper Meaning
1. Lover of the vīṇā's music (vīṇā gāna priyē)
Sarasvatī holds the vīṇā and loves its music. Music is dear to her.

2. Home of the Vedas and arts (vēda kalā nilayē)
She is the home of all knowledge and all arts. Every true skill comes from her.

3. Spotless and pure (vimalē amalē)
She is twice called pure. Learning in her presence is clean of pride.

4. Music full of feeling (bhāvālaṅkṛta gāna)
She delights in music full of bhāva, feeling. Devotion is what makes music beautiful to her.

5. Giver of liberation (mukti pradāyini)
She gives not only learning but liberation. Knowledge leads to freedom.

6. Mother of the world (jagat janani)
She is everyone's mother. We come to her as children.

7. Living in Sringeri (śṛṅgēri vāsini)
She is the Śāradā of Sringeri, worshipped by the Śaṅkarācāryas in the Guru line of Chapter 2.

8. The vīṇā in her hands
Her vīṇā is a picture of a well-tuned life: when the strings are set right, music flows.

@@ 13.5 | Nānoru Viḷaiyāṭṭu Bommaiyā |
Overall Summary
A Tamil Navarātri song to Dēvi in Rāgam Navarasa Kānaḍā: "Am I a toy for You to play with, O Lady of the world, Umā?"

"Isn't it enough that I have taken many births on this earth and suffered? Is it a joy for You to hear me crying 'Mother, Mother!' to drink the nectar of Your grace? I have come to Your holy feet with no other refuge; will Your heart not melt, Dēvi?"

Key Takeaway
The devotee speaks to the Divine Mother with a child's honest pain: "Am I just Your toy?" Yet he has come to her feet alone.
For each of us: we can be honest with God about our suffering. The Mother listens to her crying child.

Deeper Meaning
1. A toy? (viḷaiyāṭṭu bommaiyā)
The question is bold and painful. The devotee feels tossed about by life like a doll.

2. Many births (pala piravi)
He has suffered through many births. He asks: hasn't it been enough?

3. Crying "Mother" (ammā ammā)
He calls "Mother, Mother", like a child. The cry itself is devotion.

4. The nectar of grace (aruḷamudu)
He longs to drink the nectar of her grace. Only that will satisfy him.

5. No other refuge (oru pugalinri)
He has come to her feet with no other shelter. This is complete surrender.

6. Will You not melt? (tiruvuḷḷam iraṅgādā)
He asks if her heart will not melt. A mother's heart always melts for her child.

7. Love behind the complaint
The question "am I a toy?" is spoken in love, not anger. Only one who trusts the Mother speaks so freely.

8. Umā, Lady of the world (jagannāyakiyē umaiyē)
He calls her the ruler of the world. The one who rules all is also his own mother.

@@ 13.6 | Koḍu Bēga |
Overall Summary
A Kannada Navarātri song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Vasantā: "Give me quickly a divine mind, O Sarasvatī."

"Śiva, Viṣṇu and Indra are Your lords; I bow at Your feet, Mother, queen of Brahmā. You preside over all learning, the chief queen of Brahmā, who gives happiness and protects, the crown-jewel of good people. Purifier of the fallen, I have trusted You as my refuge; always show me Purandara Viṭhala."

Key Takeaway
Purandaradāsa asks Sarasvatī for a divine mind, so that he can always see Viṭhala. Learning is for reaching God.
For each of us: we can ask for wisdom, not for its own sake, but to see the Lord more clearly.

Deeper Meaning
1. Give quickly (koḍu bēga)
The devotee is eager. He wants the gift now.

2. A divine mind (divya mati)
He asks for a mind turned towards God. That is the best kind of intelligence.

3. Queen of Brahmā (brahmana rāṇi)
Sarasvatī is Brahmā's consort, the power of creative wisdom.

4. Presiding over all learning (akhila vidyābhimāni)
All forms of knowledge are under her care.

5. Purifier of the fallen (patita pāvane)
She purifies those who have fallen. The Mother is kind to all.

6. Show me Viṭhala (purandara viṭhalana tōrē)
The real purpose of the prayer is to see Viṭhala always. Wisdom leads to God.

7. Trust as refuge (gatiyendu nambidē)
He trusts Sarasvatī as his refuge. Devotion to the Mother and to Viṭhala are one path.

8. Lords of the gods bow to her (mṛḍa harihaya)
Śiva and Indra honour her. Wisdom is respected by all the gods.

9. Giver of happiness (sukhavittu)
She gives happiness and protects. Learning blessed by her brings joy, not pride.

@@ 13.7 | Śrī Lalitē |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit Navarātri song to Lalitā in Rāgam Señjuruṭṭi: "Śrī Lalitā, Mother Śrī Lalitā, together with Śiva, Mother full of happiness."

She destroyed Mahiṣāsura and is the wife of Mahādēva; she wears the king of serpents as an ornament and lifts up the whole world. Her form is pure consciousness; she lives in the Śrīcakra; her nature is peace; she protects those who come to her.

Key Takeaway
Lalitā is the Divine Mother who is both fierce against evil and peaceful in nature. She protects all who come to her.
For each of us: the Mother's strength protects us; her peace calms us.

Deeper Meaning
1. Lalitā (śrī lalitē)
"Lalitā" means the graceful, playful one. She is the Divine Mother in her most beautiful form.

2. With Śiva (śiva sahitē)
She is always with Śiva. Śiva and Śakti are inseparable.

3. Full of happiness (sukha bharitē)
She overflows with joy. Being near her brings happiness.

4. Slayer of Mahiṣāsura (mahiṣāsura mardini)
She destroyed the buffalo-demon. Navarātri celebrates this victory of good over evil.

5. Uplifter of the world (akhila uddhāriṇi)
She raises up the whole world. Her power is for our good.

6. Living in the Śrīcakra (śrīcakra vāsini)
The Śrīcakra is her sacred diagram. She lives at its centre, as in the heart.

7. Peace itself (śānta svarūpiṇi)
Her very nature is peace. After the battle with evil, she is calm.

8. Mother of all (ambā)
She is called Ambā, Mother, again and again. Every title of hers rests on this one.

9. Protector of those who come (śrita jana pālini)
She protects all who come to her. That is a mother's first duty.

10. Wearing serpents (ahivara bhūṣaṇi)
Like Śiva, she wears serpents as ornaments. She is beyond fear.

@@ 13.8 | Dē Maja Divyamati |
Overall Summary
A Marathi Navarātri song by Samarth Rāmdās in Rāgam Behāg: "Give me a divine mind, O Sarasvatī."

"Give me quick understanding, to explain the sweet story of Rāma well. Brahmā and the gods worship You; they too pray to You, Sarasvatī. Rāmdās says: what do I lack, when You are in the world, Sarasvatī?"

Key Takeaway
Rāmdās asks Sarasvatī for a sharp mind, so that he can tell Rāma's story well. Learning is for spreading the Lord's glory.
For each of us: whatever skill we have, we can use it to share what is good and true.

Deeper Meaning
1. Give me a divine mind (dē maja divyamati)
The same request as 13.6, in Marathi. Saints of every language ask the Mother for a pure, bright mind.

2. To tell Rāma's story (rāma kathā … nirūpaṇa)
Rāmdās wants skill for one purpose: to explain the sweet story of Rāma.

3. Quick understanding (śīghra gati)
He asks for a quick mind, so that he can teach well.

4. Worshipped by Brahmā (brahmādika dēva pūjitī)
Even the gods pray to her. Wisdom is honoured everywhere.

5. What do I lack? (kāya malā uṇē)
With Sarasvatī in the world, he lacks nothing. Her grace is enough.

6. Rāmdās's signature (rāmadāsa mhaṇē)
The song ends with his name. The servant of Rāma serves the Mother too.

7. Learning for devotion
All the Navarātri songs to Sarasvatī ask for the same thing: knowledge that leads to God.

8. Prayed to by the gods (prārthanā hi karitī)
Even the gods pray to Sarasvatī. If they need her, how much more do we.

@@ 14.1 | Kuzhandaiyāga Mīṇḍum |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song: "Won't Kaṇṇan be born again as a child? Won't He play His flute and enchant us?"

Won't He drive the cows and calves to graze? Won't He dance and sing and gather us in? Won't He open His tiny mouth a little, and show the whole world after eating mud? Won't He tell the Bhagavad Gītā again? And hearing it, won't we change and become good?

Key Takeaway
The devotee longs for Krishna to come again as a child. Most of all, he wants to hear the Gītā once more and become good.
For each of us: we don't need to wait for Krishna to be born again. His Gītā is here for us, and we can change now.

Deeper Meaning
1. Born again as a child (kuzhandaiyāga mīṇḍum)
The devotee wishes he could see the child Krishna as the people of Gōkula did.

2. The flute (pullāṅkuzhal)
He longs to hear the flute that enchanted everyone.

3. Grazing the cows (māḍu kanṛu ōṭṭi)
He pictures Krishna with the cows and calves. The simple life of Vṛndāvana is full of God.

4. The world in His mouth (maṇṇai uṇḍu ulagattaiyē)
When baby Krishna ate mud, Yaśōdā looked in His mouth and saw the universe. The devotee wants to see that wonder too.

5. The Gītā again (gītaiyai tān tirumbavumē)
The deepest wish is to hear the Gītā again. The child of Gōkula is also the teacher of Kurukṣētra.

6. Won't we become good? (nāmum nallavarāy māra māṭṭōmā)
The song ends with a hope for ourselves. Hearing Him, we would change.

7. A gentle challenge
The question turns back on us: the Gītā is already here. Will we listen and change?

@@ 14.2 | Garuḍagamana Tava |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song by Śrī Bhāratī Tīrtha Svāmi (as the book notes) in Rāgam Yaman Kalyāṇi: "O rider of Garuḍa, may Your lotus feet always shine here in my mind." Each verse ends: "Take away my suffering, Lord; take away my sin, Lord."

Lotus-eyed one, whose lotus feet are praised by Brahmā, Indra (slayer of Namuci) and the wise. Resting on the serpent, father of Manmatha, You remove my fear of birth and death. Holder of the conch and discus, destroyer of wicked demons, refuge of all worlds. Of countless qualities, refuge of the refugeless, who scattered the hosts of demons. Out of great mercy, protect Bhāratī Tīrtha, the best of devotees here.

Key Takeaway
The prayer is simple: may Your feet shine in my mind; take away my suffering and my sin. A great Ācārya prays like a simple devotee.
For each of us: "take away my suffering, take away my sin" is a prayer we can make every day.

Deeper Meaning
1. Feet shining in the mind (caraṇa kamalam iha manasi lasatu)
The first wish is that His feet shine in the mind always. Then everything else follows.

2. Take away my suffering (mama tāpam apākuru)
"Tāpa" is burning pain. He asks the Lord to remove it.

3. Take away my sin (mama pāpam apākuru)
Pain and sin are linked. He asks the Lord to remove both, root and fruit.

4. Fear of birth and death (janana maraṇa bhaya)
The Lord removes the deepest fear. The Ācārya asks for freedom from the cycle.

5. Refuge of the refugeless (aśaraṇa śaraṇada)
He shelters those who have no one. This gives hope to all.

6. A great Guru's humility (pāhi bhāratī tīrtham)
Śrī Bhāratī Tīrtha of Sringeri names himself and asks for protection. The greatest teachers pray humbly.

7. A refrain for the heart
The refrain is repeated after every verse. Repetition lets the prayer settle in the heart.

@@ 14.3 | Gaṅgādhīśvaram |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song to Śiva by Śrī Guru Sūrajānanda (as the book notes) in Rāgam Sindhu Bhairavi: "Śaṅkara, the Lord who holds the Gaṅgā, the Lord of the confluence."

He is half his body Ardhanārīśvara (half woman), with red matted hair, Śiva. His form is auspicious; He is Mahādēva, Mahēśvara, the auspicious one. He arose as the infinite Liṅga, full of glory; He is the sound of Om, Sadāśiva.

Key Takeaway
Śiva holds the Gaṅgā and is half Śakti. He is the auspicious Lord whose very sound is Om.
For each of us: Śiva's Ardhanārīśvara form reminds us that strength and gentleness belong together.

Deeper Meaning
1. Holder of the Gaṅgā (gaṅgādhīśvaram)
Śiva caught the Gaṅgā in His hair to protect the world from her force. He takes the shock so we may be blessed.

2. Lord of the confluence (saṅgamēśvaram)
Where rivers meet, Śiva is worshipped as Saṅgamēśvara. He is where all paths come together.

3. Half woman (ardhanārīśvaram)
Śiva and Pārvatī share one body. God is both father and mother.

4. Auspicious form (maṅgaḷākāram)
His form is auspicious. Though He wears ash and serpents, He brings only good.

5. The infinite Liṅga (liṅgōdbhavam)
He appeared as a column of light without beginning or end. Brahmā and Viṣṇu could not find its limits.

6. The sound of Om (ōṁkāra nādam)
He is the primal sound. Chanting Om is calling Him.

7. Sadāśiva
He is "always auspicious". His goodness never changes.

8. Mahēśvara, the great Lord (mahēśvaram)
He is the great Lord, above all. Yet His name is easy to say and His grace easy to receive.

9. A gentle chant
The song is short and flowing, made for singing together. Each name of Śiva is a step into peace.

@@ 14.4 | Kausalyāsuta |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song to Rāma by Śrī Bhāratī Tīrtha Svāmi (as the book notes) in Rāgam Nādanāmakriyā: "Rāma, son of Kausalyā, pledged to guard Viśvāmitra's sacrifice; lift me up, protector of those who surrender, lamp of the Sun dynasty."

He is Daśaratha's son, slayer of the demons, protector of the humble; Rāma who broke Śiva's bow, wise, best of persons. He is a forest fire to Khara, Dūṣaṇa and the other demons; His lotus feet are worshipped by Śabarī, Guha and the best devotees. He killed Vāli; He is served by Hanumān and the best of the monkeys; He is praised by Indra, Brahmā and the gods, lotus-eyed. He crushed the ten-headed Rāvaṇa and other demons, protecting the world; Sītā's Lord, quick to give boons, praised by the whole world. His body is adorned with golden ornaments, His face like the moon; Rāma, Lord of Bhadragiri, well served by His devotee Bhāratī Tīrtha.

Key Takeaway
The song tells Rāma's whole story in six verses, as reasons to ask Him: "lift me up". Śabarī and Guha are named among His dearest devotees.
For each of us: Rāma accepted the simple love of Śabarī and Guha. He accepts ours too.

Deeper Meaning
1. Lift me up (mām uddhara)
The request is the same as Vijaya Gōpāla's (5.2): lift me out. Only His grace can do it.

2. Guarding the sacrifice (makha rakṣaṇa dīkṣita)
As a boy, Rāma protected Viśvāmitra's sacrifice. He guards everything good.

3. Lamp of the Sun dynasty (ravikula dīpaka)
Rāma is the light of His family line. He lights up the path of dharma.

4. Śabarī and Guha (śabarī guha)
Śabarī, an old forest woman, and Guha, a boatman, were among Rāma's dearest devotees. He honours love, not status.

5. Served by Hanumān (vātātmaja … sēvita)
Hanumān and the monkeys served Him. Devotion makes even animals great.

6. Quick to give boons (śīghra varaprada)
He gives blessings quickly. He does not keep devotees waiting long.

7. Lord of Bhadragiri (bhadra girīśvara)
The last verse names Rāma of Bhadrācala and the Ācārya as His devotee. The Guru line and the Name tradition meet.

@@ 15.1 | Nārāyaṇa Tē Namō |
Overall Summary
A song by Śrī Annamācārya in Rāgam Behāg: "Salutations, salutations to You, Nārāyaṇa; salutations to the one praised by Nārada."

Slayer of Mura, remover of worldly life, Mukunda, Mādhava, rider of Garuḍa, lotus-naveled one. Supreme Person, freer from the bonds of worldly life, in the form of man and lion: salutations. Resting on the ocean, with the sun and moon as eyes, whose feet are praised by the lotus-born Brahmā; who bound Bali, beloved who lifted Gōvardhana, with a lotus in His navel: salutations. First God, worshipped by all the scriptures, of enchanting form in the Yadu clan, who rescued the Vedas, Śrī Vēṅkaṭa Nāyaka, who loves music: salutations.

Key Takeaway
Annamācārya bows again and again to Nārāyaṇa, the Lord of Tirumala. Each name is a reason to bow.
For each of us: "namō namō" (salutations, salutations) is a simple way to show love and respect to God.

Deeper Meaning
1. Salutations (namō namō)
Every line ends with a double bow. Bowing is the heart of worship.

2. Praised by Nārada (nārada sannuta)
Nārada, the singing sage, praises Him. The best singers sing to Nārāyaṇa.

3. Freer from bondage (bhava bandha vimōcana)
He frees us from the chains of birth and death.

4. Man-lion (naramṛga śarīra)
He took the form of Narasimha to protect Prahlāda. No form is too strange for Him to save a devotee.

5. Binder of Bali (bali bandhana)
As Vāmana He bound Bali, then made him a king in the underworld. His "punishment" is also grace.

6. Lover of music (nāda priya)
Vēṅkaṭēśa loves music. Annamācārya wrote thousands of songs for Him.

7. Lord of Vēṅkaṭa (śrī vēṅkaṭa nāyaka)
The song ends at Tirumala. The Lord of all is the Lord of the seven hills.

8. Resting on the ocean (jaladhi śayana)
As Viṣṇu, He rests on the cosmic ocean. Even at rest, He holds the world.

9. Rescuer of the Vedas (vēdōddhara)
He brought back the Vedas when they were lost. He protects the knowledge that guides us.

@@ 15.2 | Indariki Abhayambu |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Śrī Annamācārya in Rāgam Harikāmbhōji, about the Lord's hand: "The hand that gives fearlessness to everyone; the precious, fine golden hand."

It is the hand that searched out and brought back the priceless Vedas (as the Fish); the hand that holds up the mountain from below (as the Tortoise); the hand that embraced the Earth-goddess (as the Boar); the hand of the clever cowherd. It is the hand that broke the pride of the city women (in the Buddha form); the great hand that drives the horse (as Kalki). As the Lord of Tiru Vēṅkaṭācala, it is the hand that shows all beings the way to liberation.

Key Takeaway
The Lord's hand gives fearlessness to all. Every avatāra shows what that hand has done for the world.
For each of us: we can picture the Lord's raised hand, saying "do not fear", whenever we are afraid.

Deeper Meaning
1. The hand of fearlessness (abhayambu liccu cēyi)
The raised palm in images of God means "do not fear". Annamācārya sings of that hand.

2. For everyone (indariki)
It gives fearlessness to all, not only to a few. Everyone may come.

3. Bringing back the Vedas
As the Fish, His hand rescued the Vedas. He saves knowledge for the world.

4. Holding up the mountain
As the Tortoise, He supported the Mandara mountain. His hand bears the weight of the world.

5. The cowherd's hand (konagōḷḷa vāḍi)
As Krishna, it is the cowherd's hand. The same hand that held the stick for the cows holds up all.

6. Showing the way to liberation (mōkṣambu teruvu)
As Vēṅkaṭēśvara, His hand points to His feet. Tirumala's image shows the right hand pointing down: surrender at My feet.

7. A precious golden hand (manci baṅgāru cēyi)
His hand is precious like fine gold. It is the most valuable thing we could hold.

@@ 15.3 | Muddugārē Yaśōdā |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Śrī Annamācārya in Rāgam Kuriñji, comparing Krishna to the nine gems: "He is the darling pearl in Yaśōdā's courtyard, Dēvakī's son of boundless greatness."

He is the ruby in the palm of the cowherd women everywhere; the diamond to Kaṁsa who challenged Him; the green emerald that shines through the three worlds; little Krishna, who is right here with us. He is the coral of Rukmiṇī's lips in love's play; the gōmēdhika (hessonite) of Gōvardhana hill; the vaiḍūrya (cat's-eye) in the joints of the conch and discus; the lotus-eyed one who protects us as our refuge. He is the yellow sapphire covering Kāḷiṅga's heads; the blue sapphire of the Vēṅkaṭa hill He rules; the divine gem never leaving the milk-ocean; Padmanābha, who wanders about like a child.

Key Takeaway
Krishna is every precious gem in one: pearl, ruby, diamond, emerald and more. Each gem shows a different side of Him.
For each of us: the Lord is the most precious treasure we can have. Nothing in the world compares.

Deeper Meaning
1. Yaśōdā's pearl (muṅgiṭa mutyamu)
He is the pearl playing in Yaśōdā's courtyard: small, bright and precious.

2. The nine gems (navaratna)
Annamācārya compares Krishna to the nine precious gems. Each gem matches a deed or a quality.

3. A diamond to Kaṁsa (kamsuni pāli vajramu)
To His enemy He is hard as diamond. To devotees He is soft as a pearl.

4. Rukmiṇī's coral (raṅgumōvi pagaḍamu)
To Rukmiṇī He is the coral of her lips. He gives each devotee what they love.

5. Protector of us (mammu gācē kamalākṣuḍu)
Among all the gems, the lotus-eyed one protects us. That is His greatest value.

6. The blue sapphire of Vēṅkaṭa (indra nīlamu)
As Vēṅkaṭēśa, He is the blue sapphire of the hill. Annamācārya's own Lord is the jewel of jewels.

7. Wandering like a child (bāluni vale tirigē)
Padmanābha, the cosmic Lord, wanders like a little child. The greatest treasure is also the sweetest.

@@ 15.4 | Brahmamokaṭē |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Śrī Annamācārya in Rāgam Bauḷi, with a folk refrain "tandanānā": "Brahman is one; the Supreme Brahman is one."

There is no high or low here; Śrī Hari is the inner Self of all. All the kinds of creatures in this world are one; Śrī Hari is the inner Self of all. The sleep of a king on a full bed is the same as the sleep of his servant nearby. The ground a great brahmin walks on is the same as the ground an outcaste lives on. The sunshine on an elephant is the same as the sunshine on a dog on the earth. For the very holy and for sinners alike, the name of Śrī Vēṅkaṭēśvara is the same one.

Key Takeaway
There is one God in all beings. Before Him, king and servant, brahmin and outcaste, elephant and dog are equal.
For each of us: if Hari lives in everyone, we must treat everyone with respect.

Deeper Meaning
1. Brahman is one (brahmamokaṭē)
There is one Reality behind all things. Annamācārya puts the highest teaching in a folk song.

2. No high or low (hīnādhikamulu lēvu)
In God's eyes, there is no superior or inferior. Divisions are human, not divine.

3. Hari in all (andariki śrī harē antarātmā)
Śrī Hari is the inner Self of everyone. To respect people is to respect Him.

4. King and servant sleep alike (rājū … baṇṭu)
In sleep, the king and his servant are the same. Our deepest nature is equal.

5. Brahmin and outcaste share the earth (brāhmaṇuḍu … caṇḍāluḍu)
The same earth holds them both. Caste does not change the ground, or God.

6. Sun on elephant and dog (ēnugu … śunakamu)
The sun shines equally on all. God's grace is like sunlight.

7. One Name for all (vēṅkaṭēśvaru nāmamokaṭē)
The Name of Vēṅkaṭēśvara is the same for the holy and for sinners. The Name is open to everyone.

@@ 15.5 | Jō Acyutānanda |
Overall Summary
A Telugu lullaby by Śrī Annamācārya: "Sleep, sleep, Acyuta, bliss; sleep, sleep, Mukunda. Come, supreme bliss, Rāma, Gōvinda."

You went to Nanda's house, and the moon-faced women served You lovingly. You played charmingly in their houses, the thief of the herds' milk, our darling Raṅga. O my dear, who fathered Manmatha, come here; they have poured milk into a golden bowl. The women are fuming, calling You a thief; play in the front yard, O one of enchanting form. As Annamayya of Tāḷḷapāka sings Your lullaby, full of charm, may every wealth be Yours now; auspiciousness, Madana Gōpāla of Tirupati.

Key Takeaway
Annamācārya sings Krishna to sleep, smiling at His mischief. Loving God as a child brings sweetness to devotion.
For each of us: at the end of the day, a lullaby to the Lord can calm our own hearts too.

Deeper Meaning
1. A lullaby (jō jō)
"Jō jō" is what mothers sing to babies. Annamācārya sings it to the Lord.

2. Acyuta, never falling (acyuta)
"Acyuta" means one who never falls. The baby being rocked is the unchanging Lord.

3. Supreme bliss (paramānanda)
The child is bliss itself. Caring for Him fills the singer with joy.

4. The milk-thief (mandalaku doṅga)
He steals milk from the herds. His mischief is remembered with a smile.

5. The women fuming (satulu poṅgucunnārā)
The neighbours complain about the little thief. Even their complaints are full of love.

6. A golden bowl of milk (baṅgāru ginnelō pālu)
The devotee offers milk in a golden bowl. We give our best to the Lord.

7. Madana Gōpāla of Tirupati (tirupaṭla madana gōpāla)
The lullaby ends with auspiciousness to the Gōpāla of Tirupati. Annamācārya's Krishna and Vēṅkaṭēśa are one.

8. Annamayya of Tāḷḷapāka (tāḷḷapāka annayya)
The composer signs his name. Annamācārya of the Tāḷḷapāka family wrote thousands of songs for Vēṅkaṭēśa.

9. Every wealth be Yours (sakala sampadala)
The lullaby wishes the baby all wealth. A mother's blessing is offered to the Lord.

@@ 16.1 | Nāmadēva Kīrtana |
Overall Summary
A Marathi abhaṅg by Sant Janābāī, with the refrain: "When Nāmadēva, devotee Nāmadēva, Saint Nāmadēva, sings kīrtan full of love, Pāṇḍuraṅga dances."

Janābāī says: God, let me speak abhaṅgs. As abhaṅgs were sung, Raṅga (Viṭhala) was drawn into the kīrtan; in love's rhythm, God began to dance. As God danced and swayed, His yellow silk slipped; Kabīr said, "Careful, God!" Everyone became one on the sand of the Candrabhāgā river: my Nāmadēva, my Jñānadēva, Gōpāla, playing on the sand. Rolling at the saints' feet in Nāmadēva's company, Viṭhōbā frees us from the trap of birth and death.

Key Takeaway
When a true devotee sings with love, God Himself comes and dances. Nāmadēva's kīrtan made Viṭhala dance.
For each of us: singing with love is powerful. In our bhajan, the Lord comes and joins in.

Deeper Meaning
1. God dances (nācē pāṇḍuraṅgā)
When Nāmadēva sang, Pāṇḍuraṅga danced. The Lord is moved by love.

2. Janābāī's prayer (janābāyī mhaṇē)
Janābāī was a maid in Nāmadēva's house and a great saint. She asks to sing abhaṅgs too.

3. Drawn into the kīrtan (kīrtanī raṅga ōḍhavaḷā)
Viṭhala is pulled into the kīrtan by love's rhythm. Bhajan draws Him.

4. "Careful, God!" (sāvadha hōī dēvā)
The Lord danced so much His silk slipped, and Kabīr teased Him. The saints were close and playful with God.

5. All saints together (nāmadēva … jñānadēva)
Nāmadēva, Jñānadēva and Gōpāla play together on the river sand. The saints are one family.

6. Rolling at the saints' feet (santāñcē pāyīṁ)
Even Viṭhōbā rolls at the saints' feet. God honours His devotees.

7. Freed from birth and death (janma maraṇa pāsūni mukta)
In the saints' company, the Lord frees us. Good company is the doorway to liberation.

@@ 16.2 | Hari Mukhē Mhaṇā |
Overall Summary
A Marathi abhaṅg by Sant Jñānadēva: "Say 'Hari' with your mouth, say 'Hari' with your mouth; who can count the merit?"

The chant goes "jay jay Rām … Krishna Hari". One who stands for even a moment at God's door has achieved the four kinds of liberation. Viṭhala, Viṭhala, Pāṇḍuraṅga. Living in worldly life, keep your tongue busy; the Vedas and scriptures raise their arms to say so. Jñānadēva says: this is Vyāsa's sign: the king of Dvārakā (lives) in the Pāṇḍavas' house.

Key Takeaway
Saying "Hari" brings countless merit. Even a moment at God's door is enough.
For each of us: we can stay in the world and still keep the Name on our tongue. That is what the scriptures ask.

Deeper Meaning
1. Say Hari (hari mukhē mhaṇā)
The simple instruction: say "Hari". No special method is needed.

2. Countless merit (puṇyācā gaṇanā)
The merit cannot be counted. One Name holds more than we can measure.

3. A moment at His door (kṣaṇabharī)
Standing at God's door for a moment is enough for liberation. His grace is generous.

4. In worldly life (asōni saṁsārī)
We need not leave the world. Live in it, and keep the tongue busy with the Name.

5. The Vedas raise their arms (vēda śāstra ubhārī bāhya)
The scriptures raise their arms, as if to shout: say the Name!

6. Krishna in the Pāṇḍavas' house (dvārakēcā rāṇā pāṇḍavā gharī)
The king of Dvārakā lived in the Pāṇḍavas' house as a friend. God lives among those who love Him.

7. Jñānadēva's signature (jñānadēva mhaṇē)
The great saint who wrote the Jñānēśvarī sums up the path: the Name.

8. Viṭhala, Viṭhala
The chant returns to Viṭhala again and again. The Lord of Paṇḍharpur is the Name's home.

@@ 16.3 | Hari Mhaṇā |
Overall Summary
A Marathi abhaṅg: "Say 'Hari', say 'Gōvinda', say 'Gōpāla', say 'Nārāyaṇa'." Refrain: "Gōvinda Gōpāla Hari Nārāyaṇa."

Say "Rāma, the king"; say "Sītā"; say "Ātmā-Rāma"; say "Rāma". Then a chain of Rāma's names: Kōdaṇḍa Rāma, Kalyāṇa Rāma, Ayōdhyā Rāma, Ātmābhi Rāma, Paṭṭābhi Rāma, Ānanda Rāma. Say "Śyāma", "Ghanaśyāma", "Mēghaśyāma", "Rādhē Śyāma". Gōvinda who plays in the bowers, Gōvinda who steals curds, Nanda's young son who is the joy of the gōpīs' hearts, Gōvinda in yellow silk.

Key Takeaway
The abhaṅg is an easy chain of names. Saying them, one after another, is the practice.
For each of us: we can pick any name in this song and repeat it through the day.

Deeper Meaning
1. Say (mhaṇā)
Every line tells us to "say". The Name is meant to be spoken aloud.

2. You (tumhī)
"Tumhī" means "you all". The instruction is for everyone.

3. Ātmā-Rāma (ātmā rāma)
Rāma is the Self within. Saying His name turns us inward.

4. A chain of Rāmas (kōdaṇḍa rāma … ānanda rāma)
Each name of Rāma links to the next, like a chain. The mind follows easily.

5. Śyāma and Rādhē Śyāma
Krishna is the dark one, Śyāma, with Rādhā. The song moves from Rāma to Krishna without a break.

6. The curd-thief (gōrasacōrā)
He steals curds in Vraja. Even His mischief is worth remembering.

7. Joy of the gōpīs' hearts (gōpikā hṛdaya nanda kiśōrā)
He is the delight of those who love Him most. Saying His names brings us near them.

8. Paṭṭābhi Rāma (paṭṭābhi rāma)
Rāma crowned is the last in the chain of Rāmas. The chant climbs to His glory.

9. Kuñja vihārā (kuñjavihārā)
He plays in the bowers of Vṛndāvana. The chant ends in His sweet play.

@@ 16.4 | Dāsācī Sampatti |
Overall Summary
A Marathi abhaṅg by Samarth Rāmdās (the same song as 11.11): "The servant's wealth is Rāma, Lord of Sītā; the companion of my life is Rāma alone."

Rāma alone is mother, Rāma alone is father, Rāma alone is brother and kin. Learning, riches and gold, and all one's own people: Rāma alone. Rāma alone is master, Rāma is the protector, Rāma is the gain in worldly life. Rāma alone is knowledge, Rāma alone is meditation, Rāma is contentment, says Rāmdās.

Key Takeaway
For the devotee, Rāma is everything: family, wealth, knowledge and peace. Nothing else is needed.
For each of us: whatever we value, we can see Rāma in it. Then nothing we have is separate from Him.

Deeper Meaning
1. The servant's wealth (dāsācī sampatti)
A servant of Rāma has no property except Rāma. And that is the greatest wealth.

2. The companion of life (jīvācā saṅgāti)
Rāma is the one companion who never leaves, in this life and beyond.

3. Mother, father, brother (rāma ēka mātā …)
All our relationships are fulfilled in Rāma. He is the love behind every love.

4. Learning and gold (vidyā vaibhava kāñcana)
Even knowledge and wealth are Rāma. They are good when they lead us to Him.

5. The gain in worldly life (lābhayā saṁsārī)
The real profit from living in the world is Rāma. Everything else is temporary.

6. Contentment (rāma samādhāna)
Rāma is "samādhāna", peace and satisfaction. When we have Him, the mind is content.

7. The servant's name (rāmadāsī)
The abhaṅg ends with the composer's name, Rāmdās, meaning "Rāma's servant". He lived what he sang.

8. A simple list
Each line just says "Rāma alone". The repetition is the teaching: when Rāma fills every place, there is no room left for worry.

9. Same song as 11.11
The book prints this abhaṅg twice, in the pūjā (11.11) and here among the Abhaṅgs.

@@ 16.5 | Jyācē Vaṁśī |
Overall Summary
It begins with a Sanskrit verse: "May Your form, dark and lovely like green crops, always shine in my mind; may the singing of Your names grow on the tip of my tongue, O Raghupati. Lord, may my life be happy always at Your lotus feet, living in their shade as Your servant, counting the gods' heaven as straw." Then the chant: "Raghupati, may the singing of Your names grow on the tip of my tongue."

Then a Marathi abhaṅg: "O God, give me birth in a family whose family dharma is the service of Rāma. Let me talk with those whose speech is coloured by Rāma's name. Again and again I plead; O ocean of mercy, give me success quickly. Uddhava says, O great king, mass of consciousness: let me have the company of Rāmdās." It ends: "Jay jay Rām."

Key Takeaway
The devotee asks to be born among Rāma's devotees and to have their company. Good company keeps the Name alive.
For each of us: we can seek the company of people who love God. Their words colour our own.

Deeper Meaning
1. His form in the mind (tava vapuḥ cittē sadā rājatām)
The first wish is that Rāma's dark, lovely form shine in the mind always.

2. The Name on the tongue (jihvāñcalē vardhatām)
May the Name grow on the tip of the tongue, like a plant that keeps growing.

3. Heaven as straw (tṛṇīkṛta surāvāsa)
The devotee counts heaven as worthless compared to living at Rāma's feet.

4. Born in a devoted family (jyācē vaṁśī)
He asks to be born where Rāma's service is the family tradition. Family can nourish devotion.

5. Speech coloured by the Name (raṅgalī rāma nāmī)
He wants to talk with those whose words are full of Rāma. Conversation shapes us.

6. Again and again I plead (vāraṁ vāraṁ vinantī)
He keeps asking. Persistent prayer is a sign of real longing.

7. Rāmdās's company (rāmadāsācā saṅga)
Uddhava (the composer) asks for the company of Rāmdās. The saints longed for each other's company.

@@ 17.1 | Sunō Sunō Sādhujī |
Overall Summary
A Hindi song by Sant Kabīr (as the book notes), opening with salutations: victory to Sītā's Lord Rāmacandra, to Rādhā's Lord Kṛṣṇacandra, to Gaurī's Lord who wears the moon, to Hanumān, son of the Wind, and to Jñānānanda of Tapōvanam.

"Listen, listen, good people: say 'King Rāma'. He hungers only for feeling and devotion; He is not reached by yoga and tricks. When devotees forgot themselves, mad for Rāma, He gave them His own place. What good conduct did the hunter have? What was Dhruva's age? What learning did Gajēndra (the elephant) study? Vidura's caste was low. What courage did Ugrasēna have? What beauty did Kubjā have? Rāma is not met through great wealth; ask Sudāmā. Kabīr says, listen, brother sādhu: Rāma is met through devotion. Not through charms, spells and fuss; liberation is through Rāma's bhajan."

Key Takeaway
God is not won by wealth, learning, age, caste, looks or clever practices. He is reached by devotion alone.
For each of us: whatever we lack, we can still love God. That is the only qualification.

Deeper Meaning
1. Hungry for devotion (bhāva bhakti kā bhūkhā)
God's only hunger is for love. Nothing else satisfies Him.

2. Not by yoga and tricks (jōga jugata nahīṁ pāvē)
Difficult methods do not win Him. A simple loving heart does.

3. Mad for Rāma (rāma dīvānē)
When devotees forget themselves in love, He gives them His own place.

4. Examples from the stories (vyādha … dhruva … gajēndra …)
The hunter Vālmīki, the child Dhruva, the elephant, Vidura, Kubjā and Sudāmā had no great qualifications. All reached God by love.

5. Not through wealth (bhārī dhan sē)
Sudāmā was poor, yet Krishna loved him. Riches cannot buy the Lord.

6. Liberation through bhajan (rāmabhajan sē mukti)
Kabīr's conclusion: freedom comes through singing Rāma's name.

7. Jñānānanda's name
The opening salutations end with Jñānānanda of Tapōvanam. This bhajan tradition adds its own Guru to Kabīr's song.

8. Say "King Rāma" (rājā rāma kahō)
The simple instruction is to say His name. Kabīr's whole teaching fits in two words.

@@ 17.2 | Guru Kṛpā Añjana Pāyō |
Overall Summary
A Hindi song by Sant Kabīr (as the book notes): "I have received the collyrium (eye-salve) of the Guru's grace, my brother; now I see nothing without Rāma."

Rāma within, Rāma without; wherever I look, everything is fulfilled. Rāma when awake, Rāma when asleep; in my dreams too I see King Rāma. Kabīr says, this experience is wonderful: wherever I look, I see Rāma alone.

Key Takeaway
The Guru's grace opens our eyes. Then Rāma is seen everywhere, inside and outside, awake and asleep.
For each of us: we can ask for the Guru's grace to see the Divine in everything.

Deeper Meaning
1. The Guru's eye-salve (guru kṛpā añjana)
Añjana is collyrium put on the eyes. The Guru's grace is like a salve that clears our sight.

2. Nothing without Rāma (rāma binā kacu dēkhata nāhī)
After the salve, everything seen is Rāma. The world itself has not changed; the seeing has.

3. Inside and outside (andara rāma bāhara rāma)
Rāma is within and without. There is nowhere He is not.

4. Awake and asleep (jāgata … sōvata)
Even in sleep and dreams, Rāma is there. The vision is constant.

5. Fulfilment everywhere (pūraṇa hī kāma)
Wherever Kabīr looks, everything is complete. Nothing is lacking.

6. A wonderful experience (anubhava nīkā)
Kabīr calls it "anubhava", direct experience. It is not theory; it is lived.

7. The Guru first
The song begins with the Guru. In Kabīr's teaching, the Guru's grace comes before the vision of God.

8. King Rāma in dreams (sapanōṁ mē … rājā hī rāma)
Even in dreams, Rāma is king. The mind that has received grace is His kingdom.

@@ 17.3 | Śrī Rāmacandra Kṛpālu |
Overall Summary
A Hindi-Sanskrit song by Gōsvāmī Tulasīdās (as the book notes) in Rāgam Yaman Kalyāṇ: "O mind, worship Śrī Rāmacandra, the merciful, who removes the terrible fear of worldly life."

His eyes are like fresh lotuses; His face, hands and feet are lotuses too, reddish like lotuses. His beauty is greater than countless Kāmas, lovely as a fresh blue lotus; His yellow garment shines like lightning; I bow to the pure husband of Janaka's daughter. Worship the friend of the humble, the Lord of the sun, who uproots the clans of demons; Raghunandana, root of joy, moon of Kōśala, son of Daśaratha. A crown on His head, earrings, a lovely tilaka, generous ornaments on His limbs; arms reaching to His knees, holding bow and arrows, winner of the battle against Khara and Dūṣaṇa. So says Tulasīdās: He delights the minds of Śaṅkara, Śēṣa and the sages; may He live in the bower of my heart, destroying the troop of wicked desires.

Key Takeaway
Tulasīdās asks his mind to worship merciful Rāma and asks Rāma to live in his heart, destroying lust and the other inner enemies.
For each of us: we can invite Rāma to live in the "bower of our heart" and guard it.

Deeper Meaning
1. Worship, O mind (bhaju mana)
Tulasīdās speaks to his own mind. Worship begins inside.

2. Remover of fear (haraṇa bhavabhaya dāruṇam)
Rāma removes the terrible fear of worldly life. He is the refuge from deep anxiety.

3. All lotus (kañja lōcana … kañja mukha …)
His eyes, face, hands and feet are compared to lotuses. Everything about Him is pure.

4. Friend of the humble (dīnabandhu)
He is the friend of the poor and helpless. No one is too small for Him.

5. Arms to the knees (ājānubhuja)
His long arms are a sign of greatness. He can reach and protect everyone.

6. Loved by Śiva (śaṅkara … mana rañjanam)
Śiva and the sages delight in Rāma. The greatest beings love Him.

7. Living in my heart (mama hṛdaya kuñja nivāsa)
Tulasīdās asks Rāma to live in his heart and destroy lust and the other wicked desires. The Lord guards the heart from inside.

@@ ii | Sequence (Pantati) |
Summary meaning
This page explains the order (pantati) in which a Divyanama bhajan is sung: Dhyana Slokam and Thodayamangalam first, then Ashtapadis, then the Panchapati songs of the great saints, then the Pooja and Upachara songs, and finally the Divyanama Sankeertanam itself, ending with a third Ashtapadi, Dehi Mangalam and a Prarthana Abhang.
Meaning, verse by verse
1–2. Dhyana Slokam, Thodayamangalam and Ashtapadis
– We first meditate on the Lord and the Gurus, and then sing a few Ashtapadis of Jayadeva as time allows.
3. Panchapati
– Songs of Narayana Theerthar, Bhadrachala Ramadasa, Purandara Dasa, Sadasiva Brahmendra, Gopalakrishna Bharathi, Thyagaraja, Kabir Das and Meerabai, in that order.
4. Pooja and Upachara songs
– The Lord is worshipped as an honoured guest through songs.
5. Traditional Divyanama songs
– From "Raghava Rama Raghava" to "Krishna Rama Govinda", sung in the fixed traditional order.
6. Divyanama compositions
– Kritis of Bhadrachala Ramadasa, Vijaya Gopala, Narayana Theerthar and Thyagaraja, followed by Gopika Geetham, the dance songs (Kolattam, Kummi), a third Ashtapadi, Dehi Mangalam and a Prarthana Abhang.
Spiritual significance
The order is not random: it moves the heart step by step from meditation, through the songs of the saints and worship, into dancing joy and finally into prayer and surrender.

Detailed explanation
Divyanama Sankeerthanam Pantati – why should we learn it?
Divyanama Sankeerthanam is not just a musical tradition.
It is a sacred spiritual journey.
It is a divine path in which the devotion that welled up in the hearts of the great saints
took the form of music.
The Pantati is a carefully arranged, sacred sequence.
It is not a collection of songs put together casually.
It is a spiritual path that takes us into the state of mind of those saints.
From Narayana Theerthar to Thyagaraja Swamigal,
from Meerabai to Purandara Dasa —
the devotion, longing, surrender and joy they experienced —
this Pantati lets us experience all of it once again.

The greatness of the Pantati
Every kirtanam in this Pantati was chosen not merely for its beauty.
It was chosen with a spiritual purpose.
When we sing in this sacred order,
we are not merely singing;
we immerse ourselves completely in a divine experience.
We live again
the waves of devotion that flowed in the hearts of the saints.

Radha Kalyanam – a divine experience
Radha Kalyanam and Divyanama Sankeerthanam
take us to another world.
There the leela of Radha and Krishna
is not just a story;
it becomes an experience that happens within us.
It is personal,
it is intimate,
it is transforming.

The true meaning of learning
Learning this tradition
is not just knowing the songs.
It is awakening the soul.
In every song a saint's longing echoes.
With every step we walk towards the Divine.

Conclusion
In every note we sing,
the tears of a saint's devotion resound.
In every step we take,
we travel on that timeless divine path.

Divyanama Sankeerthanam – Pantati (order of performance)
Now let us look at the Divyanama Sankeerthanam Pantati, the order of the programme that we follow.

1. The beginning
We begin with:
Dhyana Slokam
Thodayamangalam
These two are the sacred beginning that focuses the mind and settles us in the thought of God.

2. Ashtapadi
After this, depending on the time available,
a few Ashtapadis are sung.
These are the living songs of Jayadeva's tradition of devotion.

3. Panchapati
Next we go to the Panchapati section.
This is a very important spiritual sequence.
In it the kirtanams of the saints are sung in this order:
Narayana Theerthar – Tharangam
Bhadrachala Ramadasar kirtanams
Purandara Dasar kirtanams
Sadasiva Brahmendra kirtanams
Gopalakrishna Bharathi
Thyagaraja Swamigal
Kabir Das
Meerabai
All of these are sacred kirtanams that bring out the many rasas of devotion.

4. Pooja and Upachara kirtanams
After the Panchapati,
the Pooja and Upachara kirtanams are sung (this part is yet to be covered in detail).

5. The Divyanama Sankeerthanam section (the main sequence)
Then begins the main Divyanama Sankeerthanam section.
It is traditionally sung in this order:
Raghava Rama Raghava
Sree Rama Sree Rama Surya
Rama Bhadra Rara
Bega Baro
Deva Bandha Namma
Yehi Gopala
Ambe Gowri Mayya
Nama Vaibhava Slokam
Dhanya Dhanya Ho Pradakshina
Paahi Paahi Gajanana
Krishna Rama Govinda

6. Special Divyanama kirtanams
After this we sing
the kirtanams that belong specially to Divyanama.
They are in this order:
Bhadrachala Ramadasar (Bhadradri kirtanams – 5 or 7)
Vijaya Gopalar
Narayana Theerthar
Thyagaraja
Gopika Geetham
Thava Charanambujam
Angana
Gopi Gopala
Joolata Radha
Kolattam
Kummi
Shiva Shiva Bhava
Dharo Jagannatha
Krishna Rama Govinda
A third Ashtapadi
Dehi Mangalam
Any Prarthana Abhang

Closing thought
This Pantati is not just a list of songs.
It is a map of a spiritual journey.
Every stage lifts us higher.
Every Name takes us deeper within.
This is the greatness of Divyanama Sankeerthanam.

@@ iii | Radha kalyana pantati |
Summary meaning
This page explains the meaning behind Radha Kalyanam. Radha stands for bhakti and Madhava for the Supreme; their wedding symbolises the devotee (jivatma) uniting with God (paramatma). The story of Nandagopa, the rain and Radha is told, and the Muthukkuthal ritual is explained as a picture of meditation.
Meaning, verse by verse
Namasankeertanam
– Krishna told Narada that He lives where His devotees sing His Names. In Kali Yuga this is the easiest, purest and surest path, open to everyone.
Radha Kalyanam
– It is Jeeva–Brahma Aikyam: the devotee becoming one with the Lord. Externally it is celebrated like any wedding, for our joy.
The story
– Krishna caused a storm so that Radha would carry Him; then He showed her Goloka, she remembered her true nature, and Brahma performed their wedding with all Vedic rites. So the Kalyanam is done with all the ceremonies.
Muthukkuthal (Kotnotsavam)
– The mortar is the pure mind, the pestle is the sound of Om, and the rice is the soul covered by maya. Pounding the rice with songs of the Lord removes the husk of maya and offers the pure soul to God.
Spiritual significance
If we perform the Kalyanam with this understanding – that it is a meditation in which the soul is purified and offered to God – the Lord's grace is assured.

Detailed explanation
Radha Kalyana Pantati – a spiritual explanation
Radha Kalyanam is not just a festival.
It is a spiritual experience; an expression of the union of the jiva and the Paramatma.
The glory of Nama Sankeerthanam
Bhagavan Sri Krishna firmly told Narada:
"Wherever My devotees do Nama Sankeerthanam, there I always dwell."
In Kali yuga, difficult practices such as yaga and yajna are all hard to do.
But Nama Sankeerthanam is the easiest, purest and surest path.
It needs nothing else.
One thing is enough — a heart full of devotion.
That is why all the scriptures and hymns
guide us to this very path of the Name.

The philosophy of Radha Kalyanam
Radha Kalyanam is the union of the jivatma and the Paramatma.
Here:
Radha — the fullness of devotion (Bhakti Swaroopa)
Madhava (Krishna) — the Paramatma (Brahman)
That is, the state in which devotion reaches God
is the spiritual meaning of Radha Kalyanam.
But outwardly, for our own experience,
it is performed as a wedding festival.
The spiritual meaning of the wedding rituals
The rituals in this festival:
Kotnotsavam (Muthukkuthal)
Varaprekshanam
Choornikai
Pravaram
Kanyadanam
Panigrahanam
Thirumangalya Dharanam
None of these are mere social customs.
All of them are symbols of union with the Paramatma.

The divine leela – the essence of the story
Once Nandagopa carried Krishna on his shoulder and went to the forest to graze the calves.
Then the sky turned dark with clouds; there was lightning and thunder and heavy rain.
Since Krishna was a little boy to Nandagopa,
he thought the child would be frightened and tried to send him home.
At that moment Radha came there.
Nandagopa called Radha, handed the child Krishna to her,
and asked her to take him home safely.
Radha held the child lovingly and walked on.
The divine change
All these changes in nature were brought about by Krishna,
to make Radha realise her true nature (swaroopa).
Suddenly Goloka appeared —
palaces, the divine abode, the whole divine setting was revealed.
Radha and Madhava rejoiced in their true divine forms.
The awakening of the jivatma
Seeing this vision,
Radha realised her true state.
In that bliss she forgot the child
and stretched out both her arms towards Krishna.
At that moment the child vanished.
The one who appeared there was Madhava (Krishna).
The divine arrangement of the wedding
Since Krishna and Radha in human form
had to follow the divine rules,
Bhagavan Krishna called Lord Brahma
and ordered him to perform the wedding at once in the Vedic way.
Lord Brahma conducted that divine wedding:
Pravaram
Kanyadanam
Panigrahanam
Homams
The wedding was completed with every rite laid down in the shastras.
The essence of the Choornikai
This leela is the essence told in the Choornikai.
The truth it shows us is this:
Radha Kalyanam is a complete divine wedding.
It took place in the Vedic way, without any lack.
Closing thought
So there is nothing wrong in performing this Radha Kalyanam
according to the Pantati given by the bhajan gurus.
Because it is:
an expression of devotion
a reminder of the union of the jiva and the Paramatma
the highest state of Nama Sankeerthanam
Conclusion
Radhe Krishna!
Hari Nama Sankeerthanam Jayati!
Kotnotsavam (Muthukkuthal) – a spiritual explanation
Kotnotsavam, or Muthukkuthal, is an important part held very sacred in the Telugu sampradaya.

The outer practice (as seen in the tradition)
In this ritual, rice (raw paddy with its husk) is placed in a wooden mortar called an ural.
It has a hollow at the top and a round base below.
The rice is pounded into powder with a long cylindrical pestle (ulakkai).
The pestle is decorated with flowers, mango leaves and red and white stripes.
The rice powder made this way
is placed in the cloth tied round the waist of the couple.
Then they are given an oil bath (Talandu Sobhanam).
After that, new clothes are given.

The inner spiritual meaning
Within this outer ritual lies a very deep philosophy.
The mortar – our mind
The pestle – the sound of Omkara
The rice – our jivatma
(the pure, sattvic soul in a state covered by maya)
Just as the husk covers the rice,
maya covers the jivatma.

The philosophy of Muthukkuthal
What is the aim of devotion and meditation?
The mind must become pure.
It must be freed from the six faults such as desire and anger.
It must move away from bonds such as the triputi.
For this:
five maidens (the five jnanendriyas – the pure senses)
place the jivatma (the rice) in the mortar.
Five devotees (the five karmendriyas)
pound it with the pestle,
singing the qualities of the Lord with the sound of Omkara.
This is Muthukkuthal.

Removing maya – winnowing the rice
In this process,
the husk (maya) that covers the rice is removed.
What comes out is:
the pure, white jivatma.
That pure soul is then
offered in the presence of the Lord.

The spiritual meaning
This is a very high philosophy:
the jivatma → is freed from maya
the mind → becomes pure
Omkara → resounds within
finally → it attains union with the Paramatma
That is the union of the jiva and the Paramatma.

A picture of meditation
The whole Muthukkuthal process is:
the five senses
the five karmendriyas
the sound of Omkara
the singing of the Lord's glory
all of these together — a process of dhyana yoga.

Closing thought
Though it looks outwardly like a wedding ceremony,
inwardly it is a very high spiritual experience.
If we enjoy the Kalyanam with this attitude,
it becomes like a yagna.
That is the way to receive the Lord's grace.
In this way, Muthukkuthal stands as a sacred symbol
that shows the state of jivan mukti.

Conclusion
Hari Nama Sankeerthanam Jayati!

Musala Nartanam – a spiritual explanation
Musala Nartanam is regarded as one of the highest expressions of devotion in the Divyanama Sankeerthanam tradition.

The outer meaning
Devotees hold a pestle (musala) in their hands
and dance in joy.
It is done together with the raga and tala of the Ashtapadi songs.
Though the scene looks outwardly like a festival,
its inner meaning is very deep.

The inner philosophy
It stands for the blissful state of a jivan mukta.
A jivan mukta is one who,
though in the body,
though living in the world,
keeps the mind always fixed in the Paramatma.
Though engaged in outer actions,
he is immersed within in complete bliss.
That bliss is Musala Nartanam.

The union of the jiva and the Paramatma
In this state:
the jivatma becomes one with the Paramatma
the feeling of duality (dvandva bhava) disappears
Omkara is no longer separate
Brahman, Omkara and the jiva — all are one
So there is no more feeling of separation.
Only the state of Sat-Chit-Ananda, where all has become one, remains.

The state of jivan mukti
One who has reached this state:
will always be in bliss
has peace within, even with actions outside
and life itself becomes a dance
This is the spiritual meaning of Musala Nartanam.

Radha Kalyanam – order of the programme
Ashtapadi Dhyana Slokam + Ashtapadi 22 + Namavali
Rama Rara Sita Rama Rara
Gowri Kalyana Vaibhogame
Sri Krishna Jaya Jaya
Allo Nere Thillo
Suvvi Balakrishna Suvvi
Pahimam Sri Krishna
Suvvi Chandrasekharalu
Chittajuni Kanyaku
Musala Nartanam – Ashtapadi 3
Vasudeva Tanayudu Krishna
Vastram and Aasirvadam
Choornikai
Pravaram + Kanyadanam
Lagnashtakam
Mangalya Dharanam
Anandam Anandam
Namavali – Kolahala
Kalyana Tarangam – "Alokaye Rukmini"
Namavali – Kalyana Krishna
Nalangu kirtanams
Anjaneya kirtanam
Prarthana Abhang

Closing thought
This whole Pantati is not an outer festival.
It is a spiritual journey.
Every song
is a step towards jivan mukti.
In the end we realise only one thing:
All is one
All is bliss
All is Krishna

Conclusion
Hari Nama Sankeerthanam Jayati!

@@ 1.1 | Puṇḍarīkam |
Overall Summary
The Puṇḍarīkam is the call of victory that opens every bhajan. The leader calls out a name and everyone answers "jay!" (victory).
First come the Lord's three great forms: Rama, the beloved of Janaki; Shiva, the Lord of Parvati; and Govinda, the very life of the gopis. Then the line of Gurus is remembered: Adi Shankara, Bodhendra Swamigal, Sridhara Ayyaval, Marudanallur Sadguru Swamigal, Gnanananda, the Shankaracharyas, Gopalakrishna Bhagavatar and Premikendra Maharaj. Then the whole assembly of saints, all the gods led by Ganesha, Pandarinath (Vittala) and finally Hanuman.

Key Takeaway
Before we sing, we invite everyone who belongs to the Lord: His forms, our Gurus, His saints and His servant Hanuman.
For each of us: we never begin alone. When we remember those who walked this path before us, the bhajan becomes theirs, and their blessing carries it.

Deeper Meaning
1. Remembering, not just announcing (smaraṇam)
"jānakī kānta smaraṇam" means "remember the beloved of Janaki". The opening is not an introduction for the audience; it turns our own minds towards the Lord before the first song.

2. Rama, Shiva and Krishna together (hara hara mahādēva, gōvindā)
The first three calls take in Rama, Shiva and Krishna in one breath. The bhajan tradition sees no rivalry between them: it is one Lord, called by many names.

3. The line of Gurus (guru paramparā)
The Name reached us through people. Bodhendra Swamigal, Sridhara Ayyaval and Marudanallur Sadguru Swamigal are the three Gurus of this bhajan tradition, and the later names carried it to our own time. Saying "jay" to each is a thank-you.

4. Every saint, every god (samasta santa maṇḍalī)
"samasta" means "all". The call is not limited to one school: all holy people and all gods are welcomed.

5. Ending with Hanuman (āñjanēya svāmī)
The last "jay" goes to Hanuman, the perfect servant of Rama. Tradition holds that wherever Rama's name is sung, Hanuman is present, listening.

@@ 1.2 | Nāmāvaḷi |
Overall Summary
A short chain of the Lord's names (nāmāvaḷi) that sets the mood of the bhajan. It calls on Hari Narayana, the remover of sins, who is supreme bliss, who is also the auspicious Shiva, dear to devotees, the lotus-eyed one. It ends with the heart of all devotion: "I am Your servant" (tava dāsōham), and the call "Govinda, Govinda".

Key Takeaway
Every name of the Lord is a quality we can lean on: He removes our sorrow, He is bliss, He loves His devotees.
For each of us: after calling Him by these names, the only thing left to say is "I am Yours". That surrender is where devotion begins.

Deeper Meaning
1. The remover of sins (durita nivāraṇa)
"durita" covers both our wrong deeds and the troubles they bring. The Name is not only praise; it washes both away.

2. Bliss itself (paramānanda)
The Lord does not merely give happiness. He is the bliss we are searching for in everything else.

3. Hari and Hara are one (sadāśiva śaṅkara)
In the middle of Vishnu's names comes Shiva's name. The tradition reminds us right at the start that the Lord is one.

4. Dear to devotees (bhakta jana priya)
He is not distant. He is fond of those who love Him, and that is why calling Him works.

5. The key phrase: "I am Your servant" (tava dāsōham)
All the names lead to this one line. Knowledge, rituals and effort are all summed up in the attitude of a servant who belongs to the Lord. The Namavali places this surrender at the very start of the bhajan.

@@ 2.6.12 | Āj Sakhī Mērē |
Overall Summary
A Hindi bhajan about the joy of the Guru coming home. "Friend, today my Satguru has come to my house, and my mind is full of joy."

At the sight of him all sins are destroyed, and sorrow and poverty go far away. Hearing his nectar-like words, darkness vanishes, and the singer finds the Lord inside his own body. The doubts of many births break, and the fear and heat of worldly life are wiped away. The last line names the poet, Brahmānanda, a servant of the Lord's servants, who clings to the Guru's lotus feet.

Key Takeaway
When the Guru comes into our home, joy, light and freedom from fear come with him.
For each of us: the Guru's visit is not only to a house. When he enters the heart, he finds the Lord already there.

Deeper Meaning
1. The Guru at the door (satguru ghar āyē)
The bhajan pictures a real visit, and the excitement of a devotee whose Guru has come home. The singer turns to a friend to share the news, because such joy cannot be kept inside.

2. Seeing him (darśan sē)
His darśan alone removes sin and sorrow. Being in his presence is itself a blessing.

3. Nectar words (amṛta vacana)
His words are like nectar: as the singer listens, the darkness of ignorance lifts.

4. God within (ghaṭa bhītar prabhu)
The great discovery: the Lord is found inside one's own body. The Guru shows us where to look.

5. Clinging to his feet (caraṇa kamala lipaṭāya)
