The poet ends as a servant of servants, holding on to the Guru's lotus feet.

6. Doubts of many births broken (janam janam kē saṃśaya ṭūṭē)
Questions carried for lifetimes are settled in one meeting. The Guru does not only answer doubts; he removes the doubt itself.

7. Poverty and sorrow gone (dukh daridra)
The greatest poverty is being far from God. In the Guru's presence, that emptiness is filled.

@@ 2.6.13 | Varuvāy Varuvāy |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song by Śrī Muraḷīdhara Svāmigaḷ, calling the Guru to come.

"Come, come, Gurunāthā, come and shower your grace. Come every day and bless us." The Guru is mother, father and Guru all in one. He clears the worries of the mind, gives joy, and is the form of all that is auspicious. The song ends with gratitude: "Our dream has come true, our mind is cooled. We have taken refuge in you, and forgotten everything else."

Key Takeaway
The Guru is everything to the devotee: mother, father and teacher, the one who cools the troubled mind.
For each of us: when we call him with love, the worries that filled our mind begin to fall away.

Deeper Meaning
1. A simple call (varuvāy varuvāy)
"Come, come." The song does not argue or explain; it simply calls, like a child calling a parent. Śrī Muraḷīdhara Svāmigaḷ, who wrote it, made the Name and the Guru the centre of his whole teaching.

2. Every day (anudinam varuvāy)
The prayer is not for one visit but for his presence every day.

3. Mother, father and Guru (mātā pitā guru nī tānē)
All the love and guidance we look for in different people, we find in him.

4. The mind cooled (manamum kuḷirndadu)
In Tamil, a cooled mind is a peaceful one. His grace takes away the heat of worry.

5. Forgetting everything (sakalamum marandōm)
After taking refuge in him, nothing else matters. Surrender brings its own rest.

6. The dream come true (kanavu palittadu)
The longing to be near the Guru is fulfilled. What once seemed only a dream has become the devotee's life.

7. Form of all that is auspicious (sarva maṅgaḷa mūrti)
Everything good and holy is gathered in him.

@@ 2.6.14 | Kaṇṇāra Kaṇḍu (on Kāñci Mahā Periyavā) |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song in Rāgam Cārukēśi on the Kāñci Mahā Periyavā, Śrī Candraśēkharēndra Sarasvatī Svāmigaḷ.

"Seeing him fully with our eyes, hearing him fully with our ears, we sing of our Kāñci Periyavā with full voice. Taking him into our hearts and holding him in our memory, we praise Śrī Candraśēkhara every day." He walks bravely on Ādi Śaṅkara's path. He is the noble, gentle one who soothes the pain this age of Kali brings. He is a part of Kāmākṣī herself, from the Kāmakōṭi line. He is the king of Advaita, the treasure of renunciates, and with him beside us there is no sorrow. Between the verses, all sing "jaya jaya Śaṅkara, hara hara Śaṅkara".

Key Takeaway
The Mahā Periyavā is to be taken in with every sense: seen, heard, sung, felt and remembered.
For each of us: a great soul is not only to be admired. We can let him fill our eyes, ears, voice and heart.

Deeper Meaning
1. With every sense (kaṇṇāra, kādāra, vāyāra)
"With full eyes, full ears, full mouth": the song uses every sense to take in the Guru completely. Devotees who had his darśan often said they could never see enough of him.

2. Walking Śaṅkara's path (śaṅkarar vazhiyil naḍaipōḍum vīrar)
The Mahā Periyavā is praised as one who walked, literally and in spirit, on Ādi Śaṅkara's path.

3. Soothing this age (kali tanda valiyai)
The age of Kali brings pain. His gentle presence eases it.

4. A part of Kāmākṣī (kāmākṣiyin amśam)
He is seen as a part of the Divine Mother of Kāñci, and the Guru of the Kāmakōṭi line.

5. No sorrow with him (tuyar illai un tuṇai)
With him as companion, sorrow has no place.

6. King of Advaita, treasure of renunciates (advaitattin arasē, tuṟavigaḷin parisē)
He lived the truth of Advaita, and his renunciation was so complete that he became the pride of all sannyāsīs.

7. Every day (nidam pōṟṟuvōm)
Praise is not for special days only. We remember him every day.

@@ 2.6.15 | Kaṇṇāra Kaṇḍu (on Rāmānuja) |
Overall Summary
The same Tamil song in Rāgam Cārukēśi, now sung to Śrī Rāmānuja.

"Seeing him fully with our eyes, hearing fully with our ears, we sing of Rāmānuja with full voice; taking him into our hearts and memory, we praise Nambi Rāmānuja every day." He walks on the path of Saḍagōpan (Nammāzhvār). He is the Bhāṣyakāra, the writer of the commentary. He is the noble one who soothes the pain of Kali. He is the elder brother of Āṇḍāḷ, who shows the way in this world and the next. He is the king of serpents (Ādiśēṣa) and the treasure of renunciates, and with him beside us there is no sorrow. The refrain is "Rāmānujā, engaḷ Rāmānujā", "Rāmānuja, our Rāmānuja".

Key Takeaway
The same words of love that praise one great Ācārya can praise another. Devotion to the Guru unites, it does not divide.
For each of us: the bhajan tradition honours every true Guru. We can learn to love all of them.

Deeper Meaning
1. One song, two Gurus (2.6.14 and 2.6.15)
The tune and most of the words are the same as the song to the Mahā Periyavā. Placing them side by side shows that the tradition honours both Ācāryas with one heart. The bhajan tradition sings to every true Guru without asking which school he belongs to.

2. On Nammāzhvār's path (saḍagōpan vazhiyil)
Rāmānuja followed Nammāzhvār, the great Tamil saint of the Divya Prabandham.

3. The commentator (bhāṣyakāra)
He wrote the Śrī Bhāṣya on the Brahma Sūtras, the foundation of Viśiṣṭādvaita.

4. Āṇḍāḷ's elder brother (āṇḍāḷin aṇṇan)
Tradition remembers that Rāmānuja fulfilled a vow Āṇḍāḷ had made to the Lord, and so she is said to have greeted him as her elder brother.

5. King of serpents (aravugaḷin arasē)
He is honoured as an incarnation of Ādiśēṣa, the serpent who serves the Lord.

6. Nambi Rāmānuja (nambi)
"Nambi" is a loving title meaning the perfect one, someone we can trust completely.

7. Our Rāmānuja (engaḷ rāmānujā)
The refrain says "our" Rāmānuja. He belongs to everyone who sings his name.

@@ 2.6.16 | Vizhi Kiḍaikkumā |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song in Rāgam Gauri Manōhari: the longing of a disciple for the Guru's glance.

"Will I receive your glance? Will I receive your hand that gives protection? Will I find shade at my Gurunātha's feet?" When sorrows come wave after wave, the singer asks whether he will hear the Guru's voice saying "anjādē" ("do not fear"). He would take a crore of births if the Guru's grace were with him. When unexpected trouble comes, will just thinking of the Guru bring that protecting hand? With the Guru's glance holding him like an anchor, why should the mind fear the storm of worldly life? He has offered true love and everything he has at the Guru's feet, and asks only for a place in the boat of the Guru's glance.

Key Takeaway
The Guru's glance is the devotee's anchor and boat in the storm of life.
For each of us: we cannot stop the waves, but with the Guru's glance on us, we do not have to be afraid of them.

Deeper Meaning
1. Only a glance (vizhi kiḍaikkumā)
The devotee does not ask for much: just the Guru's look. In this tradition, a single glance of grace can change a life. The song asks the question again and again, "kiḍaikkumā?", will I receive it?

2. "Do not fear" (anjādē)
The words the singer longs to hear are "anjādē". These words, from one we trust, are enough.

3. A crore of births (kōḍi janmam)
He is not afraid of being born again and again, if the Guru's grace is with him. Grace matters more than escape.

4. The anchor (naṅgūram pōl)
The Guru's sidelong glance is like an anchor: the ship still feels the storm, but it does not drift.

5. The boat of his glance (vizhiyōra paḍagu)
The last image: a little boat at the corner of the Guru's eye. A place in it is all the devotee asks for.

6. Everything at his feet (enadellām un aḍiyil vaittēn)
The singer has placed true love and all he has at the Guru's feet. Having given everything, he now waits only for the glance.

@@ 2.6.17 | Kāñci Mānagar Pōga Vēṇḍum |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song in Rāgam Miśra Kamās: the longing to go to Kāñcīpuram and see "our compassionate one" at the Kāmakōṭi Pīṭha.

The song describes the Guru's seat: worshipped by the noble, praised by siddhas, where the learned gather. It is full of simplicity, bowed to by all, and it takes away our worries. It removes ignorance and gives right knowledge. It is the seat of the "walking God" (the name devotees gave the Mahā Periyavā), our own Kāñci Jagadguru Pīṭha. It ends with "hara hara Śaṅkara, jaya jaya Śaṅkara, Kāñci Śaṅkara, Kāmakōṭi Śaṅkara".

Key Takeaway
Going to the Guru's place is itself a prayer; the longing to go is already a blessing.
For each of us: when we long to be near the holy, we are already moving towards it.

Deeper Meaning
1. "I must go" (pōga vēṇḍum)
The song is a longing, not a travel plan. The heart is already on its way to Kāñci. Many devotees know this feeling: the pull of a holy place before the journey begins.

2. Simplicity (eḷimai nirainda)
The seat is praised first for being full of simplicity. Holiness is often very plain.

3. Removing worry and ignorance (kavalaigaḷai pōkkum, maḍamaigaḷ pōkkum)
Going there takes away worries, and then something deeper: ignorance itself.

4. The walking God (naḍamāḍum deyvam)
Devotees called the Mahā Periyavā "the walking God", because he walked from village to village all his life, and God seemed to walk among them.

5. Ending in the Name (hara hara śaṅkara)
As in all bhajans, the song ends by turning the longing into chanting the Name.

6. Where the learned gather (kaṟṟavargaḷ kūḍum)
Scholars and siddhas come to the Pīṭha. It is a home both for simple devotees and for the most learned.

7. The compassionate one (kāruṇya mūrti)
The reason for the journey is to see "our compassionate one". The heart goes to him for his kindness.

@@ 2.6.18 | Tiru Kumaranāy Avatarittār |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song in Rāgam Śaṅkarābharaṇam on the birth of Ādi Śaṅkara.

Leaving holy Kailāsa, Śaṅkara (Shiva) was born as the blessed son of Śivaguru and Āryāmbāḷ, so that the four Vēdas could flourish and all would hail him as the Guru of the world. He came like a whirlwind to the cotton heap of disbelief, like a flame to the darkness in human hearts, like a rain cloud to make the crop of faith grow. The Lord without beginning or end was born at Kālaḍi.

The song closes with a long nāmāvaḷi: hail to Guru Śaṅkara, who set up the six ways of worship (ṣaṇmata), who was born at Kālaḍi, the ocean of compassion; may he remove our fear of time and death.

Key Takeaway
Shiva himself came as a child to revive faith when it was fading.
For each of us: when doubt and darkness grow, the Guru's teaching is the light that brings faith back.

Deeper Meaning
1. Leaving Kailāsa (tiru kayilai agaṉṟu)
The song sees Śaṅkara's birth as Shiva choosing to leave his own home for our sake.

2. So the Vēdas may flourish (catur maṟaigaḷ tazhaikka)
His purpose was to revive the four Vēdas, which were losing their place in people's lives.

3. Three images (sūrāvaḷi, cuḍar, mugil)
A whirlwind scatters a heap of cotton; a flame drives out darkness; a rain cloud makes crops grow. He clears away disbelief, lights the heart and helps faith grow.

4. The six ways of worship (ṣaṇmata sthāpaka)
He set up the six ways of worship (of Shiva, Vishnu, Śakti, Gaṇapati, Sūrya and Skanda), so that every devotee had a path, and all led to the one Truth.

5. Removing the fear of time (kāla bhayam tīrttu)
The closing prayer asks him to remove our fear of time and death, the deepest fear of all.

6. Born at Kālaḍi (kālaḍiyil avataritta)
The endless Lord took birth in a small village by a river. The Divine comes close to us in simple places.

@@ 3 | Ashtapadi |
Overall Summary
In the book, Chapter 3 does not print the Aṣṭapadis; it gives a link to them instead. In the bhajan order (paddhati) the Aṣṭapadis of Śrī Jayadēva's Gīta Gōvindam are sung at this point, after the Guru kīrtanas. In this app they are a separate book: tap "Śrī Gīta Govindam" on the home page for every Aṣṭapadi with its words and meaning.

Key Takeaway
The Aṣṭapadis come early in the bhajan because their sweetness softens the heart for the Divine Names that follow.
For each of us: the Aṣṭapadi book in the app is part of this same bhajan. Turn to it here and come back for Śrī Pārthasārathī Stuti.

@@ 3.1 | Śrī Pārthasārathī Stuti |
Overall Summary
A song in Rāgam Tōḍi to Krishna as Pārthasārathi, the charioteer of Arjuna. It opens with a verse: may Krishna protect us, the treasure of Dēvakī's merit, who holds the reins in His teeth, scratches the itching necks of the Pāṇḍavas' horses with His nails, and bathes them every day with water from His cupped hands.

The refrain says: worship Mādhava, the treasure of love and grace, dressed as the charioteer of the Pāṇḍavas and of Arjuna.

Each verse then sets His divine greatness beside the humble service He does. The gods watch Him with reverence as He sits on the battlefield with only a whip in His hand. The nails that leave marks of love on Lakṣmī now soothe the horses. The hands that hold the conch, mace, lotus and discus pour water over them. The mouth that gave out all the Vedas calls "hō hō hō" to the horses. The feet that even Lakṣmī and the sages' minds cannot reach walk to Hastinapura as Yudhiṣṭhira's messenger.

Out of love for His devotee He even sets aside His own vow so that Bhīṣma's vow is kept, His curls full of the horses' dust and sweat on His cheeks. He is kind to the humble, the refuge of the suffering, the Gōpāla of Bhadrācala who loves to care for Kuntī's sons. The Maṅgaḷam wishes auspiciousness to the butter-thief, the beloved of the gōpīs praised by Nārada, and bows to Śrī Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha as Guru.

Key Takeaway
The Lord of the universe gladly becomes a servant for those who love Him. His greatness is shown most in how low He is willing to bend.
For each of us: no work done for God is small. Whatever humble task is in front of us can be done with the same love with which Krishna washed the horses.

Deeper Meaning
1. The Lord as a servant (pāṇḍava sārathī vēṣam)
Krishna chose to be a charioteer, a servant's job, rather than a king or a warrior. "Vēṣam" means a costume: the Supreme puts on the dress of a servant for His devotees.

2. The same hands, a new use (yatkara … tatkara)
The song keeps returning to one idea. The hands that hold the divine weapons wash the horses, and the mouth that spoke the Vedas calls to them. Nothing of His greatness is lost; it is only turned into service.

3. Loving care for the horses (prēmṇā … kalayantam)
He does not tend the horses as a chore. "Prēmṇā" means with love: even animals receive His tenderness, because they serve His devotee.

4. Out of reach, yet walking as a messenger (muni manasām api dūram)
The feet that sages cannot reach even in meditation walk to the Kauravas' city as a messenger for Yudhiṣṭhira. Love brings the unreachable Lord close.

5. Breaking His own word for a devotee (tyaktvā svadharmam … bhīṣma pratijñā)
Krishna had vowed not to take up arms in the war, yet He sets that aside so that His devotee Bhīṣma's vow is kept. A devotee's honour matters more to Him than His own.

6. Dust and sweat on His face (śramajala bindu kapōlam)
The battlefield picture ends with dust in His curls and sweat on His cheeks. The tired, dusty charioteer is the most beautiful form, because it shows how hard He works for us.

@@ 11.12 | Mantra Puṣpam |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. Mantra Puṣpam is the Vedic hymn ("yō'pāṁ puṣpaṁ vēda…") chanted while offering flowers to the Lord at the end of the pooja. The chant itself is the flower offered.
Spiritual significance
The Veda is offered back to the Lord as a flower – our worship is complete only when it is offered with His own words.

@@ 11.13 | Namaskāram |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. Here everyone prostrates before the Lord, offering body, mind and speech in surrender.
Spiritual significance
Namaskaram is vandanam, one of the nine forms of bhakti: bowing down melts the ego.

@@ 11.14 | Kaṭṭiya Vacanangaḷ |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. Kaṭṭiya vacanangaḷ are the formal words of praise announced aloud before the Lord, like the herald's announcements in a king's court, proclaiming His titles and glory.
Spiritual significance
We honour the Lord as the King of kings; announcing His glory reminds us whose court we are standing in.

@@ 11.15 | Caturvēda Pārāyaṇam |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. Here passages from the four Vedas (Rig, Yajur, Sama and Atharva) are chanted before the Lord as part of the upacharas (services).
Spiritual significance
Hearing the Vedas is itself a purification; they are offered to the Lord from whom they came.

@@ 11.17 | Vinnapa Gadyam |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. A gadyam is a prayer in prose; the "vinnapa" (humble request) gadyam is a prose prayer in which the devotee humbly places their requests and surrender before the Lord.
Spiritual significance
After singing and serving, the devotee speaks to the Lord simply and directly, as a child to a parent.

@@ 11.38 | Pāmbāṭṭi Siddhar |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. Pāmbāṭṭi Siddhar was a Tamil Siddha (yogi-poet) whose songs address the "snake" – the kundalini, or the mind – telling it to dance ("āḍu pāmbē") and awaken towards God.
Spiritual significance
The Siddhars teach that the real snake to be charmed is our own restless mind.

@@ 10.7 | Baṇṭu Rīti Kolu Vīyavayya |
Overall Summary
A song by Tyāgarāja in Rāgam Cakravākam: "Give me, O Rāma, a post like that of a royal guard in Your service."

He wants to be a guard who strikes down the proud enemies, Manmatha (lust) and the others, and throws them to the ground. "The thrill of devotion will be my fine uniform; the badge saying 'Rāma's devotee' my emblem; and Rāma's name my excellent sword. These will shine on Tyāgarāja."

Key Takeaway
Tyāgarāja wants no high position, only to stand guard at Rāma's door, armed with His name, keeping out the inner enemies.
For each of us: we too can "stand guard" over our hearts, using the Name to keep out anger, greed and pride.

Deeper Meaning
1. A guard's post (baṇṭu rīti kolu)
"Baṇṭu" is a soldier or guard. Tyāgarāja asks for the humble job of guarding Rāma's door.

2. The enemies to fight (tuṇṭa viṇṭi vāni modalaina madādulu)
The enemies are inside: Manmatha (desire), pride and the rest. The guard keeps them out of the heart.

3. The uniform (rōmāñcamanu ghana kañcukamu)
His uniform is "rōmāñca", the thrill of devotion that makes the hair stand on end.

4. The badge (rāma bhaktuḍanu mudra)
His badge says "Rāma's devotee". That is the only title he wants.

5. The sword (rāma nāmamanu vara khaḍgamu)
His weapon is Rāma's name. It cuts through every inner enemy.

6. These will shine on me (rājillunayya)
Tyāgarāja says these simple things, the thrill, the badge and the Name, will shine on him more than any royal honour.

7. A humble wish
Tyāgarāja could ask for anything, and he asks for a guard's post. True devotees want nearness, not rank.

8. Guarding the heart
The door Tyāgarāja guards is the heart's door. With the Name in hand, nothing harmful enters.

@@ 10.8 | Vandanamu Raghunandana |
Overall Summary
A song by Tyāgarāja in Rāgam Sahānā: "Salutations to You, joy of the Raghus, builder of the bridge, cooling sandal-paste to devotees, Rāma."

"O Lakṣmī's beloved, should You argue with me? Is there any difference between us? Is this a joke, Rāma? Is this right? Is it to Your profit? Will You now look down on me, You who are sung by sages? Rāma's name is my welfare, my divine abode and my daily observance. Come quickly, ocean of mercy, You who dwell in Śrī Tyāgarāja's heart."

Key Takeaway
Tyāgarāja bows, argues and pleads with Rāma, but ends in surrender: Rāma's name is everything to him.
For each of us: even our questions and complaints can end in trust. The Name is our home and our daily practice.

Deeper Meaning
1. Salutations (vandanamu)
The song starts with a bow. Whatever follows, respect comes first.

2. Cooling sandal to devotees (bhakta candana)
Sandal-paste cools the body. Rāma cools the heat of worry in those who love Him.

3. Is there any difference between us? (nē bhēdamā)
He asks why Rāma keeps a distance, when there is no real difference between the Lord and His devotee.

4. Arguing in love (vādamā)
Like a close friend, Tyāgarāja can argue with Rāma. The relationship is honest.

5. The Name as everything (kṣēmamu divya dhāmamu nitya nēmamu)
The Name is his welfare, his heavenly home and his daily discipline. It is enough.

6. Living in his heart (tyāgarāja hṛdayāgarā)
The song ends by calling Rāma the one who lives in Tyāgarāja's heart. The one he calls is already inside.

7. Builder of the bridge (sētu bandhana)
Rāma built a bridge across the ocean. He makes a way to reach His devotees.

8. Come quickly (vēgarā)
After all the questions, the cry is simple: come quickly.

@@ 10.9 | Sītā Kalyāṇa Vaibhōgamē |
Overall Summary
A song by Tyāgarāja in Rāgam Kuriñji: "The splendour of Sītā's wedding! The splendour of Rāma's wedding!"

Rāma is worthy of Hanumān's praise, of holy life, whose eyes are the sun and moon, of lovely body. He protects devotees, has a quiver full of arrows, plays at giving both worldly joy and liberation, and protects the Brahmins. He is fearsome to the wicked demons, whose every wish is fulfilled; dark, delighting the world, living in Sākēta. He is the support of all worlds, the one hero of battle, far from proud men, steady as the golden mountain. He moves in the Vedas and Āgamas, of matchless form; as the mountain-holder He destroys sin; He is the support of those who bow. He is the song praised by Śiva, the boat across the ocean of worldly life, born in the Sun dynasty, praised by Tyāgarāja.

Key Takeaway
The wedding of Sītā and Rāma is a festival of joy. Singing it, we join the celebration of the Divine Couple.
For each of us: this is the song sung at the Sītā Kalyāṇam in the bhajan. Taking part, we bring that blessing into our own families.

Deeper Meaning
1. The splendour (vaibhōgamē)
"Vaibhōgam" means grandeur and festive glory. The wedding is a celebration for the whole world.

2. Sītā's and Rāma's wedding (sītā kalyāṇa … rāma kalyāṇa)
The refrain names both. The divine wedding is the union of the Lord and His Śakti.

3. Praised by Hanumān (pavanaja stuti pātra)
Hanumān, the perfect devotee, praises Rāma. The wedding song begins with the devotee.

4. Worldly joy and liberation (bhukti mukti)
Rāma gives both a good life and final freedom. Neither is outside His gift.

5. The boat across worldly life (bhava jalādhi pōta)
He is the ship that carries us across the ocean of birth and death.

6. Sung by Śiva (paramēśa nuta gīta)
Śiva Himself sings Rāma's praise. The bhajan joins that song.

7. Delight of the world (jagadabhirāma)
Rāma delights the whole world. His wedding is everyone's joy.

8. Matchless form (nirupama śarīra)
His form has no equal. Seeing the wedding is seeing that beauty.

9. Born in the Sun dynasty (taraṇi kula sañjāta)
He comes from the line of the sun: light for the whole world.

@@ 11.1 | Āyāhi Mādhava |
Overall Summary
A song of Śrī Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha in Rāgam Kāmbhōji that opens the pūjā by inviting the child Krishna: "Come, Mādhava! O Krishna, child Krishna, darling Krishna, little Krishna, come."

The morning has passed and the sun has reached midday; auspicious rites are ready for You. You are covered in sweat after playing, tired from Your games with the other boys. In my house, accept fresh butter, curds white as the moon's disc and thick as foam, and other food, O beautiful one with lovely anklets; take it with love and be happy. Your two lotus feet are softer than tender leaves, scented with jasmine; Your eyes are like lotus petals, Your cheeks lovely; You adorn the hearts of the cowherd women. Your jewelled earrings swing, You wear spotless pearls, You love childlike play, Your navel is like a lotus, You long for butter, and You are easy for Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha to reach.

Key Takeaway
Pūjā begins by inviting the Lord home as a tired, hungry child. The worship that follows is the loving care a mother gives.
For each of us: when we offer food or flowers at home, we can picture Krishna coming in from play, and serve Him with that same tenderness.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come (āyāhi)
"Āyāhi" means "come here". Every pūjā starts with an invitation, āvāhana. This song is that invitation sung with love.

2. A child home from play (aṅga ja salila āvṛtē)
Krishna is pictured covered in sweat after playing. The Lord of the universe is welcomed like a child coming in at noon.

3. Butter and curds (dadhi khaṇḍa phēna … navanīta)
The offerings are simple: curds, butter, milk. The tradition teaches that the Lord loves simple food given with love more than rich dishes.

4. In my house (mandirē mama)
"Mandira" means both a temple and a home. When He is invited, our house becomes His temple.

5. Soft lotus feet (pallava ati kōmaḷa pāda paṅkaja)
His feet are softer than young leaves. The devotee notices this and wants to care for them gently.

6. Easy to reach (nārāyaṇa tīrtha sulabha)
The last word says He is "sulabha", easily reached, by Nārāyaṇa Tīrtha. Love makes the Lord easy to find.

@@ 11.2 | Ēhi Tāta |
Overall Summary
A song by Śrī Vijaya Gōpāla Svāmi in Rāgam Nādanāmakriyā: "Come, dear one, Śrī Vāsudēva, come!" It has eighteen lines, each full of Krishna's names and qualities.

Come and protect, beloved of the gōpīs; give joy, lotus-naveled Lord. He gives unending fruit, is wise, punishes the wicked, and is pledged to free us from the fear of worldly life. He is like a mountain of collyrium, lotus-eyed, playing in the bower-houses. He is Kēśava who destroyed Palāśa, purifies the inner self and cuts the noose that binds souls. He gives His blessing hand to the best sages who have left wealth behind. He crushed Kāḷiya, cared for the elephant, and is the ornament of the sages' women. His feet are soft and smeared with saffron; He wears bracelets and a musk tilaka. He cuts down enemies with fierce valour; His cheeks shine with two earrings. He is served by gods and sages, Nanda's son, the thief of fresh butter. He bewilders Śiva and Brahmā; His curved lotus feet decorate the world. His lips are red like the bimba fruit, His neck like a conch, His hair like bees, and Tumburu sings to Him. He lifted the Mandara, killed Madhu and Kaiṭabha, and is Mukunda with a lovely lotus face. He is Sugrīva's friend, pure and lovely of body, praised by Sumitrā's son, of wonderful story. He holds the discus and mace, slays Mura, and makes gods and sages shine. He carries those who take refuge, is an ocean of mercy, and plays in love. He gives peace to the world and to those who bow, and is a terror to demons. His lotus feet live in good hearts; He is supreme in the fist-fight. He is the sun to the lotus hearts of the young women of Vraja, Vijaya Gōpāla, whose form enchants the sages.

Key Takeaway
This is an invitation for the Lord to come and receive worship. Each line adds another reason to love Him.
For each of us: "Ēhi", "come", is a word we can say to the Lord at the start of any prayer.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come, dear one (ēhi tāta)
"Tāta" is a word of affection, used for a child or a dear elder. The Lord is called with warmth.

2. Free from the fear of worldly life (bhava bhaya mōkṣaṇa dīkṣa)
He has taken a vow, as it were, to free us from fear. It is His chosen work.

3. Cutter of the noose (hṛta paśu pāśa)
"Paśu" is the bound soul, "pāśa" the rope. He cuts the ropes that tie us.

4. The blessing hand (datta abhaya vara hasta)
He gives His hand, raised in "fear not" and "receive a blessing", to those who have left everything for Him.

5. Rāma too (mitraja mitra … sumitrā suta nuta)
One line praises Rāma, friend of Sugrīva, praised by Lakṣmaṇa. Krishna and Rāma are one Lord.

6. Living in good hearts (śiṣṭa hṛdaya viniviṣṭa padāmbuja)
His feet are set firmly in good hearts. A pure heart is His throne.

7. Vijaya Gōpāla's signature
The last line names Vijaya Gōpāla, who enchants the sages. The composer and his Lord share the name.

@@ 11.3 | Bārō Murārē |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Śrī Vādirāja (as the book notes) in Rāgam Dēś: "Come, Murāri, child of the Śūra clan; You who are always thoughtful, steady and joyful."

"Get up for Your meal; Your whole body is covered in dust! Stop playing now, Śrī Krishna, merciful one!" He has feet like red lotuses, wears the Kaustubha gem, is the greatest giver, and rests on the serpent. His face is like the autumn moon, His eyes like lotuses; He lives in the best house of the Yadus, and He is Śrī Hayavadana.

Key Takeaway
The Lord is called in from play to eat, like any child. The great Lord Hayavadana is also the dusty little boy.
For each of us: inviting the Lord can be as natural as calling a child to dinner.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come (bārō)
The whole song is a loving call. In pūjā, the Lord is invited to come and receive our offering.

2. Covered in dust (maiyellā dhūḷō)
He has been playing outside and is covered in dust. The image is tender and real.

3. Stop playing now (āṭa sākēḷō)
The mother says "enough play". Tradition delights in picturing the Lord as obedient to His devotee's love.

4. The greatest giver (parama vitaraṇa)
The child who is called to eat is also the one who gives everything to the world.

5. Śrī Hayavadana (hayavadana)
Hayavadana, the horse-faced form of Viṣṇu, was Vādirāja's chosen deity. His name is the composer's signature.

6. Thoughtful and joyful (sadā vicārē santōṣa dhīrē)
He is always thoughtful and steady in joy. Even as a playful child, He is the calm Lord.

7. Lotus-red feet (aruṇābja caraṇa)
His feet are like red lotuses at dawn. In pūjā, worship always begins at His feet, the place of refuge for every devotee.

@@ 11.4 | Śaraṇāgata Vatsala |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Śrī Annamācārya (as the book notes) in Rāgam Śaṅkarābharaṇam: "O You who love those who surrender, easy for all to reach; Puruṣōttama, accept the worship from my hands."

"You carry the universes of the three worlds; shall I pour on You water from a little vessel? Let Indirā Dēvi, who is near, sprinkle You with rose-water like spring water; agree, and take a bath to cool Yourself. You showed Your queen Your universal forms; shall I put You in a box and a bag and tie You up? You were the wonder inside Kausalyā's womb, the chief queen; take that little place as Your Vaikuṇṭha. You eat the ocean-waves of the milk-sea; shall I offer You food from a small pot? Lord Vēṅkaṭēśa of Alamēlumaṅga, accept this as a feast from the Bharadvājas."

Key Takeaway
Our offerings are tiny compared to the Lord's greatness, yet He accepts them with love. That is His kindness to those who surrender.
For each of us: we may feel our worship is too small. The Lord does not measure it; He looks at the love in it.

Deeper Meaning
1. Loving to those who surrender (śaraṇāgata vatsala)
"Vatsala" is a cow's love for her calf. The Lord loves those who come to Him with that same tenderness.

2. Easy for all (sarva sulabha)
He is easy for everyone to reach. Our small worship is enough to reach Him.

3. A little water for the Lord of universes (cembulōni nīḷḷu)
Annamācārya laughs gently at himself: pouring water from a small vessel on the one who carries the universes. Yet the Lord accepts it.

4. Lakṣmī helps (indirā dēvi pannīṭi)
He asks Lakṣmī to sprinkle rose-water. The Divine Mother helps the devotee serve.

5. A small place as Vaikuṇṭha (peṭṭacōṭu vaikuṇṭhamugā)
The Lord once lived in Kausalyā's womb. If He could live in so small a place, He can accept our small shrine as Vaikuṇṭha.

6. A feast from the Bharadvājas (bharadvājula vindu)
Annamācārya belonged to the Bharadvāja family. He offers his family's simple food as a feast to Vēṅkaṭēśa.

@@ 11.5 | Jaya Jaya Dēvādi |
Overall Summary
The song for the pūjā offerings (upacāras) in Rāgam Madhyamāvati: "Victory, victory, God of the gods, O Lord, Gōpālakṛṣṇa, ocean of mercy." Each verse offers one service.

Sandal (gandham): musk, camphor, fine agaru and gōrōcana with sandal-paste: may it anoint Your feet, You who are all fragrance, father of the lotus-born Brahmā. Flowers: garlands of mandāra, lotus, mālati, maruvaka and jasmine, with a lovely tulasi garland: accept them, You who live in Vṛndāvana. Incense (dhūpam): praised by Brahmā, worshipped by Indra, Mādhava, Gōvinda, Madhusūdana, moon-faced husband of the Earth; remover of sin, lotus-eyed, Lord of Sākēta, friend in danger, protector of the helpless: accept this incense, Hari. Lamp (dīpam): jewel-adorned Lord of Mathurā, dear to devotees, freer from worldly life, giver of what is wished, Kēśava of bliss: accept this lamp. Ārati: an auspicious ārati with golden lamps for the husband of Lakṣmī, for the auspicious form of Madhusūdana, for Raṅga. Food (mahā nivēdanam): rice with honey, ghee and dāl, many vegetable dishes, five kinds of sweets, pāyasam and curds: accept them, Mādhava. Fruit: coconut, jackfruit, mango, many bananas, oranges and pickles. Sweets: honey, juices, coconut milk, cow's milk, butter-cakes, fried sweets, frothy vaḍais, drinks and fruits: eat them, our dear father, with good ghee, curd and butter. Betel (tāmbūlam): white betel leaves with areca nut, camphor and lime: accept this betel, giver of boons.

Key Takeaway
Each offering in pūjā is a way of serving the Lord as an honoured guest: fragrance, flowers, incense, light, food and betel.
For each of us: we can offer the Lord what we enjoy ourselves, with care. What is offered with love becomes prasāda for us.

Deeper Meaning
1. The upacāras (dhūpa dīpa upacāra)
"Upacāra" means service. In pūjā, the Lord is served the way a beloved guest is honoured at home.

2. Sandal for His feet (tavāṅghri vilēpanam)
The fragrant paste is offered first for His feet. Service begins at the feet.

3. Flowers and tulasi (tuḷasī mālikayā saha)
Many flowers are offered, but tulasi is named specially. Tulasi is dearest to Him.

4. Incense and the lamp (dhūpam … dīpam)
Incense pleases the sense of smell, the lamp the eyes. With the lamp we see the Lord; with His light we see everything.

5. Ārati (śṛṅgāra hārati)
The ārati is waved with joy. It is the moment when everyone sees the Lord together.

6. Food as love (śālyannam … svīkuru)
The long list of food is not about quantity. It is the love of a family who wants to feed the Lord the best they have.

7. Betel at the end (tāmbūlam)
Betel is offered after a meal, as to a guest. The pūjā ends with the courtesy of a good host.

@@ 11.6 | Śōbhānē |
Overall Summary
A short auspicious refrain (śōbhanam) in Rāgam Saurāṣṭram: "Śōbhānē, śōbhanamē", may all be auspicious, sung to the holy form of the Lord, full of splendour.

Key Takeaway
"Śōbhanam" is a blessing song. We wish the Lord well, as at a wedding or a festival.
For each of us: wishing good things for the Lord fills our own heart with good things.

Deeper Meaning
1. Auspiciousness (śōbhanam)
"Śōbhana" means beautiful, auspicious, bringing good fortune. It is sung at happy occasions like weddings.

2. To the holy form (pāvana mūrtiki)
The blessing is sung to the Lord's holy form. His presence makes everything auspicious.

3. Full of splendour (vaibhavamula)
"Vaibhavam" means glory and festivity. The pūjā has become a festival for the Lord.

4. Wishing the Lord well
It may seem strange to wish the Lord good fortune. But this is how love speaks: we wish well to those we love.

5. A short song with a big heart
The words are few, but the mood is joy. In the bhajan, this refrain marks a moment of celebration.

6. Leading into the ārati
It is sung as part of the pūjā sequence, just before the ārati, filling the hall with festive feeling.

7. Joy at the heart of worship
Pūjā is not only duty. This little refrain reminds everyone in the hall that worshipping the Lord is a happy occasion, a festival of the heart.

@@ 11.7 | Jaya Jaya Ārati |
Overall Summary
A Hindi ārati song by Sant Kabīr, sung with camphor (karpūra hārati), in Rāgam Kāpi: "Victory, victory, the ārati is Yours, Rāma; Lord of my life, Lord of the Raghus, Murāri."

It is for the Lord of the Śēṣa hill (Tirumala) with Lakṣmī, the best of persons, Gōvinda, Murāri. Before Him is His refuge Hanumān, the hero. Dās Kabīr sings Hari's qualities.

Key Takeaway
The ārati is waved with joy to the Lord of our life. Rāma, Vēṅkaṭēśa and Krishna are all praised as one.
For each of us: as the camphor flame burns away without leaving anything, we can let our ego melt in His light.

Deeper Meaning
1. Camphor ārati (karpūra hārati)
Camphor burns completely, leaving no ash. Tradition sees in it the ego that burns away in God's presence.

2. Lord of my life (prāṇanātha)
Kabīr calls Rāma the Lord of his very breath. The ārati is offered to the one we live for.

3. One Lord, many names (rāma … śēṣācalapati … gōvinda)
Rāma, the Lord of Tirumala and Gōvinda are all named in a few lines. Kabīr sees one Lord everywhere.

4. Hanumān before Him (sanmukha śaraṇā hanumanta vīrā)
Hanumān stands facing the Lord. He is the model of a devotee always in the Lord's presence.

5. Kabīr sings (dāsa kabīrā)
Kabīr calls himself "dās", servant. Great saints called themselves servants.

6. Singing His qualities (hari guṇa gāvē)
The ārati is not just a ritual; it is singing Hari's qualities. The flame and the song go together.

7. The flame and the eyes
As the ārati flame is waved, everyone looks at the Lord at once. That shared moment of seeing unites the whole gathering in devotion.

@@ 11.8 | Citta Juni |
Overall Summary
A Telugu maṅgaḷam in Rāgam Yadukula Kāmbhōji (the same song as 12.7): "Victory and auspiciousness to the one with the charming eyes, who steals the heart; to the one who becomes the hearts' best treasure through devotion that removes pride."

May all be auspicious to Gōvinda, Nanda's son, the delight of Indirā (Lakṣmī), whose lotus face outshines the lovely moon. To the form of our Paṭṭābhi Rāma Svāmi, the home of every good quality, who was Bālarāma and Rāma, giving welfare. To the brave Lord Kastūri Raṅga, full of splendour, who wears the garland, who is reached by those who come longing with deep devotion.

Key Takeaway
A maṅgaḷam wishes the Lord auspiciousness at the end of worship. Krishna, Rāma and Raṅganātha are all blessed together.
For each of us: ending prayer with a blessing for the Lord lets us leave in a happy, grateful mood.

Deeper Meaning
1. A maṅgaḷam (jaya maṅgaḷam)
"Maṅgaḷam" means auspiciousness. It is sung at the end of worship, wishing all good to the Lord.

2. The heart-stealer (citta cōra)
He steals our hearts with His charm. Losing our heart to Him is the best kind of loss.

3. Removing pride (mada harti bhakti)
Devotion takes away pride. A humble heart is where the Lord settles.

4. Krishna's face (candruni nindincu mukhāravinda)
His face is lovelier than the moon. Even the moon seems plain beside Him.

5. Paṭṭābhi Rāma (paṭṭābhi rāma)
Rāma crowned on His throne is the home of all good qualities. The maṅgaḷam honours Him in His glory.

6. Kastūri Raṅga (kastūri raṅga)
Raṅganātha, scented with musk, is reached by deep devotion. The song ends at His feet.

7. Ending in blessing
A maṅgaḷam closes the worship on a note of joy. We do not leave the Lord with requests, but with good wishes, as we would leave a dear one's home.

8. One song, two places
The book prints this song twice, here and at 12.7 in the Rādhā Kalyāṇam. The same blessing ends both the pūjā and the divine wedding.

@@ 11.9 | Aṅganāmaṇikini |
Overall Summary
A Telugu maṅgaḷam in Rāgam Nādanāmakriyā: "Auspiciousness to the jewel among women, who holds a lotus in her hand, to the auspicious one (Lakṣmī)."

Always auspiciousness to Alamēlumaṅga, beloved of Vēṅkaṭaramaṇa. Victory and auspiciousness to our Rāmabhadra; blessed auspiciousness to Śrī Rāmacandra. Victory to Gōpālakṛṣṇa; blessed auspiciousness to the one who loves Rādhā. Victory to Āñjanēya Svāmi; blessed auspiciousness to Sadguru Svāmi. Then the chant: Mukunda Rādhē, Gōvinda Rādhē, Kalyāṇi Rādhē, protect me; and finally, Viṭhōbā and Rakhumāyī, the radiant, great king Viṭhōbā with Rakhumāyī.

Key Takeaway
The maṅgaḷam blesses the whole divine family: Lakṣmī, Vēṅkaṭēśa, Rāma, Krishna with Rādhā, Hanumān, the Sadguru, and Viṭhala with Rakhumāyī.
For each of us: we can end our worship by remembering everyone dear to the Lord, the Mother, the devotees and the Guru.

Deeper Meaning
1. The Mother first (aṅganāmaṇikini)
The maṅgaḷam opens with Lakṣmī, the jewel among women. The Divine Mother is honoured first.

2. Alamēlumaṅga (alamēlu maṅgaku)
Lakṣmī as Alamēlumaṅga is the consort of Lord Vēṅkaṭēśa. Mother and Lord are blessed together.

3. Rāma and Krishna (rāmacandruniki … gōpāla kṛṣṇuniki)
Both are blessed in turn. The bhajan tradition keeps them side by side.

4. Hanumān and the Guru (āñjanēya svāmiki … sadguru svāmiki)
Hanumān and the Sadguru are blessed too. The devotee and the teacher are part of the Lord's family.

5. Rādhā's names (mukunda rādhē … kalyāṇi rādhē)
The chant calls Rādhā with Krishna's names. She is inseparable from Him.

6. Viṭhōbā and Rakhumāyī (viṭhōbā rakhumāyī)
The song ends in Paṇḍharpur, with Viṭhala and Rukmiṇī. The whole bhajan's journey is gathered in one blessing.

7. Lakṣmī with the lotus (ambuja pāṇi)
She holds a lotus in her hand. The lotus grows in mud yet stays clean, a picture of the grace she brings into worldly life.

8. Protect me (mām pāhi)
Even in a blessing song, the devotee asks for protection. Love for God and trust in Him always go together.

@@ 11.10 | Sundara Tē Dhyāna |
Overall Summary
A Marathi prayer abhaṅg by Sant Tukārām: "That beautiful form to meditate on, standing on the brick, hands on His hips."

Fish-shaped earrings shine in His ears; the Kaustubha jewel shines at His throat. A tulasi garland on His neck, yellow silk on His waist: I love this meditation always. Tukā says: this is all my happiness; I will look at His lovely face with love.

Key Takeaway
Tukārām's whole joy is to see Viṭhala's form: standing on the brick, hands on His hips. Meditating on that form is happiness itself.
For each of us: we too can hold one simple picture of the Lord in mind, and return to it whenever we can.

Deeper Meaning
1. Beautiful to meditate on (sundara tē dhyāna)
Tukārām does not describe a vision once; he says it is his constant meditation.

2. On the brick (ubhā viṭēvarī)
Viṭhala stands on a brick that His devotee Puṇḍalīka threw to Him to wait on while he served his parents. The Lord is still waiting there, patiently, for His devotees.

3. Hands on His hips (kara kaṭāvarī)
His hands rest on His hips. Tradition reads it as His promise: for those who come to Me, the ocean of worldly life is only this deep.

4. Simple ornaments (makara kuṇḍala … kaustubha … tuḷasī)
Earrings, a jewel, a tulasi garland, yellow silk. The details are few and clear, easy to hold in the mind.

5. All my happiness (hēmci sarvasukha)
Tukā says this one sight is all his happiness. Nothing else is needed.

6. His face (śrīmukha)
He longs only to look at the Lord's face with love. The abhaṅg ends in gazing.

@@ 11.11 | Dāsācī Sampatti |
Overall Summary
A Marathi prayer abhaṅg by Samarth Rāmdās (the same song as 16.4): "The servant's wealth is Rāma, Lord of Sītā; the companion of my life is Rāma alone."

Rāma alone is mother, Rāma alone is father, Rāma alone is brother and kin. Learning, riches and gold, and all one's own people: Rāma alone. Rāma alone is master, Rāma is the protector, Rāma is the gain in worldly life. Rāma alone is knowledge, Rāma alone is meditation, Rāma is contentment, says Rāmdās.

Key Takeaway
For the devotee, Rāma is everything: family, wealth, knowledge and peace. Nothing else is needed.
For each of us: whatever we value, we can see Rāma in it. Then nothing we have is separate from Him.

Deeper Meaning
1. The servant's wealth (dāsācī sampatti)
A servant of Rāma has no property except Rāma. And that is the greatest wealth.

2. The companion of life (jīvācā saṅgāti)
Rāma is the one companion who never leaves, in this life and beyond.

3. Mother, father, brother (rāma ēka mātā …)
All our relationships are fulfilled in Rāma. He is the love behind every love.

4. Learning and gold (vidyā vaibhava kāñcana)
Even knowledge and wealth are Rāma. They are good when they lead us to Him.

5. The gain in worldly life (lābhayā saṁsārī)
The real profit from living in the world is Rāma. Everything else is temporary.

6. Contentment (rāma samādhāna)
Rāma is "samādhāna", peace and satisfaction. When we have Him, the mind is content.

7. The servant's name (rāmadāsī)
The abhaṅg ends with the composer's name, Rāmdās, meaning "Rāma's servant". He lived what he sang.

8. A simple list
Each line just says "Rāma alone". The repetition is the teaching: when Rāma fills every place, there is no room left for worry.

9. Same song as 16.4
The book prints this abhaṅg twice, here in the pūjā and again among the Abhaṅgs (16.4).

@@ 11.16 | Upacāramulanu |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Tyāgarāja in Rāgam Bhairavi for the royal services: "Accept these services, O Lord who rests on the king of serpents."

"O Lord of Jānakī, wearing cloth bright as countless lightning flashes, be kind to me." Umbrella (chatram): "You hold the strings of the drama of the world, Rāma, giving what is wished for; a golden umbrella studded with countless jewels suits You alone." Whisks (cāmaram): "A pair of whisks, shining like lightning, with handles of pure gold, bright as the autumn moon, suits You alone." Fan (vyajanam): "A fan scented with jasmine, campaka, maruvam, blossoms and kuruvēru, shining splendidly, suits You alone, praised by Tyāgarāja."

Key Takeaway
The royal services, an umbrella, whisks and a fan, honour the Lord as the King of kings. Tyāgarāja says they suit Him alone.
For each of us: honouring the Lord as King reminds us whose court we live in.

Deeper Meaning
1. Royal services (upacāramulu)
After the regular pūjā, the Lord is given the honours of a king. These are the rāja-upacāras.

2. The umbrella (chatram)
The royal umbrella is a sign of kingship and protection. Under His umbrella, the whole world is sheltered.

3. The director of the world-drama (kapaṭa nāṭaka sūtradhāri)
The Lord holds the strings of the whole drama of the world. He is the real King behind every scene.

4. The whisks (cāmaram)
Whisks are waved before kings. Waving them before the Lord is an act of humble service.

5. The scented fan (vyajanam)
A fan fragrant with flowers cools Him. The devotee wants the Lord to be comfortable.

6. Suits You alone (nīkē tagunayya)
Every verse ends: "this suits You alone". No one else deserves such honour.

7. Be kind to me (dayajēsi)
Before the services, Tyāgarāja asks Rāma to be kind. Honour and love go together.

8. Wearing lightning (capalā kōṭi nibhāmbara)
Rāma's garment shines like countless flashes of lightning. The King's splendour is beyond our ornaments, yet He accepts them.

@@ 11.46 | Svajana Hṛdamala Maṅgaḷam (Kṛṣṇa Gānam) |
Overall Summary
A closing maṅgaḷam of praise to Krishna.

His soft lotus feet have bloomed in the clear lake of His devotees' hearts. His walk is majestic, His eyes long with compassion, His face cool like the moon. His broad, lovely forehead shines with gōpī-candana tilaka. Victory to the one who destroys the pride of the Kali age! Victory to the one who gives out Hari's name! Victory to the one whose gentle words do good! He is steady as the golden mountain, generous, an ocean of mercy. Victory to Śrī Mādhurī's beloved!

Key Takeaway
The maṅgaḷam praises the Lord who gives His Name freely in this age. His mercy and gentleness are celebrated at the close.
For each of us: we can carry the Name home with us after the bhajan, as His gift.

Deeper Meaning
1. Feet blooming in devotees' hearts (svajana hṛdamala sarōvara vikasita)
His lotus feet bloom in the lake of His devotees' clear hearts. A pure heart is where He blossoms.

2. Eyes long with compassion (karuṇāyata nayana)
His eyes are wide with mercy. To be seen by Him is to receive compassion.

3. The cool moon-face (kalādhara śītala vadana)
His face is cool like the moon. It calms the heat of worldly life.

4. Destroyer of Kali's pride (kalimada haraṇa)
He destroys the pride of this age. Through the Name, the difficulties of Kali lose their force.

5. Giver of Hari's name (hari nāma vitaraṇa)
He gives Hari's name freely. The Name itself is His gift to this age.

6. Gentle, helpful words (hitakara mṛdu vacana)
His words are soft and do good. The maṅgaḷam ends with gentle praise, as the bhajan closes in peace.

7. Steady as Mēru (hēmaśaila sama dhīra)
He is as steady as the golden mountain. The bhajan ends in His unshakable strength.

@@ 12.7 | Hārati (Citta Juni) |
Overall Summary
A Telugu maṅgaḷam in Rāgam Yadukula Kāmbhōji, sung as the ārati of the Rādhā Kalyāṇam (the same song as 11.8): "Victory and auspiciousness to the one with the charming eyes, who steals the heart; to the one who becomes the hearts' best treasure through devotion that removes pride."

May all be auspicious to Gōvinda, Nanda's son, the delight of Indirā (Lakṣmī), whose lotus face outshines the lovely moon. To the form of our Paṭṭābhi Rāma Svāmi, the home of every good quality, who was Bālarāma and Rāma, giving welfare. To the brave Lord Kastūri Raṅga, full of splendour, who wears the garland, who is reached by those who come longing with deep devotion.

Key Takeaway
A maṅgaḷam wishes the Lord auspiciousness at the end of worship. Krishna, Rāma and Raṅganātha are all blessed together.
For each of us: ending prayer with a blessing for the Lord lets us leave in a happy, grateful mood.

Deeper Meaning
1. A maṅgaḷam (jaya maṅgaḷam)
"Maṅgaḷam" means auspiciousness. It is sung at the end of worship, wishing all good to the Lord.

2. The heart-stealer (citta cōra)
He steals our hearts with His charm. Losing our heart to Him is the best kind of loss.

3. Removing pride (mada harti bhakti)
Devotion takes away pride. A humble heart is where the Lord settles.

4. Krishna's face (candruni nindincu mukhāravinda)
His face is lovelier than the moon. Even the moon seems plain beside Him.

5. Paṭṭābhi Rāma (paṭṭābhi rāma)
Rāma crowned on His throne is the home of all good qualities. The maṅgaḷam honours Him in His glory.

6. Kastūri Raṅga (kastūri raṅga)
Raṅganātha, scented with musk, is reached by deep devotion. The song ends at His feet.

7. Ending in blessing
A maṅgaḷam closes the worship on a note of joy. We do not leave the Lord with requests, but with good wishes, as we would leave a dear one's home.

8. One song, two places
The book prints this song twice, at 11.8 in the pūjā and here in the Rādhā Kalyāṇam. The same blessing ends both the pūjā and the divine wedding.

@@ 14.5 | Brōcēvārevarurā |
Overall Summary
A Telugu song by Śrī Mysore Vāsudēvācārya (as the book notes) in Rāgam Khamās: "Who is there to protect me, other than You, best of the Raghus?"

"I cannot let go of Your lotus feet, abode of mercy. O clever one, bowed to by Brahmā and the first gods, why this delay with me? I cannot praise Your story enough; end my worry, give me boons, and quickly protect me. Lord of Sītā, do You not care for me? You whose feet are worshipped by Hanumān, can You not hear my cries? Are You not Vāsudēva, who hurried to protect the king of elephants? Drive away all my sins, hold my hand firmly and do not let go."

Key Takeaway
There is no protector but Rāma. The devotee holds His feet and asks Him to hold his hand and never let go.
For each of us: when we feel alone, we can say: "Who else will protect me? Hold my hand and don't let go."

Deeper Meaning
1. Who else will protect me? (brōcēvārevarurā)
The question is its own answer: no one but Rāma.

2. I cannot let go of Your feet (viḍajāla)
The devotee holds on to the Lord's feet. He cannot let go.

3. Why delay? (parākēlanayya)
He asks lovingly why the Lord delays. He trusts that help will come.

4. Do You not care? (abhimānamu lēdā)
He asks Sītā's Lord if He has no affection for him. Only one who is sure of love dares to ask.

5. The elephant's rescuer (karirājunu brōcina)
He reminds the Lord of how He saved the king of elephants. His past mercy is our hope.

6. Hold my hand (nā cēyi paṭṭi viḍuvaka)
He asks Rāma to hold his hand firmly and not let go. That is all he needs.

7. Drive away my sins (pātakamella pōgoṭṭi)
He asks for cleansing too. Protection and purification go together.

8. Worshipped by Hanumān (vātātmaja arcita pāda)
Hanumān worships His feet. The devotee joins the greatest of devotees.

@@ 14.6 | Gōvinda Kṛṣṇa Harē – Calaṅgai Kaṭṭi |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song: "Gōvinda, Krishna, Hari; Gōpāla, Krishna, Hari!"

"Tie on Your anklets and come running, running; O lotus-eyed Kaṇṇā, come dancing, dancing. Searching for Your tender little feet, we have come singing, singing Your divine name." He is Dēvakī's son, Rādhā's life, Kēśava, Hari, Mādhava, slayer of Pūtanā, destroyer of sin. Come running, child of Gōkula; come dancing, child Gōpāla. Crusher of Kaṁsa, dancer on Kāḷiya, loving to those who depend on You, friend in danger. Come running, sound of Om; come dancing, song of bliss. Protector of the Pāṇḍavas and of Arjuna, destroyer of ignorance. Come running, nectar of the Gītā; come dancing, joy of the heart.

Key Takeaway
Devotees call Krishna to come running and dancing, wearing His anklets. We search for Him by singing His name.
For each of us: in the bhajan, we are the ones searching for His little feet, singing His name.

Deeper Meaning
1. Anklets and running (calaṅgai kaṭṭi ōḍi ōḍi)
The child Krishna is asked to tie on His anklets and come running. The sound of His bells brings joy.

2. Searching by singing (tēḍi tēḍi … pāḍi pāḍi)
The devotees search for His feet by singing His name. The bhajan is the search.

3. Rādhā's life (rādhā jīvana)
He is Rādhā's very life. Their love shows what devotion can be.

4. Friend in danger (āpad bāndhava)
He is the friend who comes when there is trouble.

5. Sound of Om (ōṅkāra nādamē)
The little dancing child is Om itself. The playful and the eternal are one.

6. The nectar of the Gītā (gītāmṛtamē)
He is the teacher of the Gītā. The child grows into the guide of Arjuna.

7. Joy of the heart (hṛdayānandamē)
The song ends by calling Him the joy of the heart. When He comes, the heart dances too.

@@ 14.7 | Kurai Onrum Illai |
Overall Summary
A Tamil song by Śrī C. Rājagōpālācāri (as the book notes) in Rāgamālikā: "I have no grievance at all, O Kaṇṇā, the form of the Vedas; no grievance at all, Gōvinda."

"You stand unseen by my eyes, Kaṇṇā; even though You stand unseen, I have no grievance. When You, Vēṅkaṭēśa, are there to give what I need, there is nothing else I need. Maṇivaṇṇā, Lord of the hill, Gōvinda! You stand behind the curtain, Kaṇṇā; only wise men who recite the Vedas see You; even so, I have no grievance. You stand as stone on the hill, giver of boons. Taking pity on this Kali age, You came down into stone and stand firmly in the temple, Kēśava. Lord of the hill who refuses nothing, with the Mother, the ocean of compassion, ready to give anything, on Your chest, always there: what grievance could I have?"

Key Takeaway
Even without seeing God, the devotee is content. With Vēṅkaṭēśa and Lakṣmī there to give everything, he lacks nothing.
For each of us: gratitude, "I have no grievance", is one of the deepest prayers. It sees that we already have what matters.

Deeper Meaning
1. No grievance at all (kurai onrum illai)
The song's title says it all. The devotee is content.

2. Unseen, yet enough (kaṇṇukku teriyāmal)
He cannot see the Lord with his eyes. Still, he has no complaint.

3. He gives what I need (vēṇḍiyadai tandiḍa)
Vēṅkaṭēśa is there to give what is needed. So there is no worry.

4. Behind the curtain (tiraiyin pin)
In the temple, the curtain hides the deity at times. Only the wise see Him clearly; the devotee trusts anyway.

5. Come down into stone (kallilē iraṅgi)
Out of pity for this age, the Lord came down and became a stone image in the temple. His presence is real and steady.

6. The Mother on His chest (un mārbil … karuṇai kaḍal annai)
Lakṣmī lives on Vēṅkaṭēśa's chest, ready to give anything. With her there, what could be lacking?

7. Many rāgas (rāgamālikā)
The song moves through many rāgas, like a garland. Each mood ends in the same contentment.

@@ 17.4 | Dīnan Dukh Haran Dēv |
Overall Summary
A Hindi song by Sant Sūrdās (as the book notes) in Rāgam Dēś: "O God, remover of the sorrow of the humble, doer of good to the saints."

"Ajāmiḷa, the vulture (Jaṭāyu), the hunter: tell me, which of them was a saint? You saved even the courtesan who taught her parrot to say Your name. When the crocodile seized the elephant, and when Duḥśāsana pulled Draupadī's sari, she cried 'Krishna, Krishna!' in the middle of the court. At that, Hari came, and took the form of cloth. Sūrdās stands at Your door, a blind beggar."

Key Takeaway
God saves even those who seem unworthy, the moment they call Him. Sūrdās stands at His door, asking the same mercy.
For each of us: no one is too far gone to be saved. A true call to God is always answered.

Deeper Meaning
1. Remover of the humble's sorrow (dīnan dukh haran)
He removes the pain of those who are low and helpless.

2. Which of them was a saint? (inmēṁ kahō kaun sādh)
Sūrdās asks which of these people was holy. None were, yet the Lord saved them.

3. Ajāmiḷa (ajāmīl)
Ajāmiḷa lived a bad life, but at death he called his son Nārāyaṇa, and the Lord's messengers came to save him. Even the Name said by chance has power.

4. The courtesan and the parrot (gaṇikā)
She taught her parrot to say the Lord's name and was saved. Teaching the Name, even playfully, brings grace.

5. Draupadī's cry (draupadī pukārī)
In the court, Draupadī cried "Krishna!", and He became her endless sari. Total surrender is answered at once.

6. Becoming cloth (basanan ārūḍh bhayē)
Hari "took the form of cloth" to protect her. He becomes whatever His devotee needs.

7. A blind beggar at His door (āndharō bhikārī)
Sūrdās was blind. He calls himself a beggar at God's door, asking for the same mercy.

@@ 18.1 | Ācārya Paramparā Taniyan |
Overall Summary
Sanskrit verses (taniyans) honouring the line of Śrī Vaiṣṇava teachers.

"We take refuge in Śrī Raṅganātha Yati, crown of ascetics, who received the essence of the Upaniṣads through the glance of Vēdānta Dēśika Yatīndra, of spotless qualities, foremost of the wise, bathed in the grace of Nārāyaṇa Yati." Salutations to Śrī Nārāyaṇa Yatīndra Mahādēśika and Śrī Raṅganātha Yatīndra Mahādēśika of Śrīvaṇ Śaṭhakōpa. "I bow to Śrīmat Vēṅkaṭanātha, Vēdānta Dēśika, the vessel of Rāmānuja's grace, adorned with knowledge and detachment. I bow to the line of Gurus beginning with Lakṣmī's Lord, with Nāthamuni and Yāmuna in the middle, and reaching down to my own teacher. I take refuge at the feet of Rāmānuja, our Guru, the one ocean of compassion, who, in his love for the golden pair of Acyuta's lotus feet, counted everything else as straw."

Key Takeaway
Before singing, we bow to the whole line of teachers through whom the Lord's teaching reached us, from Lakṣmī's Lord down to our own Guru.
For each of us: whatever we know of God came through someone. Remembering them with gratitude is part of devotion.

Deeper Meaning
1. Taniyan
A taniyan is a short verse of praise for an Ācārya, recited before the teachings or songs. It honours the teacher before the teaching.

2. The line of Gurus (guru paramparā)
The teaching starts with Lakṣmī's Lord Himself, passes through Nāthamuni and Yāmuna, and comes down to "my own teacher". Nothing true comes to us out of nowhere.

3. The grace of the glance (kaṭākṣa labdha)
The Upaniṣads' essence was received through the teacher's glance. A Guru's grace can transmit what books cannot.

4. Vēdānta Dēśika (śrīmat vēṅkaṭanāthārya)
Vēdānta Dēśika is praised as the vessel of Rāmānuja's grace, adorned with knowledge and detachment. Learning and humility go together.

5. Rāmānuja's single love (acyuta padāmbuja)
Rāmānuja loved the Lord's feet so much that everything else seemed like straw. That one-pointed love is the model for us.

6. Taking refuge (śaraṇaṁ prapadyē)
The last verse ends in surrender at Rāmānuja's feet. Taking refuge in the Guru is taking refuge in the Lord.

7. One tradition
This bhajan honours the Śrī Vaiṣṇava Ācāryas along with its own Gurus. The Name tradition holds all who lead to the Lord.

@@ 18.2 | Nammāzhvār Taniyan |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit verse composed by Āḷavandār (Yāmunācārya) in praise of Nammāzhvār: "Mother, father, wife, children, wealth, everything for those of my family is, by rule, only this: the beautiful pair of feet, adorned with vakuḷa flowers, of the first leader of our clan. I bow my head to them."

Key Takeaway
For the devotees of this line, Nammāzhvār's feet are everything: family, wealth and support.
For each of us: when we treat a great devotee's feet as our treasure, we learn humility and gratitude.

Deeper Meaning
1. Āḷavandār's praise (āḷavandār aruḷicceydadu)
The verse was composed by Āḷavandār, one of the great Ācāryas before Rāmānuja. The teachers praise their own teachers.

2. Nammāzhvār, the first leader (ādyasya naḥ kulapatēḥ)
Nammāzhvār is called the first leader of the Śrī Vaiṣṇava family. His Tiruvāymozhi is the heart of the Tamil Vēda.

3. Everything is in his feet (sarvaṁ yad ēva)
Mother, father, wife, children and wealth are all summed up in his feet. Everything we value finds its place in devotion.

4. Vakuḷa flowers (vakuḷābhirāmam)
Nammāzhvār is pictured wearing a garland of vakuḷa flowers. The detail makes the image tender and real.

5. By rule (niyamēna)
This is not just a feeling; it is the rule of the family. Devotion to the Āzhvār is the tradition's foundation.

6. Bowing the head (praṇamāmi mūrdhnā)
The verse ends with a bow of the head. The highest part of us is placed at the feet of the devotee.

7. Devotees as family
The verse calls the devotees "my family". Those who share love for the Lord become one family.

@@ 18.3 | Āzhvārgaḷ – Udaiyavar Taniyan |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit verse composed by Śrī Parāśara Bhaṭṭar, bowing to all the Āzhvārs and Udaiyavar (Rāmānuja): "I always bow to Bhūtam, Saras, Mahat, Bhaṭṭanātha, Śrī Bhaktisāra, Kulaśēkhara, Yōgivāha, Bhaktāṅghrirēṇu, Parakāla and Yatīndra Miśra (Rāmānuja), and to the sage Parāṅkuśa (Nammāzhvār)."

Key Takeaway
All the Āzhvārs, the Tamil saint-poets who sang the Lord's praise, and Rāmānuja are honoured in one verse.
For each of us: the great devotees of every age are our guides. Remembering their names keeps their spirit alive in us.

Deeper Meaning
1. The Āzhvārs
The Āzhvārs are the twelve Tamil saints whose songs make up the Nālāyira Divya Prabandham. They are "immersed" in love of the Lord.

2. Each by name
Bhūtattāzhvār, Poygai (Saras), Pēy (Mahat), Periyāzhvār (Bhaṭṭanātha), Tirumazhisai (Bhaktisāra), Kulaśēkhara, Tiruppāṇ (Yōgivāha), Toṇḍaraḍippoḍi (Bhaktāṅghrirēṇu) and Tirumaṅgai (Parakāla) are named one by one.

3. Rāmānuja among them (yatīndra miśrān)
Rāmānuja is counted with the Āzhvārs. He spread their teaching far and wide.

4. Nammāzhvār last and first (parāṅkuśa munim)
The verse ends with Nammāzhvār, the chief of the Āzhvārs. He is honoured as the soul of the tradition.

5. Bowing always (praṇatōsmi nityam)
"I always bow". Reverence for the saints is a daily practice, not a one-time act.

6. Bhaṭṭar's verse
Parāśara Bhaṭṭar, a great teacher after Rāmānuja, composed this. He shows how to honour those who came before.

7. A tradition that sings
The Āzhvārs sang, and so does this bhajan. Their names belong naturally at the start of the Divya Prabandham songs later in the book.

@@ 18.4 | Muraḷīdhara Svāmigaḷ Taniyan |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit verse in praise of Śrī Muraḷīdhara Svāmigaḷ: "I meditate on the Sadguru Muraḷīdhara, the God of beautiful form, always lovely to see, the destroyer of ignorance."

Key Takeaway
The Sadguru is beautiful to see and removes our ignorance. Meditating on him is the beginning of wisdom.
For each of us: the Guru's form, held in mind, can calm us and remind us of the path.

Deeper Meaning
1. Meditating on the Guru (kalayē)
"Kalayē" means "I meditate on, I hold in mind". The verse is a short meditation.

2. Beautiful form (sundarākāram)
The Guru's form is beautiful because it shines with devotion and peace.

3. Always lovely to see (sadā ika priya darśanam)
Seeing him is always a joy. A true Guru's presence uplifts.

4. Destroyer of ignorance (ajñāna nāśakam)
This is the Guru's main work: to remove the darkness of ignorance. "Guru" itself is said to mean "remover of darkness".

5. God and Guru (dēvam sadgurum)
He is called God and Sadguru. In this tradition, the Guru is honoured as the Lord's own presence.

6. Muraḷīdhara
His name means "holder of the flute", a name of Krishna. The Guru carries the Lord's name and spreads it.

7. A living Guru
Śrī Muraḷīdhara Svāmigaḷ is a living teacher of the Name in our own time. The taniyan brings the old tradition into the present.

@@ 18.5 | Kṛṣṇa Prēmi Svāmigaḷ Taniyan |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit verse in praise of Śrī Kṛṣṇa Prēmi Svāmigaḷ: "I worship the loving Sadguru who always does nāma saṅkīrtana, saying 'Hari's name, Krishna', for the welfare of the world."

It is followed by the closing calls of victory: Remember Jānakī's beloved: victory, Rāma Rāma! The name of Hara, Lord of Pārvatī: Hara Hara Mahādēva! Remember the life of the gōpīs: Gōvinda Gōvinda! Victory to Sadgurunātha Mahārāj! Victory to the whole assembly of saints! Say: Hari Viṭhala, giver of boons to Puṇḍalīka! Victory to Gaṇēśa and all the gods of Hari and Hara! Victory to Āñjanēya! Everywhere, Gōvinda nāma saṅkīrtanam: Gōvinda Gōvinda!

Key Takeaway
The Guru sings the Name for the good of the whole world. The calls of victory that follow send that Name in every direction.
For each of us: singing the Name is not only for ourselves. It is a gift to the world.

Deeper Meaning
1. Always singing (nāma saṅkīrtanam sadā)
The Guru sings the Name always. His life is the Name.

2. For the welfare of the world (lōka kṣēmāya)
He sings not for himself but for the good of all. Saṅkīrtana is a service to the world.

3. Prēmika, the loving one (prēmikaṁ sadgurum)
His name, Kṛṣṇa Prēmi, means "lover of Krishna". He is called "prēmika", full of love.

4. The calls of victory
The book places the closing jays here: to Rāma, Śiva, Gōvinda, the Sadguru, the saints, Viṭhala, Gaṇēśa and Hanumān. They echo the Puṇḍarīkam that opens the bhajan (1.1).

5. Puṇḍalīka's Lord (puṇḍalīka varadē)
Viṭhala blessed his devotee Puṇḍalīka by waiting on a brick. The call remembers that loving story.

6. Hari and Hara together (hari hara dēvakulaku)
Victory is called to all the gods, of Viṣṇu and of Śiva. The bhajan excludes no one.

7. Everywhere, the Name (sarvatra gōvinda nāma saṅkīrtanam)
The last call: let Gōvinda's name be sung everywhere. That is the Guru's wish and the bhajan's purpose.

@@ 19.1 | Harē Rāma |
Overall Summary
The great mantra, sung five times: "Harē Rāma Harē Rāma, Rāma Rāma Harē Harē; Harē Krishna Harē Krishna, Krishna Krishna Harē Harē."

Key Takeaway
This sixteen-name mantra is one of the simplest and most powerful ways to call the Lord. Anyone can sing it.
For each of us: if we remember only one thing from the bhajan, this mantra is enough.

Deeper Meaning
1. The great mantra (mahāmantra)
These sixteen names are known as the mahāmantra. The tradition teaches they are especially powerful in this age.

2. Hari, Rāma, Krishna
Only three names are used: Hari, Rāma and Krishna. Their repetition makes the mind calm and focused.

3. Harē as a call
"Harē" can be heard as a call to Hari, or to Harā, the Lord's own power of love. The mantra calls both.

4. Rāma: the one who delights
"Rāma" means one who delights, and the one in whom yogis delight. It is the name of joy.

5. Krishna: the one who attracts
"Krishna" means the one who draws all hearts. Saying His name draws the heart to Him.

6. Five times
The book asks for it to be sung five times. Repetition lets the mantra settle in the heart.

7. An invocation
It is sung as an invocation, at the start. It sets the mood for everything that follows.

@@ 19.2 | Kṛṣṇa Rāma Gōvinda Nārāyaṇa |
Overall Summary
A nāmāvaḷi on "Hari Nārāyaṇa": Krishna Rāma Gōvinda Nārāyaṇa; flute-holding Gōvinda, Hari Nārāyaṇa. Yaśōdā's child, Hari Nārāyaṇa (three times); jewel of the Yadu clan. Eternal festival (three times); eternal bliss. Then in fast tempo: lover of the flute; Vijaya Gōpāla; loving to devotees; dear to the devoted; supreme bliss (three times); who lives in Bhadrādri. It ends: Nārāyaṇa, Nārāyaṇa, Nārāyaṇa.

Key Takeaway
Each name ends with "Hari Nārāyaṇa". The nāmāvaḷi starts slow and speeds up, carrying the group into joy.
For each of us: when the tempo rises in bhajan, we can let ourselves be carried by the joy of the Name.

Deeper Meaning
1. Hari Nārāyaṇa
Every line ends with this refrain. It is the anchor of the chant.

2. Yaśōdā's child (yaśōda bāla)
Krishna is called as Yaśōdā's little one. The chant begins with tenderness.

3. Eternal festival (nitya mahōtsava)
His life is a never-ending festival. The bhajan joins it.

4. Eternal bliss (nityānanda)
He is bliss that never fades. The chant gives a taste of it.

5. Fast tempo
The book marks a faster tempo. Bhajan uses rhythm to lift the heart.

6. Vijaya Gōpāla
The name dear to this tradition appears again, the victorious cowherd.

7. Lord of Bhadrādri (bhadrādri vāsa)
The chant ends at Bhadrācala, with Rāma. Krishna, Rāma and Nārāyaṇa are one.

8. Loving to devotees, dear to the devoted (bhakta vatsala, bhāgavata priya)
These two names say the same thing from both sides: He loves those who love Him, and He is dear to those who are devoted. Love flows both ways.

9. Joining in
A nāmāvaḷi is led by one voice and answered by all. Even someone who knows nothing about music or scripture can take full part.

@@ 19.3 | Nārāyaṇa Nārāyaṇa Jai Jai Gōvinda Harē |
Overall Summary
A short chant: "Nārāyaṇa, Nārāyaṇa, victory, victory, Gōvinda Hari; Om. Nārāyaṇa, Nārāyaṇa, victory, victory, Gōpāla Hari" (three times). "Gōvinda Hari, victory, victory, Gōpāla Hari."

Key Takeaway
A simple chant of victory to Nārāyaṇa, Gōvinda and Gōpāla. It is easy to sing together.
For each of us: a few words, repeated with feeling, can fill a room with peace.

Deeper Meaning
1. Nārāyaṇa
Nārāyaṇa is the Lord who lives in all beings and in whom all beings live.

2. Victory (jai jai)
"Jai" means victory. We celebrate the Lord's victory over all that is wrong.

3. Gōvinda and Gōpāla
Both mean the protector of cows and of the world. The cowherd is the Lord of all.

4. Om
The chant includes Om, the sacred syllable. It brings the whole Veda into the simple chant.

5. Three times
The second line is sung three times. Repetition deepens the feeling.

6. Nārada's chant
"Nārāyaṇa, Nārāyaṇa" is the chant of the sage Nārada. Singing it, we join him.

7. An invocation
Like 19.1 and 19.2, this chant prepares the gathering for the songs that follow.

8. Gōvinda Harē, Gōpāla Harē
The two names are almost the same, yet the chant keeps both. Each repetition is a fresh call, not a copy of the last.

9. A short chant with a large heart
These few words hold the whole spirit of the bhajan: praise, joy and the Lord's name. That is why they are sung at the start, before the longer songs.

@@ 20.1 | Śēṣa Girīśa Jaya Śrīnivāsa |
Overall Summary
A bhajan to Lord Vēṅkaṭēśvara in Rāgam Śubhapantuvarāḷi: "Lord of the Śēṣa hill, victory, Śrīnivāsa; Lord of Śēṣācala, who lives in Tirumala; beloved of Lakṣmī, form of compassion; Lord of the seven hills, victory, Śrīnivāsa." It continues with "Gōvinda Hari Gōvinda" and "Jaya Śrīnivāsa Gōvinda, Rāma Ramaṇa Hari Gōvinda", repeated.

Key Takeaway
Vēṅkaṭēśvara of the seven hills is Lakṣmī's beloved and the form of compassion. The bhajan calls His names again and again.
For each of us: we can call Him "Gōvinda" whenever we climb the hills of our own life.

Deeper Meaning
1. Lord of the Śēṣa hill (śēṣa girīśa)
The hills of Tirumala are said to be Ādiśēṣa, the serpent on whom Viṣṇu rests. The Lord rests on them for us.

2. Śrīnivāsa
"Śrīnivāsa" means the one in whom Lakṣmī (Śrī) lives. She lives on His chest.

3. Form of compassion (karuṇā mūrti)
He is compassion in bodily form. Devotees come to Tirumala to receive it.

4. Lord of the seven hills (sapta girīśvara)
The seven hills of Tirumala are His home. Pilgrims climb them on foot, chanting "Gōvinda".

5. Gōvinda Hari Gōvinda
"Gōvinda" is the chant of Tirumala pilgrims. The bhajan brings that chant here.

6. Rāma's beloved (rāma ramaṇa)
"Ramā" is Lakṣmī; He is Ramā's beloved. The chant joins Vēṅkaṭēśa and Lakṣmī.

7. Repetition as prayer
The chant repeats many times. The more we sing, the more the Name settles in us.

8. Pilgrims' feet
Many devotees climb the steps to Tirumala on foot, chanting "Gōvinda" all the way. Singing this bhajan, we share their effort and their joy.

@@ 20.2 | Paccai Māmalai Pōl Mēni |
Overall Summary
Tamil verses in Rāgam Bauḷi from the Divya Prabandham, sung to Raṅganātha of Śrīraṅgam (traditionally from Toṇḍaraḍippoḍi Āzhvār's Tirumālai).

"Your body is like a great green mountain, Your mouth like coral, Your eyes red lotuses. O Acyuta, chief of the gods, darling of the cowherds: except for the sweetness of saying this, even if I got the taste of ruling Indra's world, I would not want it, O Lord of Śrīraṅgam. I have no village, no land, no relatives; I have no hold on this earth but Your feet, Supreme One. O dark-cloud-coloured one, my Kaṇṇā, I cry out to You; who is there to save me but You, O Lord of Śrīraṅgam? There is no purity in my mind, no sweet word in my mouth; in anger I look at people with hatred and shout harsh words. O You who wear the tulasi garland of the fields, Lord of Tiruvaraṅgam surrounded by the Ponni (Kāvēri): tell me, what is my way now, O my King who owns me?"

Key Takeaway
The Āzhvār wants nothing but to say the Lord's names. He honestly admits he has nothing and no one but the Lord.
For each of us: we can come to God as we are, faults and all, and ask: "What is my way now?"

Deeper Meaning
1. The green mountain (paccai māmalai)
The Lord's body is like a great green mountain, dark and lush. It is a picture of strength and life.

2. Better than Indra's kingdom (indira lōkam āḷum)
The Āzhvār would not trade the sweetness of saying the Lord's names for the rule of heaven.

3. No village, no land (ūrilēn kāṇi illai)
He has no home, no property and no relatives. The Lord's feet are his only hold.

4. I cry out (katharuginrēn)
He cries out to his Kaṇṇā. The cry is honest and urgent.

5. No purity in my mind (manattil ōr tūymai illai)
He confesses his faults: no purity, no sweet words, anger and harsh speech. He does not hide anything.

6. What is my way? (enakkinik gati en)
After confessing, he asks the Lord to show him the way. Surrender means letting the Lord decide.

7. The King who owns me (ennai āḷudaiya kō)
He calls the Lord "my King who owns me". Belonging to the Lord is his only hope.

@@ 21.1 | Śrī Rāma |
Overall Summary
A simple chant: "Śrī Rāma, Jaya Rāma, Jaya Jaya Rāma", repeated.

Key Takeaway
"Śrī Rāma Jaya Rāma Jaya Jaya Rāma" is the thirteen-syllable Rāma mantra. Saying it is enough.
For each of us: this short chant can be said at any time, walking, working or resting.

Deeper Meaning
1. The Rāma mantra
"Śrī Rāma Jaya Rāma Jaya Jaya Rāma" is a well-loved mantra, made famous by saints like Samarth Rāmdās. It has thirteen syllables.

2. Śrī Rāma
"Śrī" honours Rāma with Lakṣmī's auspiciousness. It is also Sītā's name in her divine form.

3. Jaya Rāma
"Victory to Rāma". The chant celebrates His victory over evil.

4. Jaya Jaya Rāma
The victory is repeated, to fill the heart with confidence.

5. Repetition
The book prints the line four times. Bhajan repeats so that the mind can rest in the Name.

6. Simplicity
There is nothing to learn, no tune to master. Anyone can join in.

7. Opening the Rāma chapter
This chant opens the chapter of Rāma bhajans. It sets the tone for all that follows.

8. Samarth Rāmdās and the mantra
Samarth Rāmdās chanted this mantra crores of times and taught it to all. It became the chant of Hanumān temples across Maharashtra, simple enough for every home.

@@ 21.2 | Daśaratha Nandana Rāma Rām |
Overall Summary
A Rāma bhajan in Rāgam Dēś: "Daśaratha's son, Rāma Rām; ocean of mercy, Rāma Rām; delight of Paśupati (Śiva), Rāma Rām; remover of sin, Rāma Rām. Served by Lakṣmaṇa, Rāma Rām; enchanter of Lakṣmī, Rāma Rām; subtle in form, Rāma Rām; beautiful-faced, Rāma Rām."

Key Takeaway
Each name of Rāma is followed by "Rāma Rām". The bhajan keeps returning to the Name.
For each of us: we can add "Rāma Rām" to any name or thought, and let it turn our mind to Him.

Deeper Meaning
1. Daśaratha's son (daśaratha nandana)
Rāma is the beloved son of King Daśaratha. He is the ideal son.

2. Ocean of mercy (dayā sāgara)
His mercy is as vast as the ocean.

3. Delight of Śiva (paśupati rañjana)
Śiva loves Rāma's name. Rāma and Śiva are close.

4. Remover of sin (pāpa vimōcana)
Rāma frees us from our faults.

5. Served by Lakṣmaṇa (lakṣmaṇa sēvita)
Lakṣmaṇa served Rāma faithfully. He is the model of loving service.

6. Subtle in form (sūkṣma svarūpa)
Rāma is beyond the senses, subtle and inner. Yet He is beautiful to see.

7. Rāma Rām
The refrain "Rāma Rām" is repeated after every name. The Name itself is the centre of the song.

8. Enchanter of Lakṣmī (lakṣmī manōhara)
Rāma charms Lakṣmī, who is Sītā. The Lord and His consort delight in each other.

9. Beautiful face (sundara vadana)
His face is lovely. The bhajan ends with a picture of His face, so that the mind can rest on it.

10. A gentle Dēś melody
Rāgam Dēś is soft and devotional, often sung at the close of evening bhajans. The melody suits the gentle names.

@@ 21.3 | Jagadāśraya Śrī Raghu Rāma |
Overall Summary
A Rāma bhajan in Rāgam Āhir Bhairavi: "Refuge of the world, Śrī Raghu Rāma; uplifter of the world, Sītā Rāma; supremely holy Jānakī Rāma; a hundred crore salutations to Śrī Rāma; Your feet, Your feet, Ātma Rāma."

Key Takeaway
Rāma is the refuge of the whole world. We offer Him countless salutations and take refuge at His feet.
For each of us: "Caraṇam caraṇam" (Your feet, Your feet) is a complete prayer of surrender.

Deeper Meaning
1. Refuge of the world (jagad āśraya)
Rāma is the shelter of the whole world. Everyone may come to Him.

2. Uplifter of the world (jagad uddhāra)
He lifts the world up from sorrow.

3. Supremely holy (parama pāvana)
He is the holiest of holy. Being near Him purifies.

4. Jānakī's Rāma
He is Sītā's beloved. Rāma is always remembered with Sītā.

5. A hundred crore salutations (śata kōṭi praṇāma)
The devotee offers countless salutations. Love wants to bow again and again.

6. Your feet (caraṇam caraṇam)
Repeating "caraṇam" means "I take refuge at Your feet". It is surrender in two words.

7. Ātma Rāma
He is Rāma within, the Self. Taking refuge at His feet is turning inward.

8. Uplifter of the world
He does not just watch; He lifts. The same hand that shelters also raises up those who have fallen.

9. Āhir Bhairavi
The rāga is tender and early-morning in mood, suited to a song of surrender at the Lord's feet.

@@ 21.4 | Śrī Rāma Rāma |
Overall Summary
A Rāma bhajan in Rāgam Yaman Kalyāṇi on the many forms of Rāma: "Śrī Rāma Rāma Rāma Rāma Rām; Śṛṅgāra (charming) Rāma; Kōdaṇḍa (bow-holding) Rāma; Paṭṭābhi (crowned) Rāma; Ayōdhyā Rāma; Kausalyā's Rāma; Kāruṇya (compassionate) Rāma; Kalyāṇa (bridegroom) Rāma."

Key Takeaway
Each name shows a different side of Rāma: charming, brave, crowned, compassionate. All are the same Rāma.
