For each of us: we can choose the Rāma we need today: the brave one, the kind one, the bridegroom.

Deeper Meaning
1. Śṛṅgāra Rāma
The charming, beautiful Rāma. His beauty draws the heart.

2. Kōdaṇḍa Rāma
Rāma holding His great bow Kōdaṇḍa. He is ready to protect.

3. Paṭṭābhi Rāma
Rāma crowned king. He rules with justice and love.

4. Ayōdhyā Rāma
Rāma of His home city. He belongs to His people.

5. Kausalyā's Rāma
His mother's darling. Even the Lord is someone's child.

6. Kāruṇya Rāma
The compassionate Rāma. His heart melts for those who suffer.

7. Kalyāṇa Rāma
Rāma the bridegroom of Sītā. The bhajan ends in His wedding joy.

8. Many Rāmas, one heart
Each name is a different moment in Rāma's life: His charm, His strength, His crown, His home, His mother, His mercy and His wedding. The bhajan walks through His whole life in a few words.

9. Repetition and rhythm
"Rāma Rāma Rāma Rām" is sung after each name. The rhythm makes it easy for a group to sing together and lets the Name fill the hall.

10. Yaman Kalyāṇi
The rāga is bright and devotional. It suits a bhajan full of joy in Rāma's forms.

@@ 21.5 | Ātmēya Rāma |
Overall Summary
A Rāma bhajan in Rāgam Śaṅkarābharaṇam: "Rāma of the Self, Rāma beyond the senses; dense cloud of bliss, fulfiller of every wish. Victory, victory, Rāma, Śrī Jaya Rāma."

He is beautiful Rāma, Rāma of truth and consciousness, captivating, lovely Rāma. Rāma who pervades and Rāma who is pervaded; Rāma, Rāma, the saving Rāma. Easy to reach and hard to reach; Rāma who enchants the mind through yoga. Rāma before the beginning and without beginning; Rāma, the resting place of Ēka Janārdana.

Key Takeaway
Rāma is both the Self within and the Lord beyond. He is easy to reach for the loving and hard to reach for the proud.
For each of us: we can look for Rāma not only in the temple but within, as our own Self.

Deeper Meaning
1. Rāma of the Self (ātmēya rāma)
Rāma is the Self, the innermost being. To know Him is to know ourselves.

2. Beyond the senses (agōcara)
He cannot be grasped by the senses. He is known by love and grace.

3. A cloud of bliss (ānanda ghana)
He is solid bliss, like a dense rain cloud. Being near Him brings joy.

4. Pervading and pervaded (vyāpya … vyāpaka)
He fills everything, and everything is in Him. There is nowhere He is not.

5. Easy and hard to reach (sulabha … durlabha)
He is easy for the humble, hard for the proud. The same Lord, depending on the heart.

6. Before the beginning (ādi … anādi)
He is the beginning, and beyond any beginning. Time does not touch Him.

7. Ēka Janārdana (ēka janārdani viśrāma)
The song names Ēka Janārdana, the signature of Sant Ēknāth. For him, Rāma was the place of rest.

@@ 21.6 | Rāmacandra Raghuvīra |
Overall Summary
A Rāma bhajan in Rāgam Ṣaṇmukhapriya, building names in pairs: "Rāmacandra, hero of the Raghus; Rāmacandra, steady in battle. Rāmacandra, my kinsman; Rāmacandra, ocean of mercy. Rāmacandra, Raghurāma; Rāmacandra, supreme abode. Rāmacandra, Lord of the Raghus; Rāmacandra, Lord of the world." It ends by joining them: hero, steady in battle, my kinsman, ocean of mercy, Raghurāma, supreme abode, Lord of the Raghus, Lord of the world.

Key Takeaway
Rāma is a hero, a kinsman and the Lord of the world, all at once. The bhajan builds these names step by step.
For each of us: Rāma is not only a great king; He is "my own kin". We can feel close to Him.

Deeper Meaning
1. Hero of the Raghus (raghuvīra)
Rāma is the great hero of His dynasty. He fights for dharma.

2. Steady in battle (raṇadhīra)
He is calm and unshaken in battle. His steadiness gives us courage.

3. My kinsman (mama bandhō)
He is "my relative", my own. The great King is part of our family.

4. Ocean of mercy (dayā sindhō)
His mercy is boundless. We can always return to Him.

5. Supreme abode (parandhāma)
He is the highest home, the final resting place of the soul.

6. Lord of the world (jagannātha)
He rules the whole world. Yet He is our kinsman.

7. Building up the names
The bhajan adds one name at a time and then joins them all. It is like climbing a staircase to Him.

8. Ṣaṇmukhapriya
The rāga is strong and stirring, well suited to a song about the hero of the Raghus.

9. Kinsman and King together
The song keeps putting "my kinsman" next to "Lord of the world". The Lord of all is also our own.

@@ 21.7 | Raghu Nandana Hē Raghu Nandana |
Overall Summary
A Hindi Rāma bhajan: "Raghunandana, O Raghunandana; my dear Nandana, Raghunandana." Lotus-eyed, Raghunandana; son of Daśaratha; crusher of the ten-headed one; life of Jānakī, Raghunandana.

Key Takeaway
Rāma is called "my dear one", with the tenderness of a parent or close friend.
For each of us: we can speak to Rāma as to someone very dear, not only as to a king.

Deeper Meaning
1. Joy of the Raghus (raghunandana)
Rāma is the joy of His family line. He brings delight to all.

2. My dear one (pyārī mērē nandana)
He is called "my dear one". The bhajan is full of affection.

3. Lotus-eyed (rājīva lōcana)
His eyes are like lotuses, gentle and kind.

4. Daśaratha's son (daśaratha tanaya)
He is the beloved son of Daśaratha. The bhajan remembers His family.

5. Crusher of Rāvaṇa (daśamukha mardana)
He destroyed the ten-headed Rāvaṇa. His love is also strong against evil.

6. Sītā's life (jānakī jīvana)
He is Sītā's very life. Their love is the heart of the Rāmāyaṇa.

7. Repetition
Each line is repeated. The bhajan is easy for all to join.

8. A Hindi bhajan
The song is in simple Hindi, often sung in North Indian bhajan circles. Rāma is loved in every language.

9. Lotus eyes and tender words
"Rājīva lōcana" and "pyārī" sit side by side: His beauty and our love meet in one line.

@@ 21.8 | Ātma Rāma Ānanda Ramaṇa |
Overall Summary
A Rāma bhajan in Rāgam Kānaḍā: "Rāma of the Self, who delights in bliss; Acyuta, Kēśava, Hari, Nārāyaṇa."

He removes the fear of worldly life, His feet are worshipped, sages serve Him, He is lotus-eyed. He is the original Nārāyaṇa, resting on Ananta; Hari, truth-consciousness-bliss, Satya Nārāyaṇa. Kōdaṇḍa Rāma, crusher of Rāvaṇa; victory, victory, Rāma, beloved of Jānakī.

Key Takeaway
Rāma is the Self and the Supreme Nārāyaṇa. He removes the fear of worldly life.
For each of us: when we are afraid, we can remember that Rāma lives within as our own Self.

Deeper Meaning
1. Rāma of the Self (ātma rāma)
Rāma lives within us as the Self. He is not far away.

2. Delighting in bliss (ānanda ramaṇa)
He delights in bliss and gives it. Being with Him is joy.

3. Remover of fear (bhava bhaya haraṇa)
He removes the fear of birth and death.

4. Served by sages (muni jana sēvita)
The sages serve Him. Wisdom bows to Him.

5. Resting on Ananta (ananta śayana)
He rests on the serpent Ananta. He is the cosmic Lord.

6. Truth-consciousness-bliss (saccidānanda)
His nature is existence, awareness and joy. These are what the soul longs for.

7. Jānakī's beloved (jānakī ramaṇa)
The bhajan ends with Rāma and Sītā. The Supreme is also the loving husband.

8. Achyuta, Kēśava, Hari, Nārāyaṇa
Rāma is called by Viṣṇu's names too. He is Viṣṇu Himself in human form.

9. Satya Nārāyaṇa
He is Nārāyaṇa who is Truth. Many homes worship Him by this name in the Satyanārāyaṇa pūjā.

@@ 21.9 | Mannu Pugazh Kōsalai |
Overall Summary
A Tamil lullaby to Rāma in Rāgam Nīlāmbari, from the Divya Prabandham (traditionally Kulaśēkhara Āzhvār's Perumāḷ Tirumozhi), each verse ending "Rāghavanē, tālēlō" (sleep, Rāghava).

"You who blessed the jewel-like womb of Kausalyā of lasting fame; who scattered the crowns of the king of southern Laṅkā; my dark gem of Kaṇṇapuram, surrounded by fine golden walls; my sweet nectar: Rāghava, sleep. You who created gods and demons and all the directions; who lies in Araṅga nagar (Śrīraṅgam), where all come to bow at Your feet, where the good Kāvēri flows; my dark gem of Kaṇṇapuram; holder of the fierce white bow: Rāghava, sleep."

Key Takeaway
The great Rāma, creator of all, is sung to sleep like a baby. The Āzhvār loves Him like a mother.
For each of us: we can love God tenderly, as a mother loves her baby.

Deeper Meaning
1. A lullaby (tālēlō)
"Tālēlō" is the Tamil lullaby word. The Āzhvār sings Rāma to sleep.

2. Kausalyā's blessed womb (kausalai maṇi vayiru)
Kausalyā is blessed to have carried Rāma. The song begins with her.

3. Victor over Rāvaṇa (ilaṅgai kōn muḍigaḷ)
He scattered Rāvaṇa's crowns. The baby is the mighty hero.

4. My dark gem (kāṛu maṇiyē)
He is "my dark gem", precious and beautiful.

5. My sweet nectar (ennuḍaiya innamudē)
He is "my own sweet nectar". Love makes the Lord one's own.

6. Creator of all (dēvaraiyum asuraraiyum … paḍaittavanē)
He created gods, demons and the directions. The baby in the cradle is the creator.

7. Lying in Śrīraṅgam (araṅga nagar tuyinṛavanē)
He lies in Śrīraṅgam by the Kāvēri. The Rāma of the lullaby is Raṅganātha too.

8. Kaṇṇapuram (kaṇapurattu)
The Āzhvār calls Rāma the dark gem of Tirukkaṇṇapuram, a temple dear to him. Rāma of the Rāmāyaṇa is worshipped in every temple of Viṣṇu.

@@ 21.10 | Nāma Rāmāyaṇam |
Overall Summary
The whole Rāmāyaṇa told in names, each line ending "Rām", in seven kāṇḍas (books), each in its own rāga and each ending "Rāma Rāma Jaya Rājā Rām, Rāma Rāma Jaya Sītā Rām".

Bāla Kāṇḍam: the pure Brahman beyond all, who rests on Śēṣa, prayed to by Brahmā and the gods, born as Daśaratha's son, Kausalyā's joy, dear to Viśvāmitra, slayer of Tāṭakā, protector of the sacrifice, freer of Ahalyā, whose feet the boatman washed, who broke Śiva's bow, received Sītā's garland, humbled Paraśurāma and ruled Ayōdhyā. Ayōdhyā Kāṇḍam: of countless qualities, loved by the Earth's daughter, who went to the forest by His father's word, whose feet Guha washed, who stayed at Citrakūṭa, whose father always thought of Him, and who gave His sandals to Bharata. Araṇya Kāṇḍam: who purified the Daṇḍaka forest, killed Virādha, was worshipped by Śarabhaṅga and Sutīkṣṇa, blessed by Agastya, served by Jaṭāyu, lived at Pañcavaṭī, punished Śūrpaṇakhā, killed Khara and Dūṣaṇa, followed the golden deer for Sītā, searched for lost Sītā, gave liberation to Jaṭāyu, ate Śabarī's fruits and cut off Kabandha's arms. Kiṣkindhā Kāṇḍam: served by Hanumān, fulfilled Sugrīva's wish, killed proud Vāli, and sent the monkey messengers. Sundara Kāṇḍam: always remembered by Hanumān, who saved Sītā's life, was told of the crow, and saw the crest-jewel. Yuddha Kāṇḍam: who set out to end Rāvaṇa, surrounded by the monkey army, who built the bridge, gave Vibhīṣaṇa fearlessness, killed Kumbhakarṇa and Rāvaṇa, was praised by Brahmā and Śiva, rejoiced to see Sītā, crowned Vibhīṣaṇa, rode the Puṣpaka, delighted Bharata, and was crowned in Ayōdhyā, protector of all beings. Uttara Kāṇḍam: praised by the sages, who ruled with justice, whose sons Kuśa and Lava delighted Him, who performed the Aśvamēdha, gave liberation to Ayōdhyā's people, freed beings from worldly bondage, established dharma, gave liberation to the devoted, and returned to Vaikuṇṭha, established in eternal bliss.

Key Takeaway
The whole Rāmāyaṇa can be sung in names. Each line recalls one event, and each ends in "Rām".
For each of us: singing the Nāma Rāmāyaṇam is a way to walk through Rāma's whole life in an hour, with His name on every step.

Deeper Meaning
1. The epic as a garland of names
The Nāma Rāmāyaṇam turns Vālmīki's long epic into short lines of names. Each name holds a story.

2. Seven kāṇḍas, seven rāgas
Each book is sung in a different rāga: Yamunā Kalyāṇi, Kānaḍā, Varāḷi, Haṁsanādam, Dēś, Śuddha Dhanyāsi and Kāpi. The music follows the mood of each part of the story.

3. Beginning with Brahman (śuddha brahma parātpara)
The first name is "pure Brahman, beyond all". The story begins by remembering who Rāma really is.

4. Love for His devotees
Guha, Jaṭāyu, Śabarī, Hanumān and Vibhīṣaṇa all appear. The story is full of His love for simple devotees.

5. The refrain (rāma rāma jaya rājā rām)
After each book, everyone sings "Rāma Rāma Jaya Rājā Rām, Rāma Rāma Jaya Sītā Rām". It gathers the group together.

6. Freer from bondage (saṁsṛti bandha vimōcaka)
The last book reminds us that Rāma frees all beings from worldly bondage.

7. Ending in eternal bliss (nityānanda pada sthita)
The last name places Rāma in eternal bliss. The story ends where it began, in the Supreme.

@@ 22.1 | Guruvāyūr Pura Śrī Hari |
Overall Summary
A Krishna bhajan: "Śrī Hari Krishna of Guruvāyūr, Nārāyaṇa, Gōpāla!" Mukunda, Mādhava, holder of the flute; holder of the conch, discus and yellow silk; Murāri who held up Gōvardhana hill: Nārāyaṇa, Gōpāla; Śrī Hari Nārāyaṇa, Gōpāla.

Key Takeaway
Krishna of Guruvāyūr is the same Nārāyaṇa who holds the conch and discus and the Gōpāla who lifted the hill.
For each of us: the Lord of a temple we love is the same Lord who lives everywhere.

Deeper Meaning
1. Guruvāyūr
Guruvāyūr in Kerala is one of the most loved Krishna temples. Devotees call Him Guruvāyūrappan.

2. Nārāyaṇa Gōpāla
The refrain joins Nārāyaṇa, the Supreme Lord, and Gōpāla, the cowherd. Both are the same Krishna.

3. Mukunda, giver of freedom
"Mukunda" means the one who gives liberation.

4. Holder of the flute (muraḷī dhārī)
His flute calls every soul home.

5. Conch, discus and yellow silk
At Guruvāyūr, Krishna is worshipped in His four-armed form with the conch and discus. He wears yellow silk.

6. Lifter of the hill (gōvardhana giri dhārī)
He held up the Gōvardhana hill to protect His people. He protects us too.

7. A simple, singable bhajan
The lines are few and repeated. Everyone can join in easily.

8. Guruvāyūrappan's grace
Devotees come to Guruvāyūr with every kind of need, from a child's first meal to illness and old age. The bhajan carries a little of that trusting love.

@@ 22.2 | Hē Muraḷī Śrīdhara |
Overall Summary
A Krishna bhajan: "O flute-holder, Lakṣmī's Lord, Rādhē Krishna, Rādhē Śyām!" Kēśava, Mādhava, son of the Yādavas; Nanda's son, butter-thief; loving to devotees, dear to the devoted. Between verses: "Rādhē Rādhē Gōvinda, Rādhē Rādhē Rādhē Gōvinda."

Key Takeaway
Krishna is called together with Rādhā in every line. Their names belong together.
For each of us: calling "Rādhē Krishna" is calling love and the Lord at once.

Deeper Meaning
1. Flute-holder (muraḷī)
Krishna with His flute is the Lord who calls us home.

2. Lakṣmī's Lord (śrīdhara)
"Śrīdhara" means the one who holds Lakṣmī. The cowherd is the Lord of fortune.

3. Rādhē Krishna, Rādhē Śyām
Every line ends with Rādhā's name joined to His. Rādhā is the heart of devotion.

4. Son of the Yādavas (yādava nandana)
He is the joy of His clan.

5. Butter-thief (navanīta cōra)
His mischief in Gōkula is lovingly remembered.

6. Loving to devotees (bhakta vatsala)
He loves those who love Him, like a mother cow with her calf.

7. Rādhē Gōvinda
The chant "Rādhē Rādhē Gōvinda" between verses keeps Rādhā's name ringing through the bhajan.

8. Kēśava and Mādhava
Kēśava is the one with beautiful hair; Mādhava is the Lord of Lakṣmī. The names honour both His beauty and His glory.

9. Rādhē Śyām
"Śyām" means the dark one. "Rādhē Śyām" joins the fair Rādhā and the dark Krishna in one breath.

@@ 22.3 | Kṛṣṇa Gōvinda Gōpāla |
Overall Summary
A Hindi bhajan in Rāgam Madhuvanti: "Keep singing 'Krishna, Gōvinda, Gōpāla'; keep saving yourself from the poison of all sense-objects."

People say God doesn't come, but they don't call Him as Draupadī did. Keep working, keep chanting the Name; keep growing the treasure of the wealth of the Name.

Key Takeaway
God comes when we call Him as Draupadī did. Keep working and keep chanting, and the Name becomes your wealth.
For each of us: we don't have to stop our work. We can chant the Name while we do it.

Deeper Meaning
1. Keep singing (gātē rahō)
The song asks us to keep singing, not once but always.

2. Saved from poison (viṣayōṁ ko viṣ sē)
Attachment to sense-pleasures is called poison. The Name protects us from it.

3. "God doesn't come" (bhagavān ātē nahīṁ)
People complain that God doesn't come. The song answers: you don't call Him with your whole heart.

4. Like Draupadī (draupadī kī taraḥ)
Draupadī called Krishna with complete surrender, and He came at once. That is the way to call.

5. Work and Name (kām kartē rahō, nām japtē rahō)
Keep working, keep chanting. Daily life and devotion go together.

6. The treasure of the Name (nām dhan kā khazānā)
The Name is wealth. Every repetition adds to the treasure.

7. A modern bhajan
The words are simple Hindi, easy to remember and sing anywhere.

8. Bhakti in daily life
The song's message is practical: you do not need to leave your work to remember God.

@@ 22.4 | Kṛṣṇa Prēmamayī Rādhā – Śrī Yugalāṣṭakam |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit hymn to the Divine Couple, each verse ending: "In life and in death, always, Rādhā and Krishna are my refuge."

Rādhā is full of love for Krishna; Hari is full of love for Rādhā. Rādhā is Krishna's life; Hari is Rādhā's life. Rādhā is Krishna's treasure; Hari is Rādhā's treasure. Rādhā melts with Krishna; Hari melts with Rādhā. Rādhā lives in Krishna's home; Hari lives in Rādhā's home. Rādhā wears blue; Hari wears yellow. Rādhā is the queen of Vṛndāvana; Krishna is the king of Vṛndāvana.

Key Takeaway
Rādhā and Krishna are each other's life, love and treasure. Together they are the devotee's refuge, in life and in death.
For each of us: we can take refuge in divine love itself, Rādhā and Krishna together.

Deeper Meaning
1. Yugala, the pair
"Yugala" means the pair. This hymn honours Rādhā and Krishna as one inseparable couple.

2. Full of love for each other (prēmamayī … prēmamayaḥ)
Each is full of love for the other. Their love is perfect and equal.

3. Each other's life (prāṇamayī)
They are each other's very breath. One cannot be without the other.

4. Each other's treasure (draviṇam)
Each is the other's wealth. Love is the only treasure they want.

5. Blue and yellow (nīlāmbara … pītāmbara)
Rādhā wears blue, the colour of Krishna; Krishna wears yellow, the colour of Rādhā. Each wears the other.

6. Queen and king of Vṛndāvana
They rule Vṛndāvana together. Love is the ruler there.

7. My refuge, in life and in death (jīvanē nidhanē nityaṁ)
The refrain is complete surrender. In life and at death, they are the devotee's refuge.

8. Each other's home (gēhē sthitā)
Rādhā lives in Krishna's home, Krishna in Rādhā's. Where one is, the other is.

@@ 22.5 | Kamala Nayana Vāsudēva |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song in Rāgam Señjuruṭṭi: "Lotus-eyed Vāsudēva, giver of boons to the elephant, protect me; Acyuta, with pure, soft lotus feet, give me joy."

He is the lover-thief, steady as Mēru, the wish-tree for good people; without any burden, deep as the ocean against terrible sinful thoughts. He loves Nārada's songs and is Nanda's Gōpāla; Brahmā is devoted to Him; His qualities are honoured. He is the father of Kāma, dark and beautiful, wearing golden cloth; His wonderful lotus feet are bowed to by Rāmadās.

Key Takeaway
Krishna protects as He protected the elephant, and gives joy to those who ask. Even Rāmadās bowed at His feet.
For each of us: "protect me, give me joy" (mām pāhi, mudam dēhi) are two requests we can always make.

Deeper Meaning
1. Lotus-eyed Vāsudēva (kamala nayana vāsudēva)
His eyes are like lotuses, gentle and kind.

2. Giver of boons to the elephant (karivarada)
He saved the king of elephants. He answers every true cry.

3. Protect me, give me joy (mām pāhi … mudam dēhi)
The devotee asks for two things: protection and joy.

4. Steady as Mēru (mēru dhīra)
He is as steady as the great mountain. Those who rely on Him become steady too.

5. Wish-tree for the good (sādhu jana mandāra)
He grants the wishes of good people.

6. Lover of Nārada's songs (nāradādi gāna lōla)
He loves the singing of Nārada and the sages. Our bhajan pleases Him too.

7. Bowed to by Rāmadās (rāmadāsa vandita)
The song names Rāmadās, who bows at His feet. The devotees of Rāma also bow to Krishna.

8. Father of Kāma (kāmajanaka)
He is the father of Kāma, the god of love. All love has its source in Him.

@@ 22.6 | Śrī Kṛṣṇa Jai Jai |
Overall Summary
A short chant: "Śrī Krishna, victory, victory; Gōpāla, victory, victory; Hari, beloved of Gōdā (Āṇḍāḷ); Gōvinda, victory, victory."

Key Takeaway
Krishna is hailed as the beloved of Āṇḍāḷ. Her love for Him is one of the great stories of devotion.
For each of us: Āṇḍāḷ shows that a young girl's pure love can win the Lord.

Deeper Meaning
1. Victory (jai jai)
The chant celebrates Krishna's victory, again and again.

2. Gōpāla
He is the cowherd, the protector of all.

3. Gōdā's beloved (gōdā ramaṇa)
Gōdā is Āṇḍāḷ, the only woman among the Āzhvārs. She loved Raṅganātha so deeply that she married Him.

4. Āṇḍāḷ's songs
Āṇḍāḷ wrote the Tiruppāvai and Nācciyār Tirumozhi. Her songs are sung every winter.

5. Gōvinda
"Gōvinda" was Āṇḍāḷ's favourite name for the Lord in the Tiruppāvai.

6. A chant for all
The words are few and easy to repeat. Anyone can join.

7. Krishna and Raṅganātha one
By calling Krishna "Gōdā's beloved", the chant joins the Krishna of Vṛndāvana with Raṅganātha of Śrīraṅgam.

8. Victory to Gōpāla
Every "jai" is a little celebration. Repeating it brings joy to the gathering.

9. Tiruppāvai in the bhajan
Many bhajan groups sing Āṇḍāḷ's Tiruppāvai in Mārgaḻi. This chant keeps her memory in every season.

10. Sung with joy
The chant is often sung fast and happily, with clapping.

@@ 22.7 | Gōvinda Hari Gōvinda |
Overall Summary
A short chant in Rāgam Mōhanam: "Gōvinda, Hari, Gōvinda; joy of Gōkula, Gōvinda."

Key Takeaway
The simplest chant: Gōvinda, the joy of Gōkula. A few words carry great love.
For each of us: saying "Gōvinda" calmly and often can quieten the busy mind.

Deeper Meaning
1. Gōvinda
"Gōvinda" means the one who protects cows, and the one who is known through the Vedas. It is one of Krishna's dearest names.

2. Hari
"Hari" means the one who takes away sorrow and steals hearts.

3. Joy of Gōkula (gōkula nandana)
He is the joy of Gōkula, where He grew up. His presence made the village a festival.

4. Rāgam Mōhanam
Mōhanam is a sweet, simple rāga. It suits the simple chant.

5. Repetition
The two lines are repeated. The chant moves gently, letting the Name sink in.

6. A chant for beginners and masters
Anyone can sing it, yet saints sing it all their lives.

7. Gōvinda in Tirumala
"Gōvinda" is the chant of pilgrims climbing to Tirumala. This chant links us to that devotion.

8. A name for all times
Gōvinda is called in joy and in sorrow, at birth and at death. It is a name for every moment of life.

@@ 22.8 | Madhuvana Muraḷī |
Overall Summary
A Hindi bhajan: "Sing the qualities of the flute-player of Madhuvana; worship the name of Madhusūdana Hari. Dear to the gōpīs of Vraja, Rādhē Śyām; Mukunda, Mādhava, cloud-dark Śyām."

Key Takeaway
The flute-player of Vṛndāvana's forests is the Lord to be sung and worshipped.
For each of us: singing His qualities is a simple way to fill the mind with good things.

Deeper Meaning
1. Madhuvana
Madhuvana is one of the forests of Vraja. Krishna played His flute there.

2. The flute-player (muraḷī)
His flute calls every soul to Him.

3. Sing His qualities (guṇa gāō)
Singing His good qualities is a form of worship.

4. Madhusūdana
He killed the demon Madhu. He removes what harms us.

5. Dear to the gōpīs (vraja gōpī priya)
He is dear to the gōpīs, the great devotees of Vraja.

6. Cloud-dark (mēgha śyām)
His dark colour is like a rain cloud, bringing life.

7. A gentle bhajan
The four lines are easy to sing and repeat, a rest within the longer songs.

8. Madhusūdana Hari's name
"Worship the name" is the instruction. The Name itself is the object of worship.

9. Mukunda Mādhava
He gives liberation and is Lakṣmī's Lord. The names carry both freedom and grace.

10. Cloud-dark Śyām
"Mēgha Śyām" ends the bhajan. The dark rain cloud is a picture of His life-giving mercy.

@@ 22.9 | Rādhā Rasika Vara |
Overall Summary
A bhajan with the refrain "Rādhā Krishna, Rādhā Krishna": "Rādhā's best connoisseur, who plays the rāsa; who loves the rāsa, lovely and good-natured; who loves to play the enchanting flute; Mukunda, Mādhava, victory, cloud-dark one."

Key Takeaway
Krishna delights in Rādhā's love and in the rāsa dance. The chant "Rādhā Krishna" keeps their names together.
For each of us: repeating "Rādhā Krishna" fills the heart with gentle love.

Deeper Meaning
1. Rādhā's rasika (rādhā rasika vara)
A "rasika" is one who deeply enjoys beauty. Krishna is the one who most enjoys Rādhā's love.

2. Player of the rāsa (rāsa vihāra)
He plays the rāsa dance with the gōpīs.

3. Lovely and good-natured (ramya suśīla)
He is beautiful and of good character. His beauty is goodness.

4. The enchanting flute (mōhana muraḷī)
His flute enchants all creation.

5. Mukunda and Mādhava
He gives liberation and is Lakṣmī's Lord.

6. Rādhā Krishna, Rādhā Krishna
The refrain repeats their names four times after each line. The names become a rhythm of love.

7. Love as devotion
The bhajan shows devotion as love, the mood of Vṛndāvana.

8. Joy in the Lord
The rasika tradition teaches that devotion is not only duty but the tasting of the Lord's sweetness. This bhajan invites us to taste it.

9. Ramya, lovely
Everything about Him is lovely: His form, His play, His nature.

@@ 22.10 | Bhaja Gōvindam |
Overall Summary
Verses of Ādi Śaṅkarācārya's Bhaja Gōvindam (Mōha Mudgaram), sung in Rāgam Ābhēri / Bhimpalās: "Worship Gōvinda, worship Gōvinda, worship Gōvinda, O foolish mind!"

When the appointed time (death) comes, grammar rules will not save you. Fool, give up the thirst for gathering wealth; turn your mind, free of craving, to good thoughts; be content with what comes from your own work. As long as you can earn, your family is attached to you; later, when your body is old and weak, no one at home even asks after you. Do not be proud of wealth, people or youth; time takes them all in a moment; leave this world of illusion and enter Brahman, knowing it. Living under a temple tree, sleeping on the ground, wearing deerskin, giving up all possessions and pleasures: whom does such detachment not make happy? One who has studied a little of the Bhagavad Gītā, drunk a drop of Gaṅgā water, worshipped Murāri even once, has no quarrel with Yama. Birth again, death again, lying in a mother's womb again; this worldly life is very hard to cross; protect me by Your boundless mercy, Murāri. Sing the Gītā and the thousand names; always meditate on Lakṣmī's Lord; lead the mind to good company; give wealth to the poor. Know that wealth is trouble; there is truly not a bit of happiness in it; the rich fear even their own sons; this is the rule everywhere. Devoted to the lotus feet of the Guru, be freed from worldly life soon; by controlling the senses and the mind, you will see the God who lives in your own heart.

Key Takeaway
Wealth, youth and cleverness will not save us at the end. Only worship of Gōvinda, good company and devotion to the Guru will.
For each of us: we can ask ourselves each day: am I spending my life on what will last?

Deeper Meaning
1. O foolish mind (mūḍhamatē)
Śaṅkara speaks sharply to the mind, to wake it up. The word is a shake, not an insult.

2. Learning won't save you (nahi nahi rakṣati ḍukṛñ karaṇē)
Tradition says Śaṅkara saw an old scholar memorising grammar and sang this. Knowledge without devotion cannot help at death.

3. Give up the thirst for wealth (dhanāgama tṛṣṇām)
Be content with what honest work brings. Craving more only brings trouble.

4. Who will ask after you? (vārtāṁ kōpi na pṛcchati)
When we can no longer earn, many forget us. Only God stays.

5. A little Gītā, a drop of Gaṅgā (kiñcid adhītā … kaṇikā pītā)
Even a little devotion is powerful. One sincere worship of Murāri is enough to free us from fear of death.

6. Again birth, again death (punarapi jananam)
The cycle of birth and death goes on. Śaṅkara asks Murāri's mercy to cross it.

7. Good company and giving (sajjana saṅgē … dīnajanāya vittam)
Keep good company and give to the poor. These are practical steps on the path.

8. The God in your heart (nija hṛdayasthaṁ dēvam)
The last verse promises: with devotion to the Guru and a disciplined mind, you will see God in your own heart.

@@ 22.11 | Rādhē Rādhē Rādhē Gōvinda |
Overall Summary
A bhajan with the refrain "Rādhē Rādhē Rādhē Gōvinda; moon of Vṛndāvana; Lord of the helpless, friend of the humble, Rādhē Gōvinda."

Nanda's son, butter-thief; the ancient Person, of holy fame; Lord of Paṇḍharpur, Pāṇḍuraṅga; victory, Viṭhala, victory, Hari Viṭhala: Rādhē Gōvinda.

Key Takeaway
Krishna of Vṛndāvana and Viṭhala of Paṇḍharpur are one. He is the Lord of the helpless.
For each of us: when we feel helpless, we can remember that He is "anātha nātha", the Lord of those who have no one.

Deeper Meaning
1. Rādhē Gōvinda
Rādhā's name comes before Gōvinda's. Love leads to the Lord.

2. Moon of Vṛndāvana (bṛndāvana canda)
He is the moon that lights up Vṛndāvana.

3. Lord of the helpless (anātha nātha)
He is the protector of those with no protector.

4. Friend of the humble (dīna bandhu)
He is the friend of the poor and lowly.

5. The ancient Person (purāṇa puruṣa)
He is the eternal Lord, older than time.

6. Pāṇḍuraṅga
The bhajan moves from Vṛndāvana to Paṇḍharpur. Krishna and Viṭhala are one.

7. Victory to Viṭhala (jai jai viṭhala)
The chant ends with "victory, Hari Viṭhala". The Name tradition of Maharashtra joins the song.

8. Of holy fame (puṇya ślōka)
His fame is holy; hearing of Him purifies. Even singing His names is a holy act.

9. A bhajan that travels
The song begins in Vṛndāvana and ends in Paṇḍharpur. The Name joins the holy places of north and west.

@@ 22.12 | Bansīdhara Kanhaiyā |
Overall Summary
A Hindi bhajan: "Flute-holder Kanhaiyā, cloud-dark and beautiful; mountain-holder, cowherd boy, who plays in Vṛndāvana; Gōvinda, Mādhava, Hari, Madhusūdana, Mukunda." Then: "Rādhē Gōvinda Rādhē, Rādhē Gōpāla Rādhē, Rādhē Rādhē Rādhē."

Key Takeaway
The bhajan paints Krishna with His flute in Vṛndāvana, then ends in Rādhā's name.
For each of us: Rādhā's name, repeated, softens the heart.

Deeper Meaning
1. Bansīdhara
"Bansī" is the flute. He is the flute-holder.

2. Kanhaiyā
A loving, homely name for Krishna, used by the people of Vraja.

3. Cloud-dark and beautiful (ghana śyāma sundara)
His dark beauty is like a rain cloud.

4. Mountain-holder (giridhārī)
He lifted the Gōvardhana hill. The cowherd boy is strong.

5. Playing in Vṛndāvana (bṛndāvana vihāra)
His play in Vṛndāvana is the delight of devotees.

6. Many names (gōvinda mādhava hari …)
The bhajan gathers His names in one line.

7. Ending in Rādhā (rādhē rādhē rādhē)
The bhajan ends by repeating Rādhā's name. In Vraja, every song ends in her.

8. Giridhārī gōpa bāla
The mountain-holder is also a cowherd boy. Strength and innocence live together in Him.

9. Madhusūdana Mukunda
He destroys what harms and gives freedom. The names promise both protection and release.

10. Rādhē Gōvinda Rādhē
Rādhā's name both begins and ends each phrase, holding Krishna in the middle like a jewel.

@@ 22.13 | Kṛṣṇa Kṛṣṇa Mukunda Janārdana |
Overall Summary
A short chant: "Krishna, Krishna, Mukunda, Janārdana; Krishna, Gōvinda, Nārāyaṇa, Hari; Acyuta, bliss, Gōvinda, Mādhava; truth-consciousness-bliss, Nārāyaṇa, Hari."

Key Takeaway
The chant names Krishna as the giver of freedom, the Lord of all and bliss itself.
For each of us: a few names, said with attention, can lift the mood of a whole day.

Deeper Meaning
1. Mukunda
The giver of liberation.

2. Janārdana
The one people pray to, who removes their troubles.

3. Nārāyaṇa
The Lord who lives in all beings.

4. Acyuta
The one who never falls, never changes.

5. Bliss (ānanda)
He is bliss itself.

6. Truth-consciousness-bliss (saccidānanda)
His very nature is existence, awareness and joy.

7. Each name a door
Each name opens a different door to the same Lord. The chant walks through them all.

8. Krishna, Krishna
His name is said twice at the start. Repetition is the heart of the Name tradition.

9. Gōvinda
The protector of cows and of the world.

10. Hari
The one who takes away sorrow.

11. One flow
The chant has no verses or story. It is a steady flow of names, letting the mind rest in Him.

12. Sung in unison
Short chants like this are often sung by the whole gathering together, slowly and then faster, uniting everyone.

@@ 22.14 | Naṭavara Lāl Giridhara Gōpāl |
Overall Summary
A Hindi bhajan: "Best of dancers, darling Giridhara Gōpāl; victory, victory, darling of Nanda and Yaśōdā."

He is the essence of all essence, Rādhā's best connoisseur, who plays the rāsa. On the clear Yamunā under the autumn full moon, the wonderful, matchless great festival of rāsa. Fair Rādhā and cloud-dark Śyām; the pair's beauty, a jewel of ornaments. A circle of gōpīs bright as crystal, and in the middle of each pair of gōpīs, emerald-dark Śyām. Blessed, blessed are the women of Vraja; blessed the bowers of Vṛndāvana; blessed all the birds, animals and trees of Vraja; blessed the dust of Vraja and the Yamunā's banks.

Key Takeaway
The rāsa under the autumn moon is the highest festival of love. Everything in Vraja is blessed by it.
For each of us: when love for God fills us, everything around us seems blessed too.

Deeper Meaning
1. Best of dancers (naṭavara)
Krishna is the greatest dancer. His dance is the joy of creation.

2. Nanda and Yaśōdā's darling
He is the beloved child of His foster parents.

3. Essence of all essence (sāra sāra)
He is the essence of everything. Nothing is more real than Him.

4. Autumn full moon (śarad pūrṇimā)
The rāsa took place on the autumn full moon night by the Yamunā.

5. Fair Rādhā, dark Śyām (gaura rādhā … ghana śyāmā)
The fair Rādhā and the dark Krishna together are beautiful beyond words.

6. Krishna between each gōpī
In the crystal-bright circle, a dark Krishna stands between each pair of gōpīs, as in 11.33.

7. Blessed is Vraja (dhanya dhanya)
The women, the bowers, the birds, the trees and the dust of Vraja are all blessed. Love makes the whole place holy.

8. Yamunā and the moon
The clear Yamunā and the bright autumn moon frame the dance. Nature itself becomes a temple.

@@ 22.15 | Ahō Madhura |
Overall Summary
A Hindi bhajan: "Oh, how sweet! Oh, how sweet! Sweet, sweet!" Rādhā and Krishna playing in the rāsa: sweet. Mōhana's flute: sweet. The moon-like face of Vraja's Nanda's son: sweet.

Key Takeaway
Everything about Krishna and Rādhā is sweet. The bhajan simply delights in that sweetness.
For each of us: sometimes the best prayer is just to enjoy God, saying "how sweet".

Deeper Meaning
1. Oh, how sweet (ahō madhura)
The bhajan is an exclamation of delight.

2. Mādhurya, sweetness
The tradition speaks of mādhurya, the sweetness of Krishna. It draws the heart more than His power.

3. Rādhā and Krishna's play (rasa vihāra)
Their play in the rāsa is the sweetest of all.

4. The flute (bansī)
His flute's music is sweet beyond words.

5. The moon-like face (indu mukha chabi)
His face is like the moon, cool and lovely.

6. Repetition of "madhura"
The word "sweet" is repeated again and again. The singer cannot stop.

7. Echoing Madhurāṣṭakam
The mood is like Vallabhācārya's famous "Madhurāṣṭakam", where everything about Krishna is called sweet.

8. Taste, not just thought
The bhajan does not explain Krishna; it tastes Him. Sweetness is known by tasting, not by argument.

9. A song for any time
When the heart feels dry, singing "madhura, madhura" can bring back the sweetness of devotion.

@@ 22.16 | Jaladhara Samanīla Varṇa |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit-Hindi bhajan: "Dark blue like a rain cloud: victory, victory, Krishna! Fair-coloured, full of grace: victory, victory, Rādhē!" The pair's form is full of auspiciousness, playing in the bowers; Lord Giridhara, playing in the flavour of love.

Key Takeaway
Krishna and Rādhā together are an auspicious pair. One is dark like a cloud, the other fair and full of grace.
For each of us: the pair reminds us that love joins differences into one beauty.

Deeper Meaning
1. Dark as a rain cloud (jaladhara samanīla)
Krishna's colour is like a cloud full of rain, life-giving.

2. Fair and full of grace (gaura varṇā kṛpā pūrṇā)
Rādhā is fair and full of mercy. Her grace leads us to Krishna.

3. Victory to both (jaya jaya)
Both are hailed with victory.

4. The pair (yugala rūpa)
They are worshipped as a pair. Love and the Lord are one.

5. Full of auspiciousness (maṅgala maya)
Their form brings all good things.

6. Playing in the bowers (kuñja vihārī)
They play in Vṛndāvana's bowers. Their love is joyful.

7. The flavour of love (prēma rasa)
Giridhara plays in the flavour of love. Devotion is the tasting of that love.

8. Giridhara prabhu
The mountain-holder is called "prabhu", master. His love is also His lordship.

9. Seeing the pair
Picturing Rādhā and Krishna together is a common meditation in Vṛndāvana.

10. Grace through Rādhā
Rādhā is full of grace. Through her, Krishna's love reaches us.

@@ 22.17 | Yamunā Tīra Vihārī |
Overall Summary
A short bhajan: "Who plays on the Yamunā's bank, who wanders in Vṛndāvana; who lifted Gōvardhana; Gōpāla Krishna, Murāri."

Key Takeaway
Krishna is remembered in three places: the Yamunā bank, Vṛndāvana and the Gōvardhana hill.
For each of us: picturing these holy places brings Krishna's presence near.

Deeper Meaning
1. On the Yamunā's bank (yamunā tīra vihārī)
He played by the river Yamunā. Its banks are holy to devotees.

2. Wandering in Vṛndāvana (bṛndāvana sañcārī)
He wandered through Vṛndāvana's forests with the cows and gōpas.

3. Lifter of Gōvardhana (gōvardhana dhārī)
He held up the hill to protect everyone from Indra's storm.

4. Gōpāla Krishna
The cowherd Krishna, protector of all.

5. Murāri
Slayer of the demon Mura. He removes evil.

6. Three places, one Lord
River, forest and hill: the whole land of Vraja is His playground.

7. A short chant
Two lines, easy to repeat, to rest the mind in Vraja.

8. Places of His play
The tradition loves to remember the very places where Krishna played. Pilgrims still walk those banks and hills.

9. Holding the hill
His holding of Gōvardhana is a promise: He will hold up whatever is too heavy for us.

10. Sung slowly
Even a two-line chant can carry deep feeling when sung slowly, with the mind on each word.

@@ 22.18 | Cal Cal Rādhē Bṛndāvanēśvarī |
Overall Summary
A Hindi bhajan: "Come, come, Rādhē, queen of Vṛndāvana; come, come to the bower-temple. Your anklets and jewel garlands are jingling 'ghal ghal'."

Key Takeaway
Rādhā, queen of Vṛndāvana, is invited to the bower where Krishna waits. Her anklets ring as she comes.
For each of us: Rādhā going to Krishna is the soul going to God. We can follow her.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come, come (cal cal)
The song urges Rādhā to come. It is the voice of her friend.

2. Queen of Vṛndāvana (bṛndāvanēśvarī)
Rādhā rules Vṛndāvana. Her love is its law.

3. The bower-temple (nikuñja mandira)
The bower where Krishna waits is called a temple. Love's meeting place is holy.

4. Anklets and garlands (nūpura … maṇi mālāyē)
Her ornaments ring as she walks. The sound announces her coming.

5. "Ghal ghal"
The sound of her anklets brings the scene alive.

6. The soul's journey
Tradition sees Rādhā's walk to the bower as the soul's journey to God.

7. A friend's help
The one who calls Rādhā is her sakhī, her friend. Good friends help us reach the Lord.

8. Her readiness
Rādhā is not reluctant; she is ready and adorned. The soul that longs for God prepares itself.

9. The bower is near
The bower-temple is close by. The Lord is never far from the one who seeks Him.

@@ 22.19 | Priyē Rādhē |
Overall Summary
A short chant: "Dear Rādhē, dear Rādhē, Rādhē Rādhē, dear one, dear one; dear Śyāmā, dear Śyāmā, Śyāma Śyāma, dear one, dear one."

Key Takeaway
Rādhā and Śyām are called "dear one" by each other. The chant is pure affection.
For each of us: calling God "dear one" is a simple, true prayer.

Deeper Meaning
1. Dear Rādhē (priyē rādhē)
Krishna calls Rādhā "dear one". Her love is dear to Him above all.

2. Dear Śyām (priya śyāmā)
Rādhā calls Krishna "dear Śyām". Their love is mutual.

3. Repetition
The names repeat again and again, like the beating of a loving heart.

4. Love without words
The chant has almost no words except names and "dear". Love needs few words.

5. Rādhā's place
In this tradition, Rādhā's love is the highest. Through her we approach Krishna.

6. Simple for everyone
Anyone, young or old, can sing it.

7. A chant to end in quiet
After the long rāsa songs, this short chant lets the heart rest in love.

8. Two lines, one love
The first line calls Rādhā, the second calls Śyām. Together they make one circle of love.

9. Quiet devotion
This chant is often sung softly, almost whispered, as a moment of closeness.

@@ 22.20 | Kṛṣṇa Karuṇā Lōla |
Overall Summary
A short chant: "Krishna, who delights in compassion; Gōpāla, holder of the Gōvardhana hill."

Key Takeaway
Krishna delights in being compassionate. The one who held up the hill to save His people loves to show mercy.
For each of us: we never trouble God by asking for mercy. He enjoys giving it.

Deeper Meaning
1. Delighting in compassion (karuṇā lōla)
"Lōla" means one who delights in or loves something. Krishna loves to be merciful.

2. Mercy is His joy
He does not give mercy reluctantly. Giving it makes Him happy.

3. Holder of the hill (gōvardhana giridhara)
He lifted the hill to protect everyone. That is His mercy in action.

4. Gōpāla
The cowherd who protects His people as He protects the cows.

5. Two lines
The chant is just two lines. It is meant to be repeated until it sinks in.

6. A comfort
When we feel unworthy, this chant reminds us that His joy is to forgive and help.

7. Compassion and strength
The two lines join compassion and strength. His mercy is backed by power.

8. Karuṇā (compassion)
Compassion is His nature. When we remember it, our fear of Him melts.

9. Easy to hold
Two short lines are easy to carry through the day, like a small lamp in the pocket.

@@ 22.21 | Nīla Mēgha Śyāmala Rūpa Gōpāla |
Overall Summary
A bhajan in Rāgam Pīlu, each line ending "Gōpāla": "Gōpāla, dark like a blue rain cloud; Krishna Gōpāla, whose nature is eternal bliss; God of gods, Dēvakī's child; Vasudēva's son with lotus eyes; whose feet are served by Śaṅkara and the gods; Lord holding the conch and discus; king of kings, delighting in the flute; Rādhikā's beloved, delighting in the rāsa; protector of the circle of loving devotees; who loves to play in Vṛndāvana's bowers."

Key Takeaway
Gōpāla is the eternal bliss, the God of gods and the protector of loving devotees.
For each of us: we can see in Gōpāla both the Lord of all and the friend who protects us.

Deeper Meaning
1. Dark as a rain cloud (nīla mēgha śyāmala)
His colour is like a blue rain cloud: dark, deep and refreshing.

2. Eternal bliss (nityānanda svarūpa)
His nature is unending joy.

3. God of gods (dēva dēva)
He is above all gods.

4. Served by Śiva (śaṅkarādi sēvita)
Even Śiva serves His feet. The gods honour each other.

5. Rādhā's beloved (rādhikā ramaṇa)
He delights in the rāsa with Rādhā.

6. Protector of loving devotees (prēma bhakta maṇḍala pāla)
He protects those who love Him.

7. In Vṛndāvana's bowers (bṛndāvana nikuñja)
He loves to play in Vṛndāvana. The bhajan ends in His favourite place.

8. Holder of the conch and discus
He holds the divine weapons, yet He is the cowherd. Power and gentleness meet in Him.

9. Vasudēva's son
He is the son of Vasudēva, born in prison to free His people.

10. King of kings, lover of the flute
He rules all, yet loves to play His flute. His kingship is full of music.

@@ 22.22 | Kṛṣṇa Muraḷī Gōpāla |
Overall Summary
A short bhajan: "Krishna, flute-playing Gōpāla; Dēvakī's son, lotus-eyed; lotus-eyed Krishna; flute-playing Gōpāla Krishna; lotus-eyed, flute-playing Gōpāla."

Key Takeaway
Krishna is the lotus-eyed flute-player, Dēvakī's son. The names circle gently around Him.
For each of us: picturing His lotus eyes and flute is a gentle way to meditate.

Deeper Meaning
1. Krishna Muraḷī
Krishna with His flute: the most loved picture of Him.

2. Gōpāla
The cowherd, protector of all.

3. Dēvakī's son (dēvakī nandana)
He is the child of Dēvakī, born in prison to free the world.

4. Lotus-eyed (aravinda nayana)
His eyes are like lotuses: gentle, kind and beautiful.

5. Circling names
The same few names are arranged in different orders. The mind moves around Him like a devotee circling a shrine.

6. Easy to remember
The bhajan is so simple it stays in the mind long after.

7. A meditation
Each name gives one image: the flute, the cowherd, the mother, the eyes. Together they make a picture to hold.

8. Aravinda Nayana Krishna
The lotus-eyed Krishna. His gaze is soft like a lotus opening at dawn.

9. A song to rest in
After longer songs, short ones like this let the gathering rest in the Lord's names.

@@ 22.23 | Vanamālī Rādhā Ramaṇa Giridhārī Gōvinda |
Overall Summary
A bhajan: "Wearing the forest garland, Rādhā's beloved, mountain-holder, Gōvinda; beautiful as a blue cloud, Nārāyaṇa Gōvinda; the wish-tree of devotees' hearts, beautiful as millions of suns; Nanda's son with the herd of cowherds, Nārāyaṇa Gōvinda." It ends: "Nārāyaṇa, Nārāyaṇa, Nārāyaṇa, Gōvinda."

Key Takeaway
Krishna is the wish-tree of His devotees' hearts. He wears the forest garland and loves Rādhā.
For each of us: whatever we truly need, the Lord can give. He is the wish-tree in our hearts.

Deeper Meaning
1. Forest garland (vanamālī)
He wears a garland of forest flowers, simple and fragrant.

2. Rādhā's beloved (rādhā ramaṇa)
He delights in Rādhā.

3. Mountain-holder (giridhārī)
He held up Gōvardhana to protect His people.

4. Beautiful as a blue cloud (nīla mēgha sundara)
His beauty is like a dark rain cloud.

5. Wish-tree of devotees' hearts (bhakta hṛdaya mandāra)
The mandāra is a heavenly wish-granting tree. He grows in devotees' hearts and grants their wishes.

6. Bright as millions of suns (bhānu kōṭi sundara)
His beauty is brighter than millions of suns.

7. Nārāyaṇa Gōvinda
The bhajan ends with Nārāyaṇa, joining the cowherd and the Supreme Lord.

8. Nanda's son among the cowherds
He is with His friends, the cowherd boys. The Lord loves companionship.

9. Nārāyaṇa three times
The last line repeats Nārāyaṇa. The bhajan settles into the Supreme Name.

10. Giridhārī Gōvinda
The mountain-holder and the protector of cows: He holds up the world and cares for each creature.

@@ 22.24 | Gōvinda Mādhava |
Overall Summary
A bhajan: "Gōvinda, Mādhava, Gōpāla, Kēśava; Nanda's Mukunda, butter-thief, Rādhē Gōvinda; mountain-holder, mountain-holder, Rādhē Gōvinda; cloud-dark, dark one, dark one, dark one, Rādhē Gōvinda."

Key Takeaway
A string of Krishna's names, each followed by "Rādhē Gōvinda". The bhajan circles His names and Rādhā's.
For each of us: we can let the names flow, without thinking too much, and simply enjoy them.

Deeper Meaning
1. Gōvinda and Mādhava
Gōvinda the protector, Mādhava the Lord of Lakṣmī.

2. Gōpāla and Kēśava
The cowherd and the beautiful-haired one.

3. Nanda's Mukunda
Nanda's son who gives liberation.

4. Butter-thief (navanīta cōra)
His playful mischief is loved.

5. Mountain-holder (giridhārī)
Repeated twice: He is the one who protects.

6. The dark one (śyāmā)
"Śyāmā" is repeated four times. The dark beauty of Krishna fills the mind.

7. Rādhē Gōvinda
Every line ends with Rādhā's name joined to His.

8. Circling names
The names go round and round, like devotees walking around a shrine. Each round brings the mind closer.

9. Love in repetition
A mother calls her child's name again and again. The bhajan calls Krishna the same way.

10. Rādhā's place
Even in a bhajan about Krishna's names, Rādhā is never left out.

11. Easy for everyone
These simple names can be sung by children and elders alike, in any language.

@@ 22.25 | Vanamālī Vāsudēva |
Overall Summary
A short bhajan: "Wearer of the forest garland, Vāsudēva; victory, Gōkula's Rādhā Ramaṇa; Gōvinda, Gōpāla, O darling of Nanda."

Krishna, with His forest garland, is Rādhā's beloved and Nanda's darling.
For each of us: the simplest bhajans hold the deepest love.

He wears a garland of forest flowers. His beauty is natural and simple.

2. Vāsudēva
Son of Vasudēva, and the Lord who lives in all beings.

He delights in Rādhā in Gōkula.

We hail Him with joy.

The protector of cows and of the world.

Gōkula is the village of His childhood, full of cows, cowherds and love.

9. He Nanda Lālā
"O Nanda's darling!" The call is tender and personal.

The words are few, so the feeling matters most. Each "Gōvinda" can carry the whole heart.

11. A doorway
Short bhajans like this are doorways. They lead the mind into the Lord's presence quickly.

@@ 22.26 | Kṛṣṇa Bhajō Kṛṣṇa Bhajō |
Overall Summary
A Hindi bhajan: "Worship Krishna, worship Krishna; worship Muraḷī Gōvinda; worship Rādhē Gōvinda; worship Rādhē Gōpāla. Worship Giridhārī Śyām; worship Śyām Gōpāla who wanders in Madhuvana."

Key Takeaway
The bhajan simply says: worship Krishna, in all His forms.
For each of us: "Krishna bhajō" is an invitation we can give ourselves every day.

Deeper Meaning
1. Worship (bhajō)
"Bhajō" means worship, sing to, love. It is the root of "bhajan".

2. Krishna
The first word is the whole teaching: Krishna.

3. Muraḷī Gōvinda
Krishna with the flute, the protector.

4. Rādhē Gōvinda, Rādhē Gōpāla
Rādhā's name joins His again.

5. Giridhārī Śyām
The dark one who held the hill.

6. Wandering in Madhuvana (madhuvana sañcārī)
He wanders in the forest of Madhuvana.

7. An invitation to all
The song is addressed to everyone. Its call is simple: worship Krishna.

8. Repetition as practice
"Bhajō" is repeated again and again. The song itself is the practice it asks for.

9. Many forms, one Krishna
Muraḷī, Rādhē, Giridhārī, Śyām: every form is the same Krishna.

10. Madhuvana
His wandering in the forest of Madhuvana is a picture of His freedom and joy.

11. Bhajan as life
To worship Krishna is not only to sing once; it is to live with Him in mind.

@@ 22.27 | Gōpāla Rādhā Lōla |
Overall Summary
A bhajan in Rāgam Māṇḍ: "Gōpāla, who loves to be with Rādhā; who loves the flute, Nanda's darling; Kēśava, Mādhava, Janārdana; Nanda's darling, child of Vṛndāvana."

Krishna loves Rādhā and His flute. He is Nanda's darling and the child of Vṛndāvana.

The one with beautiful hair, who killed the demon Kēśi.

The one people pray to, who removes their troubles.

The bhajan returns to "Nanda Lālā". Love keeps coming back to the beloved.

@@ 22.28 | Pāsuram – Tuppuḍaiyārai |
Overall Summary
A Tamil verse from the Divya Prabandham in Rāgam Tilaṅg (traditionally by Periyāzhvār), to Raṅganātha: "I have come to You, the one people go to as the only true support, without tiring, though I have no merit. You are the wonder who showed grace to the elephant. When old age and weakness come and torment me, I will not be able to think of You then. So I am telling You now, for that time: O You who sleep in Śrīraṅgam."

Key Takeaway
The Āzhvār asks the Lord now, while he can, to remember him at the end, when he will be too weak to call.
For each of us: we can pray today for the end of our lives. Trusting God now prepares us for then.

Deeper Meaning
1. The true support (tuppuḍaiyārai)
People go to the Lord as the strong, true support. The Āzhvār has come to Him too.

2. Without merit (oppu ilēn āhilum)
He admits he has no merit, yet he has come. Surrender does not wait for worthiness.

3. The elephant's rescuer (yānaiyukku nī aruḷ seyda)
The Lord saved the elephant who called Him. The Āzhvār hopes for the same.

4. When old age comes (eyppu ennai vandu naliyum pōdu)
At the end, weakness will make it hard to think of God.

5. I tell You now (appōdaikku ippōdē solli vaittēn)
So he tells the Lord now, while he can: remember me then.

6. Sleeping in Śrīraṅgam (araṅgattaravaṇaip paḷḷiyānē)
The Lord sleeps on the serpent in Śrīraṅgam. His rest is our safety.

7. A lifelong prayer
This verse is often recited by the elderly and by all who want the Lord's care at the end.

@@ 22.29 | Mukha Lāvaṇyācī Khāṇī |
Overall Summary
A Marathi abhaṅg (signed by Nīḷā): "His face is a mine of beauty, like the full moon."

A crown, earrings and forest garland; a tilaka of saffron and musk. Jewelled bracelets and rings; yellow silk tied neatly at the waist. A girdle shining at His waist; His curved anklet-bells ring. Nīḷā says: hands on His hips, His body's glow is enchanting.

Key Takeaway
The abhaṅg describes Viṭhala (Krishna) from head to foot, as a devotee gazes lovingly at Him.
For each of us: looking carefully at the Lord's form, in an image or in the mind, is a way to meditate.

Deeper Meaning
1. A mine of beauty (lāvaṇyācī khāṇī)
His face is like a mine full of beauty. The more we look, the more we find.

2. Like the full moon (candra pūrṇa)
His face is bright and cool like the full moon.

3. Crown, earrings and garland
The abhaṅg begins at His head and moves down, like a devotee's eyes.

4. Saffron and musk tilaka
His forehead mark is fragrant. Every detail is precious.

5. Yellow silk and girdle
His waist is adorned. The devotee notices every ornament.

6. Hands on His hips (kaṭīkarā)
This is Viṭhala's pose in Paṇḍharpur, hands on His hips, waiting for His devotees.

7. Nīḷā's signature (nīḷā mhaṇē)
Nīḷā, a Vārkarī poet, signs the abhaṅg. The devotees' gaze becomes song.

8. Looking with love
The abhaṅg is like a devotee standing before the image, eyes moving from crown to feet, missing nothing.

9. Simple details
Each ornament is named simply. Devotion makes ordinary details precious.

@@ 22.30 | Nāgaralāl Naravaralāl |
Overall Summary
A Hindi bhajan in Rāgam Dvijāvanti: "Clever darling, best among men, darling! Beautiful darling, served by the king of gods; best of gods, served by Brahmā and others; best of the Yadus, who taught a lesson to the Yavana; darling of very great beauty; delightful darling, served by the lord of love."

Key Takeaway
Krishna is called "lāl", darling, with every quality. He is clever, beautiful and served even by the gods.
For each of us: calling God "darling" is not disrespect. It is the language of love.

Deeper Meaning
1. Lāl, darling
"Lāl" is a word of deep affection, used for a beloved child or dear one.

2. Clever (nāgara)
He is clever and refined. Mīrā called Him "Giridhara Nāgara".

3. Best among men (naravara)
He is the finest of all men.

4. Served by Indra and Brahmā (surapati … brahmādi sēvita)
The gods serve Him.

5. Teacher of the Yavana (yavana śikṣaka)
He defeated the invader Kālayavana. He protects His people.

6. Very great beauty (ati ati lāvaṇya)
His beauty is repeated twice for emphasis.

7. Served by the lord of love (ratipati sēvita)
Even Kāma, the god of love, serves Him. All love comes from Him.

8. A Hindi bhajan
Sung in Hindi, this bhajan brings the mood of Vraja into the gathering.

9. Lāl again and again
Each line ends with "lāl". The word of affection becomes the song's heartbeat.

10. Dvijāvanti
The rāga is gentle and evening in mood, fit for tender praise.

@@ 22.31 | Kṛṣṇaṁ Vandē Nanda Kumāram |

Krishna and Rāma are bowed to together. The Krishna chapter ends by joining both.
For each of us: we can bow to every form of God in which we find love.

The bhajan is a simple act of bowing.

2. Krishna, Nanda's son
The cowherd child, loved by His foster family.

4. Butter-thief
His mischief is remembered with fondness.
