The prince of Ayōdhyā.

6. Sītā's Lord, jewel of the Raghus
Rāma with Sītā, the pride of His dynasty.

7. One bow for both
The same bow is given to Krishna and Rāma. In this tradition, they are one Lord.

8. Raghu kula tilaka
Rāma is the jewel on the forehead of His dynasty, its glory and pride.

9. Navanīta cōra
Krishna as butter-thief and Rāma as jewel of His clan: playfulness and dignity side by side.

10. A fitting end
The Krishna chapter closes by bowing to both Krishna and Rāma, reminding us that they are one Lord.

11. Vande, I bow
"Vandē" means "I bow". The bhajan ends in humility.

@@ 23.1 | Viṭhalā Yērē Paṇḍharī Rāyā |
Overall Summary
A Marathi abhaṅg (signed by Ēka Janārdana): "Viṭhala, come; King of Paṇḍharpur, Viṭhala, come!"

Holder of the conch and discus, of the Vaijayantī garland, of yellow silk, of the Gōvardhana hill; shining with the tulasi garland; Hari who rides Garuḍa, lotus-naveled. With the liṅga on His head, of beautiful form, in the company of good people; jewel of the Yadu clan, father of Manmatha; purifier of the fallen, ocean of mercy. Form of the three worlds, universal form; who removes the fear and heat of worldly life and sin; Rukmiṇī's husband, joy of beauty. Mother and father, there is none but You. On the bank of the Candrabhāgā, You stand on the brick; Raṅga stands at the great door; Garuḍa at the main gate. Janārdana Svāmi pleads, says Ēknāth.

Key Takeaway
Viṭhala of Paṇḍharpur is mother and father to His devotees. The abhaṅg calls Him to come.
For each of us: we can call the Lord with the simple word "come", as the Vārkarīs do.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come (yērē)
The whole abhaṅg is a call: come, Viṭhala!

2. King of Paṇḍharpur (paṇḍharī rāyā)
Viṭhala is the king of Paṇḍharpur, beloved of the Marathi saints.

3. Śiva on His head (mastaka liṅgā)
Viṭhala's image is said to carry a Śiva-liṅga on His head. Hari and Hara are one.

4. Mother and father (janani janaka)
He is both mother and father. There is no one else.

5. On the brick (ubhā vīṭēvarī)
He stands on the brick by the Candrabhāgā river, waiting for His devotees.

6. Remover of fear and sin (bhava bhaya tāpa)
He removes the fear and pain of worldly life.

7. Ēka Janārdana
The signature names Ēknāth, devotee of his Guru Janārdana Svāmi.

8. Hari on Garuḍa
He rides Garuḍa, His swift eagle. He comes quickly to His devotees.

9. Holder of the hill
He held Gōvardhana; He holds us up too.

10. The Vārkarī call
"Yērē" is the call heard all along the road to Paṇḍharpur.

@@ 23.2 | Dhanya Dhanya Hē Nagarā |
Overall Summary
A Marathi abhaṅg (signed by Bhānudās): "Blessed, blessed is this city, Paṇḍharpur, Vaikuṇṭha on earth! Blessed is the Candrabhāgā, with Puṇḍalīka standing in its middle. Blessed is the flute-player, Gōvinda, playing there. Blessed is Padmālaya's Lord (Viṭhala), who came with the forest garland. Blessed is living in Paṇḍharpur, sings God's servant Bhānudās."

Key Takeaway
Paṇḍharpur is called Vaikuṇṭha on earth. Everything about it is blessed.
For each of us: a holy place reminds us that heaven can be found on earth, where devotees gather.

Deeper Meaning
1. Blessed (dhanya)
The word is repeated for every part of Paṇḍharpur.

2. Vaikuṇṭha on earth (bhū vaikuṇṭha)
Paṇḍharpur is called heaven on earth. The Lord lives there among His people.

3. The Candrabhāgā
The river Bhīmā, called Candrabhāgā at Paṇḍharpur because it curves like a half-moon.

4. Puṇḍalīka in the middle (madhyē puṇḍalīka ubhā)
Puṇḍalīka's shrine stands in the river. His devotion brought Viṭhala here.

5. The flute-player (vēṇu nātha)
Viṭhala is Krishna with the flute.

6. Living in Paṇḍharpur (paṇḍharīcā vāsa)
Living there is a blessing, close to the Lord and His devotees.

7. Bhānudās
The abhaṅg is signed by Bhānudās, a saint who brought Viṭhala's image back to Paṇḍharpur.

8. God's servant Bhānudās (dēvā gāyē bhānu dāsā)
Bhānudās calls himself God's servant. The saints were happiest as servants.

9. Dvārakā and Paṇḍharpur
Viṭhala is Krishna who came from Dvārakā to Paṇḍharpur for Puṇḍalīka's sake.

10. Heaven among devotees
Paṇḍharpur is heaven because the Lord and His devotees are there together.

@@ 23.3 | Nāmācā Bhajāra Paṇḍharī |
Overall Summary
A Marathi Paṇḍharī abhaṅg: "Paṇḍharpur is the market of the Name! Hari's name, the Lord's name, Viṭhala's name: Paṇḍharpur is the market of the Name."

Taking the Name and giving the Name lifts up crores of generations. Saints meet saints, crying out "jay jay". In Āṣāḍha and Kārtika the pilgrimages fill it; Viṭhala is the chief of it. The Gōpāla-purī feast takes place, with the shower of puffed rice. The servants, in lakhs, have held on to Hari's door.

Key Takeaway
Paṇḍharpur is a "market" where the Name is traded freely. Taking and giving it lifts up whole families.
For each of us: we can share the Name freely, like a trader who wants everyone to have it.

Deeper Meaning
1. The market of the Name (nāmācā bajāra)
In a market things are bought and sold. In Paṇḍharpur, the only goods are the Name.

2. Taking and giving (ghētī … dētī)
Devotees take the Name and give it to others. The more it is shared, the more it grows.

3. Crores of generations lifted (kōṭi kuḷaṁ uddhāra)
The Name lifts up countless generations of a family.

4. Saints meeting saints (sant milāvē)
Saints meet and cry out "victory". The company of devotees is joy.

5. The pilgrimages (āṣāḍha kārtika yātrā)
Twice a year, huge crowds of Vārkarīs walk to Paṇḍharpur, singing.

6. The Gōpāla-purī feast (gōpāla purī kālā)
At the end of the pilgrimage, a feast is shared. Food and devotion go together.

7. Holding Hari's door (dharilē hariccē dvārā)
Lakhs of devotees hold on to the Lord's door. They will not let go.

8. Viṭhala is the chief
In this market, Viṭhala is the chief trader, giving His Name freely to all.

@@ 23.4 | Rāgi Tandīrā Bhikṣakē |
Overall Summary
The same Kannada song by Purandaradāsa as 7.4, in Rāgam Āhir Bhairavi, opening "Viṭhala, Viṭhala, Viṭhala, Pāṇḍuraṅga".

A wandering devotee asks: "Have you brought rāgi (alms) for the beggar?" But he means: have you become worthy, become fit to enjoy, become fortunate? Have you become one who gives food and builds rest-houses for the hungry? Have you given up idle talk and do you sing bhajans every day? Do you serve your mother and father, have you given up sinful deeds, do you live by right conduct and avoid craving for fame? Do you always remember Lakṣmī's Lord, do you bow to your Guru, have you crossed the worldly life without melting, and do you serve Purandara Viṭhala?

Key Takeaway
The real alms God asks of us is a good life. Each "rāgi" is a quality to become, not grain to give.
For each of us: the song is a checklist for a devotee's life: feed others, serve parents, live honestly, sing His name, honour the Guru.

Deeper Meaning
1. A clever play on words (rāgi)
"Rāgi" is millet, the simple grain given to a beggar. It also sounds like "-āgi", "having become". Purandaradāsa turns a request for alms into a question about who we have become.

2. Becoming worthy (yōgyarāgi)
The first thing asked is whether we have become worthy. Devotion changes us, step by step.

3. Feeding others (annadānava māḍuvarāgi)
Giving food is praised again and again in this tradition. Feeding the hungry is feeding God.

4. Daily bhajan (anudina bhajanaya)
Leaving idle talk and singing His name every day is one of the marks of a devotee.

5. Parents and Guru (mātā pitaranu … guruvigē bāgōvanta)
Serving parents and bowing to the Guru are part of the path. Respect for those who raised and taught us is respect for God.

6. Serving Viṭhala (purandara viṭhalana sēvipa)
Every quality in the list leads to this: becoming one who serves the Lord. That is the true alms.

7. Same song, two places
The book has this song in the Purandaradāsa chapter (7.4) and again in the Viṭhala chapter. Both carry the same teaching.

@@ 23.5 | Rūpa Pāhatāṁ |
Overall Summary
A Marathi abhaṅg of Sant Jñānēśvar (signed "Bāpa Rakhumādēvī varu"), in Rāgam Basant: "Seeing His form with my eyes, O friend, I became happy. He is the fine Viṭhala; He is the fine Mādhava." The chant "Viṭhala Viṭhala Pāṇḍuraṅga" follows. "Because of a store of many good deeds, I came to love Viṭhala. He is the home of all happiness: the husband of Rakhumādēvī, my father."

Key Takeaway
Seeing Viṭhala's form brings pure happiness. Jñānēśvar says it is the fruit of many good deeds.
For each of us: if we feel drawn to God, it is a blessing earned over many lives. We can treasure it.

Deeper Meaning
1. Seeing His form (rūpa pāhatāṁ)
Just seeing Viṭhala with one's own eyes brings joy.

2. O friend (sājaṇī)
Jñānēśvar speaks to a friend, sharing his joy.

3. The fine Viṭhala, the fine Mādhava (baravā)
He calls Viṭhala and Mādhava "fine", lovely. Simple words carry great love.

4. A store of good deeds (sukṛtāñcī jōḍī)
Love for Viṭhala came from many good deeds. Devotion is a precious gift.

5. Home of all happiness (sarva sukhācēṁ āgāra)
Viṭhala is the source of every happiness.

6. Rakhumādēvī's husband (rakhumādēvī varū)
Viṭhala is Rukmiṇī's husband. Jñānēśvar's signature, "Bāpa Rakhumādēvī varu", calls Him "father".

7. Jñānēśvar
Sant Jñānēśvar, the great saint-poet of Maharashtra, wrote the Jñānēśvarī. His abhaṅgs are full of simple love.

8. A friend's joy
He cannot keep the joy to himself; he tells his friend. Real devotion wants to share.

9. Basant
Rāgam Basant (Vasant) has the freshness of spring. It suits the joy of seeing Viṭhala.

@@ 23.6 | Viṭhala Viṭhala |
Overall Summary
A short chant: "Viṭhala, Viṭhala, Viṭhala, Viṭhala, Pāṇḍuraṅga." Then: "Victory, Hanumān; victory, victory, Hanumān."

Key Takeaway
Viṭhala's name, chanted again and again, is followed by victory to Hanumān. The Lord and His servant are honoured together.
For each of us: "Viṭhala" is a gentle name we can repeat whenever we need comfort.

Deeper Meaning
1. Viṭhala
Viṭhala is Krishna standing on the brick in Paṇḍharpur. His name is the heart of the Vārkarī tradition.

2. Pāṇḍuraṅga
"Pāṇḍuraṅga" means the white-bright one, another name of Viṭhala.

3. Repetition
The name is repeated four times in one breath. The rhythm draws the mind in.

4. Hanumān
The chant then turns to Hanumān, the perfect servant. Wherever the Lord is sung, His servant is honoured.

5. Victory (jai)
"Jai" celebrates Hanumān's strength and devotion.

6. Two moods
The gentle Viṭhala and the mighty Hanumān sit side by side. Devotion holds tenderness and strength.

7. A short break
This short chant gives a breath between longer songs, keeping the Name flowing.

8. Viṭhala and Hanumān together
In Maharashtra, Hanumān shrines stand near Viṭhala temples. The chant joins them as the devotees do.

9. Sung in a circle
Short chants like this are often sung while walking in a circle, with cymbals.

10. A breath of joy
The chant is brief, but it leaves the heart lighter.

@@ 23.7 | Gōvinda Raṅga Viṭhala |
Overall Summary
A chant: "Gōvinda Raṅga Viṭhala; Śrī Gōpāla Raṅga Viṭhala; Raṅga Raṅga Viṭhala; Śrī Pāṇḍuraṅga Viṭhala; friend of the humble, Viṭhala; O ocean of mercy, Viṭhala; Pāṇḍuraṅga Viṭhala; Viṭhala of Paṇḍharpur."

Key Takeaway
Gōvinda, Raṅga and Viṭhala are one Lord. He is the friend of the humble and an ocean of mercy.
For each of us: when we feel low, we can remember He is "dīna bandhu", the friend of the humble.

Deeper Meaning
1. Gōvinda Raṅga Viṭhala
The chant joins Krishna (Gōvinda), Raṅganātha (Raṅga) and Viṭhala.

2. Gōpāla Raṅga
The cowherd and the Lord of Śrīraṅgam are one.

3. Raṅga Raṅga
"Raṅga" repeated: the Lord who delights.

4. Pāṇḍuraṅga
The white-bright Lord of Paṇḍharpur.

5. Friend of the humble (dīna bandhu)
He is the friend of those who are poor and lowly.

6. Ocean of mercy (dayā sindhu)
His mercy has no shore.

7. Viṭhala of Paṇḍharpur
The chant ends at Paṇḍharpur, the home of Viṭhala.

8. Raṅga and Viṭhala
Raṅganātha of the south and Viṭhala of the west are one Lord. The chant joins two great traditions.

9. Śrī Gōpāla
Krishna as cowherd, with Lakṣmī's grace (Śrī).

10. Many names, one heart
Each line has a different name, but the heart behind them is one: love for the Lord.

11. A chant for walking
Vārkarīs sing chants like this as they walk to Paṇḍharpur. Each step becomes a name.

12. Easy to learn
The chant is simple enough to learn in one hearing and carry home.

@@ 24.1 | Bhāgyada Lakṣmī Bārammā |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Madhyamāvati: "Come, Lakṣmī of good fortune, come, our Mother, Lakṣmī of blessed fortune!"

Come step by step, making the sound of the bells on your feet; come at the time of the good people's worship, like butter rising in buttermilk. Come showering gold; show the fulfilment of the heart's wishes; Sītā, daughter of King Janaka, shining with the light of a crore of suns. Stay always in the devotees' homes, with constant festivals and constant auspiciousness; the doll shining in the hearts of good people who show the truth. Give countless good fortune; come turning the bangles on your hands; lotus-eyed one marked with kuṅkum, proud queen of Vēṅkaṭaramaṇa. Come at the time of the Friday worship, making streams of sugar and ghee flow; queen of the loving Alagiri Raṅga, of the handsome Purandara Viṭhala.

Key Takeaway
Lakṣmī is invited into the home like a beloved mother, step by step, with jingling anklets. She brings every good fortune.
For each of us: we can welcome good fortune into our homes by welcoming the Mother with devotion and good conduct.

Deeper Meaning
1. Come (bārammā)
The song is an invitation to Lakṣmī to come into the home.

2. Step by step (hejjeya mēle hejjeya)
She is asked to come slowly, step by step. Grace enters gently.

3. Like butter in buttermilk (majjige yoḷagina beṇṇe)
Butter rises from buttermilk after churning. Lakṣmī rises in the home where good people worship.

4. Showering gold (kanaka vṛṣṭi)
She brings prosperity. Wealth blessed by her is good.

5. Stay in devotees' homes (bhaktara maneyali)
She is asked to stay in the homes of devotees, with constant festivals.

6. Queen of Vēṅkaṭēśa (vēṅkaṭaramaṇana rāṇi)
She is the consort of the Lord of Tirumala.

7. Friday worship (śukravārada pūje)
Friday is Lakṣmī's day. This song is sung at Friday pūjās in many homes.

8. Jingling anklets (gejje kālgaḷa dhvani)
Her anklets ring as she walks in. The sound itself is auspicious, like a festival beginning.

9. Sītā too
She is also Sītā, Janaka's daughter. Lakṣmī comes as Sītā with Rāma.

@@ 24.2 | Kṣīrābdhi Kannike Śrī Mahālakṣmī |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa, asking Lakṣmī whose bride she will be: "Daughter of the milk-ocean, Śrī Mahālakṣmī, whose bride will you be?"

To Rāmacandra who bridged the sea? To the Supreme Soul Ananta Padmanābha? To Śrī Janārdana with the lotus navel? To the Raṅganātha of Śrīraṅgapaṭṭaṇa between the two Kāvēris? To the handsome Cenna Kēśava of Bēlūr? Tell me, friend, to Śrī Krishna of Uḍupi? To Pāṇḍuraṅga Viṭhala on earth? Tell me, lotus-eyed one, to Badari Nārāyaṇa? To Bindu Mādhava, fragrant like sandal? To the easily-reached Puruṣōttama? To the giver of fruits and constant blessings? Don't be shy, lovely one, tell me: to Śrī Vēṅkaṭēśa? To Varadarāja of Kāñci, worshipped by Indra? To Ādi Varāha of Śrī Muṣṇam? To Śrīman Nārāyaṇa resting on Śēṣa? To Alagiri's Lord of a thousand names? To Sāraṅgapāṇi, protector of those who surrender? To Śrīnivāsa, giver of boons? To Rājagōpāla who ended the Kuru clan? To the steady Purandara Viṭhala?

Key Takeaway
Lakṣmī is teased: which Lord will you marry? The list of temples shows that every one is the same Lord, her husband.
For each of us: wherever we worship Viṣṇu, the same Lord and Mother are there.

Deeper Meaning
1. Daughter of the milk-ocean (kṣīrābdhi kannike)
Lakṣmī rose from the churning of the milk-ocean.

2. Whose bride? (yārige vadhuvāguve)
The question is playful, like friends teasing a bride.

3. A pilgrimage of temples
The song names Śrīraṅgapaṭṭaṇa, Bēlūr, Uḍupi, Paṇḍharpur, Badarī, Kāñci, Śrī Muṣṇam, Tirumala, Kumbakōṇam (Sāraṅgapāṇi) and more. It is a pilgrimage in song.

4. All are one
Every Lord named is Viṣṇu. Lakṣmī is the bride of all, because they are one.

5. Don't be shy (nāchade hēḷu)
Her friends ask her to speak without shyness. The tone is warm and family-like.

6. The steady one (sthiravāgi)
The song ends with "the steady Purandara Viṭhala". The composer's own Lord is the answer.

7. Mother and Lord together
Lakṣmī is never apart from Viṣṇu. Wherever He is, she is.

8. Daughter of the ocean
Born from the milk-ocean, Lakṣmī is the pure treasure that came out of the churning.

9. A teasing song
The playful questions are like those friends ask a bride. Devotion can be warm and joyful.

@@ 25.1 | Vandē Śāntam |
Overall Summary
A Sanskrit song in Rāgam Madhuvanti: "I bow to the peaceful Śrī Hanumān, Rāma's servant, spotless and mighty."

His throat is full of the nectar of love; tears flow from his eyes; his body shines with thrill. He sees everything as full of Rāma; he always chants "Rāma, Rāma". He walks the path of true devotion, and brings joy to those bound to Viṭhala.

Key Takeaway
Hanumān is peaceful, mighty and full of love for Rāma. He sees Rāma everywhere and chants His name always.
For each of us: Hanumān shows that great strength and deep gentleness can live together in a devotee.

Deeper Meaning
1. Peaceful (śāntam)
The mighty Hanumān is called peaceful. True strength is calm.

2. Rāma's servant (rāma dāsa)
His greatest title is "servant of Rāma".

3. Spotless and mighty (amalam balavantam)
He is pure and strong. His strength serves only good.

4. Throat full of love (prēmāmṛta galam)
His throat is choked with the nectar of love. He is moved to tears by Rāma's name.

5. Thrill in his body (pulakāṅkita vapuṣa)
His body thrills with devotion. Love fills him completely.

6. Seeing Rāma everywhere (sarvaṁ rāma mayaṁ)
Everything he sees is full of Rāma.

7. Always chanting (sadā japantam)
He chants "Rāma, Rāma" always. He is the model of nāma japa.

8. The true path of devotion (sat bhakti patham)
Hanumān walks the true path of devotion and shows it to others.

9. Joy to Viṭhala's devotees
He brings happiness to those bound to Viṭhala. The Marathi saints loved Hanumān too.

10. Madhuvanti
The rāga is soft and devotional, fitting a song about Hanumān's tender love.

@@ 25.2 | Sāri Bandanē |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Pūrvikalyāṇi about Mukhya Prāṇa (Hanumān, Bhīma and Madhva): "He has come, announcing himself; the Lord of life has come!"

He came, crossed into Laṅkā and saw the proud Rāvaṇa: son of the Wind, Śrī Rāma's messenger, who lovingly brought the ring to Sītā. He is Bhīmasēna, Kuntī's son, who stayed in King Virāṭa's city and killed Kīcaka. He is Madhvarāya, best of the all-knowing, who uplifted the Madhva teaching: the messenger of dear Purandara Viṭhala.

Key Takeaway
In the Madhva tradition, Hanumān, Bhīma and Madhvācārya are three forms of the same devoted soul. Each served the Lord in his age.
For each of us: devotion can show itself in many forms: as courage, as strength and as teaching.

Deeper Meaning
1. He has come (sāri bandanē)
The song announces his arrival with joy.

2. Lord of life (prāṇēśa)
In the Madhva tradition, Vāyu (the Wind, life-breath) is the chief devotee of Viṣṇu. Hanumān, Bhīma and Madhva are his forms.

3. Hanumān in Laṅkā
He crossed to Laṅkā, faced Rāvaṇa and brought Rāma's ring to Sītā.

4. Bhīma and Kīcaka
As Bhīma, he protected Draupadī by killing Kīcaka in Virāṭa's city.

5. Madhvācārya (madhva rāya)
As Madhvācārya, he taught the path of devotion to Viṣṇu.

6. Three ages, one devotion
In each age, the same devoted soul served the Lord.

7. Purandara Viṭhala's messenger
Purandaradāsa, a follower of Madhva, calls him "the messenger of Purandara Viṭhala".

8. The ring for Sītā (mudrikeya tandittavanē)
Hanumān brought Rāma's ring to Sītā in Laṅkā, giving her hope. His service carried love between the Lord and His beloved.

9. Purandaradāsa's devotion
Purandaradāsa was a follower of Madhva. This song shows his reverence for the line of devotion.

@@ 25.3 | Āñjanēya Āñjanēya Rāma |
Overall Summary
A chant to Hanumān sung to an ārati tune: "Āñjanēya, Āñjanēya, Rāma! Brave Āñjanēya!"

Lord who achieves the impossible, brave one; of wonderful valour, steady one. Who enjoys drinking the nectar of Rāma's name; protector of devotees who chant Rāma's name. Form of intelligence, strength, fame and courage; destroyer of ghosts, spirits and black magic. Son of the Wind, bearing a part of Śiva; protect, protect, You who are praised by Muraḷīdhara.

Key Takeaway
Hanumān achieves the impossible and protects all who chant Rāma's name. He is strength, wisdom and courage.
For each of us: when a task seems impossible, we can remember Hanumān and Rāma's name.

Deeper Meaning
1. Āñjanēya
Son of Añjanā. The chant calls him by his mother's name.

2. Achiever of the impossible (asādhya sādhaka)
He did what no one else could: leaping the ocean, finding Sītā, carrying the mountain.

3. Wonderful valour (adbhuta parākrama)
His courage is beyond imagining.

4. Drinking Rāma's name (rāma nāmāmṛta pāna)
He enjoys Rāma's name like nectar.

5. Protector of those who chant (rāma nāma japa bhakta pālaka)
He protects all who chant Rāma's name.

6. Intelligence, strength, fame, courage (buddhi bala yaśō dhairya)
These are the gifts of remembering Hanumān, as the famous verse says.

7. Praised by Muraḷīdhara (muraḷīdhara nuta)
The last line names Muraḷīdhara, the composer, possibly Śrī Muraḷīdhara Svāmigaḷ.

8. Bearing Śiva's part (śiva aṁśa dhāra)
Tradition says Hanumān carries a part of Śiva. He joins the devotion of Śiva and Viṣṇu.

9. Destroyer of evil spirits
He drives away ghosts, spirits and black magic. Devotees chant his name for protection.

@@ 25.4 | Kūsina Kaṇḍīrā |
Overall Summary
A Kannada song by Purandaradāsa in Rāgam Sindhu Bhairavi: "Have you seen the baby? Have you seen Mukhya Prāṇa? Have you seen the child? Have you seen the very strong one?"

The baby was born from Añjanā's womb; the baby bowed at Rāma's feet; the baby gave the ring to Sītā; the baby burnt the city of Laṅkā. (As Bhīma) the baby swallowed a cartload of food, took Baka's life, ate the poisoned laḍḍu, and gave the flower to his wife. (As Madhva) the baby defeated the māyāvādins and uplifted the Madhva teaching. By Purandara Viṭhala's grace, the baby simply stood in Uḍupi.

Key Takeaway
Purandaradāsa lovingly calls the mighty Hanumān-Bhīma-Madhva "the baby". His great deeds are told with tenderness.
For each of us: even the mightiest devotee can be loved as a child.

Deeper Meaning
1. The baby (kūsu)
"Kūsu" is a baby. Purandaradāsa calls Hanumān a baby out of affection.

2. Born of Añjanā (añjanē udaradi)
Hanumān was born to Añjanā.

3. Rāma's servant
He bowed at Rāma's feet, gave Sītā the ring and burnt Laṅkā.

4. As Bhīma
He ate a cartload of food, killed Baka and survived the poisoned sweets.

5. The flower for his wife (maḍadige puṣpa)
Bhīma brought the Saugandhika flower for Draupadī. Love for family is honoured.

6. As Madhva
He defeated those who opposed devotion and uplifted Madhva's teaching.

7. Standing in Uḍupi (uḍupili niṇṭitu)
By Viṭhala's grace, he stands in Uḍupi, near Krishna's temple founded by Madhva.

8. A loving tease
Calling the mighty Hanumān a "baby" is Purandaradāsa's loving joke. Great devotees are close enough to tease.

9. One soul, three lives
Each line moves through Hanumān, Bhīma and Madhva. The baby grows through the ages.

@@ 25.5 | Māruti Rāyā Vānarā Rāyā |
Overall Summary
A Marathi song in Rāgam Señjuruṭṭi: "Māruti, king; king of the monkeys; I bow at your feet. You worship Śrī Rāma and sing God's qualities."

Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa, Bharata and Śatrughna, with their protector Hanumān; the one Śrī Bhagavān rules the kingdom of Ayōdhyā. Rāma-Jānakī, Krishna-Vāsudēva, Rādhē Gōvinda; Harē Rāma Harē Krishna, Rakhumāī Pāṇḍuraṅga; Rāma-Sītā, Krishna-Rādhā, Viṭhala-Rakhumāī; enchanter of the gōpīs' hearts, all-pervading Hari who holds everything. Vēṅkaṭaramaṇa, remover of troubles, with lotus-petal eyes.

Key Takeaway
Hanumān is bowed to as the protector of Rāma's family. The song then joins all the divine couples: Rāma-Sītā, Krishna-Rādhā, Viṭhala-Rakhumāī.
For each of us: honouring Hanumān leads us to Rāma and to every form of the Lord.

Deeper Meaning
1. Māruti, king (māruti rāyā)
Hanumān, son of the Wind, is honoured as a king.

2. King of the monkeys (vānarā rāyā)
He is the leader among the vānaras.

3. I bow at your feet (namitō mī pāyā)
The devotee bows to Hanumān.

4. Singing God's qualities (guṇa gāyā)
Hanumān sings Rāma's praise. He is the model singer.

5. Protector of Rāma's brothers (kaivārī hanumān)
He protected Rāma and His brothers.

6. All the divine couples
The song joins Rāma-Sītā, Krishna-Rādhā and Viṭhala-Rakhumāī. They are one Lord with His Śakti.

7. Vēṅkaṭaramaṇa
It ends with the Lord of Tirumala, remover of troubles.

8. Rakhumāī Pāṇḍuraṅga
Viṭhala and Rukmiṇī are named. The song brings Paṇḍharpur into Hanumān's praise.

9. All-pervading Hari
The Lord is everywhere and holds everything. Hanumān leads us to Him.

10. Remover of troubles
Vēṅkaṭaramaṇa removes difficulties. The song ends with a promise of help.

11. Bowing to Hanumān
Bowing to the servant pleases the Master.

@@ 26.1 | Pavamāna Sutuḍu (Maṅgaḷam) |
Overall Summary
A Telugu maṅgaḷam by Tyāgarāja: "To the lotus feet held by the son of the Wind (Hanumān), to Your name and form, eternal victory and auspiciousness!"

To Your name and form, praised by Prahlāda, Nārada and other devotees: eternal victory and auspiciousness. O lotus-eyed one, praised by Tyāgarāja and others: to Your name and form, eternal victory and auspiciousness.

Key Takeaway
The bhajan ends by wishing eternal victory to the Lord's name and form. Hanumān holds His feet; the devotees praise Him.
For each of us: we leave the bhajan with a blessing on the Name we have been singing.

Deeper Meaning
1. Held by Hanumān (pavamāna sutuḍu baṭṭu)
Hanumān holds the Lord's lotus feet. He is the model of loving service.

2. Name and form (nāma rūpamulaku)
The blessing is for the Lord's name and form. The bhajan has been about both.

3. Eternal victory and auspiciousness (nitya jaya maṅgaḷam)
We wish the Lord eternal victory and blessing.

4. Praised by Prahlāda and Nārada
The great devotees praised Him. We join them.

5. Lotus-eyed (rājīva nayana)
His eyes are like lotuses, gentle and kind.

6. Tyāgarāja's signature (tyāgarājādi vinuta)
Tyāgarāja names himself among those who praise the Lord.

7. A closing blessing
This maṅgaḷam is sung at the very end of many concerts and bhajans, closing with grace.

8. Prahlāda and Nārada
Prahlāda's devotion as a child and Nārada's endless singing are the models of the Name tradition. We bless the Lord they praised.

9. Ending in blessing
We do not leave the Lord with requests, but with good wishes. That is how a bhajan ends.

@@ 26.2 | Māṇikkam Kaṭṭi – Divya Prabandham |
Overall Summary
A Tamil lullaby to Krishna in Rāgam Nīlāmbari from the Divya Prabandham (traditionally by Periyāzhvār), each verse ending "tālēlō" (sleep).

"Brahmā sent You, with love, a lovely little cradle made of fine gold, set with rubies and with diamonds in between: O little Vāmana, sleep; You who measured the world, sleep. The gods in the vast sky brought You a right-whorled conch, little bells for Your red feet, bangles for Your hands and a waist-chain: O dark cloud with red eyes, sleep; O lion of Dēvakī, sleep."

Key Takeaway
The gods bring gifts for baby Krishna's cradle. The Āzhvār sings the Lord of the universe to sleep.
For each of us: the bhajan ends with tenderness, putting the Lord to sleep in our hearts.

Deeper Meaning
1. A lullaby (tālēlō)
The book ends with a lullaby. After the long bhajan, the Lord is sung to rest.

2. A golden cradle (māṇikkam kaṭṭi … ponnāl seyda)
Brahmā sent a golden cradle set with rubies and diamonds. The gods honour the baby.

3. Little Vāmana (māṇi kuṛaḷanē)
The baby is called the little Vāmana, the dwarf who grew to measure the world.

4. Who measured the world (vaiyam aḷandānē)
The tiny baby once took three steps across the universe. Smallness and greatness are one in Him.

5. Gifts from the gods
The gods bring a conch, little bells, bangles and a waist-chain. Every devotee wants to give.

6. Lion of Dēvakī (dēvaki siṅgamē)
The baby is called Dēvakī's lion cub. He is gentle and strong.

7. Ending in love
The book ends not with philosophy but with a lullaby. Love is the last word.

8. Periyāzhvār's love
Periyāzhvār, who loved Krishna like a mother, sang many lullabies to Him. Āṇḍāḷ was his foster daughter.
